The Lady Under the Waves

The Lady Under the Waves

A novel — first draft

Part One The salvage partnership

Chapter 1 Impossible recoveries

The charge for drying the wool was larger than the charge for bringing it up from the sea.

Lyra put her thumb beneath the entry and read it again. Outside her window a woman was calling for the boy who delivered charcoal. She had been calling for some time. Each call added to what she intended to do when he came.

The wool had spent six months in the wreck of the Mallow. Its recovery had taken two days, according to the statement. Eleven bales had been accepted in Silkstrand, one refused, and the eleven had then spent seventeen days in Master Hest’s drying house at a daily rate which ought to have purchased them a room with a view.

She turned to the captain’s account. It gave the depth as forty-six fathoms.

Below that, in another hand, someone had written that the twelfth bale had been sold.

Lyra took a fresh sheet. She had meant to finish a letter about a delayed marriage portion before breakfast, and the portion was already late enough that no one involved would thank her for a well-composed additional delay. She copied three questions about the wool, set the disputed account aside, and returned to the letter. The father’s promises improved considerably when she removed the phrases about affection.

Her employer came up while she was sealing it.

“You haven’t eaten.”

“I haven’t finished.”

“The woman downstairs says the two are connected.”

“She would like her plate back.”

“She would. Have you looked at Hest’s account?”

Lyra showed her the questions.

The woman read them standing, her gloves caught beneath one arm. She handled correspondence for four houses now, though she continued to speak of the fourth as though it had imposed itself upon her during a moment of weakness. Lyra had begun by reading one letter for her. These days they divided commissions according to whose clients were most likely to wait.

“They want an answer before noon,” she said.

“They’ll have questions before noon. Who brought this?”

“A man with a chain round his waist.”

“A great many men have chains round their waists.”

“This one’s looked as though it had been there longer than he had. He paid the reading fee.”

Lyra glanced at the little pile of silver left beside the ink. Full payment, without a request to deduct the cost from whatever she recovered. She separated the woman’s share and put her own in the drawer.

“Low Bell Yard,” her employer said. “Ask for Maeron.”

“The captain?”

“I didn’t ask. I was trying to find out whether the water coming off his coat was going to stain the floor.”

After she left, Lyra ate the cold egg. The yolk had set against the plate. She scraped it up with bread and read the depth once more.

Forty-six fathoms, if the figures were true. Two days, if the figures were true. There was enough wrong with the ordinary charges that she would prefer the extraordinary ones to be somebody else’s concern.

Low Bell Yard stood behind a row of houses whose upper rooms had been joined across the lane. Washing hung beneath the joining passage, low enough that Lyra had to move a damp sleeve away from her face. A bell without a clapper was fixed above the gate. Someone had painted the words DELIVERIES AFTER FIRST WATER on a board below it, then crossed out AFTER and written BEFORE.

A woman was combing wool on a bench in the yard. She had a shaved patch above one ear and hair on the other side long enough to put behind her shoulder. Her bare feet rested on a coil of rope.

“Maeron?” Lyra asked.

“Which business?”

“Hest’s.”

The woman set the comb down.

“At last.”

“I’ve had it since yesterday.”

“Then you are quicker than Hest.” She pointed toward a door. “In there. Mind the step.”

The step was missing. Lyra crossed the gap and found a narrow room with a stove at one end and a table occupying most of the rest. Three men stood around an open chest. One held a candle over it although daylight reached the window. The smell was wet iron, sheep grease, and a sharp sweetness she could not place.

The man at the head of the table looked up.

“Mistress Lyra?”

“Yes.”

He put down a piece of cloth he had been inspecting. He was older than she had first taken him for, with close-cut dark hair and lines around a mouth that rested slightly open. One sleeve had been mended with thread of a different color. The repair was neat. Someone had taken trouble over it.

“I’m Maeron. Thank you for coming.”

“I haven’t agreed that anything is owed.”

“Corven said you would tell us whether it was.”

The largest man moved away from the window. He had a length of dark chain wrapped twice around his waist. The links were flattened on one side, and markings filled the worn metal. He lifted a cup from the only unoccupied stool.

“Sit,” he said.

Lyra sat. Maeron drew the chest away from the place where she would put her papers. He did it himself, though the younger man had already reached to help.

“May I see the original agreement?” she asked.

Corven opened a leather wallet and laid out three folded sheets. Their corners had gone soft with damp. Lyra put her own copy beside them.

“You agreed to deliver at Hest’s landing.”

“We delivered there.”

“And he refused a bale.”

“He said water had got into it.”

“Had it?”

Corven looked at the open chest. “It came from a wreck.”

“That’s not quite the same answer.”

The younger man laughed. Corven gave him a brief look, and he occupied himself with the candle.

“Yes,” Maeron said. “The outer wrapping had opened. The wool had taken water.”

“The other eleven?”

“Come and see,” said the woman from the yard.

She had followed Lyra into the room. She picked up the piece Maeron had been inspecting and passed it over. The fleece was supple beneath its layer of grease. Lyra brought it to her nose. Beneath the smell of sheep was the sea, faint but distinct.

“He kept the refused bale,” the woman said. “I asked for it back. He said that once a load had entered his drying house it could not be removed until the account was settled.”

“Your name?”

“Neris.”

“Were you present for the sale?”

“I carried the bale out for the man who bought it.”

Lyra looked up.

Neris shrugged. “He was old. It was heavy.”

“Do you know his name?”

“His boat had a green cabin and a yellow kettle nailed over the door.”

The younger man said he knew it. His sister bought lamp oil from the owner. Corven sent him to find out whether the boat remained at its usual place.

Lyra read the original agreement twice. Hest had included drying space in the purchase, subject to inspection within two days. The wool had been inspected on the second. He had charged for drying from the first through the seventeenth, and for storage after purchase.

“He owns them from the inspection,” she said.

Maeron leaned forward. “That’s what I understood.”

“Then he’s charging you to store his wool.”

“He says the purchase is incomplete until we accept his account.”

“He has sold part of it.”

Neris grinned, showing a chipped front tooth. “Tell him that.”

“I intend to.”

They were quiet while she copied the relevant wording. She could hear a little movement in the chest, too slight to be a loose object falling. When she glanced toward it, Maeron put the lid down.

“What is the depth on this statement?” she asked.

“Forty-six fathoms,” Neris said.

“And you raised this wool in two days?”

“We were lucky with the water.”

Lyra waited.

Neris leaned against the table. The nails on two fingers were ridged and pale. “It was where we expected. That’s most of a recovery. You can lose a week looking at the wrong bit of bottom.”

“You were down there?”

“Yes.”

Corven unwound the chain from his waist and laid it carefully on a folded cloth. Each link made less sound than Lyra expected.

“Does the depth alter what Hest owes?” he asked.

“No.”

“Then leave it for now.”

She looked at him. He did not seem angry. He had moved so that his back touched the wall, though there was room beside the stove.

Maeron said, “We can speak about the work when you’ve seen what comes of it.”

Lyra returned to the agreement.

Hest’s drying house smelled worse than Low Bell Yard. Heat rose through grates in the floor, carrying damp wool, smoke, and the sourness of water that had stood too long in a bucket. Two girls were turning fleeces with wooden forks. Hest sat in an upper office reached by a stair so narrow that people going in opposite directions had to settle the matter before either began.

He kept Lyra waiting. She used the time to ask one of the girls where the rejected wool had stood. The girl pointed with her fork, then looked toward the office.

“I don’t know which one you mean.”

“That’s all right.”

Lyra moved away before Hest could look down and find them speaking.

He came to the top of the stairs and called her up without asking her name. His coat was unbuttoned at the throat. A bowl of preserved plums sat beside the papers on his desk.

“I’ve explained the account,” he said.

“To people who couldn’t reconcile it with your agreement.”

“They can barely read their own agreement.”

“They hired someone.”

He looked at her properly then.

She laid the two statements before him. There was nowhere for her to sit. Hest did not offer his chair.

For the first few minutes he explained storage, drying, and inspection, as though her difficulty were with the words. Lyra let him finish. She asked when he had taken possession. He pointed to the inspection date. She asked when he had sold the refused bale. He said no such sale had occurred.

“The buyer’s downstairs,” she said.

He glanced at the stair.

“He remembers paying for it. He would like you to agree that he owns it before he sells the yarn.”

Hest put his hand over one of the papers.

“You people make these things unnecessarily difficult.”

“Which people?”

He took a plum. The stone came out cleanly between his fingers.

“There are arrangements in this ward.”

“I am asking about this arrangement.”

“And I am explaining why I can’t alter a standard charge for every boat that comes in.”

Lyra moved the original agreement from beneath his hand. “You wrote that the space was included. You can correct the account, or I can ask the receiving house which of your two instructions they intend to honor. They have three cargoes waiting on your marks this week.”

She knew one of the houses. Her employer handled its correspondence. The other two were names visible on the open page at Hest’s elbow. She had not promised that they would agree with her.

He made her wait while he sent for a clerk. The clerk was younger than Lyra, with a badly healed burn across his wrist. Hest told him there had been a misunderstanding over the date of transfer. The man looked at the agreement, at his own earlier account, and then at Lyra.

“All the storage after inspection?” he asked.

“All of it,” said Hest.

“And drying before?”

Hest breathed out through his nose. “The agreed allowance.”

Lyra stayed until the corrected amount had been counted into a bag. She paid the commission she had promised the wool buyer for his time. He returned two coins, saying he had been going that way in any case, and kept the third without apology.

When she brought the money to Low Bell Yard, Neris was standing in a tub washing the cuffs of her trousers. She stepped out with both feet at once.

“He paid?”

“Some charges were proper. I left those.”

“He paid,” Neris told Corven, who had appeared behind her.

Maeron counted the money at the table. He asked Lyra to explain each correction so that the others could recognize it on another account. By the time she finished, the young man with the candle had brought a second paper and Neris had remembered a sale of copper that might have been handled the same way.

“One at a time,” Lyra said.

Maeron smiled. “Will you take supper with us?”

She had intended to go home. There was another letter to finish, and someone would be annoyed by the morning if it remained unwritten. But Neris was already asking whether she ate fish, with the concern of a host whose menu would not survive the wrong answer.

“I do,” Lyra said.

“Good. There’s a great deal of it.”

Maeron gave her the agreed fee before supper. She put it away herself.

Chapter 2 The injured diver

The first thing Neris said after the sling failed was that the jars must be moved.

One had broken against the edge of the landing. Oil spread between the planks, yellow in the sunlight. Lyra stood with a list in her hand while people shouted conflicting instructions at the man holding the crane’s brake. The remaining jars swung in a wicker cradle above the boat. Beneath them, Neris sat against a mooring post with her left arm tucked across her belly.

“Move them,” she said again. “He can’t hold that.”

Corven went to the brake. The man already there had both feet braced and his chin against his chest. Together they brought the cradle inward until the load struck the quay. Someone dragged the broken jar away. Someone else fetched sand.

Lyra knelt beside Neris.

“Let me see.”

“It’s the arm.”

“I know.”

“It went between.”

Blood ran through the cuff in a steady, glossy stream. Lyra cut the sleeve up from the wrist with the small knife she used for sharpening pens. The cloth resisted, then opened. A narrow piece of wicker had driven into the flesh beneath the elbow. The wound beside it was longer and deeper than she wanted to see.

“Don’t pull that out,” she told the man reaching past her.

He withdrew his hand.

Neris watched her face. “Bad?”

“I’m going to press beside it. You must keep still.”

“I was planning a walk.”

Lyra folded her neckcloth and set it against the wound. Neris’s humor left her with the first pressure. She gripped Lyra’s shoulder and made a sound low in her throat.

The words came back before Lyra meant to seek them. One for the boundary of the injury, one for the joining. She could place her fingers on either side. She could hear where the phrase ought to lengthen. Her mouth began the first sound.

Then she saw a coat darkening beneath her hands, the edges drawing together in one place while blood continued to come from somewhere she could not reach.

“Lyra.”

Neris’s hand tightened.

She pushed the cloth harder against the wound. “I need clean linen. And someone who can take that piece out without breaking it.”

Corven knelt on the other side. He had brought a roll of cloth from a chest in the boat. He looked at the wound, then at her.

“I can do it.”

“Have you done it before?”

“Yes.”

She made him describe what he intended. He answered without taking offense. Neris listened too, her eyes fixed on his mouth. When he had finished, she nodded once.

“Get it done.”

They moved her into the shade. Corven held the arm and drew the splinter back along the way it had entered. Neris struck him in the chest with her good hand. He let her. The piece came free with a wet dragging sensation Lyra felt through the cloth. More blood followed. She held the wound until the flow slowed, then helped bind it above and below the elbow.

By the time a physician arrived from the next landing, Neris was swearing at the boy who had summoned him for saying that her arm had been torn off.

“It was very much on,” she said. “I showed you.”

The physician examined their work. He was a stout man who breathed through his mouth and had brought an apprentice to carry his case. He asked Neris to move each finger. She did so, with increasing impatience.

“You can keep the hand,” he told her.

“Thank you.”

“Whether you can keep your temper remains doubtful.”

He stitched the open part of the wound while Corven held her. Lyra watched the needle enter and leave. The physician’s apprentice caught each drop of blood before it could run down into her sleeve. No one asked why Lyra had not done more.

When they lifted Neris onto a cart, she asked for her knife.

“It’s here,” Corven said.

“Here where?”

He put it in her good hand. She closed her fingers over the sheath and let them carry her.

The shore house was two canals away, on a narrow lane where the upper windows leaned toward one another. Its door stood open. A woman with flour on her forearms came out when she heard the cart.

“Which room?” Corven asked.

“The front one. I changed the bed this morning, which ought to have prevented this.”

“Ask for your linen back,” Neris said. “See whether the crane cares.”

They carried her in. Lyra followed with the physician’s instructions, though Neris had heard every word and was unlikely to welcome a repetition.

The house smelled of baking, boiled fish, and a wash that had not dried completely. A man slept on a bench with his boots beside his head. Two women sat at a table cutting a large coat into smaller pieces. One looked up and asked whether Neris wanted her brother fetched.

“He’ll shout.”

“He’ll shout later if we don’t.”

“Then later.”

The woman with flour on her arms was Marda. She had already put a basin by the bed. She asked the physician what he needed, listened closely, and sent someone for each item. Her instructions were brisk without being hurried. She made room for the injured diver by moving everyone else.

Maeron arrived while Neris was drinking. He had been across the ward with a family whose boat was overdue. He sat beside the bed and waited until she finished the cup.

“They put the wrong sling on,” Neris told him.

“I’ve heard.”

“It was marked for jars, but someone tied the mark on the other one.”

“We’ll find out.”

“Don’t say it was bad fortune. I want the sling.”

“Corven has it.”

She settled back a little. Maeron took her good hand and held it while she described how the load had moved. He asked about a detail Lyra had missed: who had ordered the crane to turn after Neris shouted. Neris named the man, then said he had not heard her.

“Don’t make it his fault.”

“We can ask without deciding first.”

When Neris grew tired, Maeron stood. He tucked the blanket around her feet with an awkwardness that suggested he did not usually do that part of the work. Marda corrected one corner after he turned away.

Lyra went into the kitchen to wash the blood from her hands. It had worked beneath two fingernails. She scraped at it with the blunt edge of her knife until Marda put a brush beside the basin.

“You’ll take the skin off.”

Lyra used the brush.

Maeron came to the doorway. “You knew what to do.”

“I knew how to hold a cloth.”

“That’s often what is needed.”

She worked at a small dark crescent near her thumb.

He did not move closer.

“Neris thought you began a working.”

“No.”

After a moment he said, “You began to begin one.”

The brush stopped.

Marda took a tray from the table and went into the passage. She had not pretended to miss the exchange. Lyra preferred that to a sudden unnecessary interest in the fire.

“I used to know a little,” she said.

“Who taught you?”

“A man who knew more.”

Maeron accepted the answer. “Would you like to speak about it sometime?”

“With you?”

“If you wish.”

“Are you a physician?”

“No.”

“Then I don’t see why you would know.”

“I might not.”

She looked at him. His manner had not changed. He seemed quite willing to be useless to her. She had no answer ready for that.

“I know a few ways to begin again,” he said. “One might suit you.”

“And sometimes they make it worse.”

“Yes.”

He turned to answer Marda, who wanted to know whether the room at the back was still being kept for the overdue boat’s family. Maeron said it was. Marda asked how many nights, and he told her he would find the money for another week.

“Find it before the baker asks me,” she said.

“I will.”

He left without returning to his offer.

Lyra came back the following afternoon with the corrected inventory and a packet of dried apricots. Neris took one, complained that the physician had forbidden wine but apparently permitted fruit hard enough to break a tooth, and asked for a second.

Her brother had arrived. He was a broad man with a younger man’s small beard, and he had indeed shouted. He became quiet when Lyra entered, which told her that some of the shouting had concerned people he did not know well enough to accuse to their faces.

“She shouldn’t have been under the load,” he said at last.

“Nobody should have been under the load,” Neris said. “We were all beside it until it moved.”

“You know what I mean.”

“I know what you’re saying.”

Lyra put the inventory on the table. “The damaged sling has been set aside. No one will use it.”

“That helps this arm,” he said.

Neris turned her face away.

Her brother saw it. He sat down and put a hand on the blanket near her ankle.

“I brought your blue shirt.”

“The one with the patched cuff?”

“There are three with patched cuffs.”

“Then you had a good chance.”

He laughed, reluctantly, and she looked back at him. Lyra went into the kitchen to leave them alone.

Maeron was at the table with a boy who had drawn a boat on the back of a spoiled account. They were arguing about the sail. The boy said that if it were made larger the boat would go faster. Maeron asked what would happen when it turned over. The boy drew a second boat beneath the first.

“Then this one takes over.”

“That is expensive.”

“You always say that.”

“Marda has taught me.”

He looked up when Lyra sat across from him. The boy gathered his drawing and went to show Marda, who said the second boat would leak because he had forgotten its bottom.

“I don’t want you to sing at me,” Lyra said.

Maeron set his cup down.

“All right.”

“I want to know what you intend before you do it. If there is a working, tell me what it is meant to change. And if I tell you to stop, you stop.”

“Yes.”

She had prepared a longer argument. Its absence left her feeling exposed.

“I have reasons.”

“You don’t have to give them to me.”

“I may want to.”

“Then I will listen.”

From the other room came Neris’s voice, raised over her brother’s. They were disputing the color of something he had brought.

“Tomorrow,” Lyra said. “I can come after noon.”

Maeron asked whether she preferred the kitchen or the small room above it. She chose the kitchen, where people could pass through and Marda would be occupied with her own work.

“Bring what you used to practice with,” he said.

“I don’t have it.”

“Tell me what it was.”

She thought of Gyllan making her explain a procedure before he let her put her hands on a wound. The annoyance she had felt then returned so abruptly that she almost smiled.

“Usually somebody who had made a foolish decision.”

Maeron looked toward the bedchamber.

“We should be able to find one.”

She laughed. It surprised her, and she let it continue.

Chapter 3 The remembered working

Maeron had put a jug of water on the kitchen table, three clean cloths, and a bowl with a crack in its rim.

Lyra stopped beside the chair.

“I can’t mend pottery.”

“Neither can I. Marda says we must use that one because the good bowls are for food.”

He moved the chair so that she could see both doors. She noticed the movement and disliked herself for noticing it with gratitude. Then she sat.

Neris was asleep. Her brother had gone back to his own work after extracting promises from everyone in the house. Marda was at the far end of the kitchen cutting onions. Each time she finished one she wiped her eyes with the back of her wrist and blamed the man who had sold it to her.

“Where did you learn?” Maeron asked.

“At the University first. Later, on the road.”

She had been young when the Chain-Lanterns brought her there. Her Hyro village had burned; that much she knew. What came back was the cold edge of a doorstep under her feet, a bell swinging beside someone’s knee. A face hidden in a hood. Someone carrying her with her mouth against rough cloth and soot bitter on her tongue. She could not put those things in order. She did not remember arriving at the University.

“From the same man?”

“Gyllan. I became his apprentice there, later.”

He repeated the name once, to make sure he had heard it.

“What did he have you do first?”

Lyra looked at the cloths. “Describe the injury.”

“Then do that.”

“There isn’t an injury.”

“Describe the one you saw yesterday.”

She began with the arm. Maeron asked where the wound lay in relation to the elbow, what the fingers could do, how the blood came. Some of his questions were useful. One was foolish, and she told him so. He asked why. She explained the difference between what she had seen and what his question supposed.

“Good,” he said.

“You asked it on purpose?”

“No. I know less about arms than you.”

Marda laughed over the onions.

Lyra went back to the description. The details came readily when she did not have to put her hands anywhere. Neris’s fingers had been warm. She had moved them before the binding. The wound was deep enough to make pressure necessary but had not taken the large vessel Lyra had feared.

“And what did you mean the working to do?”

“Bring the edges together. Help the smaller vessels close.”

“All the edges?”

“No. Not with that piece still in it.”

“Did you know that yesterday?”

“Yes.”

“Say the words.”

She did, quietly. The phrase was in the form Gyllan had taught her, with an older word he insisted was more exact than the one in the common exercises. Maeron listened with his head slightly tilted. She stopped before the final clause.

“There,” he said.

Her hands had closed against the table.

“I know where I stopped.”

“What comes next?”

She told him.

“And what does it ask?”

“For the joining to remain after I take my hands away.”

“You can say that without doing it.”

“I know.”

He waited.

Outside, a cart wheel struck a loose stone with the same hollow sound three times. Lyra listened to it go down the lane. She put her hands in her lap so he could not see how firmly she had been pressing them against the wood.

“I couldn’t make it remain,” she said.

Maeron said nothing.

“There was more than one wound. I knew that. It wasn’t because I hadn’t seen.”

Her throat hurt. She had not intended to speak about this in a kitchen with an onion pan heating a few feet away. She wanted the bowls removed, the cloths folded, the lesson finished in a way that did not require her to say she was leaving.

“Were you alone?” he asked.

“No.”

“Who was with you?”

“Friends.”

He nodded. “Did one of them help you?”

“They all tried.”

Marda left the room carrying the pan. The onions went on sizzling as the sound moved away.

Lyra drew a long breath through her nose. “He was my teacher.”

Maeron rested his hands on the table, where she could see them.

“Gyllan?”

“Yes.”

For a while neither spoke. The silence had the ordinary noises of the house in it: a footstep overhead, a door sticking, someone coughing in the passage. Lyra could still hear Neris breathing in the next room. She held to that sound until she could look at him again.

“I thought you meant to help me do a working,” she said.

“I did.”

“Then help.”

He drew the cracked bowl closer. “Put your hands around this.”

“Why?”

“So that you can hear the whole phrase without anyone waiting for you to save them. The bowl will remain broken.”

She gave him a hard look, then set her hands on the cool pottery.

The first attempt ended in the same place. On the second she managed the final clause, though the last word came out too quickly. Maeron asked her to do it again without hurrying. By the fourth, irritation had overtaken embarrassment.

“I can pronounce it.”

“You can.”

“You don’t need to look pleased every time.”

He looked down, but she saw him smile.

She completed it once more. Nothing happened to the bowl. She lifted her hands.

“There,” she said.

“There.”

Marda returned and told them they must clear the table if they intended to use it for anything except eating. The lesson ended because twelve people wanted their supper bowls. Lyra helped put them out.

She did not return for three days.

There was work. A trader wanted a letter rendered into Vhasian without its suggestion that he had agreed to a marriage, and the suggestion occupied most of the letter. A woman needed an inventory made before her husband’s brothers came to count the contents of the house for themselves. Lyra went with her, wrote down what was present, and remained while the brothers arrived.

That evening she found the old phrase written on the back of an account she had meant to copy cleanly. She had put it there without deciding to. The final clause ran uphill toward the margin.

She read it aloud. Her room was warm. The lamp needed trimming, and the woman below was singing to herself while she worked. Lyra read the phrase again and went downstairs to borrow another wick.

On the fourth day, Neris met her at the shore-house door wearing a shirt her brother had cut along the sleeve so that she could get the binding through. The cut had been made with insufficient regard for how the shirt would later be repaired.

“He says a shirt costs less than an arm.”

“He’s right.”

“It was my good shirt.”

“He knows your bad ones. He brought three.”

Neris laughed and moved aside.

The physician had come that morning. The wound was clean. He had left a small area open where he wanted it to drain and had forbidden Neris from lifting anything heavier than a cup. She showed Lyra that she could lift two cups with one hand. Lyra told her that counting them differently did not make them lighter.

“You sound like him.”

“Your physician?”

“My brother.”

Maeron came down from the upper room carrying a torn net. He put it aside when he saw her.

“Would you like to continue?”

She had decided on the walk over that she would ask. It was easier to nod.

They began with Neris’s thumb, where a separate shallow cut had gone stiff at its edges. Neris agreed to let her work on it, then made Maeron fetch the bowl because it was his lesson and he could carry something.

Lyra washed her hands. She described what she saw and what she intended to change. Neris listened with unusual patience.

“Will it hurt?”

“It may pull.”

“Then say when.”

Maeron sat where Lyra could see him. He did not sing. When her breath shortened, he lifted one finger, then lowered it as she breathed out. It annoyed her enough to make her obey.

She placed two fingertips beside the cut. The skin beneath them was warm, its roughness familiar. She spoke the phrase, keeping her attention where the tissue ought to meet. At the last clause she felt the old hesitation rise, carrying the memory of blood through cloth.

Neris moved her free hand to steady the bowl.

Lyra finished.

The edges drew together. There was a small dark line between them, then a paler one. The working settled beneath her fingertips with a sensation like a thread ceasing to pull.

She lifted her hands.

Neris bent the thumb. Once, carefully, then farther.

“Well,” she said.

Lyra stared at it.

“It doesn’t catch.” Neris turned the hand over. “It was catching every time I did that.”

“Don’t keep doing it.”

“You have both become my brother.”

Maeron was smiling, but he did not speak until Lyra looked at him.

“You remembered.”

She put her fingers on the table. They were shaking.

“It’s a small cut.”

“It was mine,” Neris said. “I’m pleased.”

That made Lyra laugh, and then cry in a way she could not easily stop. Neris reached for her with the wrong arm, winced, and offered the good hand instead. Lyra held it. Maeron passed her a cloth and looked toward the window until she asked him for water.

Her first attempt alone was less graceful.

The woman in the room below had nicked a finger opening a packet with a kitchen knife. The cut was shallow and troublesome, on the side that touched her pen. Lyra asked whether she would like her to try something.

“With your little knife?”

“No.”

The woman listened to her explanation and considered the finger.

“It would save me getting ink in it.”

Lyra brought warm water. She took too long arranging the cloths, and the woman pointed out that the cut was likely to close on its own if they sat there until morning.

“You can say no,” Lyra said.

“I said yes. Sit down.”

She sat. There was no one across the table to remind her to breathe. She found her own pace. The woman watched with frank curiosity while the edges closed.

When it was done, she held the hand near the lamp.

“How useful.”

Lyra let out a breath that became a laugh.

“Would you rather I said it was marvelous?”

“No.”

“It is. But I shall be thinking about the ink.”

They had supper together. Lyra ate more than she had meant to and slept heavily. In the morning she went to Low Bell Yard before beginning her letters.

Maeron was speaking with a man whose daughter had taken a berth on a coastal boat. He finished the conversation before turning to her. She waited, her news getting harder to keep to herself.

“I did it again,” she said.

He did not ask what.

“Without you.”

“Good.”

“It was another small thing.”

“Was the person glad?”

“She was worried about ink.”

Maeron laughed. She wanted him to laugh again, and told him the rest of it: the bowl, the lamp, the woman’s accusation that she was arranging cloths until nature could spare her the trouble.

He listened as though there were nowhere else he needed to be. When Corven came to ask about the tide, Maeron raised a hand and let Lyra finish.

Chapter 4 After the hands are lifted

Neris wanted to go back to work before the physician thought she should.

She offered increasingly narrow definitions of work. Standing on a boat was not lifting. Holding a rope was not pulling it. Inspecting a sling was mostly looking, and if looking tired the arm she would close one eye.

Lyra found her on the landing with a coil over her good shoulder.

“Put it down.”

“I am taking it to someone who will put it down.”

“I can do that.”

Neris looked at her hands, clean except for a little ink beneath one nail.

“You’ll get rope on your clothes.”

“That is where rope goes when you carry it.”

She took the coil. It was heavier than she expected. Neris watched her adjust it without offering to take it back.

“Where?” Lyra asked.

“Corven’s boat.”

Corven was kneeling beside an open chest, scraping a pale deposit from a metal fitting. He looked from the coil to Neris’s bound arm.

“I told her,” Neris said before he could speak.

“Told her what?”

“That you needed it.”

He took the rope from Lyra and set it under an awning. Neris climbed onto the boat despite both of them telling her to wait. She sat on a closed chest with the satisfaction of someone who had reached a position from which she would be difficult to remove.

The boat was broader than the fishing craft moored beside it. Its deck had been strengthened around the mast, and a small winding frame occupied the stern. A copper helmet hung under the awning with its face turned toward the hull. Lyra touched the edge where someone had repaired a deep dent.

“Can you breathe in that?”

“For a while,” Corven said.

“What happens afterward?”

“You come up.”

Neris snorted.

He glanced at her. “Usually.”

Lyra moved her hand away. On a shelf beneath the awning stood a glass vessel wrapped in cord, its mouth covered with fine mesh. Something black moved inside it.

“Don’t tap,” Corven said.

“I wasn’t going to.”

“Everyone does.”

He lifted the vessel and tilted it toward the daylight. The creature within was no longer than his hand, a narrow eel with a blunt pale head. Lyra looked for eyes and found none. Its mouth opened once.

“Does it have a name?”

“No.”

“He calls it that,” Neris said.

“I don’t.”

“He says No every time it tries to bite him.”

Corven set the vessel down and took a little fish from a covered dish. He cut a piece small enough for the eel, removed the mesh, and let the food fall. The creature moved with abrupt purpose. For a moment its body seemed too long for the vessel, folded around itself in a space that could not have held it straight.

“What does it do?” Lyra asked.

“Tells me when the weather is turning.”

“How?”

“Badly, until you learn it.”

He replaced the mesh. Lyra watched the eel settle into the shadow of its own vessel.

Corven resumed scraping the fitting. It guided a turning rope clear of the boat’s side. The pale crust had worked between its moving surfaces.

“Wouldn’t it be quicker to replace it?”

“Yes.”

“Then why don’t you?”

“We don’t have another.”

She looked at the rest of the chest. Several fittings lay in compartments, each different enough that she could see why they would not serve. There were new pieces of leather beside old buckles, needles, wax, and a roll of cloth too fine for ordinary wiping.

“How long to have one made?”

“Four days if the maker has the metal. Nine if he has to buy it. Longer if we wait to pay.”

“And this takes?”

“Until it’s done.”

She crouched beside him. “That is the answer people give when they don’t want a cheaper arrangement.”

He stopped scraping.

Neris looked delighted.

“Two mornings,” Corven said.

“How much is a morning of yours worth when the boat can go out?”

“You tell me. You’re the one reading our accounts.”

She took a piece of spoiled paper from her bag and began asking questions. Corven answered reluctantly at first, then with more precision when she distinguished work he could do while they were moored from work that delayed everyone else. Neris supplied figures for a rope they had bought twice because the first had been cut into shorter lengths for another job.

By noon Lyra had a list of five things that cost more to lack than to purchase. Corven tried to add a new boat. She handed the paper back and asked him to show her where he intended to keep it.

That week they took on a lighter sunk beside a ruined quay. It lay in shallow water compared with the Mallow. The cargo belonged to three people, two of whom had already paid someone else to tell them it could not be reached without breaking it.

Lyra went to inspect the approach because she did not understand Corven’s drawing. He had marked the quay with one line and the boat with another. Between them he had written BAD.

The reason became apparent when they arrived. Broken masonry lay beneath the water, its edges visible when the surface cleared. A mooring post leaned over the gap where the quay had failed. Children had tied a rope to it and used it to lower a bucket.

“Nobody goes on that,” Corven told them.

One boy said his father used it.

“Then tell your father.”

“He won’t listen to me.”

Corven considered that, took a piece of chalk from Lyra’s bag without asking, and marked the post with a large cross.

“Tell him I did that.”

The boy looked from the chain at Corven’s waist to the mark.

“Who are you?”

“Ask at Low Bell Yard.”

Lyra held out her hand for the chalk. He returned it.

They walked the sound part of the quay. The narrow lane behind it would admit a handcart but not a wagon. A baker’s oven projected from one wall at knee height, and the house opposite had a step that took another portion of the passage.

“You planned to bring everything up here?” she asked.

“Where else?”

“Into the boat, then to a landing that takes a cart.”

“Another journey.”

“One journey with a load instead of twelve journeys carrying it through someone’s bread.”

He looked at the oven. Its owner was watching them through a door.

“He won’t let us use the lane until he’s finished his baking.”

“When does he finish?”

“Ask him.”

She did. The baker said he finished when the bread was ready, which brought Corven a little satisfaction. Lyra bought a roll and asked where the baker received his flour. He showed her a courtyard reached by a wider lane on the other side of the block.

The courtyard belonged to a woman who sold empty barrels. She would allow use of it if they moved nothing without asking and kept the broken masonry away from her stock. Lyra offered a fixed payment for the day and a second, smaller one if they needed the next morning. The woman wanted the full amount twice. They settled somewhere between.

On the way back, Corven said, “You haven’t asked how we’ll lift it.”

“Would my answer improve yours?”

“Probably not.”

“Then I would like to see.”

They worked in the early water, while mist still lay between the moored boats. Lyra stood in the hired courtyard checking the cargo as it came in. She had borrowed a stool and put it beside the wall. Each time a cart passed, the man pushing it warned her about the same loose stone.

On the third warning she moved the stone herself.

Near midday, a shouted request brought her to the quay. One chest had lodged beneath a fallen beam. Corven was in the water. He had been below long enough that Lyra kept looking at the place where the rope entered it.

Maeron stood at the edge with his hands on the line. His sleeves were rolled above his elbows. Neris sat in the boat, permitted to watch on condition that she lifted nothing. She was already making suggestions nobody wanted.

“Can he come up?” Lyra asked.

“He can,” Maeron said.

“Then why doesn’t he?”

“He is deciding whether the beam will move.”

The rope shifted. Maeron bent and touched the water with two fingers.

Lyra heard nothing at first. She felt the sensation in her back teeth, a pressure as though she had clenched her jaw without knowing it. The surface beside the rope went smooth. Little ripples continued elsewhere, but within that patch the water seemed to have forgotten the wind.

A bubble rose. Then three more, large and slow.

The line jerked twice.

Maeron lifted his fingers. The pressure eased so suddenly that Lyra swallowed.

Corven’s head broke the surface beside the boat. He took a breath, put his hands on the gunwale, and stayed there while Neris swore at him for making the wrong signal first.

“The beam’s free,” he said.

“You could have said that from up here.”

“I am up here.”

They pulled the chest out. Its wood had darkened to the color of wet earth. One iron band was bent inward, though the others remained straight.

Lyra went back to the courtyard and watched them open it before the owner. Inside were wrapped bundles of copper wire. The owner put his hand over his mouth and stood that way until someone asked him to move so the lid could be taken clear.

“My daughter sold her wedding chain for this,” he said.

No one answered. He lifted a bundle, checked its weight, and laughed once with his hand still over his mouth.

Lyra marked the recovery and let him sit on her stool.

Neris’s brother brought supper to the shore house that evening. He had bought meat, which made Marda inspect him with the attention she usually gave someone arriving injured.

“What have you done?”

“Nothing.”

“Then why all this?”

“We thought she might like it.”

Neris came into the kitchen with her arm supported against her chest. Her brother put the covered dish down and drew out a chair. She sat in it before he had finished arranging it, obliging him to let go.

Lyra had stayed to give Maeron the recovery account. She offered to take her bowl elsewhere, but Neris told her to remain.

“He’ll have bought enough for six. He doesn’t know what people eat.”

“There are potatoes beneath it,” her brother said.

“Then eight.”

They ate together. The meat had been cooked in a sauce so thick that it held the marks of the spoon. Neris asked who had made it. Her brother named a woman at his yard, and Neris became interested in his private life with an energy he appeared to regret encouraging.

“She cooks for everyone,” he said.

“Does everyone get a covered dish?”

“I bought the dish.”

“You thought of it yourself?”

He looked toward Lyra for support. She took another potato.

After a while he stopped defending the meal and told them about a boat he was repairing. The owner insisted that a leak in the stern had begun only after the repair to the bow. Neris asked whether it had previously been too full of water to notice where the rest came in. Her brother laughed, then gave her the exact words he intended to use when he explained the problem to the owner.

He said the owner had paid with a coin fit for a dead man’s eye, which led Neris into the story of the smuggler who took such a coin and found its owner sitting in his hold. He couldn’t give it away or throw it overboard; it kept coming back.

“You have to take him to the water where he drowned,” she said. “He was saving his passage money.”

“Does the smuggler get home?” Lyra asked.

“With a hold full of fish. Unless my brother tells it. Then the ghost wants the boat repaired first.”

Her brother reached for the last potato. “A man can drown twice in some of the boats people bring me.”

Lyra listened as they went back to timber and fittings. Neris knew the work ashore as well as the work below it. She knew which timber would take the curve he needed and which supplier would charge for seasoning the wood had never received. Her brother listened to her advice without the anxious interruptions he used when discussing her arm.

“You could stay with us until you’re fit,” he said when the food was gone.

Neris wiped the edge of her plate with bread.

“I have a bed here.”

“I know. There’s a room over the shop now. We moved the sailcloth.”

“With that great window.”

“We could hang something.”

She looked at him properly. He had been rehearsing this, Lyra thought, while buying the dish and getting it covered for the walk.

“You’d spend the whole day asking whether I needed anything.”

“I work during the day.”

“You’d send someone.”

He smiled because she had guessed correctly.

Neris put the bread down. “I will come for a meal when I can carry something back. I don’t want to move all my things for a few weeks.”

“You have hardly any things.”

“Then it should be easy to leave them where they are.”

He accepted the refusal with less difficulty than Lyra expected. He asked whether she had enough clean shirts and was told not to cut any more of them. When he left, he took the empty dish and promised to return it to the woman who had cooked.

Neris watched him go. She sat a little longer before getting up.

“He’d make the room nice,” she said.

“I think so.”

“He’d keep asking whether it was nice enough.”

Lyra stacked their plates. “Would you tell him?”

“Eventually.”

Marda came to take the dishes. Neris thanked her for the bed with a sudden directness that made the older woman pause.

“You can have it as long as you need,” Marda said.

Neris nodded. She went back to the front room and closed the shutter halfway before lying down.

Neris’s arm did not heal as quickly as she hoped. At the next dressing, Lyra found the skin drawing tightly when she bent the elbow. She could have softened it with a working. She knew how, and it made her impatient to leave Neris uncomfortable when her hands might help.

“Would it help tomorrow?” she asked the physician.

He looked at her over the binding.

“Or only now?” she added.

“A sensible question.”

“It isn’t mine.”

“You asked it.”

They examined the arm together. He had her watch the fingers as Neris bent them. They agreed on an exercise, a lighter binding, and no further working that afternoon.

Neris accepted the exercise more readily than Lyra expected. She practiced it while complaining about the physician’s fee.

Later, Lyra sat with Maeron in the small yard behind the shore house. Someone had planted herbs in a broken washing tub. Marda was drying stockings on a line above them, and water dropped intermittently onto the table.

“Gyllan used to ask what would happen when I took my hands away,” Lyra said.

Maeron turned his cup to avoid a falling drop.

“Did you find it irritating?”

“Very.”

“You miss being irritated by him.”

She looked at the herbs. “I miss him being there when I have an answer.”

Maeron sat with that for a while.

“You can tell me,” he said at last. “I won’t always understand it.”

“I know.”

He laughed, and she leaned back in her chair with her cup between her hands. She told him about the physician, and Neris, and the man who had put his face into the recovered copper as though he could smell his daughter’s chain in it. Maeron listened. When Marda brought out the last of a cake, they divided it carefully and ate without speaking.

Chapter 5 A share of the work

By the time the heat settled over the canals, Lyra had three boxes of papers at Low Bell Yard and no agreed place to put her feet.

The table had been moved twice. Each time, someone promised more room and someone else ended up standing in the light. She had acquired a chair whose back leaned farther than she liked, a cup Neris insisted was hers because nobody else would tolerate its handle, and a reputation for being present when Maeron could not be found.

A boy came to ask whether he should buy the cheaper rope.

“What did Corven say?”

“To buy the good one.”

“Then buy it.”

“The man says this one’s good.”

“Is he the man selling it?”

The boy considered this, then went away.

She returned to the freight account. A few minutes later Marda came to ask whether the allowance for the shore house could be increased before the next recovery. Three people had arrived with a boat damaged too badly to repair in a week. One had brought his mother.

“Does Maeron know?”

“He sent them.”

“Then he ought to know what it costs.”

“He knows afterward.”

Marda set a list beside the freight account. The larger items were food and lamp oil. Beneath them she had written thread, shoes, and a second bucket. Lyra asked about the shoes.

“One of the men sold his before he came here.”

“Will the work pay for them?”

“His foot’s swollen. He won’t work this week.”

Lyra moved the other papers away. They worked through the list together. Marda had remembered things that never appeared on Maeron’s estimates: fuel consumed by heating water for the ill, linen that did not survive washing, a fee paid to the woman who sat with a frightened guest through the night so Marda could sleep.

“You should be paid too,” Lyra said.

“I am.”

“Separately from what the house needs.”

Marda looked at her. “Then it would need more.”

They found money by postponing the purchase of a decorative lamp intended for the front room. Maeron had seen it in a shop and thought the room deserved something beautiful. Marda said it did, but on balance she preferred shoes.

Maeron came in while they were calculating what remained.

“My lamp?” he asked.

“Your lamp,” Marda said.

He sat on a chest and listened. When they finished, he agreed to the increased allowance and asked Lyra to stay after Marda had gone.

Lyra gathered the freight papers.

“If you want someone to admire the lamp with you, ask Neris.”

“I want you to tell me what you charge for this.”

“For what?”

He indicated the table, the boxes, the cup with its uncomfortable handle.

“All of it.”

She stopped arranging the papers.

He had paid each commission promptly, never asking gratitude to cover the fee. Yet the separate commissions had accumulated until she was spending three afternoons a week in his yard and thinking about its missing fittings during work for someone else.

“I’ll make an account,” she said.

“That isn’t what I meant.”

“It’s what you asked.”

He smiled a little. “Then let me ask properly.”

Outside, someone called for Corven. A hammer began striking metal in irregular pairs. Maeron waited until the noise stopped.

“The crews know how to reach what is below. We know people who will buy it. Between those two things, we keep losing money we should have brought home. You have shown us how often.”

“Some losses are ordinary.”

“I know. You tell me about those as well.”

“And some recoveries are too expensive to attempt.”

“Which you tell me before we attempt them.”

He leaned forward, his forearms on his knees.

“I would like you to take a share.”

Lyra looked at the open door. A man carried a broken oar past it with the careful attention ordinarily given to something valuable.

“A share of which part?”

“The business. Its earnings, after the crews and expenses. Its losses as well, within what we agree.”

“And what would I be allowed to decide?”

“What you have already begun deciding.”

“That won’t do.”

“Then say what will.”

She wanted to answer at once. The wanting made her cautious. She had been useful in rooms where useful people acquired duties more readily than authority. She had been given access that could be withdrawn when she used it inconveniently. Maeron was watching her, and she could not yet tell whether he understood the difference she meant to insist upon.

“I need to think,” she said.

“Take the time.”

“You may dislike the answer.”

“I may.”

He rose when someone called his name from the yard. At the door he turned back.

“It would be good work, Lyra.”

She was angry with him for saying the thing she had already begun to think.

Her employer was less surprised than she expected.

“I wondered how long it would take them.”

“To ask?”

“To notice that you were making decisions for them.”

They sat in the lower room with the windows open. The street below smelled of hot stone and a drain that needed clearing. Lyra had brought the remaining letters she owed. She had not yet brought an answer to the offer.

“You could keep the northern correspondence,” the woman said. “I have nobody else who gets the tone right without making the men sound more important than they are.”

“I haven’t accepted.”

“No. But you have considered which work you would miss.”

Lyra laid the letters down.

“I don’t want to leave you short.”

“Then don’t leave on a morning when three ships have arrived.”

“You make it sound simple.”

“Finding another reader won’t be simple. That is my difficulty.”

The woman shifted her chair nearer the window. The movement let Lyra see the strain in her face, the practical concern beneath the generous answer.

“Are they paying you properly?”

“They have.”

“And if the ships stop coming up?”

“That is one of the questions.”

“Make them answer before you decide how grateful you are.”

Lyra smiled. “I have heard myself say that.”

“I have heard you say it to me.”

They arranged a month in which Lyra would finish her outstanding commissions and help another reader learn the difficult houses. She would retain two clients. If the salvage partnership failed, she would have work to return to, though less of it than before.

Walking home, she bought fish she had meant to leave until it was cheaper. The seller wrapped it in a leaf and asked whether she wanted the head. She said yes, though she had no use for it, and had to go back to ask how it should be cooked.

She ate late. Afterward she wrote to Riarhin about the work. She described the recovered copper and the injured diver. Near the end she put down that she had begun practicing again. She did not explain how difficult it had been to begin.

She reread the letter in the morning and let the sentence remain.

Corven found the proposal too long.

“If a man has to read this much before agreeing, he’ll be dead before the boat leaves.”

“You don’t have to read it before every boat.”

“That’s fortunate.”

Maeron sat on the other side of the table with a clean copy before him. He had read it twice and made three small marks beside passages he wanted explained. Neris leaned in the doorway, officially present to supply figures and in practice to enjoy Corven being made to listen.

Lyra began with the crews. When money came in, they would set aside the agreed wages before paying for another recovery. They would also provide for anyone injured on the job. Equipment promised to one recovery could not be lent to another unless the person running the first agreed.

Corven stopped objecting at that point.

“Put the collars in by name.”

“They are equipment.”

“Someone will decide they aren’t.”

She added the collars.

Maeron asked about the authority to refuse a recovery. Lyra explained that she would not be answerable for paying people if he could commit money she had already promised elsewhere.

“Sometimes a family needs something brought up before we can be certain what else is there,” he said.

“Then we decide how much we can afford to spend helping them.”

“And when it is more than that?”

“We ask for help. We don’t quietly take it from a man whose wages are due.”

He looked down at the proposal.

“Will you tell the family?”

“If it is my refusal, yes.”

He made another mark. “Then I agree to that.”

They argued over the share. Lyra asked for less than Maeron first offered because she could not bear the same proportion of an unlimited loss. He said he had not meant to make the loss unlimited. She pointed to where they needed to say so.

Neris fetched more water. The morning lengthened. Once, Corven got up to settle a dispute outside and returned carrying a scale weight, which he put on Lyra’s papers to keep them flat.

The last question concerned the work below.

“I don’t know what your rites cost,” Lyra said. “I know what a rope costs, and a fitting, and the time of the people using them. When a working leaves someone unable to take the next berth, I need to know that too.”

“Sometimes we cannot know beforehand,” Maeron said.

“Then afterward. And before we ask the person again.”

“Yes.”

Corven looked at Maeron, then back at the page.

“And nobody loses their place at the house because they refuse a dive,” Neris said.

Lyra turned toward her.

“That belongs there.”

Neris did not smile. “I think so.”

They put it in.

The signing was less ceremonious than Lyra expected. Maeron wrote a clear, narrow hand. Corven made his name slowly, then asked whether he must do it on every copy. Neris signed after washing the grease from her good hand. There were two blots, one of them Lyra’s.

When it was finished, Maeron took a small brass key from his pocket.

“The money chest,” he said.

“Who has the other?”

“I do.”

She took it. It was warm from his coat.

Her first refusal came before the ink had fully dried on the last copy.

A woman wanted the partnership to recover a chest from a boat lost near the outer bar. She had brought a drawing made by the boat’s owner, who was dead. The chest contained tools her son needed to take up his father’s work.

Corven inspected the drawing. Maeron asked about the loss. Lyra asked what had already been recovered and who had tried. By the time the woman finished, they knew the boat had broken against shifting stone and that the chest might have been carried well away from where it sank.

They could spend a week looking. They could also lose equipment they needed for a promised recovery that would pay six households before the next rent day.

Lyra said they could not undertake it at present.

The woman waited for Maeron to correct her.

He did not.

“My son can work with you,” she said. “Until it’s paid.”

Lyra asked his age. He was old enough to work, young enough that his mother still gave the age carefully, as though a different answer might help.

“What does he know?”

“His father’s trade.”

“Then he ought to be doing that.”

“He needs the tools.”

There was no useful answer she could give without repeating the refusal. She felt Maeron watching her. She kept her attention on the woman.

“Leave us a list,” she said. “I can ask whether some can be borrowed.”

“Borrowed tools have to go back.”

“Yes.”

The woman left the list and went away. Lyra sat for a while with the key in her hand.

Maeron said, “You could have asked me to tell her.”

“I said I would.”

“I know.”

That afternoon he found three of the tools through people who came to the shore house. Lyra arranged the loan and paid a small sum for the repair of a fourth. The woman’s son came to collect them. He thanked them with a reserve that made Lyra think his mother had described the morning accurately.

He did not join a salvage crew. He went to work in another yard and brought the borrowed tools back in the autumn.

The crew held supper for the partnership in the yard. Someone scrubbed the table. Someone else found a cloth that nearly covered it. Neris brought a bottle she had been saving for a reason she refused to explain, and Corven arrived with a loaf so large that Marda accused him of buying a seat cushion.

Lyra had intended to wear her ordinary coat. At the last moment she changed it for one with better cuffs. Neris noticed and made room beside her without comment.

There were more people than Lyra knew by name. A wife who repaired gloves, a boatman who sang badly after his second cup, a man who had lost most of one ear and kept asking whether the fish had bones. Children came and went, carrying bread away from the table in pieces too large for their hands.

Maeron waited until they had begun eating before raising his cup.

“To Lyra,” he said.

The others took it up. She felt herself smile and could not arrange her face into anything less pleased.

“You have made us richer already,” Neris said.

“Hest made you poorer first.”

“We drank to him too. Different words.”

They laughed. Lyra drank. The wine was better than she expected, and Neris looked satisfied when she said so.

Later, when the plates were empty and the children had been sent home in reluctant groups, Maeron came to sit beside her. The yard had grown quiet enough that they could hear Corven washing cups at the pump.

“Are you glad?” Maeron asked.

She looked at the table, the lamp hung from a repaired beam, the people who had stayed to help clear away.

“Yes.”

He rested his hand beside hers. She took it and held it for a moment.

“So am I,” he said.

Neither of them hurried to add anything.

Chapter 6 The shore congregation

Ernet brought the wrong ink.

He had been the young man holding the candle when Lyra first came to the yard. Since then he had learned to recognize three grades of rope and had married a woman who considered his employment a hopeful beginning. He was still capable of returning from a shop with something that had been described to him as almost the same.

“It is black,” he said.

“It is black dye.”

“The man said you could write with it.”

“You could write with lamp soot if you had nothing else.”

“Should I get that?”

She looked at him. He began to grin.

“I’ll take it back.”

“Ask for the sample I gave you.”

“He kept it.”

“Then he has everything he needs.”

Ernet took the bottle and went out. Maeron, who had been listening from the door, sat in the chair opposite hers.

“He’s better with a boat.”

“Then send him for a boat.”

“We can’t afford one this week.”

She put down her pen. “Have you come to ask for money?”

“To ask whether you would come with us tonight.”

“Where?”

“The lower chapel.”

She knew the place by sight. The roof stood just above a quay that no longer took loaded carts. At high water the lowest windows disappeared. There were other buildings in Silkstrand that had surrendered their lower floors to the canal, but people usually moved the furniture upward.

“What happens there?”

“We gather. There is an observance.”

“For Khemesh.”

“Yes.”

He had named the patron before. So had Corven, when showing her why the markings on his chain needed to be immersed in salt water. No one at the yard appeared to consider it a secret. Lyra had made it a subject she would ask about when there was more time.

“Do I have to do anything?”

“No.”

“Do people mean that when they say it there?”

“I will mean it.”

She tapped the end of her pen against the dry part of the cloth.

“Why do you want me to come?”

“Because you have become part of our work. You should see what we do together when there isn’t cargo between us.”

She wanted to see. She also wanted him to have asked because he enjoyed her company, and the recognition of that wish made her set the pen down more carefully.

“I’ll come,” she said. “Will you walk with me?”

“Of course.”

They left the shore house near evening. Marda wore a clean shawl over the dress in which she had cooked. Neris had tied back her hair with a ribbon her brother said was too fine for where she intended to put it. Corven carried a covered pail in one hand and a folded cloth in the other.

“Is that the eel?” Lyra asked.

“It wanted to come.”

“Did it say?”

“It bit me when I tried to leave.”

Neris laughed and took the pail from him so he could fasten his coat.

They walked without procession or song. People joined them along the way: a woman Lyra knew from the drying racks, two men who repaired hulls, the owner of a small shop whose wife remained at the door while he locked it. Some greeted Maeron with affection. Others simply moved closer to him and matched his pace.

Lyra counted faces she knew and stopped when she found she was doing it to reassure herself.

The chapel door stood above the water reached by the evening tide. A narrow stair descended inside. Lamps had been set on shelves, each flame doubled in the black water below. The air smelled of salt, old mortar, and the oil used on boat fittings.

Corven went first with the pail. He checked a rope fixed along the wall and stood at the bottom to help anyone whose footing was uncertain. Lyra lifted her skirt enough to keep it from the water and found that the lowest step was dry. The chapel floor lay beneath a shallow covering through which she could see worn paving stones.

A raised ledge ran around the room. People sat there, taking off shoes or settling them above the water. Marda put a folded cloth beside her and patted it. Lyra sat.

At the far end stood a stone basin. No statue occupied the niche above it. A dark mark on the wall showed where something had once been fixed. Lyra could not tell whether it had been taken away recently or generations before.

Maeron set his coat on the ledge and entered the water in his ordinary shoes. He spoke first to a woman who had brought a bundle of clothes. They belonged to someone lost at sea. The woman wanted to know whether another attempt could be made at the wreck.

“Corven has been to look,” Maeron said.

Corven leaned forward with his elbows on his knees. “The stern has gone under the fallen rock. I couldn’t reach the place you described.”

“You went yourself?”

“Yes.”

She held the bundle more tightly.

“Would he still be there?”

Corven took time before answering. “I don’t know.”

The woman looked at Maeron. He came closer and crouched, wetting the hem of his shirt.

“We have a place for you tonight,” he said.

“I have a place.”

“Then eat with us before you go home.”

She nodded. He stayed beside her while she untied the bundle. She took out a coat, folded it again, and put it back. No one hurried her.

Afterward there were names to be said and news to be given. A boat expected from the northern coast had reached another port. A diver who had been ill could stand without help. The daughter of the man Lyra had seen speaking to Maeron had written that she hated her first berth and intended to finish it anyway.

There was laughter. The low roof brought the sound back from several places.

Then Maeron asked them to be quiet.

Lyra felt the beginning of the sound in her chest. It had no clear note she could follow. The water touching the lowest step drew into a thin, straight line, though nothing held it there. Someone beside her breathed out for a long time.

She looked toward the stair. Corven remained near it. The door at the top was open. Through it she could see a strip of sky, still pale above the roofs.

Maeron’s mouth moved. The sound seemed to come from the floor beneath her feet.

The sensation lasted long enough that Lyra began to want it to stop. Before she decided whether to say so, it did. Water moved again around the stones. A little wave touched the toe of her shoe.

Marda reached for the shawl that had slipped from her shoulders.

“Cold tonight,” she murmured.

Lyra looked at Maeron. He was holding the edge of the basin with both hands. He waited until his breathing settled before turning to them.

There were cups on a shelf. People took them in order of proximity, without anyone calling names. Corven brought one to Marda, who turned her left hand upward. A pale spiral lay in her palm among several ordinary scars.

She opened a small knife, cleaned its edge, and drew it along part of the spiral. The cut was slight. She let a little blood fall into the cup and added water from the basin.

Lyra watched her drink.

Then Marda bent her head over what remained and whispered.

Lyra could not hear the words. She heard the answer.

It was Marda’s voice, small and close, as though the woman had spoken from the place inside the cup where her mouth should have cast a shadow.

Marda held still. After a moment she emptied the cup into the basin and wiped it with the cloth.

“What did it say?” Lyra asked.

Marda looked at her.

“What I said. Almost.”

“Almost?”

“Sometimes I hear it differently afterward.”

She offered the cup. Lyra shook her head.

Marda set it beside her without disappointment and went to help the woman with the bundle.

They ate at the shore house after the gathering. Lyra found that she was hungry in a way she had not expected. She took bread twice and listened while Neris described a meal on a boat whose cook had claimed that everything improved if enough vinegar was added.

“It did not improve the bread.”

“It preserved it,” Corven said.

“It preserved what was wrong with it.”

Maeron sat across from Lyra. He did not ask what she thought of the chapel until most of the others had gone.

“Could you hear what they whispered?” she asked.

“No.”

“The answer?”

“Sometimes. If I’m near.”

“What answers?”

He looked at the cup in his hands.

“I don’t know how to give you an answer that doesn’t claim more than I know.”

“Try the shorter one.”

“The person who asks hears something returned.”

“That is what happens. It isn’t what does it.”

“No.”

She put her bread down.

“Do you think Khemesh listens?”

“I think the deep changes what reaches it.”

“That includes people.”

“Yes.”

“Corven.”

“Yes.”

“You.”

He nodded.

“What did it change in you?”

Maeron rubbed the inside of one wrist with his thumb. She had seen him make that movement when he was tired.

“I used to be afraid before every descent. There was a morning when I couldn’t make my hands close on the rope. I had gone down for years, and suddenly the thought of it was more than I could carry into the boat.”

“What happened?”

“I went anyway. Another man took my hand and put it where it needed to be.”

“And you stopped being afraid?”

“Not that day.”

She waited.

“Later, I found that I could do the work without the same fear. I could stay where other people had to leave. I began to hear something in the water that I had mistaken for the sounds of the boat.”

“Your song?”

“What became it.”

He spoke with no particular flourish. Marda was washing bowls behind him. A door banged in the upper passage, and he looked up at the noise.

“Was it worth it?” Lyra asked.

“Some mornings.”

That answer stayed with her on the walk home.

She returned to the chapel twice before taking a cup.

At the second gathering she helped a woman who could not manage the stair. At the third, Neris gave her a place beside the basin without asking whether she wanted it. By then Lyra knew where the stones lay beneath the water and which lamp smoked if its wick was raised too high.

She had begun learning other things from Maeron. How to feel the pressure against a closed container. How to distinguish the cold of water held in shade from the cold of a working that continued after its maker stepped away. The first time she tried to chill a jug, she made the handle too cold to hold and left the water almost unchanged.

Maeron laughed when she swore, then showed her how she had attended to the part she touched rather than what it contained.

She practiced with ordinary water. Sometimes it worked. Sometimes she persuaded herself it had worked until Marda tasted it and told her to put it back in the well.

At the yard, the new equipment began earning more than it had cost. A recovery they would once have abandoned became possible because the spare collar was where Corven expected it to be. Ernet learned to check the maker’s mark on a rope before bringing it home. His wife came to the yard to thank Lyra for the regular pay, then asked whether the next payment might include what he had forgotten to bring back from a purchase.

“He said he left it in his other coat.”

“Did he?”

“He owns one coat.”

Lyra found the missing money in an account he had not yet delivered. The wife came back with a small pot of plum preserve and insisted the incident had not been a complaint.

By autumn Lyra spent more evenings at the shore house than in her own room. She kept the room. There was pleasure in returning to a place where nobody expected an answer from her, and she had no wish to give it up.

The night she took the cup, the woman whose husband had been lost was absent. Someone said she had gone to stay with a sister inland. Maeron asked whether she had enough for the journey. Marda said the money had been sent ahead.

Lyra turned the cup in her hands.

The knife was small and clean. She cut too shallowly the first time and had to do it again. Marda pressed a cloth against the heel of her hand afterward, keeping the blood away from her cuff.

“Slowly,” she said.

The brine was harsher than Lyra expected. She swallowed and felt it pull at the back of her tongue. The cup had warmed where she held it.

She had considered what she would whisper on the walk there. None of the prepared words seemed right now. At work she knew what to do with her hands and her hours. She could see the yard, the repaired equipment, Neris sitting at the table, Maeron looking up when she came through the door.

“Let me keep the work,” she whispered.

The answer came close enough to her own voice that she first thought she had repeated herself.

Let me keep them.

She lifted her head.

Nobody had moved. Marda sat beside her, holding the folded cloth. Across the water Maeron was helping an old man steady his cup.

Lyra looked down again. Her reflection broke on the little movement of the brine.

She emptied it into the basin.

For two days she considered telling Maeron exactly what she had heard. On the third she asked whether people ever misunderstood the answer.

“Often.”

“Do you?”

“Yes.”

“Then why repeat it?”

“Because I am not certain the first understanding was the useful one.”

She leaned against the table. They were alone in the counting room. Rain had begun, soft enough that the men outside continued working beneath it.

“I thought it would tell me something.”

“Did it?”

“It changed a word.”

“Which one?”

She almost told him. Then she shook her head.

He accepted that and went to close the shutter against the slanting rain.

Lyra watched him. “Does everyone here belong?”

“To what?”

She gave him an impatient look.

“No,” he said. “Some come for work. Some come because Marda feeds them. Some come to the chapel and never return.”

“And those who do return?”

“Usually find something they want.”

“What do you want from them?”

The shutter stuck. He freed it with his shoulder, then came back to the table.

“Company. Help. People who understand the work well enough to go on doing it.”

“And from me?”

His answer took longer.

“I would like you to stay.”

She could have made him say in what capacity. She could have asked whether the business would remain as it was if she stopped attending the chapel. Instead she found that she wanted to answer the question he had actually put before her.

“I mean to,” she said.

He reached across the table. She let him take her uncut hand.

After he had gone, she copied the final wages for the month. When she opened the money chest, the brass key touched the little healing line in her palm. She shifted it away from the mark and kept working.

Chapter 7 The cold in both houses

The collectors came while the last load was still over the water.

There were four of them at the gate and two more waiting beside a handcart. The man in front wore a short dark coat with a rose worked at the fastening. He had kept his boots clean by using the raised walk along the canal, and looked annoyed to find that the yard offered no continuation of it.

Lyra was checking the crates as they came off the boat. She put the list beneath a weight and went to meet him.

“Who are you here for?”

“Who receives the goods?”

“I do.”

He looked past her toward Maeron’s room.

“For this recovery,” she said.

The man’s name was Orset. He gave it as though it ought to have shortened the conversation. Lyra waited for the rest.

“The receiving charge is outstanding.”

She asked which charge. He took a folded paper from his coat and opened it far enough to show the mark at its foot. A hand closed around a rose, its thorned stem disappearing between the fingers.

Lyra held out her hand. He did not immediately give her the paper.

“I can read it more quickly if you let me hold it.”

Behind her, a wheel creaked on the crane. Someone called to keep the load clear of the landing.

Orset passed the paper over.

It named the cargo correctly. A quantity of tin fittings and sealed jars of dye, recovered from a coastal vessel that had gone down outside the harbor. Lyra recognized the receiving house and the arrangement under which the original goods had been consigned. Beneath those particulars a new charge had been added for the use of protected water.

“The recovery agreement includes harbor entry,” she said.

“This isn’t harbor entry.”

“Then what is it?”

“Protection of the approach.”

“Provided by whom?”

He touched the rose at the foot of the sheet.

Lyra read the last lines. The payment was due before unloading. Until paid, sufficient goods could be retained against it, with costs arising from the retention also due from the receiver.

“The load is already coming off,” Orset said.

“I can see that.”

“Then we should settle it.”

She looked at the handcart behind him. It was large enough for three crates.

“Wait here,” she said. “I’ll bring the agreement.”

He took a step after her. She stopped.

“The yard is working. If you walk beneath a load, the men will have to stop.”

“Then tell them to stop.”

She considered him for a moment, then turned to Ernet.

“Keep the gate clear. Nobody takes that cart inside until I’ve finished reading.”

Orset smiled slightly.

Lyra went to the counting room. Her hands were steady, which made her less comfortable than if they had begun to shake. She knew the men had come expecting refusal. They had brought enough bodies to make it expensive.

Maeron was away at the shore house. Corven stood on the boat beneath the crane, guiding the last cradle away from the mast. Neris had the unloading list. There were seven workers in the yard, including a carter who had nothing to do with the partnership and would reasonably wish to leave.

Lyra took the original agreement, a spare copy, and a small purse. She sent the carter’s boy to fetch the receiving house’s clerk, telling him to say there was a dispute over goods already accepted for delivery. Then she sent another man toward the shore house with a shorter message for Maeron.

When she returned, Orset had moved his people inside.

One stood beside Ernet with a hand resting on his shoulder.

“Take your hand off him,” Lyra said.

The man looked at Orset.

Orset nodded. The hand lifted.

She set the agreement on an empty crate and held it open with her fingers.

“Your receiving house agreed to this approach. The payment includes permission to bring the goods through it.”

“I told you this is another charge.”

“And I am asking who agreed to it.”

“You’re using the water.”

“That doesn’t tell me who agreed.”

He glanced toward the crane. Corven had looked up. His hands remained on the line.

“Maeron knows the arrangements,” Orset said.

“Then he can explain when he gets here. Meanwhile, the goods stay where they are.”

One of the men beside the cart laughed.

Lyra looked at him. He had a thick scar across his lower lip and a short iron bar tucked beneath his coat. He stopped laughing without becoming any less pleased.
