Orset folded his copy of the charge.

“If you would prefer the account to grow, that can be arranged.”

“Put the basis of it in writing.”

“It is in writing.”

“The basis. Who granted the protection, what service it covers, and who accepted the terms.”

He watched her for a moment.

“You have worked in better rooms than this.”

She felt the remark enter more deeply than it deserved.

“I work in this one.”

“So I see.”

The receiving clerk arrived before Maeron. He was an elderly man who had dressed too warmly for the walk and needed to sit down before speaking. Lyra gave him her chair. Orset greeted him by name.

The clerk read both papers. He did not appear surprised by either.

“The original delivery is proper,” he said.

“And this?” Lyra asked.

“A separate matter.”

“Then it can be settled separately.”

Orset said, “The goods answer for it.”

“Whose goods?” Lyra asked the clerk.

The old man looked unhappy. The fittings had been sold forward. The dye belonged to a house that would object strongly to any interruption. He read the agreement again, though the answer had not changed.

“Once accepted at the landing,” he said.

“They were accepted.”

“Yes.”

“Then taking them will delay your buyers.”

Orset moved closer to the table. “We needn’t delay anyone if this woman will pay what is due.”

Lyra disliked being referred to while sitting between them. She let it pass.

“I will place the disputed sum with the receiving house until the claim is examined.”

The clerk looked relieved. Orset did not.

“Against your own money?” the clerk asked.

“Against the amount due to us on this delivery. You can keep that portion until the question is answered.”

She had counted it before making the offer. It would leave enough for wages and the shore house, but not for replacing the damaged quay ladder. They would have to mend it again.

The clerk nodded. “That can be done.”

“No,” Orset said.

They both looked at him.

“It is payable to the holder of the claim.”

“The money will exist,” Lyra said. “It will be available if the claim is found proper.”

“It has been found proper.”

“By you.”

Orset took the charge from the table and folded it along its original crease.

“Three crates,” he told the men.

They moved before Lyra could answer.

The first man struck Ernet with the heel of his hand. The boy went backward into the gatepost and stayed there, stunned, while two others brought the cart through.

Lyra stepped in front of the nearest crate.

“Those have been accepted,” she said.

The man with the scarred lip took her by the upper arm.

She hit his wrist with the edge of the account board. It hurt him enough to make him let go. He brought the other hand up, and she saw the iron bar.

Corven made a small sound from the landing.

The man opened his mouth. Nothing came out.

He stared toward the boat. His hand tightened on the bar.

Corven had a cracked shell in his palm. Water spilled over its edge without falling. Lyra had a moment to see the suspended drops before he closed his fingers and came up the landing.

“Get behind the crate,” he told her.

She moved. The scarred man swung the bar as Corven came within reach. Corven caught the arm against his shoulder and drove his forehead into the man’s face. The nose broke with a short, intimate sound. Blood ran over the already damaged lip.

The man tried to shout. His mouth worked silently.

One of Orset’s people pulled a knife.

Lyra saw it before Corven did.

“Left.”

He turned enough that the blade went through his coat below the shoulder instead of into his back. He caught the attacker’s hand. The chain at his waist had come loose; a length of it struck the man’s wrist and stayed there.

The man stopped moving.

His knees bent slowly. He looked as though somebody much larger had laid a weight across his shoulders. Corven held the chain with one hand and reached for the short boat hook beside the landing.

Orset shouted to stop.

No one obeyed.

The cart lurched as one of its men tried to pull it out of the way. Its wheel struck a brace beneath the loading platform. The timber shifted. Above them the cradle of dye jars swung toward the yard.

Neris was still at the brake.

“Clear it,” she shouted.

Two men ran. The carter’s boy did not. Lyra caught his coat and pulled him behind the wall of the counting room as the first jar struck the planks.

It burst across the place where he had been standing. Dark red dye ran over the wood and the broken pottery. Another jar fell, then the cradle came down at an angle and took part of the railing with it.

Neris disappeared behind the falling brace.

Lyra heard her shout once.

Corven let the bound man fall to his knees and turned toward the wreckage. The man gasped, found his breath, and crawled away. Orset was trying to get his people toward the gate. The receiving clerk remained in Lyra’s chair, both hands pressed flat against his thighs.

“Get him out,” Lyra told Ernet.

Ernet wiped blood from his mouth. She had to say it twice before he understood.

She climbed over the broken crate. A nail caught her skirt and held it. She tore the cloth free.

Neris lay on her side beneath a beam that had trapped one leg against the platform. A broken jar had cut her forehead. She was awake.

“Don’t pull,” she said as Lyra reached her. “It’s caught. Don’t pull.”

“I won’t.”

Corven got both hands beneath the beam. The muscles in his neck stood out. It moved a little, then settled.

Neris cried out.

Lyra looked toward the crane. Its remaining line had twisted beneath the fallen cradle. Two workers were trying to free it. The beam was part of the support for the crane itself. If they cut it wrong, the rest would come down.

“Bring the second sling,” she called.

“It’s under.”

“Then the rope from the chest.”

Corven looked at her. Blood ran from his sleeve.

“We need it braced.”

She saw where he meant. A short iron post had bent beneath the fallen timber. If they could hold that upright, the rope might take enough weight to let Neris slide clear.

She went to the post. It was slick with dye. She wrapped part of her torn skirt around it and pushed.

Nothing moved.

Footsteps sounded behind her. Maeron came over the broken crate, stopped once to understand what he was seeing, then knelt beside Neris.

“Can you feel your foot?”

“Yes. I can feel all of it. Get it off me.”

He looked at Corven, then at the remaining crane support.

“Keep the rope ready.”

Lyra felt the sound begin beneath her ribs.

This time there was no slow beginning she could mistake for the movement of water. Her teeth hurt. The iron post trembled against her hands. Beyond it, the wood enclosing the bent fitting began to split along its grain.

“Keep clear,” Maeron said.

His voice sounded ordinary. The pressure continued beneath it.

Corven hauled on the rope. One of the other men joined him. The beam lifted enough that Lyra could see daylight between it and Neris’s leg.

“Now,” she said.

Neris pushed with her good foot. Lyra took her beneath the shoulders and drew her backward. Something caught at the boot. Neris shouted. Lyra stopped, reached down, and cut the lace that had wound around a splinter.

The beam moved again.

For one instant Lyra could not breathe. The whole yard seemed to have narrowed to the space occupied by her chest. Her fingers closed so hard on Neris’s coat that the stitching broke.

Then Corven pulled them both away.

The support collapsed behind them. Wood struck the landing, followed by the last of the jars. Red liquid spread through the debris and poured between the planks into the canal.

Maeron remained on his knees.

He had one hand over his mouth. Blood came between his fingers.

Orset had gone. Two of his men had left with him. The man whose nose Corven had broken lay beside the gate with a cloth over his face; the other knelt against the wall, holding his wrist.

The receiving clerk was making a statement.

Lyra could hear him telling Ernet that he had seen the cart strike the brace. He repeated it carefully, as though accuracy might protect him from something he had already allowed to happen.

Corven sat on an upturned crate while a worker cut away the torn part of his sleeve. The wound was ugly but shallow. The knife had crossed the flesh above his elbow.

Lyra’s hands would not warm.

She noticed it while helping bind Neris’s forehead. The diver flinched when Lyra touched her temple.

“Sorry.”

“Your fingers.”

“I know.”

Neris reached up with her own hand and held Lyra’s wrist. Her fingertips were cold too.

Across the yard, Maeron drank water and spat it out. He had bitten the inside of his mouth badly enough that the blood kept returning. He refused the cloth Corven offered, then accepted it when Marda’s physician arrived and told him to stop wasting clean water.

The physician examined Neris’s leg. He made her move her toes, pressed along the bone, and asked questions she answered through clenched teeth. The leg was bruised and badly scraped. He could not promise there was no small fracture.

“She’ll need rest,” he said.

Neris closed her eyes.

Lyra covered her with a coat. Her own fingers felt as though they had been held too long around a cup of cold water. She put them beneath her arms and waited for sensation to return.

It did not.

Marda was standing by the kitchen fire when they brought Neris to the shore house.

Her hands were wrapped in towels.

“What happened to you?” Lyra asked.

Marda looked at the injured woman first. “Put her in the front room. The bed’s ready.”

Corven and the other man carried Neris past. Lyra stayed in the kitchen.

“Marda.”

The older woman pulled the towel back. Her fingers were pale, the joints stiff.

“I was making bread.”

“Did you burn them?”

“No.”

She touched Lyra’s hand. They looked at one another.

“When?” Lyra asked.

“Before the flour boy came back. I had the dough in the bowl. I thought someone had put cold water in without telling me.”

“You were here?”

“Where else would I be?”

The yard was two canals away. From this kitchen a person could hear the market bells and, in a quiet hour, the ferrymen calling at the crossing. Low Bell Yard lay behind a row of warehouses, beyond a bridge and another turn of the water.

“Did you hear him?” Lyra asked.

“Who?”

“Maeron.”

Marda shook her head.

Lyra went to the fire and held out her hands. The heat stung the skin on her wrists. Her fingers remained cold.

Maeron came to the kitchen after the physician had finished with him. He moved carefully, one hand against the doorframe. There were dark stains down the front of his shirt.

Lyra turned from the fire.

“Marda wasn’t there.”

“I know.”

“She didn’t hear you.”

“No.”

“What happened to her?”

He sat on the stool beside the table. The movement cost him. Lyra watched him settle and resisted the impulse to fetch water.

“What happened to me?” she asked.

Maeron looked at her hands.

“The working carried through us.”

“Through whom?”

“Those who follow.”

“Marda was making bread.”

“Yes.”

“Nobody asked her.”

“No.”

The plain answer left Lyra without the argument she had expected. He had not made her prove what they both knew.

“How far?” she asked.

“I cannot give you a distance.”

“How many?”

“We must ask.”

“Before you do it again.”

“Yes.”

She moved away from the fire. The warmth had begun to hurt her face.

“You told me people changed.”

“They do.”

“You told me about going down. About choosing a working. You did not tell me you could change someone who was nowhere near you.”

His eyes lifted to hers.

“I should have.”

“Yes.”

“I should have told you before you took part.”

She stood with both hands pressed against the edge of the table. Maeron looked exhausted. It would have been easy to end the conversation because he had saved Neris and was bleeding into his own mouth. She disliked how strongly she wanted to do that.

“Will it pass?”

“Some of what you feel now may ease.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

“No.”

Marda came in with fresh towels. She set one beside Maeron and handed the other to Lyra.

“Mine have done this before,” she said.

Lyra turned toward her.

“Like this?”

“Not as strong.”

“And you stayed.”

Marda looked into the other room, where Neris was telling the physician what she intended to do with his instructions.

“I stayed,” she said.

Lyra waited for the reason. Marda did not give one. She went to check the bread, which had risen badly while everyone was elsewhere.

Lyra went home that night.

Maeron did not ask her to remain. Corven walked with her as far as the bridge because Orset’s men might have stayed nearby. His arm was bound beneath his coat. She could see where the cloth pulled when he moved.

“Did you know it could reach her?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“Me?”

He stopped walking.

“Once you had begun to follow him. Yes.”

“And you didn’t tell me.”

“I thought he had.”

She looked at him. There was no useful way to prove whether that was true.

“Would you have done anything differently today?”

Corven leaned against the bridge rail. A boat passed beneath them with a lamp at its bow. For a moment its light showed the chain beneath his open coat.

“I would have put a man at the crane before they came through the gate.”

“And afterward?”

He looked at his bound arm.

“I don’t know how to take the beam off her without him.”

They stood until the boat had gone.

“I’ll walk the rest,” Lyra said.

He began to object, saw her face, and nodded.

At home she filled a basin from the kettle and put her hands in it. The water was hot enough that she had to withdraw them. She added cold, tried again, and sat watching the surface until her fingers began to hurt.

The little spiral in her palm had nearly healed.

She dried her hands and went to bed without answering the letter that had arrived that afternoon. She lay awake listening to the ordinary movements of the house. Once she got up to open the window, then closed it because the room beyond the lamplight seemed too large.

In the morning she went to the receiving house.

The clerk’s statement was ready. It named the cart, the struck brace, the damaged cargo, and the people present. It also described the disputed charge as unsettled. Lyra read every line, asked for one correction, and took a copy.

The buyer agreed that he had accepted the goods before they were damaged. He would pursue Orset’s people himself. Lyra would receive payment for the sound cargo now; the damaged portion would take longer to settle.

She returned to Low Bell Yard with enough money for wages.

Ernet was repairing the gate. His lower lip had swollen, and his wife stood beside him holding nails in the fold of her apron. Corven had found a new brace. Two men were clearing broken pottery into a tub.

Maeron waited in the counting room.

Lyra put the money on the table.

“The crews first. Then Marda’s allowance. No new recovery until the platform has been inspected.”

He nodded.

“And I want the names of everyone who changed yesterday. What happened to them. Whether it has happened before.”

“I’ve begun asking.”

“I will ask too.”

“Yes.”

She sat. The cup with the uncomfortable handle stood beside her papers. When she lifted it, Maeron watched her fingers close around it.

“I can find someone to take the accounts,” he said.

“I know.”

“You needn’t decide today.”

She looked through the open doorway. Ernet’s wife was laughing at the way he held a nail between his teeth with his injured lip. He took it out and said something that made her laugh harder.

“I have decisions to make today,” Lyra said. “These people are waiting to be paid.”

Maeron moved the papers closer.

She counted the first man’s wages twice because the coins kept slipping against her cold fingers. When he came in, she made him sign where he always signed and asked how his hands had felt the previous afternoon.

He showed her.

She listened, wrote down what he said, and paid him.

For eight days Lyra left Low Bell before the lamps were lit.

On the first evening Maeron followed her to the counting-room door. She had put her gloves on, though it was warm outside.

“I’ll be here in the morning,” she said. “For the work. I don’t want a lesson.”

“All right.”

“Or supper.”

He stepped back to let her pass.

Marda sent food home with her twice. Lyra returned the bowls herself, visited Neris, and left before Maeron came in. Nobody made her explain. At home she ate sitting on the bed, with a letter open beside her. She answered it. She had been promising to answer it for weeks.

The accounts of the changes came slowly. A man had gone to visit his daughter; another would say nothing in front of Corven. Lyra went to him alone. He had bitten his cheek when the cold struck and still could not eat on that side. His wife had been beside him. Nothing had happened to her.

While they were still asking, the divers found a hatch Maeron could have freed. He declined to go down. Corven hired another boat and brought men back with cutting tools. They lost a day’s work and damaged the hatch getting through. Lyra put her hand on the torn metal when it came ashore. She had wanted something she could point to if Maeron said that waiting was impossible.

On the ninth evening she brought him the last man’s account. She found him in the counting room, his sleeve caught beneath the earlier pages. When she reached past him to free it, their hands touched.

His was as cold as hers.

“Have you done any working?” she asked.

“Since the yard? No.”

She turned her hand over. The skin looked ordinary.

“If I stopped coming to the house altogether. Would the next one still reach me?”

“I don’t know.”

“You must have known people who left.”

“Yes.”

“What happened to them?”

“Some I haven’t seen again. The ones I’ve seen were still changed. I couldn’t tell you whether anything more had happened to them.”

She let go of his sleeve.

“I thought I’d feel different by now.”

He started to speak, then stopped.

“What?”

“I was going to say it takes time.”

She waited.

“I don’t know that either,” he said.

Outside, Ernet dropped something and swore. She could have taken the papers home. There was enough food there, and work for the morning; nobody needed her to stay tonight. She had spent the afternoon wanting to tell Maeron about a buyer who had tried to sell her own damaged goods back to her. She still wanted to hear him laugh.

“I’ve missed you,” she said.

His face changed. He reached for her hand, slowly enough that she could have moved it.

“I’ve missed you too.”

She held on to him.

“Will you show me the water again?”

He looked at the pages between them. “Lyra—”

“I’ve read them.”

For a moment he stayed where he was. Then he went to fetch the basin. She put the papers aside and pulled her chair beside his.

Part Two The business of protection

Chapter 8 Three working landings

The man showing Lyra the store had brought a stool so that she could look through the high window. He set it beneath the sill, pressed a foot against the lowest rung, and waited.

She went past him to the water door.

“That one’s sound,” he said. “We renewed it last autumn.”

The sill had a pale line across it. Above the line the wood was gray and rough; below, where the water reached, somebody had scraped it and applied fresh oil. Lyra squatted. A tuft of tow protruded from a joint beside the iron shoe.

“How high did it come?”

“High water comes everywhere.”

“How high did it come here?”

The man looked toward Tesset, who had been engaged for three mornings and was carrying Lyra’s papers beneath her coat. Tesset shifted the papers to her other arm.

“Just above the shoe,” he said.

“While the door was shut?”

“Yes.”

Lyra put her fingertips against the stone beside the joint. The cold in them had ceased troubling her while she worked. It returned when she tried to warm a cup, or touched somebody who did not expect it. Here she could feel a small movement behind the wood, an uneven press and release. Maeron had told her to begin with water she could see and check her impressions afterward. She had been doing so for six weeks. More often than she liked, the water contradicted her.

She stood and asked the man to open the door.

Beyond it lay Ropewalk Steps, green under the last retreat of the tide. A cart could approach from the street and turn in the yard without backing over anybody’s washing. The store’s roof was poor, its lower door leaked, and the owner had convinced himself that its position excused both. Lyra had inspected four places worse than this one.

“We’d put dry goods at the upper end,” she said to Tesset. “Fittings near this door. Nothing that swells.”

“Cloth above the water line?”

“Cloth elsewhere. Water doesn’t read a mark on a wall before coming in.”

The man laughed. He had decided this was the moment for it.

“I can have a fellow see to the door.”

“Before we pay.”

“He’s busy.”

“Then we’ll pay when he’s less busy.”

The stool remained under the window. Tesset put her packet on it and took out a sheet. She had a broad, competent hand, less graceful than the writing she had shown Lyra when asking for work. That had pleased Lyra. A woman who could make a beautiful copy and chose to write quickly when it mattered might be useful.

The owner gave them a date. Tesset entered it. When he named the advance, she stopped writing.

“You said twelve for the first quarter.”

“Twelve plus use of the steps.”

“You said use of the steps included.”

“I meant ordinary use.”

Tesset glanced at Lyra. She had a little scar in one eyebrow which made a questioning look seem more permanent than it was.

“What sort of use did you imagine?” Lyra asked.

“Nothing that blocks the fish carts.”

“We’ve brought you the hours. Put them in. Put twelve in as well.”

He took the sheet, found he could not read it while holding it at that angle, and turned toward the open door. A woman outside was gutting fish over a pail. Behind her, a boy whose shirt was too big was holding a gull at bay with a broom. The woman called to ask whether they would be bringing men who pissed on her wall.

“No,” Lyra said.

“Good. He did.” She pointed her knife at the owner.

“I was seeing to a leak.”

“You made another.”

Tesset pressed her mouth shut and attended to the paper.

By the time the new door closed properly, the small hand winch had broken at the first yard and the spare lifting collar had gone out with a crew who were three days late returning it.

The missing collar was worth less than the goods they couldn’t land without it. Their remaining one was too small. They could unload by sling, provided nobody minded replacing the first row of crushed jars. Lyra found Corven in the repair shed with a cracked wheel across his knees. The eel’s vessel stood beside his boot, wrapped against an accidental kick. A piece of fish floated on the dark water. Nothing appeared to be eating it.

“Is that ours?” she asked.

“It is now.”

“Why?”

“Because I let a man hammer the pin out before I looked at the wood.”

The split ran from the bore to the rim. Corven pressed the halves together and released them. He had stripped off his coat, showing the dark marks that went beneath his sleeve. A small cut on his forearm had dried without closing. Lyra noticed that he had put the arm where the daylight did not touch it.

“We need the collar back.”

“We need another of the larger size.”

“We can afford one.”

“We have one. Somewhere.”

She sat on the upturned chest opposite him. Outside, men were arguing about whose turn it had been to take the sling in from the rain. They had stopped arguing about the collectors, at least when she was near. The red dye stain on the landing wall remained. It had resisted sand, lye, and an offer from Marda to paint over it. A worker had hung his hat on the damaged bracket beside it, and now everyone used the bracket for hats.

“What would you buy first?” Lyra asked.

“Collar. Then proper blocks for Ropewalk. If the block fails, a new collar won’t help.”

“And the winch?”

Corven named a man who would hire one out. Lyra knew his rates.

“He charges for the day you return it.”

“He charges for days he thinks you might return it.”

“I’ll speak to him.”

Corven reached into his pocket and put a wrapped screw beside her on the chest.

“For the door upstairs.”

She had mentioned that her office latch was loose. She had not asked him to mend it. He said the old screw had stripped its wood and he would set a plug if she wished, and then he bent over the wheel again. Lyra took the screw. It was warm from his pocket, or seemed so against her fingers.

“Does yours come back?” she asked.

He looked up.

“The warmth.”

“Sometimes. After sleep. Never as much as I expect.”

“Maeron says it settles.”

“It does.”

She waited for him to say into what. He picked a splinter from the wheel and laid it carefully aside.

Tesset’s first correction came on a day when Lyra wanted nothing corrected.

A buyer had accepted three recovered copper kettles and a chest of sound fittings, found a flaw in one kettle, and withheld the whole payment until his cousin could examine it. His cousin lived upriver. The crew’s wages were due before the cousin could possibly arrive. Lyra had written, visited, returned with a witness, and been invited to consider the damage a hasty dispute could do to future purchases.

She brought her own small reserve from her room in a cloth bag. It had been enough to leave the city, if leaving became necessary. She had calculated that use so often that she could still see the distances in the coins while she counted them into smaller heaps.

Tesset stood at the end of the desk with a work return in her hand.

“What is it?”

“Seldo.”

“He’ll be here after noon. Leave his money with Marda if he doesn’t come.”

“His name is down for four days.”

“He worked four days.”

“He worked five.”

“He came on the fifth to collect his tools.”

“And you asked him to mend the shutter. Then to take the broken pulley to Corven. He helped get the door upright at Ropewalk. I was there.”

Lyra pressed a coin flat with her thumb.

“Did he ask you to bring this?”

“No.”

“Then why didn’t he tell me?”

“I don’t know. Shall I ask him?”

Outside, a cart had jammed between the gate and a stack of drying poles. Somebody shouted for the poles to be moved. Lyra had asked three times for them to be put elsewhere. She heard one crack, followed by the sudden silence of men assessing whether they could pretend it had been cracked before.

“Give him the fifth day,” she said.

Tesset remained.

“There isn’t enough in his heap.”

“I can see that.”

Tesset put the return down. She was older than Lyra had first taken her for, with fine lines beside the eyes and a burn on one wrist. Twenty-seven, she’d said at the interview. Lyra had been tired and hadn’t quite believed her. Now the calm way she waited was maddening.

She took the extra coins from the bag.

“Here.”

“Thank you.”

“Don’t thank me for paying him.”

Tesset’s hand stopped over the money.

“All right.”

Lyra heard what she had done. The broken pole made another noise as someone tried to move it without lifting the cart. She closed her eyes for a moment.

“You were right to tell me.”

“I thought so.”

“And I would rather you did it before we’re short.”

“I found it this morning.”

“I know.”

Tesset took the coins and went to find Seldo. Lyra stayed where she was until she could pick up the pen without pressing too hard.

At noon she went into the yard. Seldo, who had not yet left, was showing two younger men how to free the cart wheel without levering against the axle. Lyra told him there would be food after they finished and went to the kitchen herself to arrange it.

The buyer paid eleven days later, after Lyra sold the next recovery to a house whose owner was married to his sister. The sister sent back a note describing the kettles as excellent. Lyra kept it in the same drawer as the disputed account, where she could find it when annoyed.

She bought the second collar, hired a winch on terms that named the hour of return, and took a narrow place at Eel Stair for shallow boats. Eel Stair had no cart access. Men hired to block a road would need considerably more money to stand waist-deep in water.

Tesset argued for a desk at Ropewalk and received a table with one short leg. She corrected that with a folded notice from a harbor temple announcing a lecture on patience.

“Are you coming tonight?” Lyra asked her, late in the second month.

“Where?”

“The chapel. There’s food afterward.”

“I eat at my sister’s on that night.”

“You could bring her.”

“She has three children.”

“Children do come.”

Tesset tested the table with both palms.

“I don’t go to services, Mistress Lyra.”

“Lyra will do here.”

“I don’t go to services, Lyra.”

The repetition made Lyra smile. She had been told no in a great many less useful forms.

“Your sister lives far?”

“Above the dyers. I’m staying there until I can afford a room with no children.”

“All three in yours?”

“Only when I want to sleep.”

Lyra gave her the next morning free and did not mention the chapel again.

At the shore house Maeron had saved her a place near the wall. He was helping a child pick gristle from a piece of meat, using the tip of his own knife. The child’s mother sat opposite with her hands around a cup. She had received news of a ship two weeks overdue and had not yet decided whether to believe it.

“Three landings,” Maeron said when Lyra sat.

“Three rents.”

“And a clerk.”

“She has already found something I did wrong.”

“I shall try to bear it.”

“She won’t come to the chapel.”

“Does she do the work?”

“Yes.”

“Then I hope you keep her.”

He slid the plate toward the child. A little later he listened while Lyra explained the dispute over the kettle, and when she came to the sister’s note he laughed hard enough to have to put down his cup. She had never heard him laugh like that. It changed his face, drawing the worn places together into a younger man she had no other way of knowing.

Marda brought more bread. Her fingers had the same pallor as Lyra’s, but the flour no longer stuck to them as it had before. She placed her hands around the jug when she poured and did not ask anyone else to do it.

“The new cook says I ruin her dough,” she said.

“Do you?” Maeron asked.

“Apparently it knows the difference between a hand and a lump of winter.”

“We could give you the fish,” Lyra said.

“I’ve fed fifty people for years. I shall not be sent out with the fish.”

“No. You shall stand over the cook and instruct her.”

“That was my thought.”

The child’s mother smiled into her cup. Maeron passed Marda his chair and went to fetch another for himself.

After supper he asked Lyra to help clear the bowls. In the kitchen he found a cracked saucer, poured a little water into it, and set it on the workbench.

“Try this.”

She had been practicing cold in a cup. This was shallower than he usually gave her. She laid a finger on the rim and attended to the water until the kitchen sounds seemed a little farther away. The familiar tightening came beneath her breastbone. She had learned to stop there rather than lean into it.

A thin white skin formed at the edge of the saucer.

Maeron touched the water and nodded. He did not praise her at once. She liked that he checked.

“Again,” she said.

“Eat something first.”

“I have eaten.”

“I watched you talk over a bowl until Marda took it away.”

He broke a heel from the bread, spread it with the last of the soft cheese, and gave it to her. She ate standing at the workbench. The kitchen door was open to the yard, where someone was tuning a fiddle by repeating the same unhappy note. Maeron washed the saucer and set it down again.

This time the ice reached almost to the middle. Lyra laughed.

“There,” he said, smiling at her. “You felt the place to stop.”

She made him leave it where it was. They went outside with the bread between them and listened until the fiddler found the note.

Chapter 9 The Court makes room

The invitation arrived folded around a list of buyers.

Lyra knew three of the names. One had refused to see her, one had seen her and declined to purchase anything, and the third had bought good cloth from the man who had purchased it cheaply from her. Beside each name someone had written the goods required. Copper, sail canvas, seasoned timber. Nothing fanciful. Nothing they could not supply.

Tesset read the list over her shoulder.

“Are those real?”

“The people are.”

“And the wants?”

“Perhaps.”

“It’s a great deal of perhaps.”

Lyra turned the invitation. Madam Serafine, Mistress of the Velvet Court, would receive her on the following afternoon. There was no stated purpose. The handwriting was beautiful. Lyra ran her eye down the even strokes and wondered how long the writer had been made to practice them.

She went to Ropewalk first and looked at the unsold timber beneath its new cover. She put a hand against an end grain and came away smelling of old salt and fresh resin. A buyer with a large enough yard could keep it until the harbor repairs began and charge twice what she was asking. She needed the space now.

That evening she took out her better dress. It had been brushed and mended so often that the inside of its sleeve contained three different shades of thread. She considered having another made, found herself calculating the number of wages involved, and put it on.

Marda looked up from the shore house table when she came down.

“Someone dead?”

“I’m going to see Serafine.”

“Oh.” Marda examined the dress again. “Turn round.”

Lyra turned. Marda came with a needle and bent to the fastening at the neck.

“You’ve put it through the old eye.”

“There’s a new one?”

“I put it in last week. Stand still.”

Lyra stood while Marda worked. The woman’s fingers touched the nape of her neck, cold through the fine hairs there. It no longer startled either of them.

“She’ll be able to see I don’t belong in it,” Lyra said.

“It’s your dress.”

“In the room.”

“Let her move her furniture.”

Serafine had furnished the smaller reception room so that nobody would need to raise a voice. The chairs stood close enough for a hand to cross between them. There were fruit and narrow cakes on a table, a little brazier whose lid had been pierced with leaves, and a girl in pale blue who poured the tea in front of Lyra and withdrew.

The door remained open a hand’s breadth. Lyra noticed that Serafine watched her notice it.

“I hope you were not troubled on the way.”

“No.”

“A carrier has been making the corner difficult. I shall have him moved.”

“He was unloading flour.”

“Then I shall have him finish.”

Serafine’s veil lay back against her hair. She had a damaged tooth which appeared when she smiled, and Lyra found herself looking away too quickly. Serafine allowed the silence to pass over that small discourtesy without helping her.

“You have been busy,” she said.

“We’ve found work.”

“So I see. Three landings, and a clerk with a better hand than the man who used to write your bills.”

“That was me.”

“I said better.”

Lyra laughed. Serafine smiled and passed the cakes.

“The buyers are interested. Darel needs timber before the month ends. He has an order for repair of the outer quays and cannot fulfill it with promises, though I understand he has tried. You have timber standing at Ropewalk. I thought you might be useful to each other.”

“Thank you.”

“Don’t thank me yet. I expect to be useful as well.”

She set a folded agreement on the table between the cakes and the tea. Lyra did not take it immediately. Serafine lifted her own cup and waited.

The first page named introductions, access to bonded storage, the right to put goods through a private weighhouse, and assistance with disputed recoveries. The proposed charges were lower than Lyra had expected. A schedule attached to the back would give her enough orders to justify hiring a second boat outright.

The restrictions appeared between provisions she wanted. New buyers were to be submitted for review. Exceptional recoveries, a phrase left undefined, would be offered first to houses named by the Court. Violence involving her employees or associates would be referred through Serafine’s appointed agent. The last clause required her partnership to retain an adviser empowered to suspend transactions likely to compromise shared interests.

“Who is the adviser?” Lyra asked.

“A sensible woman. You would like her.”

“Would I be able to dismiss her?”

“If you gave me sufficient reason.”

“Would she be able to dismiss me?”

Serafine put down her cup.

“You own your share, don’t you?”

“Yes.”

“Then she could hardly dismiss you from it.”

“She could stop me using it.”

“She could stop a foolish purchase while somebody explained why it was foolish.”

Lyra read the provision again. It allowed suspension without naming a period. She had once copied an instruction giving a man temporary authority over a gate. He had still held it when the man who wrote the instruction was buried.

“I could accept a review period,” she said. “Two days. After that the decision stays with the partners.”

“No.”

“And exceptional recoveries would need to be defined.”

“That can be done.”

“By us.”

“By agreement.”

“With the same two-day limit.”

Serafine leaned back. The chair made no sound. Lyra became aware of the brazier’s heat beneath her skirt and moved one foot away from it.

“You think you have come to sell me a piece of timber.”

“I think I’ve come to discuss how much of my business you would like.”

“Most of the dangerous part. You may keep the work.”

The answer was so direct that Lyra looked up. Serafine was watching her without amusement now.

“Your collectors wrecked our crane.”

“Did he?”

“They were collecting for you.”

“Many people say so. It makes a discussion shorter.”

“They used your seal.”

“Then I shall have a discussion with him.”

“The crane had to be replaced.”

“Yes,” Serafine said. “I imagine it did.”

There was no apology for Lyra to accept or refuse. She set the agreement beside her cup. Through the gap at the door she could see a patch of blue skirt and the toe of a slipper. The girl had stayed near enough to hear her mistress call.

Serafine reached for a cake and broke it in two. She gave Lyra the larger piece.

“I know that you want your own room,” she said. “Many women come to me because they want one. The error usually concerns how much of the street they must keep friendly in order to retain the key.”

Lyra held the cake. There were seeds on top. One had lodged beneath her nail.

“My present work has made certain things possible,” she said. “I won’t agree that somebody else may stop them without my consent.”

“Then you will continue making your own arrangements.”

“Yes.”

“And paying for them.”

“Yes.”

Serafine ate her half of the cake. She seemed almost tired, and for a moment Lyra wanted to ask what she would have accepted at the beginning, before the furniture and the girl at the door. The question was useless. It would offer too much for whatever answer Serafine chose to give.

“Keep the list,” Serafine said. “The wants are real.”

Lyra thanked her for the tea and left with the agreement unsigned.

The weighhouse could not receive their cart on the following morning. A beam required inspection. It had been inspected the week before, but that inspection, the attendant explained, had concerned the suspension and this one concerned the beam.

The receiving factor would accept another way of weighing the whole consignment if he could watch it done. Lyra remembered the elephant on the ferry, and went to Low Bell to hire a lighter. They put the goods aboard, sent everyone ashore and marked the water against the hull. Then they unloaded the goods and brought aboard market sacks of known weight until the boat sat at the mark again. The factor counted the sacks himself. He made them wait for the water to settle twice before he would sign.

Corven leaned on the empty handcart. “How big was the elephant?”

“Too big for the scales. That’s all I remember.”

“I remember the viceroy refusing to pay.”

“Ours has signed.” Lyra held the receipt while the ink dried.

The rope seller had no suitable rope. The length Lyra could see behind him belonged to a customer who had paid in advance. He could take her order for the next delivery if she paid in advance too.

At Ropewalk, the owner came to discuss complaints about noise. He had been receiving complaints for some time. Lyra asked from whom, and he said the important thing was to preserve good relations. The fish woman came across the yard with her hands wet and asked whether he would care to name her. He left before she finished drying them.

The weighing had cost her a morning, the lighter’s hire and two extra men’s wages. She spent the afternoon securing rope from a sailmaker who would sell only if it was carried away in a covered cart. She paid the full cost and bought canvas to cover it. By the end of the day she had solved every immediate difficulty and could no longer afford the boat.

Tesset found her in the office with her hat still on.

“Shall I send the offers?”

“Which?”

“The timber. To those people.”

Lyra took off the hat and put it beside the lamp.

“Yes.”

“Do you think they’ll answer?”

“I think they need timber.”

Tesset collected the letters. At the door she hesitated.

“My sister says you should have agreed.”

“Does she?”

“She says it’s what everybody does.”

“What did you tell her?”

“That I don’t know anything about it.”

Lyra waited.

“And?”

“And she said that had never stopped me before.”

Lyra laughed, too suddenly. She put a hand over her eyes. Tesset waited without treating the laugh as an invitation to join it, and Lyra was grateful for that too.

“Go home,” she said. “Send them on the way.”

After the office emptied, Maeron came carrying a wrapped fish and a jar of something yellow. He had heard from Marda that Lyra had missed supper. He set the parcel on the desk and looked at the cold lamp.

“Shall I light that?”

“Yes.”

He lit it while she unwrapped the food. The fish had been cooked with sour fruit and enough oil to soak the cloth. Lyra ate with her fingers and sucked the sour oil from her thumb. Maeron sat on the window ledge.

“She offered you work?”

“She offered me all the work I could wish for.”

“And?”

“She wanted to decide which of it I should do.”

“Ah.”

“Don’t say it like that.”

“Like what?”

“As if I passed an examination.”

He considered her, then lowered his eyes to the parcel.

“I was glad you came back.”

“I didn’t go there to leave.”

“I know.”

She ate another piece of fish. He stayed while she finished, making no proposal. When he went, he took the greasy cloth and left the jar. Inside was pickled rind, made by a woman at the shore house who remembered Lyra liking it months before.

The first man Corven restrained after she hired him had come for a coil of rope.

He had brought a knife. That was what altered the matter. Lyra had been prepared to recover the rope, identify him, and send him out. He saw Corven come through the gate and put the blade against Seldo’s throat instead.

Nobody moved for the length of one breath. Lyra could see Seldo looking at her. The knife was held too high, near the jaw, and his chin had risen to follow it. He was trying not to swallow.

Corven put down the vessel containing his eel. He did it slowly, beside the wall, and the man watched his hand rather than his mouth.

The chain fell from Corven’s sleeve.

There was a small noise like a wet board bending. The man lost the knife. He remained upright, his shoulder pressed against the gatepost, but his knees began moving without carrying him anywhere. Seldo stepped away and touched his neck. Blood came through his fingers, a shallow cut made in the instant of release.

“Pick it up,” Lyra said.

Seldo stared at her.

“The knife.”

He bent and took it. The man against the gatepost had his mouth open. Darkness spread beneath his eyes.

“Let him go,” Lyra said.

Corven gathered the chain. The man fell forward onto his hands and vomited between them.

They searched him while he was on his knees. He carried no message, no seal, and no money worth the trouble of robbing him for. He said he had come for rope. Lyra believed him. She gave the knife to Seldo and sent the man out by the smaller gate, where the fish woman could watch him go.

Corven found her later cleaning the cut on Seldo’s neck. He waited until Seldo had gone back to work.

“Too long?”

“I don’t know how long is too long.”

“Neither did he.”

“I need to.”

He looked toward the gate. A little water remained where someone had washed the paving.

“You tell me when.”

“When I tell you to release someone, you do it.”

“Yes.”

“Even if you think I shouldn’t.”

“If he’s still holding a knife?”

“Tell me.”

“If there’s no time?”

“Then keep him from using it. But after that, I decide.”

Corven laid the chain across his palm. Several links bore small, close marks she could not follow. He turned one with his thumb until it lay as he wanted.

“All right.”

She took out his terms. He would receive regular wages whether anyone threatened them or not. He would keep his own equipment; the business would replace materials he used protecting them. He read slowly, asking twice about a word. When he reached the end, he set the sheet down.

“If one of ours wants to come home?”

“We bring them.”

“If the work isn’t done?”

“We bring them.”

“Put that in.”

She did. He watched the letters take shape, then put his name beneath hers. His writing was cramped and pressed so hard that the pen caught. Lyra sanded it before the spreading blot could swallow the last stroke.

At the door, he turned back.

“Do I stand behind you when you work?”

“Only if you can bear being hit with a chair whenever I get up.”

He looked at the space, at her chair, and at his own knees.

“I’ll stand outside.”

“That would be best.”

Chapter 10 The Prince needs deliveries

Katerina Volkov came through the yard at the hour when a conversation meant dodging loads. She waited for a suspended crate to pass, stepped over a coil of wet line, and asked Tesset whether Lyra could spare half an hour.

Her voice was so quiet that Tesset made her repeat it. The second time she leaned closer, and saw the scar across Katerina’s throat.

“I’ll ask.”

“Tell her I can pay for the time.”

Lyra heard that from the stairs. She stopped on the bottom step.

“How much?”

Katerina looked up. She was younger than the care with which she moved had first suggested. Her coat was plain and expensive, cut to keep clear of her hands.

“Tell me after.”

“No. Tell me before.”

Katerina named a sum sufficient to replace what Lyra had spent securing the second supply of rope. Lyra came down the last step and took her into the office.

Katerina laid out a sheet with no headings. Three loads, three destinations, the time taken between each. Before sitting, she moved the chair far enough from the wall to clear the short scabbard at her hip. She set her cup on the left, leaving her right hand free. Lyra studied the sheet. Katerina did not touch the cup.

“Who wrote this?”

“I did.”

“Where are the waiting times?”

“In those figures.”

“Separate them.”

Katerina took the pen. Her hand shook slightly at the beginning of each line, then steadied as she wrote. Lyra watched a drop of ink lengthen beneath the nib without falling.

“The second boat waited here?”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

“The first had not been cleared.”

“By the receiving house?”

“By a man who wanted more money.”

“Was he paid?”

“No.”

“What happened to him?”

Katerina looked up.

“Would it change the time?”

“It might change who will unload the next boat.”

“He’s alive.”

Lyra drew the sheet closer. Someone had sent laden boats deep into narrow water to save splitting the loads. At one landing men were paid to wait while the only suitable cart stood idle elsewhere. One delay held up all three loads. Men with weapons had accompanied each load. Their wages were the one expense entered separately.

“You have enough boats,” Lyra said. “And too many people telling them when to move.”

“Would you say that to the Prince?”

“Am I being paid to?”

Katerina smiled, scarcely moving her lips.

The Salt Prince received her in a room above a working store. Salt dust lay in the joints of the stair. The man who opened the upper door had a white crust at the corners of his mouth and a little split in the skin beside one eye. When he stepped aside, Lyra heard a dry clicking from somewhere beneath his coat.

Corven came no farther than the landing. He had been told that was where he would wait. Lyra disliked leaving him there, and disliked more the fact that she would have disliked coming alone. Katerina walked ahead without looking back to see whether she followed.

The Prince stood beside an open window. Below him, men were sewing mouths onto sacks. One of them sang the first line of a song, forgot the next, and continued with a hum. It was ordinary work on a bright afternoon. The Prince turned, and the skin hanging from his shoulders moved after him.

Lyra had heard descriptions. She had assumed some portion of them was intended to frighten people into repeating them. The cloak had been cured and dressed, its edges salted white. Near the fastening she saw a shape she recognized as a human navel. She fixed her eyes on his face.

“My father,” he said. “He was reluctant to retire.”

Lyra pressed her thumb into the edge of her papers.

“You sell speed,” he said.

“I sell what I can arrange.”

“Can you arrange these?”

Katerina put the sheet on a low table. He gestured toward a chair. Lyra sat, placing her own papers on her knees.

“Who has authority to receive the goods?” she asked.

“I do.”

“At each landing.”

“My men.”

“Which men?”

He looked at Katerina. She began naming them. Lyra interrupted on the third.

“That one was not present.”

“He had other work.”

“Then his boat waited because nobody else could say yes.”

The Prince took the sheet. His nails were thick and pale, the ends worn flat. There was a sound under his breathing which she could not locate, as though somebody were rubbing coarse cloth behind him. It continued through the long pause between his breaths.

“He was dealing with a threat.”

“So was the man whose boat you kept at a landing until midnight. His crew wanted to go home.”

“He could have paid them.”

“With what authority?”

Katerina turned her face away. The Prince looked at Lyra for a long moment. The corners of his mouth drew back without moving his cheeks. He put two fingers to his jaw, settled it, and set the sheet down.

“What would you do?”

She showed him. Smaller boats on the shallow passage, the heavy cargo landed where the carts could turn, a receiving officer who could accept a load without sending for permission. Reserves held inland, beyond the reach of a single bridge. Men paid when they completed their part, so that the whole journey did not depend on their willingness to wait for everybody else.

“And the Court?” he asked.

“Will interfere.”

“Can you prevent it?”

“No.”

His mouth moved. She thought he might smile, but he reached for his cup instead.

“What can you prevent?”

“One interference stopping everything.”

He drank. A ring of pale sediment remained on the inside of the cup. Lyra felt thirst and declined to look toward the pitcher.

“The Court will have to eat through a dispute,” he said. “I want its kitchens supplied from my stores. Its lamps too. Let them keep the chandeliers.”

The offer was generous enough to make her read it twice. Advance money for two boats, use of storage at three sites, guaranteed work through the following winter. In exchange the Prince wanted priority, discretion, and the right to appoint armed escorts.

“Priority over what?”

“Your other customers.”

“For how many days?”

“For as long as my goods need moving.”

“Then you want the company.”

“I could buy it.”

“No. You could buy our boats and stores. You would still need somebody to send the right boat to the right door.”

Katerina’s hand rested flat on the table. The Prince looked at it, then at Lyra.

“Seven days’ notice,” he said.

“Of each requirement. With quantities. I may decline what exceeds the capacity agreed.”

“You may provide more capacity.”

“If you pay for it.”

They worked through the terms. It took longer than half an hour. The light moved across the floor until the Prince closed the shutter himself. When he returned to his chair, the cloak caught briefly on the arm and he freed it with an impatient movement that made Lyra’s stomach turn.

There would be sealed consignments. Their contents would be described by handling requirements, weight, and the authority under which they moved. Lyra asked for a right to inspect suspected leakage, heat, or danger to the crew. She obtained it after agreeing that an appointed man would be present. She asked that no person be carried as cargo.

The Prince put down his pen.

“Do you expect me to ask it?”

“I expect it to be clear.”

“My people travel with my goods.”

“As people. Named and counted.”

Katerina said something too softly for Lyra to hear. The Prince turned his head toward her. Whatever had been said, he did not have it repeated.

“Very well.”

The Prince wanted deliveries to the lower stores of the Opera House. Lyra knew the building by sight. She asked who controlled its entrances.

“Mine, below,” the Prince said. “The Court has its amusements above.”

“And the stair between?”

“A matter on which we have reached an understanding.”

“I’ll need to see it.”

Katerina rose.

“You will.”

They had brought no witness of Lyra’s choosing. She took the agreement away unsigned, together with an appointment at the Opera House and a smaller payment for the work she had already done. The Prince watched her count it.

“Do you think I would trouble to cheat you of that?”

“I think we should both know that I count.”

On the stair Corven fell into step beside her. He looked once at the packet under her arm, then at her face.

“Outside,” she said.

He waited until they were two streets away before asking whether she was all right.

Maeron read the agreement at the kitchen table while Marda dressed a child’s scraped knee. The boy had fallen running along a wall and was more angry with the wall than with his injury.

“Does it hurt?” Marda asked.

“Yes.”

“Then hold still and let me finish.”

Maeron reached the condition concerning people carried as cargo. He touched the line with one finger and continued reading.

“He needs you,” he said at last.

“He needs deliveries.”

“At present that appears to be the same thing.”

Lyra took a strip of clean cloth from the basket and passed it to Marda. The boy had stopped watching his knee and begun watching Maeron. Lyra recognized the particular quiet that came over people when they waited for his judgment.

“Would you take it?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“Why?”

“Because we have been buying rope as if it were stolen. Because an injured diver still needs feeding. Because you know more about this work than the people who have been doing it for him.”

“He will use us.”

“Yes.”

“And the sealed loads?”

“You have required them to be safe.”

“I’ve required him to say they are.”

Maeron looked again at the sheet.

“Then make the authority named. A person you can summon when a seal leaks.”

It was useful advice. Lyra wrote the addition before she could lose it among her other doubts. Marda finished the bandage and sent the boy to ask his mother whether he could stay for supper.

When he had gone, Marda said, “If you take the work, people will say we belong to him.”

“They already say we belong to somebody,” Lyra answered.

“I know. I should still like them to be wrong.”

Katerina showed her the Opera House two days later, before the first delivery was due.

They entered through the west receiving court, between a cart full of painted trees and three women carrying covered trays. One of the trees had caught in the arch. Its leaves trembled while a man on the cart tried to bend it without snapping the trunk. From somewhere above came the voice of a singer going through the same phrase with increasing anger.

“The public doors are on the other side,” Katerina said.

“I assumed so.”

“Some people are disappointed.”

The hoist room smelled of grease, damp rope, and the sour water used to wash floors. Its working stair descended beside the shaft to the salt level. The loads for the Prince went farther down than scenery, and the men handling them carried different tokens. Katerina showed Lyra the mark she would need and named the man empowered to admit her crews.

From the salt level a narrow corridor ran under the stage. Two guards stood at its far end. One had the same dry sound inside his coat as the man at the Prince’s door. Katerina turned away before they reached him.

“Your deliveries stop at the marked floor.”

“Which one?”

She pointed out the red-painted strip where the corridor met the larger room. Lyra knelt to examine the turn. A heavy load could come down the stair only if its carrying poles were removed before the bend.

“You’ll damage the walls bringing anything long this way.”

“We use the hoist.”

“If it stops?”

“We repair it.”

“While the people below wait?”

Katerina regarded her with an expression Lyra could not read.

“I’ll show you the other stair.”

They went down another short flight to Store Six. It had been used for barrels, but only the dark rings remained. A raised north return stair led toward the working stair above, keeping clear of the shallow water at the floor’s lower end. On the opposite wall a maintenance walk led to an iron inspection gate. Its counterweight hung in a guarded shaft, and a hand-release stood on their side. Beyond the gate was a wheel for a lower sluice. Lyra tested the release with Katerina’s permission. The gate dropped a finger’s breadth before she checked it with the lowering rope.

“That needs oil.”

“It gets salt.”

“It can get both.”

A separate low opening led to the canal. At low water a small boat could enter. At high water nobody could stand beneath its lintel. Lyra measured the opening with her hands and asked when it had last been used.

“Last month.”

“For what?”

“Taking out spoiled sacks.”

“Could a man get up the north stair carrying another man?”

Katerina looked at the steps. “If he were strong.”

“Could two carry a stretcher?”

“Measure it.”

Lyra did. At the turn she needed to take off her coat and use its length to mark the floor. Katerina held one end without comment. They would need a short litter, or a chair with carrying straps. Lyra made a note to have both available.

“This store is unused?”

“For now.”

“Put its use in the agreement. I need somewhere below where a crew can set down equipment without somebody carrying it away.”

“That may be arranged.”

The first load came on the following morning. Lyra attended it herself. There were sacks of salt, casks of lamp oil, iron fittings, and two sealed boxes which fitted easily on the hoist. The receiving man had not expected the full load and tried to send half away. Lyra produced his authorization, obtained the signature, and had the boxes set inside the painted line before he could send for someone else.

A boy carrying a tray stopped to watch. His sleeves were too long, and he had tucked them back unevenly. Lyra helped him roll the nearer one while the hoist descended for another load.

“Will you be here often?” he asked.

“If they pay.”

“They pay the singers.”

“Then perhaps I should learn.”

He laughed. The guard at the corridor turned his head toward them, and the boy picked up his tray and hurried up the stair.

By midday everything had been accepted. Her crews were eating in the west court, sitting on the empty carrying poles. One of the painted trees had lost a branch. A woman brought it past and offered a handful of false fruit to the men. Seldo put a pear in his cap.

Lyra sat on the stone beside them and opened the food Marda had packed. She had been paid in advance. The men would be paid before they went home. Above the court, a singer began the difficult phrase again and this time completed it.

Chapter 11 Sealed consignments

The first broken seal was an accident.

A carrying pole caught the door at Ropewalk, tilted the case, and drove one corner against the iron shoe. The wood held. The wax did not. A neat half-moon broke away from the impression of a split reed and dropped into the mud.

Lyra arrived to find the case beneath a tarpaulin and six men refusing to touch it.

“Who moved it here?”

“I did,” Seldo said.

“Then why have you stopped?”

“Jorin said we’re not to.”

Jorin had been working for her for a month. He was narrow in the shoulders and strong in a way that became apparent only when he lifted something. His wife took in linen from three inns and had sent him out to find work because he kept putting the wet sheets on the wrong line.

“I said we’re to ask,” he said. “That’s what you told us.”

“You were right.”

Seldo made a small movement with his hands, conceding that the mistake had become his by stages.

Lyra went beneath the tarpaulin and knelt. The case was shallow and oblong, bound with blue cord. There were no wet patches beneath it. She held a hand over the cracked corner, felt nothing unusual, and checked with the back of her fingers. Her hands could make anything seem warmer than it was.

“Get a bowl of clean water,” she said. “Put it beside that corner. Don’t touch the lid.”

“What for?” Jorin asked.

“To see if it cools.”

She sent for the appointed examiner. He arrived two hours later, by which time the crew had completed another job and the bowl had acquired a small beetle. The examiner wore gloves and brought two guards. He seemed disappointed to find nobody waiting in fear.

“You should have remained with the goods.”

“They remained in our store.”

“The seal was compromised.”

“I know. We sent for you.”

He told the guards to clear the passage. Lyra stayed. He looked at her, then at the case.

“You need not attend.”

“My agreement says I may.”

He broke the remaining wax, loosened the blue cord, and opened the lid just far enough to look inside. Lyra saw felt packing and the rounded tops of narrow vessels. A scent rose from them, mineral over something sweet, and dried the back of her tongue.

“Undamaged,” he said.

“What are the vessels made of?”

“Glass.”

“What happens if one breaks?”

“You leave it and summon me.”

“And if one of my people is cut?”

“Wash the injury with water. Send for a surgeon if the cut is deep.”

The answers were reasonable. They were also what she might have been told about a box of scented oil. She asked him to name the preparation on the handling sheet.

“Mineral preparations,” he wrote.

“That is not a name.”

“It is the description authorized.”

He showed her his authority. The Prince’s seal stood at the bottom, clear and whole. Lyra could have stopped the load. It would have meant calling Katerina, delaying the receiving house, and explaining why a broken piece of wax justified her asking for information she had agreed not to require. She read the handling instructions again. Keep cool. Return empties with fittings undisturbed. Do not expose to flame.

“Write what you’ve just told me about injuries.”

He did so with a little show of patience. She made a copy for the store and let the case go.

By the following month she knew which deliveries could travel together and which men would refuse to share a boat. Soap could go with iron if the soap was covered. Fish oil could go with almost anything provided nobody expected to sell the wrapping. The sealed cases required dry space, shade, and a man at each end who would sign without looking inside. Their empties returned far faster than other containers. Once she began sending them back with the morning laundry boats, nobody had to make a separate voyage for them.

Tesset marked the saved hire in red. Lyra read the figure twice, pleased with herself.

She bought herself a new dress from a woman who measured her twice and asked whether she meant to eat more. It was dark green, practical at the wrist, with enough cloth in the skirt to let her climb stairs. She wore it to the shore house and received compliments from Marda, who immediately checked the inside seams.

Maeron looked up when she entered.

“Oh,” he said.

She had prepared for something clever and found she liked that better.

“Don’t spill anything on me.”

“I shall sit farther away.”

“Then who will pour?”

He held out her chair. They ate late, after most of the house had settled. Marda had left a dish in the warmest part of the hearth and threatened both of them if they forgot it. Lyra told him about the woman who made the dress. He told her about a diver who had brought back three copper rings and proposed to sell each one as the ring of a drowned king.

“Were there three kings?”

“He was prepared to argue it.”

“Three fingers might have been easier.”

“I shall tell him you said so.”

She laughed with her mouth full and had to put a hand over it. Maeron gave her the cloth. The sound of a child coughing came from upstairs. They both paused until they heard Marda moving and the cough settled.

Later, while they cleared away, Lyra asked him to show her again how to hold cold in a closed vessel. She had kept cream sweet for two days through a week of heat, but the third time the vessel had cracked.

“What were you thinking about?” he asked.

“The cream.”

“And when the pot began to resist?”

“That I had done it before.”

He nodded.

“That is a difficult thought to let go.”

She turned the cracked pot in her hands. A trickle had dried down the side, leaving a pale track.

“Gyllan would have asked why I needed it to be this pot.”

“What would you have told him?”

“That it was the pot I had.”

“And then?”

She smiled before she answered.

“He’d have given me another and asked whether I still needed it.”

Maeron went to the shelf and took down two jars. He placed them beside each other, one earthenware and one thick green glass. Lyra touched the rims. She remembered Gyllan setting things within reach as though he had always meant to leave them there, letting her discover the part of his assistance she could accept without feeling corrected.

At the University he had once tried to sit with her at a children’s table. His knees would not go beneath it. She could not remember the lesson, only laughing while he asked whether he must spend the afternoon sitting sideways.

“Which?” Maeron asked.

“This one.” She took the glass.

“Why?”

“So I can see it fail.”

He gave a quiet laugh and sat beside her. They worked until Marda appeared at the kitchen door in her nightclothes and asked whether the Maw had forbidden sleep as well as warm fingers.

Corven objected to the delivery at Copper Lane before he objected to the men receiving it.

“There is one way out.”

“There’s the water door.”

“At that tide there’s the water.”

“They receive at that hour.”

“Then they can receive elsewhere.”

Lyra had spent the morning arranging the load around two other deliveries. Moving it would require a second boat or leave the oil casks at Ropewalk until the following day. Rain had softened the yard’s surface, and a cart wheel had already sunk to its hub beside the stack she needed moved.

She took the route sheet from him.

“Show me.”

He took her to Copper Lane himself. The passage began wide enough for three people, narrowed between two projecting walls, and ended at a door beneath an upper gallery. A man could stand in the gallery and drop a stone onto anyone knocking. Beyond the door lay a private landing whose steps vanished under brown water.

“Who owns it?” Lyra asked.

“The people paying us.”

“That doesn’t answer the question.”

“No.”

A woman watched from the gallery while they measured the width of the passage. She wore a shawl despite the heat and scratched at one wrist until a man drew her back through the door.

Lyra looked down at the water. A light boat could pass the opening, but could not wait safely while men brought heavy goods through the house. Even if nobody attacked, somebody could be crushed between the boat and the steps.

“We’ll unload at Eel Stair,” she said. “They can come to us.”

“They’ll say no.”

“Then they’ll receive nothing.”

Katerina came to the office before evening. She had the route sheet folded in her hand.

“This was agreed.”

“Without a safe landing.”

“Your men have used it before.”

“Mine haven’t.”

“The receiving people won’t come into a public place.”

“They need not. Eel Stair is ours for the hour. They can bring their own men and take the load beyond it.”

“Discretion was part of the agreement.”

“So was keeping the crews alive.”

Katerina rubbed a finger along the fold. Lyra watched her choose between arguments. The woman’s whisper compelled attention more effectively than a raised voice; Lyra resented finding herself leaning toward it.

“I can give you guards.”

“There is nowhere for a guard to stand that stops the tide.”

Katerina almost smiled.

“Eel Stair. One hour. Covered goods.”

“Paid handling time if they keep us longer.”

“Yes.”

After she left, Corven took the route sheet and put it with the revised instructions. Lyra expected him to thank her. He began checking the buckles on the carrying slings instead. His lack of gratitude annoyed her until she remembered that she paid him to find precisely these difficulties.

The delivery went through Eel Stair without an accident. The receiving men wore gloves. One had a patch of white skin extending from his cuff, cracked in a pattern that reminded Lyra of dry salt mud. He gave the correct countersign and counted the cases twice. When a porter asked him to move his foot, he looked down as if he had forgotten it was there.

At the end of the quarter Tesset asked for two days away. Her sister’s eldest had been promised a visit to the outer beaches, and she meant to go with them before the child became too old to enjoy being taken.

“She’s eleven,” she said. “Already she thinks she has seen everything.”

“Has she seen the beach?”

“No. That’s why I want to catch her before she decides it’s unnecessary.”

Lyra approved the days, then looked at the work Tesset had laid out for her absence. There were instructions for the copying clerk, letters ready for signatures, and a piece of cord tied around the drawer whose contents ought to be left alone.

“Am I allowed to open that?”

“You’re allowed to. I’ve put a note inside asking you to close it again.”

“Why?”

“Because the entries are in the order I need, and if you make them more sensible I won’t find them.”

Lyra untied the cord. The note said exactly what Tesset had promised. Beneath it lay a sweet wrapped in paper.

“What’s this for?”

“To occupy your hands while you put the note back.”

She laughed and did as she had been asked. Tesset had earned a share of the improved quarter’s returns. Lyra gave it to her before she left, in a small bag separate from her ordinary wages.

Tesset opened it, looked inside, and sat down again.

“Is this correct?”

“I checked.”

“So did you when Seldo worked five days.”

“Check again, then.”

Tesset counted. When she had finished she remained at the desk with her hand around the bag. The little bird watched them from beside the ink.

“I can take the room,” she said.

“The one by your sister?”

“The one below her. The old tenant’s leaving. She says it will be almost as if I haven’t moved.”

“Will it?”

“There will be a ceiling between me and the children. I intend to treasure it.”

Lyra wanted to celebrate. She asked Tesset to eat with her. The girl glanced toward the unfinished letters, and Lyra put her coat over them.

“Now,” she said.

They went to a stall near the northern market where fish was fried in a shallow pan and served on flat bread. Tesset knew the woman cooking and received an extra piece which she passed to Lyra. They sat on a bench that had been repaired with a plank from a painted sign. Between their skirts a pair of red lips advertised something neither could identify.

The market was closing. Stalls had been folded beneath their own covers. Two men with a cage of little birds were arguing over which of them had agreed to carry it home. Farther along, a woman swept discarded leaves into the gutter while her son followed, rescuing any vegetable he thought could still be sold.

Lyra ate slowly. She had forgotten how hot food could be when it went directly from a pan to the mouth. Tesset watched her take the first bite and laughed when she had to open her mouth and breathe over it.

“You might have warned me.”

“You were looking so certain.”

“About fish?”

“About everything.”

Lyra looked down at the bread. Oil had reached the cuff of her dress.

“Does it seem that way?”

“Sometimes. At work.”

“And now?”

“Now you have fish on your sleeve.”

Tesset gave her a clean corner of the wrapping. Lyra wiped the oil and let the question pass.

A man stopped at their bench to ask whether the Opera House would need extra porters that week. He had been sent away twice and wanted someone to tell him which mornings were worth missing other work for. Lyra asked his name, told him when to come to Ropewalk, and promised he wouldn’t leave without an answer. Afterward Tesset picked a bone from her fish.

“You’ll be taking names in your sleep.”

“He needed to know.”

“Yes.”

The boy rescuing vegetables found a whole onion beneath a basket. He held it up to his mother, triumphant. She examined it and put it into her pocket. Tesset followed them with her eyes until they disappeared around the corner.

“My brother-in-law used to wait like that,” she said. “At the gates. Some days they took him. Some days they let him wait until they knew they didn’t need him.”

“What does he do now?”

“He works at a dyehouse. His hands are blue, but we know when he’s coming back.”

Lyra thought of the man at the Opera House, the one with the dry clicking beneath his coat. She pulled at the bread until a little of its filling fell through the tear.

Tesset caught it in the paper.

“Here. You’re losing your supper.”

They stayed until the cook had put the fire out. On the walk home Tesset bought a length of bright ribbon for her sister’s younger daughter. She spent a long time choosing between two nearly identical colors and finally bought both. Lyra stood beside her without suggesting a use for the difference in cost.

At the turn to the dyers’ street Tesset hugged her. It was brief and unexpected. Lyra put her arms around her a moment late, feeling the warmth of another body through an ordinary coat.

“Enjoy the beach,” she said.

“I mean to.”

Lyra walked the rest of the way alone. At the shore house she found herself wanting to tell Maeron about the ribbon and Tesset’s new room, and went in before returning home.

Jorin’s wife came to collect his wages on a morning of heavy rain. She had wrapped her head in a flour sack, the printing turned inward, and carried a basket of linen beneath her arm.

“He said you would let me.”

“He did.” Lyra fetched the money. “Is he ill?”

“He’s asleep.”

“He was due here.”

“He took the night work. At the Opera House.”

Lyra paused with the bag in her hand.

“What night work?”

“The new work. Carrying in the lower rooms.”

“He didn’t tell me.”

“He said they would.”

Ada took off the flour sack and shook it by the door. She had a red mark across her forehead where its seam had pressed. Rainwater ran from the end of her braid.

“It’s more money,” she said.

“I imagined it might be.”

“Do you need him to give notice?”

“Yes. But tell him I’ll speak to him when he’s rested.”

“He’s been wanting a better room. I said we could have one if he stopped bringing the whole inn’s washing home on his back and making me do it at night.” She smiled, then saw that Lyra had not joined her. “Is the work bad?”

“I haven’t seen it.”

“Could you ask?”

“Yes.”

Lyra gave her the full amount and the extra half-day Jorin had earned waiting for the examiner. Ada counted it carefully, tucked it beneath her dress, and went back into the rain.

Jorin came the next afternoon. He apologized for the notice, said the offer had required an immediate answer, and offered to work both jobs until she replaced him. Lyra told him he would fall asleep beneath a load. He grinned as if she had praised his willingness.

“Who hired you?”

“The stores master. He has a paper from the Prince.”

“Have you a copy?”

“No.”

“Get one.”

“They don’t like things taken out.”

“Your terms of work belong to you.”

He looked down at his hands. His nails were clean for once. Lyra remembered how often he had torn them carrying wet rope and found that she did not want to be the woman who made him feel foolish for wanting easier work.

“What do you carry?”

“Cases. Water. People sometimes, when they’re sick.”

“What people?”

“The ones working there. I haven’t been into all the rooms.”

“And the night hours?”

“Quiet. They give us something if we can’t settle afterward.”

“What?”

He shrugged. “Drops. You put them in water. I haven’t needed any.”

She asked Katerina. Katerina said the Prince employed many people on difficult hours and kept a physician. The preparations were supplied through him. Lyra asked for the physician’s name and received it, together with an invitation to submit a written question. She sent one concerning fatigue and working hours. The answer said rest was required between shifts and the arrangements provided it.

She showed the answer to Jorin when he came to collect his last pay. He folded it and put it inside his coat. He had brought Ada’s thanks for finding the missing half-day. They had taken a room with a window that opened.

For the next few months Lyra saw him in the Opera House corridors, sometimes carrying water, sometimes walking with men who did not look at her. He always raised a hand. She asked whether he was sleeping. He said yes.

The business grew through those months. Ropewalk required another roof repair, which she could now pay for without counting what remained afterward. She took on a second clerk for copying, hired boats by the season, and had to learn which decisions she could leave with Tesset. She took a room two streets from Low Bell, with a window she could open without letting the rain in. When she returned the old key, her former employer said she would still expect to see her. Lyra promised she would. Some days she came to the office and found everything already done that she had hurried there to do.

She disliked those days until she discovered she could leave early.

On one of them she went to the shore house in daylight and found Maeron alone in the yard, washing sand from a little shell. He asked whether something had gone wrong. She said no and sat beside him.

For a while they did nothing useful. Beyond the wall the city worked noisily without her. Maeron showed her the shell, its inner curl the color of a child’s ear, and she held it in her cold palm until its tiny weight ceased to feel like something she had to account for.

Chapter 12 A passage held open

The bridge was to be closed on the morning of the delivery. Lyra learned this from a woman who sold hot peas beside its western foot and wanted to know whether she should order less charcoal.

“They’ve put a notice up,” the woman said. “But I can’t make out if it’s the whole day or until they’ve done whatever it is.”

Lyra went to read it. Repairs to the paving. No wheeled traffic from dawn until release by the appointed inspector. The inspector’s name had been added in a different ink. She knew him. He had once asked her for a fee to examine timber which he never approached closely enough to touch.

“How much charcoal have you ordered?” she asked.

“Enough for the men from the carts.”

“Keep the order. There’ll be men.”

She went back to the office and asked Tesset for the unused boat lists.
