Two days later Bast arrived to discuss his fee. His employer had not paid him for six weeks, but had continued instructing him to keep his lighter ready. He owned the poles and most of the rope. The boat belonged to a woman he hadn’t paid either.

“If I take your job, he’ll say I’ve stolen her boat.”

“Then bring her.”

“She can’t get down the stairs.”

“I can get up them.”

The owner lived above a shop full of cracked pitchers. Lyra sat at her bedside while Bast waited near the door, trying not to tread on a cat. They agreed on hire through the owner, wages paid directly to Bast, and a sum set aside for the work he had already done. It would be recovered from the employer’s next payment if that payment arrived. Lyra expected it would not.

“You’re the woman with the divers,” the owner said.

“Yes.”

“Will you make him go under?”

“No.”

“I go under when the boat does,” Bast said. “It’s in my agreement.”

The old woman waved him quiet. She wanted the answer from the person paying.

“He’ll work above water,” Lyra said. “If he wishes to stop, he can tell us.”

“He never wishes. That’s the trouble.”

Bast grinned at the cat. He had a broad face, an old break in his nose, and a habit of listening with his head on one side. Lyra asked whether he had worked the passage beneath Cooper’s Bridge. He said yes, and stopped grinning when she showed him the proposed load.

“Take that first,” he said, pointing at the iron. “Before the tide turns. Afterward you’ll scrape.”

She moved it on the sheet.

He leaned closer.

“And give me another man. One who knows the pole’s for pushing against stone and not waving at people.”

On the evening before departure, Lyra spread her route sheets across Marda’s kitchen table. Three routes would carry the goods. The largest load would leave early for the cart landing; Bast’s lighter would take the shallow passage; a third crew would move food and spare fittings through Eel Stair while the Court’s men watched the closed bridge.

The Prince had begun buying businesses whose owners could no longer afford protection. He needed supplies delivered regularly enough that he could keep them open while their neighbors refused to trade with them. At one of the salt houses he had bought, she had watched an old foreman teach his replacement where the roof dripped.

“They’ll see what we’re doing,” Corven said.

“Yes.”

“They’ll choose a place.”

“They’ll have to choose before they know which load matters most.”

“Which does?”

“The people.”

He looked at her. She moved the sheet showing Bast’s passage nearer him.

“That has the least room. Go with it.”

Maeron stood at the hearth, holding a cup above the pot because there was nowhere left on the table to put it. Neris sat on the floor with her back against the wall, cleaning grit from a coupling. Lyra had asked her to accompany the shallow boat in case they needed someone below to free a caught rope.

“What do you need me for?” Maeron asked.

“The lower passage has an old wall. Bast thinks we may scrape at the turn. I want you there before anybody tries to force a boat through.”

“You expect the paving works to be more than paving?”

“Yes.”

He nodded. Neris put down the coupling and asked whether she would be paid for waiting if nobody needed her below.

“Yes,” Lyra said.

“Good. Then I hope to be very idle.”

Marda reached between the papers to put down a bowl of onions. Everyone moved a hand to save a sheet. She had warned them twice that she needed the table.

“You can plan how to survive while you’re eating,” she said.

Lyra rolled the papers. They ate standing or sitting on whatever remained free. Corven gave Neris half his bread without asking whether she wanted it. She took it and gave him the coupling, which he inspected with bread held between his teeth.

Lyra slept badly. In the morning she found she had laid out the new dress before remembering what work she meant to do. She put it away and took the old one, with a wool coat for the boat.

At first the difficulties were ordinary. A cask had been marked for the wrong landing. One man arrived late because his youngest child had been sick. The first boat left with its spare pole on the bank, and Seldo ran alongside until somebody caught it. Lyra sent the late man to the slower crew and made the cask move with him.

Bast stood in the stern of his lighter, trying its response against the pole. The boat had been patched with three shades of wood. He had rubbed the newest seam himself and asked Lyra to look at it before any load went aboard.

“It will hold?” she asked.

“It held yesterday.”

“That’s not much of a promise.”

“It’s the only day I’ve tested.”

Seldo would follow the towpath with a spare pole and help at the arch if the lighter caught. He left while Bast was still testing the stern line.

Lyra stepped in, felt the boat move under her, and sat where he pointed. Neris was already beneath the small canvas cover, her head in her arms. Corven stood near the bow. His eel’s vessel had been secured inside a padded box, with air holes at the top. He lifted the lid once before they left, touched the water, and closed it gently.

The early light reached the canal in broken strips. Men opened shutters above them. A woman lowered a basket to a boat carrying bread. Lyra smelled wet stone, smoke, and the sharp sweetness of a peel someone had thrown into the water.

They passed the first turn without scraping. Bast’s additional man handled the forward pole well. Corven shifted his weight only when told. At the next landing Maeron waited beside the narrow arch which opened beneath the bridge approach. He had taken off his coat and folded it on a dry stone.

“Room enough,” he said.

Bast put a hand against the wall as they entered. It came away with a smear of new mortar.

“Someone’s been busy.”

Lyra leaned to look. The patch was low, above the water at ordinary tide, and had been spread hastily over old stone. She told him to stop.

The first bolt struck the cask beside her hand.

For a moment she looked at the shaft without understanding why it was there. Then Corven pushed her down. The second bolt hit the canvas cover and went through. Neris rolled sideways, tore at the canvas, and disappeared beneath the water before Lyra could call her name.

“Hold,” Bast said.

The forward pole jammed. His man pulled, lost his footing, and sat down hard among the sacks. Above the arch a voice told them to leave the goods and get out.

Maeron’s song stirred the stone against Lyra’s shoulder before she heard it. The arch shuddered. Dust fell from a joint overhead.

Corven went over the side into water up to his knees. There was a maintenance ledge beneath it; he had asked Bast where it ran before they boarded. He held the chain in one hand and the cracked shell in the other. A man came through the far opening, short sword raised, and tried to shout back to somebody behind him.

His mouth moved without sound.

Corven struck him across the wrist with the chain. The sword splashed into the water. A second man thrust past the first and caught Corven beneath the ribs before Corven could turn. Lyra saw the blade go in, saw Corven’s mouth open, and thought he had been silenced too.

He caught the man’s sword arm against the wall. With his other hand he touched the short iron tool at his belt. His lips moved. He drew it and drove the hooked end beneath the man’s breastplate.

She heard a hard scrape, then a wet cough. Corven pulled sideways. The man fell against him. Corven shoved him away and staggered, his own coat darkening at the side.

Lyra crawled over the sacks to Bast.

“Can you get us out?”

“If the pole comes free.”

“Cut it loose.”

“It isn’t tied.”

The boat lurched. Neris surfaced at the bow with one hand on the trapped pole and the other braced against the wall. She spat water and shouted for them to move. Bast drove with his own pole. The lighter slid forward a foot.

Something cracked overhead.

Lyra looked up in time to see the fresh mortar split. A squared stone dropped from the side of the arch and struck the water beside the boat. Another shifted. Maeron’s song deepened until her teeth hurt. The fallen stone stopped sinking. It hung against the wall, water squeezed white around its edges.

The upper stones pressed together with a noise like bones inside a tight fist.

“Go,” Maeron said, and the word came through the song without interrupting it.

Bast drove again. The boat moved. The man whose voice Corven had taken came out of the water holding a knife. He caught the stern and hauled himself halfway over it. Bast turned the pole against his shoulder. The man thrust upward beneath it.

Seldo, who had reached the near opening from the bank, struck the man across the face with another pole. His grip broke. He dropped into the water, leaving the knife in Bast’s leg.

Bast made an irritated sound, almost a complaint, and tried to keep pushing. His knee gave way. Lyra caught his shoulder as he fell and felt the sudden hot wetness under her other hand.

The blade had entered high on the inside of his thigh. He reached down, found the handle, and began to pull.

“Leave it.”

He looked at her as if she had given him an unreasonable instruction. Blood was already running past the blade. She pressed hard above it with the heel of her hand. He cried out, then apologized.

“Don’t,” she said. “Lie still.”

The boat scraped. Neris was beside them again, pushing from the water. She had been under longer than Lyra had seen anyone remain, longer even than the dives which had first made her doubt their accounts. Her eyes were wide and clear. She took a breath, went down, and the boat moved another foot.

Lyra called for cloth. Seldo threw in his folded shirt. It landed on the sacks beyond her reach. She had to choose between taking her hand away and letting Bast bleed while she tried to drag it with her foot.

“Bring it,” she said, too sharply.

He climbed into the boat. The arch groaned overhead. Maeron stood with both hands against the wall, blood running from one nostril. He was looking at them and could not come closer.

Lyra pressed the cloth around the blade. Bast’s head rolled against a cask. His skin had gone gray beneath the sunburn.

The old working rose in her memory, the sequence Gyllan had made her repeat until she could do it while distracted. She began it. There was too much blood. She could bring tissue toward tissue; she could not find the depth of the torn place through the flood filling her fingers. She heard Gyllan telling her to stop, to see what she was holding, and knew the voice was memory because it used exactly the words it always used.

“Lyra,” Seldo said.

“I know.”

Bast tried to speak. She bent nearer. He said something about the boat owner, about the money, and she told him to be quiet with an anger that frightened her.

A vessel could hold. A closed jar. A cup. A folded cloth laid firmly against an opening.

She made the shape with her hands and attended to the space beneath them. The cold she knew in her fingers moved inward. Bast jerked. She pressed harder, found the edge of the wound through the cloth, and gave the pressure somewhere to settle.

The blood slowed.

She did not look away. Seldo said her name again. She told him to draw the knife straight, slowly, and hold the cloth where she showed him. As the blade came free she expected the pressure to break. It shifted instead, filling the place the iron had left.

The skin around her hands turned pale. The pallor spread in an uneven triangle toward his groin. Blood stopped running over her wrist.

Bast breathed. A thin, ugly breath, but another followed it.

“Keep us moving,” she said.

They reached open water as the arch gave a long, grinding shudder behind them. Maeron stepped away from the wall. Corven caught him before he fell. The stones settled into one another and the passage narrowed with a rush of dust and dirty water.

The boat was through. Lyra looked for Neris and could not see her.

“Neris.”

No answer came. She could not leave Bast. The pale flesh remained cold beneath her palms, colder than her own hands, and she did not know how long it would hold if she stopped attending to it.

Corven climbed onto the bank and bent double. Blood dripped from his coat onto the stone. Maeron tried to help him, then turned as something moved beside the boat.

Neris rose without hurry, took hold of the gunwale, and looked at Lyra.

“Are we clear?” she asked.

“Yes.”

She climbed in. There was no breathlessness in her, only an irritated attempt to get her wet hair out of her mouth. Lyra stared until Neris looked down at Bast and asked what was needed.

They went to Eel Stair. The third crew had finished unloading and was preparing to leave. Lyra ordered two men to stay, sent one for a surgeon, and made the others bring every blanket they could find. She remained beside Bast while they lifted him. When the movement pulled at his wound he screamed, and she felt a fierce relief that he had strength to make the sound.

The surgeon arrived complaining about the messenger, who had told him three different locations before settling on the right one. He stopped complaining when he saw Corven.

“You first.”

“Bast,” Corven said.

“The man on the table is lying down. You’re leaking on my shoes.”

Lyra showed him Bast’s wound. He touched the pale margin, pressed one finger below it, and looked sharply at her.

“What did you do?”

“Stopped the bleeding.”

“I can see that. How?”

She explained as well as she could. He asked her to lift her hands slowly. She did. The wound remained closed, but the triangle of flesh stayed white.

“Can you release it?”

“I don’t know.”

“Then don’t try until I’ve had a look.”

He worked while she stood beside him, answering questions. Bast slept or fainted; Lyra could not tell which. His pulse strengthened. The surgeon said that mattered, and sent her away to wash.

At the basin she discovered her hands were shaking. Water turned pink around them. She rubbed at the blood until it came away and the skin beneath looked unfamiliar, too smooth and pale along the joints.

Maeron stood at the door. He had washed his face, but a dark line remained beneath one nostril.

“You brought him through,” he said.

“He isn’t through yet.”

“No.”

She looked at him. He came closer only after she held out her hand.

The cold where their fingers met was so complete that she could not tell whose skin she felt. Somewhere in the room a cup trembled against its saucer. Lyra became aware that she had not breathed for several moments. She drew air deliberately and found she did not want very much of it.

Maeron watched her discover this.

“How many?” she asked.

He understood.

“I don’t know.”

Marda sent word before evening. Three of the people at the shore house had felt something change while carrying laundry upstairs. One had sat down on the landing and remained there until she brought a chair to enclose the space behind him. Marda herself could no longer bear the kitchen fire at her back. She had moved the table away from it and asked the cook to do the hot work.

The messenger had also brought food. Marda had known they would forget to eat.

Corven lay on a cot with his shirt open and a bandage running beneath his ribs. The surgeon had drawn the wound together and said he was fortunate. Corven asked whether that cost extra. It had hurt to speak; Lyra saw him regret the joke and watched the surgeon pretend not to.

Bast woke after dark. He asked for water, then asked where his boat was. Lyra told him it was tied safely at Eel Stair and that the owner would be paid for the damage.

“Did the iron get there?”

“Most of it.”

“The last cask was loose.”

“We caught it.”

He closed his eyes. After a while he opened them again and looked toward his bandaged leg.

“Feels dead.”

“The surgeon will look again in the morning.”

“Am I going to lose it?”

“I don’t know.”

He watched her. She forced herself to leave the answer where it was.

“Tell him I need it,” he said.

“I have.”

She stayed until he slept. In the passage Katerina waited with news that two other loads had arrived. The Prince’s men had kept their businesses open. He wanted the same arrangements for the following week.

“Not the same,” Lyra said.

Katerina looked through the door at the cots.

“No.”

“He’ll pay for this.”

“He will pay what you ask if the work continues.”

Lyra took the packet Katerina held out. It contained the promised sum and an additional amount she had not yet requested. She counted neither until Katerina had gone.

From outside came voices. Neris and two others had brought the boat round, though Lyra had told them to rest. They spoke quietly, pleased with a difficult piece of handling. Lyra went to the door and saw Neris standing in the shallows beside the mooring, water at her waist, smiling up at something Seldo had said.

“Come inside,” Lyra called.

“In a moment.”

The men climbed the steps. Neris remained where she was until they had gone in. Then she put both hands on the stone and hauled herself out, slowly, as though leaving the water had become the tiring part.

Chapter 13 Rooms fit for the changed

Neris brought Lyra a watch from the wreck.

It had no hands. The glass had gone green at the edges, and a little creature had built a white tube around the place where the winding key entered. Neris laid it on the desk with a solemnity that lasted until Lyra looked up.

“So you needn’t keep asking how long I was down.”

Lyra turned it over. The case had been engraved with initials too worn to read.

“Does it run?”

“Very badly.”

“It should fit in here.”

Neris laughed and sat on the windowsill. She had a scrape across one cheek and a new breadth of ease in her movements. She looked healthier than when Lyra first treated her. The scar on her arm had faded. Her hair, still wet from the harbor, hung in a dark rope over one shoulder.

“How long?” Lyra asked.

“Long enough.”

“Neris.”

“They pulled twice. I answered twice.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

“I know.”

Lyra set the watch on the desk. It had been a successful recovery: good brass, a sealed chest of instruments, most of a cargo of dressed stone which could be sold close to where it lay. The crew had returned without injury. Neris had found a way beneath the wreck’s tilted side that saved them the trouble of breaking through the deck.

“You did well,” Lyra said.

Neris stopped swinging her heel against the wall.

“I did.”

The pleasure in her voice made Lyra smile. She offered her a share of the fruit Tesset had bought on the way in. Neris took a slice, bit it, and frowned.

“Too ripe?”

“No.”

She tried another bite and put the rest on the edge of the plate. Lyra had seen her eat fruit with the juice running to her elbows. Now she wiped her fingers and looked toward the door.

“Hot in here.”

“The window’s open.”

“I can see.”

She stood, moved the stool away from the wall, and put it back. Her shoulders had risen. Lyra opened the door as well.

“Better?”

“No. It’s all right.”

“It doesn’t look all right.”

Neris pressed the heels of her hands against her eyes. When she lowered them, the whites were threaded with dark lines.

“I should go.”

“Shall I come?”

“No.”

She left the fruit and went down the stairs quickly. Lyra followed as far as the landing, where she could see her cross the yard without anyone trying to stop her. At the water door Neris halted and leaned against the frame. She stayed there until her shoulders lowered.

The watch on Lyra’s desk made a small wet mark where it lay.

Neris’s sister came three mornings later. She brought a pillow.

“I thought you might know where to put her,” she said.

Lyra rose from her chair.

“Where is she?”

“At the house. She won’t come up.”

“Is she hurt?”

“I don’t know.”

The sister’s name was Tavva. Lyra had met her at a meal after the recovery and remembered two little girls fighting over who could sit beside Neris. Tavva held the pillow against her stomach. Its case had been embroidered with blue flowers. One corner was damp.

“She slept on the kitchen floor. Then the passage. Last night she put herself under the stairs.”

“Did she say why?”

“She said the room was too large. It’s the same room we’ve had for six years.”

Lyra fetched her coat. On the way she sent a messenger for Maeron and asked Tesset to postpone the morning appointments. Tavva walked quickly, looking back whenever Lyra fell half a step behind.

The house stood above a narrow shop which sold lamp wicks. Neris sat beneath the first turn of the stairs with her knees drawn up. A little girl crouched nearby holding a spoon. She looked at Lyra with relief.

“She won’t have it.”

“What is it?”

“Soup.”

“Give her a moment.”

Tavva took the bowl and called the girl into the kitchen. Neris kept her face turned toward the wall.

“It’s Lyra.”

“I know.”

“May I sit?”

“If you can.”

The space was smaller than it looked. Lyra folded herself onto the lower step and found her shoulder pressed against Neris’s knee. She could feel cold through the cloth.

“Your sister’s worried.”

“So am I.”

“What happens if you come out?”

Neris pulled her sleeve over her hand.

“Nothing.”

Lyra waited.

“Nothing happens. That’s what’s wrong. I know it won’t. I came out yesterday and stood in the room. Tavva opened the shutters. The children were playing. There was space all round me.”

She stopped. Lyra heard the girl complain in the kitchen that the soup was getting cold.

“I could feel the space,” Neris said. “As if something ought to be there. I kept waiting for it to come against me. I couldn’t tell how far away things were.”

“Does this help?”

“The stairs do. The wall does.”

“Can you eat here?”

“I can. I just don’t want that soup again.”

Lyra laughed softly. Neris’s mouth moved, and for a moment she looked like herself on the office sill.

“Then we’ll ask for something else.”

Maeron arrived while Tavva was finding bread. He knelt outside the narrow space and spoke to Neris by name. She looked at him at once.

“It has come strongly,” he said.

“Yes.”

“You needn’t force yourself into the old arrangements.”

“They’re my arrangements.”

He bowed his head a little, accepting the correction.

“Then we will change as little as you need.”

He asked her to breathe with him. Lyra watched. His own breathing slowed until she could scarcely see it. Neris followed, her shoulders sinking. The pressure of her knee against Lyra’s arm eased.

“No song,” Lyra said.

Maeron looked at her.

“I wasn’t going to sing.”

“Good.”

They stayed until Neris could move from beneath the stairs to the passage bench. Tavva put the flowered pillow behind her and sat on the floor nearby. The younger child brought bread and watched her aunt eat it with the fierce attention of somebody overseeing a difficult repair.

The shore house had changed before Lyra found the money to change it properly.

Marda had shifted beds against walls, hung cloth across the larger sleeping spaces, and moved two families into the attic so that three divers could use the cool rooms below. A man who had once slept wherever a blanket was put now required the door to be shut and a chair against it. His wife hated the closed door. They had begun taking turns in the bed.

In the kitchen, the new cook worked alone near the fire. Marda prepared food in the scullery. She had discovered she could keep fish sound for longer than anyone else, and had forbidden Lyra to point out that she had once rejected precisely that employment.

“I’m supervising,” she said, cutting away a fin.

“I wouldn’t suggest otherwise.”

“Then take this upstairs.”

Lyra carried the dish to a woman waiting for her husband to wake. He had been home for two days and had slept through most of them. The woman thanked her and asked whether he would be going out again soon.

“Does he want to?”

“He says he does.”

“Do you?”

The woman looked at the sleeping figure.

“He’s better on the boat.”

Lyra sat with her for a while. The man breathed so slowly that she found herself counting despite the watch Neris had given her. His hand lay outside the blanket, palm upward. There were little circular marks on two fingers where a line had burned him. Ordinary damage, healing ordinarily.

When Lyra went downstairs, Marda was arguing with a man about the number of chairs he wanted in a room already crowded with them.

“He needs them against his sides,” the man said.

“He needs people who can reach him.”

“He says—”

“I heard what he says. I also heard him ask for water. Carry this.”

She gave him a pitcher and sent him away. Then she put both hands flat on the table and stood without moving.

“Can you get us more room?”

“Yes.”

“Don’t answer until you’ve seen what room will cost.”

“I can get it.”

Marda closed her eyes. When she opened them, she looked older than Lyra had allowed herself to notice.

“Good,” she said. “I can’t keep doing this by moving furniture.”

The rooms Lyra found had belonged to a boat repairer who preferred working ashore. They lay against the canal below a high street, reached by a broad stair from the yard. Their floors remained dry at ordinary tide. One chamber contained a working well through which a diver could reach the underside of a moored boat; another had stone walls thick enough to keep the worst heat out. There was a small upper room for a keeper and a door wide enough to carry a sick person through without turning them on their side.

The owner wanted payment for a full year. Lyra negotiated six months, then bought a year after she calculated how much it would cost to move everyone twice.

She took Neris to inspect it. Tavva came with them, carrying the same flowered pillow.

Neris descended the steps slowly. She put her hand against the wall and left it there as they entered the cool chamber. Lyra waited in the doorway. Tavva looked from one to the other, afraid to speak first.

“This one,” Neris said.

“You can choose where the bed goes.”

“Against that wall.”

“It will be.”

“And no curtain across the door.”

“I thought you wanted—”

“I want to see it. I want the wall here and the door there.”

Lyra nodded. Tavva crossed the room and put the pillow on the floor where the bed would stand. She sat beside it. After a moment Neris sat too.

“You can bring the girls,” she said.

“I know.”

“Even if they make a noise.”

“They will.”

Neris laughed and leaned her head against her sister’s shoulder. Lyra went to inspect the working well, leaving them together.

Maeron had proposed using the submerged chamber to repair fittings without repeatedly bringing them into air. Certain inscribed pieces degraded when kept dry; chains returned from depth had to be immersed; the familiar vessels needed clean salt water. Neris and the others could do the work between voyages. Lyra had written the wages before agreeing to the space.

Corven tested the stair while carrying a sack of stone. His wound had healed enough to let him lift, but his face went tight at the turn. Lyra told him to put the sack down.

“I need to know.”

“You’ve learned.”

He lowered it, breathed carefully, and stood with one hand on the wall.

“Two can carry a man here.”

“Yes.”

“Keep it clear.”

“I will.”

“Someone always wants to put a box on a stair.”

“Then you may throw the box at them.”

He gave her a look. She corrected herself.

“Put it outside.”

“That sounds more like you.”

She did not know whether he meant it kindly. He went back down to inspect the well, leaving the sack where she could see that he intended to try the stair again.

They called the place the Tide Rooms, though Marda insisted that any tide entering the beds would be charged for the washing. Lyra paid a woman trained in nursing to attend every morning and retained the surgeon who had treated Bast. He came reluctantly at first, then began bringing a younger assistant and staying longer than his agreed hour.

“What do you know of these changes?” Lyra asked him.

He had only what people told him. He wanted to examine them, if they would let him.

She asked Maeron to speak to them. He did, standing in the cool room with his hands open, telling the people there that care was part of their endurance. Several agreed to be examined. One refused and was left alone. Lyra saw the refusal respected and allowed herself to be reassured.

She also paid for the families to visit: a boat kept free, hours arranged so a woman needn’t lose a day’s washing. Ada helped her find two other women who could cover such work. Lyra paid them directly. Tesset complained that the new pages no longer fitted inside the old binding.

“Get another book.”

“I’ve got another book. I need another desk.”

“Get that too.”

Tesset looked pleased enough to make Lyra smile. It was good to say yes without putting something else off.

When Lyra counted out the advance, Tesset said she ought to spend it before sundown. Her mother used to tell of a girl who found a warm coin in a gutter: it bought anything she wanted, but if she kept it after dark it would buy something from her instead.

“What did she buy?” Lyra asked.

“Supper. Then a blanket, the next time.” Tesset gathered the money. “I used to wish she’d come past our door.”

Lyra added a copper from her own purse. “Have supper on the way. Marda will have stopped serving by the time you find a desk.”

In the evenings she practiced at the Tide Rooms. A jar of water, a fitted lid, the felt resistance which came just before the glass complained. Maeron sat nearby, sometimes watching, sometimes speaking to somebody else. She learned to hold a vessel under pressure without chilling its contents quite so deeply. She could preserve food and stop a small wound bleeding. She had no wish to attempt Bast’s injury again.

She visited him before he left the surgeon’s care. He could stand with help and complained that the exercises hurt. The pale triangle remained. The surgeon said he had sensation below it, strength returning, and work to do before he could push a loaded boat. Lyra arranged wages until he could.

Bast took her hand.

“I haven’t said it properly.”

“You don’t need to.”

“I want to.”

She stayed. He thanked her, awkwardly at first and then with tears in his eyes, for making it possible to go home. Lyra had no prepared answer. She held his hand until he was ready to let hers go.

Neris refused the next deep recovery on a morning when three other things had already gone wrong.

The buyer required the instruments before a ship left for the eastern coast. A replacement coupling had arrived with its bore too narrow. One of the divers had a fever. Neris knew the wreck and had made the difficult passage through its side on the previous visit. Without her they would need another day of preparation and a second boat to keep the lighter steady.

She came to the office carrying her work gloves.

“I’m not going.”

Lyra looked up from the coupling measurements.

“Are you ill?”

“No.”

“Has something happened?”

“Yes.”

She waited while Neris found the words.

“Yesterday I went into the working well to fasten a line. I finished. I knew I’d finished. I could see the way up.”

Her fingers worked the edge of a glove.

“I didn’t want it.”

“The work?”

“The way up.”

Lyra put down the pen.

“I stayed until Corven pulled. I answered him because I knew he’d come down if I didn’t. I came up because he was there.”

“Then we’ll put him on your line.”

“No.”

The answer came so quickly that Lyra felt herself recoil. She had offered the first useful thing she could think of. Neris looked at the gloves and spoke more carefully.

“I don’t want to find out what happens when I stop answering.”

Maeron entered while they were still sitting opposite each other. He had come to inspect the coupling. Lyra told him Neris would not be going. Neris repeated the account, leaving out nothing and looking directly at him when she finished.

“You know more of the depth than you did,” he said.

“I know that.”

“There is a time when the old fear changes. It can feel like losing a part of yourself.”

“I’m frightened now.”

“Yes.”

“Does that count?”

He knelt beside her chair. Lyra disliked the tenderness of the movement and disliked herself for disliking it.

“Of course it counts.”

“Then I want to stop.”

“For a time.”

“I didn’t say for a time.”

The coupling lay between Lyra’s hands, its bore too narrow. She could imagine the extra work, the buyer’s response, the men waiting for her to make the recovery possible. That was what she had become good at.

“We can use another diver,” she said.

Maeron turned toward her.

“It will take longer.”

“Yes.”

“And the instruments?”

“I’ll speak to the buyer.”

Neris let out a breath. It shamed Lyra that the sound annoyed her. She wanted Neris to understand how much work the refusal made, and knew that asking her to acknowledge it would turn the permission into something uglier.

“The room?” Neris asked.

“What?”

“Do I keep it?”

“Yes. The same terms as the shore house.”

“And Tavva can come?”

“Yes.”

Neris put the gloves on the desk. Lyra pushed them back.

“You may want them for other work.”

After she left, Maeron remained kneeling beside the empty chair. He stood slowly.

“She will find her way.”

“She may find it ashore.”

“Perhaps.”

Lyra took up the pen. She made herself write the message to the buyer before asking Maeron who could replace Neris.

That evening she walked to the Tide Rooms with a small bag of the fruit Neris used to like. At the doorway she saw Tavva and the girls sitting on the floor. Neris had made a game with shells, and one child had accused the other of moving the winning piece beneath her skirt. They were all laughing.

Lyra nearly left the bag outside. Then Neris looked up and called her in.

She sat against the wall beside them. The older girl explained the game incorrectly, and Neris corrected her, and the younger one ate three pieces of fruit while everyone argued about whose turn had been lost. Lyra stayed until the bag was empty.

Chapter 14 Before the ship was lost

Tesset brought the problem on a morning when Lyra had promised herself to finish early.

A woman from the old correspondence house had invited her to supper. She had accepted, bought a small gift, and left the better dress hanging where it would remind her that she intended to go. Eighteen months had passed since the collectors came to Low Bell. It could survive an evening without her.

“Do you remember this hire?” Tesset asked.

Lyra looked up. The paper had been folded into a narrow strip and used as a marker in the salvage book. One end was greasy from the thumb that had held it beneath a meal plate.

“Which one?”

“The shallow lighter. The extra lifting rope. Paid from the recovery reserve.”

She remembered paying. There had been several requests that week, and Maeron had taken time to explain why one of the usual boats could not be used. Its owner had promised it elsewhere. She remembered being pleased that he had asked before assuming she would meet the additional cost.

“The Mercy Bell.”

“Yes.”

“What’s wrong?”

Tesset placed another sheet beside it.

“That is when the Mercy Bell was lost.”

Lyra read the date. She read it again against the hire. Eleven days separated them. The preparations had come first.

“Was it entered late?”

“I thought so. The boat owner says he was paid on that day.”

“Who asked him?”

“I did. I was checking the outstanding hires.”

Lyra took the marker out of the book. The entry named prospecting and soundings. She had approved dozens in similar terms. Crews needed to look before committing to a recovery; equipment had to be reserved before everyone learned a profitable wreck lay within reach.

“It may have been another job.”

“Yes.”

“Have you asked Maeron?”

“No. You signed it.”

The pen stroke was hers, clear and hurried. She had drawn the last letter into the next entry because she had begun answering somebody before lifting her hand.

“I’ll ask which ground they meant to examine.”

Tesset hesitated.

“There are two others.”

She brought them out. Lyra disliked the way her own attention sharpened, as if Tesset had offered a more interesting puzzle. She put a hand on the papers to keep them from sliding.

“Leave them with me.”

“Do you want copies?”

“Yes. And the owner’s answer. His words, as nearly as you remember them.”

Tesset went back to her new desk, where she could leave two books open at once. She had bought herself a little earthen bird to stand beside the ink. One wing had broken before purchase. She kept its unbroken side toward the room.

Lyra looked at the date again.

The boat owner remembered the hire because his daughter had been married on the following day.

“They wanted me to take her out,” he said, meaning the boat. “I told them no. They could have the vessel and my nephew. They weren’t having me.”

He sat under an awning mending a strap. The nephew, a long boy with a downy upper lip, pretended not to listen while shifting buckets nearby.

“Where did it go?” Lyra asked.

“South ground.”

“Which part?”

The owner called the boy over. He came with a bucket still in his hand.

“Tell her where you took the Chorus men.”

“The sand shelf. Then farther out.”

“Before or after your cousin’s wedding?”

“Before. You said I could finish after noon if they came back.”

“Did they?”

“Late.”

The owner spread his hands, having supplied more detail than he had expected to need.

“Who was aboard?” Lyra asked the boy.

He named two men she knew and one he described by a burn across his chin. Maeron had not gone. Corven had not gone. There had been coils of rope, a lead weight, and empty frames for securing recovered goods.

“Did they bring anything up?”

“A piece of iron. They put it back.”

“Why?”

“They said it wasn’t what they wanted.”

“What did they want?”

The boy looked at his uncle. His uncle told him to answer if he knew.

“I don’t.”

That much was believable. Lyra thanked them, paid the remaining hire she had come prepared to settle, and walked back along the canal. The day was warm. She had ceased wearing gloves because they made it difficult to feel a pen, but she kept her hands in her sleeves when not using them. A dog asleep beside a stall woke as she passed and moved beneath the cart.

She stopped at the fish woman’s place near Ropewalk and bought something for supper before remembering where she was supposed to eat it. The woman wrapped it anyway.

“Take it tomorrow. I’ll salt it.”

Lyra thanked her and left the money. Halfway up the street she remembered she had not told her what tomorrow’s fish should cost.

Maeron was at the Tide Rooms with Orrel, one of the younger winchmen. Orrel had a broad back and a narrow scar through his lower lip. Lyra knew him chiefly from his requests for steadier hours after his child’s birth. He had stopped asking when the Prince’s work made the hours plentiful.

They were examining a length of chain which had spent several days in the working well. Orrel held it up while Maeron passed his fingers over the links. A residue fell from them like fine black sand.

“Finished early?” Maeron asked when he saw her.

“Almost.”

She asked after the nurse’s patients. Maeron answered, naming each one. One man had begun sleeping through the night. Another had gone home for two days and returned because the journey to work exhausted him more than the work itself. Neris was teaching a new crew to examine lifting gear without trusting its appearance.

“She says I miss things,” Orrel said.

“Do you?”

“Yes.”

Maeron smiled and let the chain rest on the stone bench.

“I wanted to ask about the south-ground soundings,” Lyra said. “Before the Mercy Bell.”

Orrel’s hands moved on the chain. The link he held slipped and struck the bench with a sharp clank.

“Which soundings?” Maeron asked.

“The lighter hire. We put it against the Mercy Bell recovery, but the hire came eleven days before the loss.”

“We were examining several places.”

“Do you remember what was there?”

“Old fittings. Ground that might take a laden hull without breaking it beyond use.”

“For salvage?”

“Yes.”

“Which salvage?”

He looked toward the working well. A man below called for the line to be lowered, and Orrel went to attend to it. Maeron waited until the request had been answered.

“I would need to look. Will you bring the entries?”

“I have copies being made.”

“Then we can go through them.”

It was what she had hoped he would say. She looked at Orrel’s back. He stood very still, holding the line.

“Tomorrow?” Maeron asked.

“I have appointments.”

“After them.”

“Yes.”

He took her hand as she turned to leave. He had done so before, companionably, a touch that asked her to remain a moment without holding her there. She did remain. His fingers enclosed hers with familiar cold.

“You look tired.”

“I am.”

“Go to your supper. Marda says you’ve been trying to avoid it all week.”

“I haven’t.”

“Then prove her wrong.”

He let her go. At the top of the stairs she looked back. Orrel had turned toward Maeron and was speaking too quietly for her to hear.

She went to supper.

The woman who had employed her still kept the birdcage in the courtyard. A climbing plant had reached it and put a single shoot through the open door. She had decided to leave it there. Lyra brought a small jar of preserved fruit and was told she had become extravagant.

“I could take it home.”

“You could not.”

They ate in the room below Lyra’s old lodging. Two new copyists worked for the house now, and the woman complained that one wrote too slowly while the other wrote too much. She asked after Tesset and listened to the account of the fifth day’s wages with unkind pleasure.

“You used to do that to me.”

“I remember.”

“Good. I hope you find it improves your character.”

Lyra laughed. The woman had made the thick bean dish she used to bring upstairs when Lyra forgot to eat. It tasted the same, though she had left out one herb because the boy who fetched it could no longer be trusted to distinguish it from something that made people ill.

They spoke about ordinary things. The damp in the upper room. A marriage negotiated through so many letters that the bride and groom had begun sending messages to each other to discover whether either still wanted it. A clerk who had taken a position with a richer house and returned three weeks later, saying he preferred to be insulted in a language he understood.

Lyra listened and found herself wanting to tell everything. The impossible dives, the cold, the boats arriving on time, the man whose blood she had stopped. She wanted the woman to understand the size of the life she had made from the small beginning offered here. She also wanted to sit in this room without that life requiring explanation.

“You’re quiet,” the woman said.

“I’ve been busy.”

“Those are different difficulties.”

“Yes.”

“Is there something I can do?”

Lyra looked toward the courtyard. A leaf had caught against the cage wire and folded around it.

“Can I stay a little?”

The woman put more beans on her plate. She did not ask again.

When Lyra left, she went up to look at the old room. Another woman occupied it now, but she was away visiting her mother and had given permission. The chest had changed. The table was still there. Its leg retained the small dent where Lyra once dropped an iron on it while trying to press a collar flat.

She stood by the window. The fastening remained sound. Below, her former employer called that she had forgotten the jar’s lid.

Lyra went down to get it.

On the following morning she asked Tesset to bring the oldest salvage accounts as well as the recent ones.

They worked in the upper office with the door open. People came for instructions, and Lyra gave them. A damaged sling needed replacing. The fish at Ropewalk needed collecting. A buyer had sent a quantity different from the one he had promised, and Tesset wrote back asking which quantity he intended to pay for.

Between those interruptions they compared the dates.

The Mallow offered no difficulty. Its loss had been recorded months before Lyra met the crew. There were witnesses, charges for failed attempts, a weather account from another vessel, and the sale of goods she had handled herself. Other early recoveries were equally plain. They had gone down after ships that were already lost. Their advantage had been reaching them.

The newer entries required more explanation. In one case the first payment had covered an unsuccessful search which could reasonably have supplied equipment for the next. In another, the date on the copy differed from the date on the original. Tesset marked both and refused to include them in the remaining group.

“Perhaps all of it is like that,” she said.

“Perhaps.”

“But the Mercy Bell isn’t.”

“We don’t know what they were looking for.”

“No.”

Lyra set the agreed hire beside the recovery report. The Mercy Bell had been carrying lamp oil, iron fittings, and three locked chests of instruments belonging to a Court house. Its loss had occurred in weather which damaged no other vessel on that route. The report said eight crew had been safely removed by the eastern shoal. It named no receiving boat or shore landing, and gave no number for those aboard before the loss.

“Find me the notice of arrival,” she said.

“Whose?”

“The crew.”

Tesset read the report herself. Then she went to the shelf for the harbor returns.

There was no matching entry. The men might have landed beyond the harbor authority’s reach, or been taken to a private house. Perhaps no one had thought to report them. That happened often enough to make silence a poor witness.

Lyra looked at the blank space Tesset had left for the arrival.

“Don’t ask at the yard.”

Tesset’s face altered.

“Where should I ask?”

“I will.”

“Lyra.”

It was the first time the girl had spoken her name that way, without either business or a question after it. Lyra looked up.

“What?”

“Should I keep these here?”

She glanced at the door. Seldo was coming up the stairs with a hinge in his hand.

“Make a second set. Take it home.”

Tesset nodded and turned to greet Seldo, who wanted to know whether the hinge should be replaced now or after it fell off. Lyra told him now. He looked satisfied, as if that settled a private argument with the hinge, and went away.

The nurse at the Tide Rooms knew a woman who had treated men brought in from a wreck. She would not promise that it was the same wreck. Sailors often gave a ship’s name differently from the people who owned it, and two men could describe the same coast without agreeing on a single place along it.

“I can ask,” she said.

“Quietly.”

The nurse looked at her.

“Are they in trouble?”

“I don’t know.”

“Are you?”

“I don’t know that either.”

She agreed to ask. Lyra went from the Tide Rooms to the shore house and found Maeron waiting with the copies she had sent. He had made room at the kitchen table. There were two cups and a plate of bread.

“I’ve remembered part of it,” he said.

She sat. He showed her the old-ground survey which preceded the hire, explained how the lighter had been retained when the Mercy Bell was reported lost, and pointed out a sum she had already identified as belonging to another voyage. Everything he said fitted the papers he used.

“And the crew?” she asked.

“They were taken off.”

“All of them?”

“I wasn’t on the receiving boat.”

“Who was?”

“I’ll find out.”

“Please do.”

He watched her across the table. The harder she studied his quiet face, the less she could read in it.

“Has someone said otherwise?”

“The report doesn’t say.”

“We can make it clearer.”

“Yes.”

He pushed the bread toward her. She took a piece because refusing it would have said something she was not ready to say. He told her about a family who wanted to leave the Tide Rooms for a house farther inland, and asked whether she would arrange the cart. She said she would. They discussed the stairs at the receiving house, the beds, the need to send the cooking pot with the first load so that the family could eat that evening.

It was work they knew how to do together. When she left, the cart was ordered and the matter of the Mercy Bell remained open between them.

The answer came two days later, folded inside a list of linen required for the nursing rooms.

A man named Iven had survived the Mercy Bell. He had been staying above a sailmaker’s shop near the outer harbor. He would speak to Lyra if she came alone. No diver, no priest, no man with a chain. The nurse had underlined the last condition because Iven had repeated it.

Lyra read the message twice and put it inside her coat.

Corven was in the yard, attempting to carry a box without letting Seldo see that it hurt. She took one end and helped him set it down.

“You needn’t do that,” he said.

“Neither need you.”

“Where are you going?”

“To speak to someone.”

“I’ll come.”

“I’m going alone.”

He looked at her coat, at the small packet beneath it, then at her face.

“Where?”

She gave him the sailmaker’s address.

“How long?”

“If I’m not back by the second evening bell, send Tesset. She’ll know what to ask.”

“Send Tesset.”

“Yes.”

He disliked it. She watched him draw breath to argue, and waited. At last he nodded.

“Mind the outer stair. They rot where the sailcloth keeps them wet.”

“I will.”

She went by the main streets. Once she nearly turned toward the shore house out of habit. At the corner she stopped, felt for the message inside her coat, and continued toward the harbor.

The sailmaker’s shop was easy to find. Cloth hung from the upper windows, and a woman on a low bench worked a needle through a folded edge using a leather pad against her palm. Lyra asked for Iven. The woman pointed to the stair without asking her name.

There was a nail in the rail where a peg had broken away. Lyra avoided it and put her weight carefully on the first tread.

Part Three The Lady Under the Waves

Chapter 15 A hull opened inward

Someone had left a needle in the step where a visitor would put a hand to climb. Lyra saw the thread first. She lifted it, worked the needle free, and pushed it through the scrap of leather hanging from the stair rail.

Above her, a man said, “You came alone.”

“You asked me to.”

“That doesn’t answer it.”

She looked up. The man crouched on the landing, one bare foot tucked beneath him. His other foot wore a boot cut open from toe to ankle. He held a short sailmaker’s knife with its blade resting against his knee.

“It does,” she said. “Whether you believe me is another matter.”

He considered her, then shifted far enough to let her pass.

The loft smelled of hemp and the sour paste used on mildew. Three sails hung from the beams. Another woman, older than the one below, sat between them sewing a patch, her feet planted on a board to keep them above the floor’s draught. She did not look at Lyra. There was a cup on the bench beside her, and a second cup, inverted, where nobody was sitting.

Iven shut the stair door.

“She’s deaf.”

The old woman looked up.

“I am working,” she said.

“She’s not going to tell anyone.”

“I have not yet decided.” She pulled her stitch tight with both hands. “Sit where I can see you, girl.”

Lyra took the empty stool. Iven remained standing until the woman pointed at the bench. He sat, turning the injured foot awkwardly so it would not touch the floor.

“The nurse said you wanted to know about the Mercy Bell.”

“About how she was lost.”

“You people bought what came up.”

“Yes.”

“Your people brought it up.”

“Yes.”

He waited, apparently expecting a qualification. Lyra took the folded copy of the recovery account from her pocket and laid it on her knee. The paper had acquired the shape of her leg during the walk. She smoothed it, then stopped. He was watching her hands.

“Are you going to write what I say?”

“If you let me.”

“Will you put my name on it?”

“Not unless you ask.”

“Then how do you prove you didn’t invent it?”

“I may not be able to.”

His shoulders eased. He leaned back against a coil of unfinished rope. His face was broad and wind-damaged. A pale line crossed the bridge of his nose, new skin without the freckles around it.

“There were twelve of us,” he said. “If you’ve come to say eight, you can go.”

“The return says eight taken off.”

“Taken off where?”

“By the eastern shoal.”

“Then that’s a lie already. We weren’t near the shoal.”

Lyra unfolded the paper.

Iven watched her read. She knew the entry. She had looked at it until the figures separated from their meanings: eight crew safely removed; no dependants’ claim admitted against the sale; hull beyond recovery. The goods had been lamp oil and iron fittings, with three locked chests of instruments. Those chests had paid for the conversion of the submerged workroom. She had congratulated Maeron on reaching them without breaking their seals.

“Where were you?”

“On the roadstead. Waiting on permission to come in. They’d sent a boat to say there was sickness at the berth and we should stand off until morning. We anchored. Nobody liked it. We could see other ships coming in.”

“Who sent the boat?”

“A harbor man. Had a harbor coat.”

“Did you know him?”

“No.”

She let that rest. The room’s single shutter clicked against its latch. Below, a cart went through a puddle, followed by a driver abusing someone too far away for Lyra to hear the reply.

“The first thing was my teeth,” Iven said. “Thought I’d bitten down wrong. Then the kettle started. It was on the stove and the lid went up and down with no fire under it. Our cook said he’d heard a bell.”

The sailmaker’s hands had stopped.

“I went below. Water was coming in through the seams. Straight through. Not one place. All along. We stuffed what we could into them. Then I heard Dannel in the next hold, yelling about his ears.”

“Did you see what had struck the hull?”

“Nothing struck it.”

“Could something have caught beneath her?”

He closed his eyes for a moment. When he opened them she expected anger. Instead he asked, “You’ve seen a ship hit something?”

“Afterward.”

“You hear it when you’re aboard. You feel it. This was everywhere. The wood was moving. I could see nails coming toward me.”

Lyra pressed her thumb into the fold of the paper. She had heard Maeron sing against stone while the passage shook. She remembered the sound arriving in her body before she could say she heard it.

“What happened to your foot?”

“A chest got it. I was getting Dannel up the ladder. He’d gone over backward with his hands on his face. The chest slid when she listed and took me across the foot. I couldn’t get free. He went back down and lifted it.”

“Where is Dannel?”

Iven looked at the sailmaker.

“He got me up,” he said. “That’s what happened to him.”

The woman resumed sewing. Her needle passed through the sail with a little hard sound.

Lyra laid the paper aside.

“Who else didn’t come back?”

“The cook. Perren. Ovit. Dannel. They found Perren. The other three they haven’t.”

“Who saw you ashore?”

“A little boat from the west. Fishermen. They took us off the rail. There was another boat out there, but it waited until we were clear.”

“Did you know anyone aboard it?”

“No.”

“Did you hear them?”

“You keep asking me if I heard.”

She waited.

“One man said to leave the line. Our line. We’d paid it out astern for the boat. I could hear him well enough. I heard someone laughing, too. That may have been one of ours. A man laughs sometimes when he’s got out.”

“Did you hear singing?”

“No.” His answer came quickly. “I didn’t hear a man standing on the sea singing at us, if that’s what you want. I heard my cook say a bell, and then I heard him calling for somebody to bring the lantern down.”

Heat rose beneath Lyra’s collar until her skin ached. She had come for his account and was trying to put a song into it.

“I shouldn’t have asked it that way.”

“Then don’t write it as though I did.”

“I won’t.”

He rubbed the scar on his nose. The sailmaker put down her work, rose with effort, and took a wrapped object from beneath the bench.

“My sister’s husband repairs the quay boats,” she said. “They brought him wood from your recovery. Wanted it split for patches and kindling.”

The cloth unfolded around an iron strap. Three square-headed nails still sat in it, bent nearly flat. A sliver of timber clung beneath one head.

“Iven wouldn’t let him throw this out.”

Lyra picked it up. The metal was cold even to her. She turned the strap to see the maker’s cuts along the edge, the shallow paired grooves that identified the Mercy Bell’s repairs in the shipyard account. Somebody had recently scrubbed the wood clean. A dark stain remained in its grain.

“From the inside,” Iven said. “The nails came in.”

She set it on the table, unable to stop seeing the head of each nail move, slowly, through a plank with a man working beside it.

“May I take this?”

“No.”

“May I bring someone to look?”

“No.”

“Then I need to draw it.”

The sailmaker fetched a board and shoved her own sewing aside. Iven watched Lyra make the drawing. Her first attempt was poor; she wrote the measurements beside it and drew it again. She asked the woman for a length of thread to measure the curve. The woman supplied it without comment.

When Lyra had finished, Iven said, “How much did you make?”

She told him.

He looked past her toward the sails.

“Dannel used to say he wanted to be worth something. He used to say it when we were paid late. Said there must be somewhere a man who could lift what he could was worth paying.”

Lyra had no useful answer. She let him finish. After a time he reached for his boot and worked his toes into a less painful position.

“I can bring a healer,” she said.

“No.”

“I know a woman outside our house. You could go to her.”

“I know what your people do with healing.”

The refusal struck harder than she expected. She gathered the copied account without folding it.

“Will you let me pay for food?”

He looked at the old woman again. She kept her face turned toward the window.

“Give it to her,” he said. “If you want. It doesn’t buy this.” He put his palm over the iron.

“I understand.”

He looked at her hands until she put them in her lap.

The man who had received the salvaged hull came to the yard after the evening meal. Lyra had sent for him through a supplier who did not owe the Chorus anything. He arrived with rain in his beard and demanded to be paid before he looked at the drawing.

She paid him.

“No,” he said, after studying it. “Could be the way it broke.”

“Could every strap bend in?”

“How many have you seen?”

“One.”

“Then every strap is a thing you’ve said.”

She liked him for that. It made the next hour harder. He took her through what recovered wood could and could not tell her, using a packing case, a nail, and eventually his own belt. Timber could twist in sinking. A cargo could move. A salvage chain applied badly could pull an inner fitting through wood that had already softened. She wrote each possibility on a separate sheet.

“Suppose the fitting was recovered before the hull was lifted.”

“Was it?”

“I need to ask.”

“Ask.” He pushed the nail back across the table. “But if you’re asking whether she could have opened along three sound seams at anchor without a touch on the bottom, that’s not something I can give you a decent explanation for.”

“Would you say so before witnesses?”

“Who are the witnesses?”

She looked at him.

“That’s not me being difficult,” he said. “I’ve two boys and a rented shed. Tell me who’ll be standing in it.”

She paid him for the hour and promised to ask before naming him. When he left, Tesset came up from the office below carrying the sale books.

“The first five,” she said. “You asked for all the recoveries before I came.”

“Did anyone see you fetch them?”

“Everybody in the office. They’re books, Lyra.”

The rebuke was mild enough to let her ignore it. She made room on the table.

They worked until the wax in the lamp cup had formed a thick shelf around the wick. The early books were a mess. The dates did not agree in three places. Maeron had paid for food from his own small purse and later entered the sum twice, once as money received and once as money owed. A boy who had copied the figures had made four different signs for half a silver.

Lyra remembered correcting it. She had been proud of how patient she was with him.

For these first losses, they could find witnesses and records beyond their own books: a ship lost in a storm witnessed from shore; a freighter abandoned after striking a known rock; a fishing boat missing for two seasons before Corven found its bell. She and Tesset laid these to one side. The stores and buyers began to be ready in advance only after they rented the second warehouse, when the Chorus had crews to pay every week.

“Does that make it better?” Tesset asked.

“What?”

“That they weren’t doing it when you came.”

Lyra had been holding a page at its bottom corner. She let it fall.

“It tells me what happened.”

Tesset drew another volume toward her. “I’ve put the names of the buyers on the cover sheet.”

Near midnight Corven came to collect his eel’s vessel from the shelf by the window. He had left it in the office because the smith working below complained that the animal made his dog howl. The eel lay folded around the pot’s inner curve, its dark body showing through the brown glass.

Corven looked at the books. Then he set the pot down again.

“You’ve spoken to somebody.”

“Yes.”

“About the Mercy Bell.”

Lyra pushed back her chair. “Were you there?”

“No.”

“Do you know who was?”

He did not answer immediately. Tesset closed the book in front of her, marking her place with a clean scrap.

“Corven.”

“Orrel was on the recovery boat.”

“Bring him.”

“He’s asleep.”

“Wake him.”

Corven put a hand on the vessel. “Do you want me to bring him, or ask him to come?”

The lamp hissed. Lyra’s face burned.

“Ask him.”

Orrel arrived with his shirt unlaced and his hair pressed flat on one side. He was younger than Lyra had remembered, a broad-shouldered man who could stand all morning at the winch without changing hands. His wife had brought cakes when their first child was born. He looked at Corven, then at the open books, and stayed by the door.

Lyra showed him the copied return.

“Did you write this?”

“I gave the numbers.”

“Eight safely removed.”

“Eight in the boat.”

“Were there twelve on the ship?”

He lowered his eyes.

“Orrel.”

“I don’t know what was aboard when she sailed.”

“Did you know people were missing?”

“The captain said he had men below.”

“Before the ship went down?”

“Yes.”

“What did you do?”

He touched the loose cord at his throat. “We were told to keep clear until the deck settled.”

“By whom?”

He looked at Corven again. Corven was sitting on the chest beside the window, both hands resting between his knees.

“Tell her,” he said.

“Maeron.”

Lyra had expected the name. Hearing it made the lamp’s light seem briefly too bright.

“Was he singing?”

Orrel nodded.

“Before she began to sink?”

“Yes.”

“Had he done that before?”

“Not when I was aboard.”

“Did you ask him to stop?”

The young man pulled the cord so hard the knot closed against his neck. “I said there were men below.”

“What did he say?”

“He said the boats were there.”

She looked at the eight. She had written the sale total underneath it in her own clear hand. A little stroke from the last figure ran below the line.

“What did you think he meant?”

“I thought he’d bring them up.”

“Did he?”

Orrel shook his head. For the first time he looked directly at her.

“I thought he could. He’d brought us out of worse. I thought if he said to wait, it was because he knew where they were.”

Corven got up and went to the window. His back was rigid beneath the shirt.

“Did you collect their belongings?” Lyra asked.

“Some.”

“Where?”

“Still in the little store. We were waiting for names.”

She stood. Orrel moved aside before she reached him.

“Show me.”

The little store had no lamp. Orrel brought one from the office, shielding it with his hand, and unlocked a cupboard full of rope ends, damaged tools, and things too poor to sell. Four bundles lay on the bottom shelf. One held a pair of boots wrapped in a shirt. The shirt had been cut away at the shoulder. Lyra opened another and found a wooden comb, a tin cup, and a knitted cap too small for an adult. A sailor’s gift to somebody ashore.

“Which is Dannel’s?”

“I don’t know.”

“Then we’ll find out.”

She sat on an overturned crate and took each object out separately. Orrel held the lamp while she made the list. When his arm began to shake she asked Corven to take it. None of them spoke of stopping.

At dawn, Lyra washed the soot from her fingers in the office basin. She had an appointment with Maeron after the first meal, a lesson they had moved twice because of work. He was bringing a small vessel of trench water. She had been looking forward to it all week.

She dried her hands and laid the iron drawing on top of the accounts.

Chapter 16 Permission for the next voyage

Maeron brought pears.

They were the small hard kind that ripened late and went brown inside before their skins admitted it. He set them on the corner of Lyra’s desk in a cloth he had washed himself; one end still showed a stain from whatever meal it had carried before. From his other pocket he took an iron vial scarcely longer than her thumb.

“You look tired.”

“I’ve been up.”

“I can come back.”

“Shut the door.”

He glanced at the papers, then shut it. The office below had already begun to fill. Lyra heard Tesset directing a carter toward the other entrance, a familiar correction made patiently for the third time that week.

Maeron put the vial beside the pears.

“I went to see Iven,” Lyra said. “From the Mercy Bell.”

His hand remained beside the little iron vessel. A wet ring was forming beneath it.

“How is his foot?”

“You know about his foot.”

“Yes.”

“Did you go to him?”

“I sent someone.”

“He didn’t want them.”

“No.”

She put the account before him. “Four people died.”

He drew out the chair and sat as though they had a day’s accounts to go through. She had been awake too long; she wanted him shaken, unable to sit still.

“I know about Perren,” he said.

“You know about the other three.”

“We searched.”

“After you sank them.”

Maeron looked down at the account, then at her drawing of the strap. Outside, a man called the weight of a load to the men below. They answered him together. Lyra had arranged that call after a sack had fallen between two workers who had each believed the other was holding it.

“The vessel carried fittings for a Court armory,” Maeron said.

“And lamp oil. And men.”

“The boats were there.”

“Dannel went below to help Iven. You kept singing.”

“I did.”

“Did you think he was out?”

Maeron folded his hands. “Once she started to list, the upper cargo shifted. I could keep the pressure even or let the hull turn. There were men still on the rail.”

“Then you had already put them where one of those things would happen.”

“Yes.”

She had been ready to fight him into admitting it. Now he sat waiting while she separated the preparation sheets from the recovery account.

“The lighter was paid for eleven days before the loss.”

“We needed a boat that could come close under the hull.”

“The buyers were ready.”

“Yes.”

“Who decided?”

“I did.”

“Who else knew?”

“The men who came out with me knew we intended to stop her.”

“Did they know how?”

“Some.”

“Which?”

He named them. She wrote each name. When he reached Orrel he paused.

“He was told the crew would be removed.”

“Orrel says he told you they were still below.”

“He did.”

Lyra’s hand tightened around the pen. She put it down before it broke.

“You let me spend their money.”

“Our money.”

“Do you want to argue over that word?”

“No.”

“Then don’t.”

For a while neither spoke. He reached for a pear, turned it in his hand, and put it down again.

“There were thirty-one people sleeping in the house that month,” he said. “Six could work. The rest were children, old people, or people no employer would take. Marda was watering the broth. You saw it.”

“I was there.”

“You got us better prices. You made a good boat out of three that should have been burned. You cannot raise a cargo that isn’t there.”

“People manage to be poor without killing four sailors.”

“They die slowly and separately. Their employers don’t call it killing.”

She stood, forcing the chair back against the wall.

“Do not make me defend them in order to ask you about Dannel.”

Maeron looked up. She could remember him sitting in precisely that way while she struggled over a healing phrase, waiting until she had worked out for herself why her hands had begun to shake. He had known when to speak then. He seemed to know now that anything more would cost him.

“I’m asking you about a man you could have stopped before you put him in danger,” she said. “You knew his name afterward. You knew his things were in our store. You let me read an account saying everyone had been taken off.”

“I thought the absence of a claim would protect the households receiving the money.”

“You mean you thought I might stop spending it.”

“Yes.”

It was the first answer that hurt in the right place.

She went to the window. Corven was standing beneath the crane with two workers, showing them how to clear a jammed pulley without putting their fingers under the rope. One worker laughed at something he said. He made them both repeat the movement.

“I won’t put the money back in the water,” Lyra said.

“I haven’t asked you to.”

“I will send money to the households. All four. The survivors too, for the wages they’ve lost. They can take it without saying anything about us.”

“Of course.”

“Don’t say that. If it were of course, it would have happened.”

He lowered his eyes.

“I want every deliberate loss. The preparations, who went out, what you were paid outside the sale. I want it by tomorrow.”

“I was not paid outside the sale.”

“If I find out otherwise, the partnership ends.”

Saying it steadied her. She could leave. The rent on this room had six months to run; she knew which buyers would still employ her and which would close their doors. She could take the little money she had kept apart, pay Tesset through the season, go back to other people’s letters. Maeron watched her with a sadness she resented because it made leaving feel like an injury she would be doing.

“No vessel is to be touched without my agreement,” she said. “None. No song against a hull. No waiting boat. If you believe it’s necessary, you come to me while it can still be refused.”

“And if it can save this house?”

“Then you’ll have to persuade me.”

He sat very still. She could hear him breathing through his nose.

“You have become good at refusing,” he said.

“I had to practice.”

“I know.”

“No. You know some of it.”

He nodded and put his hand flat on the account.

“I will bring you what you ask.”

Lyra sat again. The chair’s wood felt unpleasantly warm against her back. She moved forward so that only her legs touched it.

“Why did you help me?” she asked.

He looked at her.

“With my hands. Before any of this. Why?”

“Because you could do something and were afraid to try.”

“Was that useful to you?”

“Yes.”

“Was that why?”

She let him take his time.

“I liked you,” he said. “I liked the way you asked the next question. I still do, although it is less comfortable this morning.”

Her eyes stung, which made her angrier than anything he had said. She looked away until it passed.

“Leave the papers. Take the pears.”

He gathered the cloth. He left the vial where it was.

“We needn’t have the lesson today,” he said.

“No.”

At the door he stopped. “Will you tell the others?”

“The households will hear what happened. I won’t buy their silence.”

“And the people here?”

“I haven’t decided.”

He went down the stairs. She heard him answer a greeting from below in his ordinary voice.

Iven would not take the money himself. The sailmaker accepted it in front of him, counted it twice, and made Lyra write down that there was no debt against it. Perren’s mother refused the first offer because she believed it came from the ship’s owner. When Lyra explained where it came from, the woman threw the coins into the lane. Lyra picked them up while two boys watched, then left them with the woman’s landlord against future rent. She wrote Perren’s mother a letter saying what she had done. The letter came back unopened.

The cook had a husband in another port. Finding him took three weeks. Dannel’s little cap was intended for his sister’s daughter. Lyra delivered it herself.

On the way back she bought a packet of cherries from a stall because the seller pressed one into her hand and told her to taste it. They were very sweet. She ate four before she remembered she had not wanted anything to eat. The pleasure made her cry so suddenly that she had to turn into an alley and stand beside a stack of firewood until she could see.

She returned to the office with red fingers. Tesset took the remaining cherries, washed them, and put them between the books without asking where she had been.

The accounts Maeron supplied showed three losses deliberately made. Each had begun as a useful answer to someone else’s obstruction. A captain refused to unload goods belonging to men who had withheld his pay. A Court agent tried to move confiscated tools out of reach. A cargo of iron instruments offered a profit large enough to fit out a room beneath the tide. One loss had left no dead. One had left two. The Mercy Bell had left four.

Lyra made a separate provision for the six households. She did not enter their names in the ordinary charity book. When Tesset asked what heading she wanted, she said, “Compensation.”

“For what?”

“Death.”

Tesset held the pen above the page.

“Will Maeron sign?”

“Yes.”

“Will you?”

Lyra took the book and signed beneath the heading.

The next ship was called the Grey Petrel. She was due on a falling tide with crossbows in the hold and steel hooks intended for the men who had learned to pull Chorus divers off a landing. Katerina brought the cargo list without an accompanying letter.

“Where did you get this?” Lyra asked.

“A man who wants to live.”

“Then give me something I can check without killing him.”

Katerina tapped two entries. Her nail was cut very short. “The crate maker. The carrier to the quay. Both have been paid.”

Lyra checked them. The carrier claimed the goods were agricultural tools until she asked him which farms planted quarrels. Then he said the labels were none of his business and returned the money she had given him for his time. The crate maker had kept a sample bolt because he thought the metal poor. It pierced the leather backing of Corven’s old coat when he tested it against a block.

“They’ll use these anyway,” Maeron said at the meeting.

They sat in the store with the ruined coat between them. Corven stood by the door. Tesset had declined to attend after Lyra told her what was being discussed.

“I know what they’ll use them for,” Lyra said.

“We can stop them.”

“Can you stop the ship without sinking her?”

Maeron put two fingers on the table. “I can open a seam above her loaded waterline. She’ll take water if they keep her under sail. At anchor she should stay afloat.”

“Should.”

“I’ve never done it to that vessel.”

“Then say that first.”

He drew a breath. “I can oblige her to stop. Your boats can take off the crew and the weapons. We can leave her where a tug will reach her.”

Corven shifted his weight.

“What?” Lyra asked.

“A crew seeing armed boats will fight.”

“We can send a warning.”

“A warning says we’re coming. It also gives their hired men time to load.”

She looked at the hole in his coat. It was a small, unremarkable hole. A person wearing it would bleed very quickly.

“How many crew?”

“Seventeen,” Katerina said. “Six guards.”

“Seven boats,” Lyra said. “Three for the crew, one standing clear to carry the injured, two for cargo, one with you. No loaded crossbows in our boats until they fire. We offer the captain money to stand off and let the cargo be taken. If he agrees, you don’t sing.”

Katerina’s mouth moved almost into a smile. “He has been paid to bring it in.”

“He can be paid to lose it.”

“And if he refuses?” Maeron asked.

Lyra looked at him. He did not lower his eyes this time.

“If he refuses, you stop the ship.”

Corven lifted the coat. “I want Orrel in my boat.”

“Why?”

“He knows what it looks like when we wait too long.”

Lyra wrote the order. She left a space for Maeron’s name and another for the boat master. Her own name went at the bottom, followed by the sum available for the captain. She signed slowly enough to make every letter distinct.

The Grey Petrel’s captain refused the money.

Lyra heard the answer from the launch that carried her out through the evening chop. He shouted it from his quarterdeck with a hand on his sword and said something about the kind of woman who sent men to steal for her. She could not catch all of it. Katerina, seated opposite, appeared to hear quite clearly.

“Last opportunity,” she whispered.

Lyra held up the lamp. Across the water Corven’s boat changed direction. Maeron stood in the stern of the smallest launch with his feet apart and one hand on a post.

The song began through the seat beneath her. The boatman ceased rowing for a stroke, stared at his own hands, and resumed. On the Petrel a hanging lantern rattled against its glass. Somebody shouted for a rope. Then a long white line appeared along the hull as pale timber showed between the dark strakes.

The captain took a step back. A guard raised a crossbow.

“Down,” Katerina said, and drove Lyra under the gunwale with a hand between her shoulders.

The bolt struck the boatman’s oar. Splinters fell on Lyra’s sleeve. A second bolt passed above them. She heard an answering cry from Corven’s boat and a heavy splash.

She crawled along the boards until she could see. A man from the Petrel was in the water, one arm working, the other trailing. He could not reach the rope thrown toward him. The ship’s side creaked. A seam opened farther aft, below where the water touched her.

Lyra lifted the lamp high and covered it twice.

Maeron’s face turned toward her. For one dreadful moment she thought he would continue. Then the vibration left the boat.

“Go to him,” she told the rower.

He looked at Katerina.

“To the man,” Lyra said.

They reached him after Corven’s crew had got a line under his arm. Lyra held his wrist while the boatman hauled. He was too heavy; water streamed from him, and his good hand struck her face as he struggled. She kept hold. Katerina caught his collar. Between them they dragged him across the gunwale and laid him in the bottom of the launch.

His shoulder had taken a bolt. He was scarcely older than Orrel. He looked up at Lyra, saw the knife at her belt, and tried to get away.

“Lie still,” she said. “I’m going to bind it.”

“You shot me.”

She tore cloth from the bundle she had brought. The Petrel’s captain was still shouting. Men climbed down ropes into the waiting boats. Above them Corven stepped onto the deck, his chain dark against his coat.

“You shot me,” the young guard said again.

“Yes,” Lyra said. “Hold this.”

He held the cloth because his blood was running through it.

By midnight the weapons were ashore, the captain had accepted half the original payment, and the Petrel rested on the mud beside a repair slip. No one had died. Lyra made the boat masters account for every crew member twice. Then she paid the guard’s care from the proceeds and returned to the office, where Tesset had left a clean towel beside the basin.

She washed away the young man’s blood. Her nose had begun to swell where his hand struck it. When Maeron came to ask whether she wanted food, she said yes.

They ate at the same table as they always had.

Chapter 17 The patient who returns
