Bast arrived carrying Seldo’s shirt, which had come home with him after the fighting beneath the bridge.

His wife had washed it. The cloth had dried stiff, and a brown patch remained in the folds that had been pressed into the wound. He laid it on the table with the care of someone returning borrowed linen.

“I thought you’d want to see where it was,” he said.

“I remember.”

“Yes. I know. But this shows.”

He touched the faded stain, dull yellow at its edges. He was wearing good trousers, brushed at the knees, and a coat too heavy for the day. When Lyra offered him a chair he turned it and sat across it so that the seat’s edge did not touch the injured leg.

“How long has it been hurting?”

“Doesn’t hurt.”

“What does it do?”

“Nothing. That’s why I came.”

His wife, who had followed him into the room, closed the door. She was a small woman with a red mark across one wrist where a basket handle had rested. She put the basket on the floor between her feet and remained standing.

“It was his foot first,” she said. “He kept putting it against me in bed. I told him to stop. Then he said he couldn’t tell which foot he was doing it with.”

“Only sometimes,” Bast said.

“Every night this week.”

Lyra asked him to show her. He looked at the door, then at the window. She drew the curtain across. It was the workroom above the shore house, a space they had begun using for treatment because patients were disturbed by the sound of the winch in the yard. Marda had put a clean cloth on the bed. Somebody had arranged three sprigs of rosemary in a cup; their tips were already turning black in the room’s cold.

Bast undid his belt. His wife helped him lower the trousers without bending the leg. The scar on his inner thigh was thin and closed. Around it a pale triangle extended toward his knee, smooth as wax and edged with a faint blue line. The skin farther down was mottled. Lyra touched his ankle, then the pale area, then the unmarked thigh.

“Tell me when you feel it.”

She moved her fingertip from ordinary flesh to the edge of the patch. His answer came before she reached the blue line. She tried again from another direction. He looked toward his wife.

“Look at me,” Lyra said. “Don’t try to help.”

He watched her face. She touched the cold flesh. He said nothing.

The old bandage had left a groove across the back of his leg. His wife had been wrapping it tighter because warmth returned for a while after rubbing. Lyra unwound the cloth and asked Marda to bring clean water, then sent a boy for the surgeon who treated the ropeworkers.

“Is it going to come off?” Bast asked.

“I don’t know.”

His wife sat quickly beside him, as though she had been waiting for permission.

“It can’t,” she said.

Lyra put the folded shirt aside.

“I want somebody who has seen this kind of failing flesh. I’ll stay while she looks.”

“You said it would keep him alive,” the woman said.

“It did.”

“I know it did. I was thanking you.”

Lyra looked at her. The woman was holding Bast’s hand in both of hers, rubbing his knuckles though it was his leg that was cold.

“I’m sorry,” Lyra said.

The surgeon arrived with a bag wrapped in oilskin and two assistants who had expected to find a fresh wound. She smelled of smoke and onions. She listened to Lyra’s account, asked Bast about his water and his appetite, and spent a long time looking at both legs. She used the blunt end of a pin. At the pale patch, Bast said he could feel nothing.

“Can you undo the working?” she asked Lyra.

“The pressure stopped the bleeding. I ended the hold.”

“Was this there immediately?”

“Paler. Smaller.”

“Then something remained.”

She had seen the pallor at the bridge, while watching for each breath and telling them how to carry him. Now the patch extended beyond the place her hands had covered.

“I can work on the flesh,” she said. “The ordinary way. Help it take blood again.”

“Do it somewhere I can watch before you do all of it.”

Bast put a hand over the scar. “Will it open?”

“It may,” Lyra said.

“Will you close it?”

“We’ll be ready.”

He looked at his wife. She nodded once, very firmly, and then looked down because her face had begun to change.

Lyra washed her hands. She took longer than necessary, working soap between fingers that remained cold in the warm water. Then she put her palms on either side of the lower edge of the patch, well away from the major wound, and began the small working Gyllan had taught her. She knew each transition. She could feel the difference between asking damaged flesh to mend and holding it where it was. Under her hands the skin softened.

Bast drew breath sharply.

“There,” he said. “Something.”

“Pain?”

“A prickling.”

The surgeon leaned closer. A little color returned at the lower edge. Lyra continued, carefully, refusing the impulse to force more through because he was watching with such hope. The pink spread less than the width of a fingernail. Beneath it, at the edge of the scar, a dark bead appeared.

“Stop,” the surgeon said.

Lyra stopped. The woman pressed clean cloth over the bead. It grew into a little red circle before slowing.

Bast’s wife made a sound deep in her throat.

“That’s enough for today,” the surgeon said. “We know it can change.”

“Is that good?” Bast asked.

“It gives us something to do.”

They arranged a bed with room beside it for his wife. Lyra sent money to the bargeman who had promised Bast work, buying his release from the promise without naming him unfit for future employment. She had the surgeon write what supplies she wanted and when she would return. None of this warmed the cold triangle under the cloth.

When they were alone, Bast caught Lyra’s wrist.

“I’d still have asked you,” he said.

She looked at his hand.

“Under the bridge. If I’d known. I wasn’t going to get home otherwise.”

“You shouldn’t have to make that comfort me.”

“I wasn’t. I was telling you.”

She sat beside him until his wife came back with their things.

Maeron visited in the evening. Lyra remained in the room while he spoke to Bast, and the surgeon’s instruction against further workings lay on the table where nobody could miss it.

He examined the pale flesh without touching it. Then he asked Bast whether cold water troubled him less than it had before.

“I haven’t been in any.”

“Have you dreamed of it?”

Bast’s wife took a step closer to the bed.

“He hasn’t come here to join you.”

Maeron looked at her. “I didn’t ask him to.”

“Then tell us what you can do.”

“I can help him bear what has happened.”

“The surgeon is trying to mend it.”

“They need not be enemies.”

Lyra saw the wife’s face settle into politeness. She knew that face. It was what people wore when refusing a man might cost them the bed they occupied.

“No observances,” Lyra said. “No initiation. He’s here for care.”

“Of course.”

“And the care continues if he chooses never to see you again.”

Bast looked from one to the other. Maeron inclined his head.

Outside the room he stopped her on the landing.

“You believe I would turn him out?”

“I believe she needed to hear it.”

“I would have told her.”

“Then next time tell her first.”

A boy came up carrying a bowl with both hands. They moved aside. Through the open doorway Lyra heard Bast’s wife tell him it was good soup. He said she had not tasted it. She answered that she could see it was good, and he laughed.

Maeron listened too. His expression softened. Lyra was angry with herself for being glad of it.

“The cold kept the wound from opening while they carried him,” he said. “He has time he wouldn’t have had.”

“And now we have to use it.”

“Yes.”

“Without deciding the cold is what he ought to become.”

Maeron’s gaze moved toward the door. “I don’t think every change is an injury.”

“This one prevents him feeling his foot.”

“Then help him feel it.”

She wanted a quarrel less easily ended. She wanted him to deny what she could point to. Instead he offered to pay the surgeon from his own share and asked whether he should fetch a second blanket for the wife. Lyra said yes. He brought it before leaving.

The iron vial had stayed in Lyra’s desk for nearly a month. When she took it out, a little ring of damp marked the wood underneath. She held it in a folded cloth and went to Corven’s workshop.

He was oiling a pulley. His eel lay motionless in its vessel beside the window. A child from the house sat on the floor drawing the creature with charcoal, trying to give it eyes. Each time he did, Corven told him it had none. The child rubbed them away and began again in a different place.

“I want to test the threshold working,” Lyra said.

Corven set the pulley down. “Here?”

“Somewhere nobody will be inside if it holds.”

“That rules out Marda’s pantry. Shame.”

The child looked up. “She knows you take the dried fish.”

“Then it rules out secrecy as well.”

Lyra laughed. It surprised her, the ease of it. Corven looked pleased and turned away before she could resent being watched.

They used an old door frame in the repair shed. It had come from a vessel whose cabin was too rotten to preserve; the carpenter had rebuilt its lower joint, and someone had fitted an ordinary plank door for testing locks. Corven braced it against the wall and checked the hinges. He tied a rope through the handle, passed it over a pulley, and hung a bucket from the free end.

“Water goes in,” he said. “The load pulls the door. If it breaks, we lose a bucket.”

“What if the frame breaks?”

“We lose a frame. Stand to the side.”

She had read Maeron’s instructions repeatedly. They were written on ordinary paper because they described her actions rather than carrying a Runekeeper’s rites: what to touch, where to put the drop, how to keep the spoken enclosure from following the wrong surface. The marks that held the actual working could not be preserved that way. She had practiced their movement with plain water until her hand could find the joint without searching.

She uncapped the vial. The air above it shimmered. Inside, the liquid clung to the iron even when she tilted it. She drew a drop onto a little glass rod and set it at the place where the door met its frame.

Her first attempt made the wood groan. Then the drop ran down the jamb, and the door opened under the empty bucket’s weight.

Corven caught the bucket before it struck the floor.

“You were holding the door,” he said.

“Yes.”

“You need the place it shuts against.”

“That’s what I was trying to hold.”

“Try it slower.”

“I have.”

“Then try it slower again.”

She gave him a look. He smiled without apology.

They reset the door. Lyra put one hand on the fixed jamb and the other on the door’s face. She followed the seam until she could feel where the pressure should sit, then spoke the phrase into that narrow darkness. This time the wood settled. A dull sound passed through the frame, as though a much heavier door had closed somewhere underneath it.

Corven poured water into the bucket. The rope tightened. The door stayed shut.

More water. A slow creak from the hinge. Lyra felt a tremor across her palms, familiar from holding a wound closed, and resisted the urge to tighten every muscle against it.

“How much?” she asked.

“Enough to hurt somebody if it fell.”

“How much, Corven?”

He told her the weight. She held it while he filled another jug. Her breath came short. The frame’s lower joint shifted.

“Stop.”

He set the jug down.

“Take the load.”

He lifted the bucket. She released the working. The door swung open and struck the brace behind it.

For a moment her hands would not leave the wood. She peeled them away one finger at a time. Pale lines crossed her palms. Corven moved the stool behind her without speaking, and she sat before her knees could make it a decision.

“The joint would have gone,” he said.

“I felt it.”

“It won’t make weak wood sound.”

“No.”

“And it doesn’t care which side a man is on.”

She looked at him.

“A door stays closed,” he said. “That may be the wrong thing when someone starts shouting.”

“Then I stop.”

“If you can hear him.”

They tested it again two days later, with the child forbidden from the shed and a sandbag placed where a hand might be caught. Lyra learned to hold the seam through a gap too narrow for the bag to pass, and to release before the rope’s weight tore the joint. Moving water was harder. It found weaknesses her hands could not see. The first test at the little sluice drenched her to the waist when she let the pressure travel along the water instead of resting it against the fixed stone.

Corven hauled her back by the coat. She sat on the steps coughing while he laughed so hard he had to lean against the rail.

“You said it would be difficult.”

“It was.”

“You might have said wet.”

“I thought the water explained that.”

She threw a handful at him. He accepted it, still laughing, then offered his arm to help her up.

By the end of the week she could hold the sluice against a modest flow long enough for a man to put a bar through its staple, then let go without falling. She wrote the limits in her practice book: sound frame; known direction of load; brief hold under force; no mending of broken fittings. Beneath them she added, after a pause, Somebody able to take the weight.

On the last afternoon, Corven asked her to stay while he put his chain into the water. She had seen him clean it before, but he usually did the other part alone. He lowered each length until every marked link lay below the surface, then braced his forearms on the stone and bent his head.

Lyra sat on the step above him. The light was failing. His eel moved in its vessel beside her boot, feeling the change before the first drops of rain reached the canal.

“Does it hurt?” she asked.

He kept his head down. “Sometimes.”

“Today?”

“It’s rearranged itself near the joining link. I was late putting it under.”

“What happens if you read it wrong?”

“I try not to.”

“That isn’t an answer.”

“No.” He looked up. “I once made a room so quiet I couldn’t hear the man behind me warning that the ladder had gone.”

“Were you below?”

“Yes.”

“How did you get out?”

“The man behind me came back.”

She waited for the rest. Corven rubbed a thumb along a submerged link. The marks seemed different whenever the water moved, and she stopped trying to follow them.

“He had to put down what he’d carried,” Corven said. “A good recovery. More than he’d ever made on a dive. He came back for me, and we lost it.”

“Did you pay him?”

“I tried. He wouldn’t have it.”

“What did he want?”

“He wanted me to remember I was part of the crew.”

The rain thickened. Lyra moved the eel’s vessel under the shelter of her coat. Corven watched her do it, then returned to the chain.

“Do you still see him?” she asked.

“No. He went home.”

“Where?”

“North. Had a place there he’d left in a temper. Took him twelve years to get over it.”

She smiled. “Did you write?”

“Once. Took me most of an evening. He sent back a page about his pigs.”

“Were they well?”

“Better than I was, by his account.”

Corven laughed, and she could hear the affection remain after it. He lifted a section of chain to inspect the marks. Water ran from it over his wrist. For a moment Lyra saw the man he must have been beside someone he trusted, willing to be teased, willing to admit he had needed carrying. She had employed him so thoroughly that she sometimes forgot there were people who would have recognized him before they recognized his chain.

“Would you like to go north?” she asked.

“Not at present.”

“If you did?”

“I’d tell you.”

She nodded. He lowered the links again, and they sat together while the rain struck the canal. When the marks had settled, she held the cloth open for him. He coiled the chain into it carefully, making sure no link lay beneath another before he folded it closed.

Bast’s patch shrank by less than a finger’s width that week. The surgeon was pleased. Bast was disappointed. His wife learned the dressing changes and did them better than either assistant because she knew exactly when he was pretending the cloth did not hurt.

Lyra visited after the sluice test, still damp. He asked why her hair was wet. When she told him, he laughed until the movement pulled at the wound.

“Could you hold a lock gate?” he asked.

“A small one. For a little while.”

“Useful.”

“I hope so.”

He worked a finger beneath the edge of his blanket. “I’m glad you’re still learning.”

She looked at the cold flesh of his leg, then at the practice book under her arm.

“I’ll come tomorrow,” she said.

“Bring something to read. We’ve done all the words on the medicine bottles.”

She brought him a voyage account with terrible spelling and three descriptions of the same storm. He read the first page aloud to his wife, stopping to disagree with the author, and Lyra sat by the bed while the evening light narrowed across the floor.

Chapter 18 The delayed release

Harl’s hired cart stood beneath his rooms with a travel chest already tied down.

Lyra crossed the yard carrying the two crews’ unpaid wage accounts. Beside them she had folded copies of the unloading times Harl had sold to a Court intermediary. A woman was arguing with the driver about a second chest, saying Harl had told her it would fit. The driver said Harl could come down and put it on himself.

Corven walked beside Lyra without his chain visible. He had brought the eel in its covered vessel inside his satchel. The cracked shell rested in his coat pocket. She had watched him fill it before they left, pouring clear water that clouded as soon as it touched the broken lip.

“Your people are at the other stair?” she asked.

“Two.”

“And the loading door?”

“Orrel.”

“No one leaves with the accounts.”

Corven looked at the woman by the cart. “What about her?”

“Ask if she’s owed anything.”

He went ahead. Lyra crossed to the warehouse office, where a clerk she knew by sight was trying to close a drawer on too many papers. He rose when he saw her. The drawer caught his sleeve.

“Where is he?” she asked.

“Gone.”

“His cart is outside.”

“Then he’s going.”

She went past him toward the inner stair. He made a movement to stop her, saw her face, and lowered his arm. She had paid Harl’s rents on time for eight months. She had allowed him to deduct the cost of repairing a shutter broken by her own carter. He had taken those payments, written her receipts, and sold the same carter’s name to men waiting at a narrow turn.

At the top of the stairs Harl was stuffing a shirt into a bag. He wore his traveling coat over his house slippers. The mismatch might have been funny on another morning.

“You can’t come up here,” he said.

“Pay the crews.”

“They’ve been paid.”

“Then show me where.”

He looked toward the window. She moved so that he would have to pass close to her to reach it.

“Harl.”

“My clerk has the books.”

“Your clerk said you’d gone.”

“I was expecting you to make trouble. You people make trouble and leave someone else to explain it.”

She heard Corven on the stairs. Harl heard him too. His hand went under the shirt in the bag.

“Don’t,” Lyra said.

He brought out a narrow knife and drove toward her so quickly that she did not see which hand held it. She stepped back against the bed. The blade caught the loose sleeve of her coat and cut through it. Then Corven was between them, catching Harl’s wrist against the doorpost with a sound like wood splitting.

Harl shouted. The knife fell. Lyra kicked it beneath the bed.

“Let him stand,” she said.

Corven changed his grip. Harl stumbled against the wall, cradling his wrist. His mouth was open, but the next shout did not come. Corven held the cracked shell in his left hand.

“Enough of that,” Lyra said.

The room’s pressure eased. Harl coughed and found his voice.

“You brought a thing into my house.”

“You brought a knife to my sleeve.”

“He broke my wrist.”

“You can show the surgeon after you show me the money.”

There was a small leather purse on the table. Harl pointed at it. Lyra opened it and counted enough silver for one week of one crew’s wages, less his unlawful storage charge. She set it aside.

“The rest.”

“That’s all there is.”

She went to the travel bag. He moved toward it. Corven stopped him with one hand on his chest.

“Harl,” she said. “I know what came in. I know what went out. I know the buyer paid you in full because I went to ask him before I came here. You have the money.”

“Then ask the buyer where he put it.”

She opened the bag. Shirts, a pair of boots wrapped in stockings, a little painting of a woman with a flower. Under them lay a packet of letters tied with red thread. Harl looked away.

“Who are these for?”

“My sister.”

“Where?”

“None of your business.”

Lyra read the address on the outer letter and set the packet aside. The painting stayed in the bag. She checked the boots. Empty. She heard the driver outside say he was leaving if nobody paid him.

Corven leaned close to Harl and spoke quietly. Lyra could not hear the words. Harl shook his head. Corven drew a few links of chain from beneath his coat.

“Wait,” Harl said.

Lyra turned.

“Behind the bed. The lower board. It’s loose.”

She pulled the mattress aside and found the board. Behind it lay two purses and a roll of small gold pieces. She carried them to the table. The sums matched what the crews were owed, with enough left over to explain the cart and several months elsewhere.

“That’s yours,” she told him, indicating the small purse after counting. “Less the surgeon.”

He stared at the money she had left him, then at her, as though taking only what she claimed was another affront.

“Get the books,” she said to Corven.

He called down the stair. One of the men waiting below brought up the clerk’s volumes. Lyra checked the wage entries against her own slips. Harl had entered payment on the day the buyer’s money arrived. Beside each crew leader’s name was a mark intended to look like a signature.

“You’ve copied Orrel’s mark backward.”

“He can’t read it either way.”

She looked up. Harl closed his mouth.

The clerk stood at the door holding a smaller book. He was shaking. Lyra asked whether Harl owed him wages. He said yes, then no, then gave the amount. She counted it out and put it in his hand.

“Take the downstairs receipts to Tesset,” she said. “Stay while she checks them.”

“Will I be coming back?”

“That depends on what they say.”

He looked at Harl. Harl said nothing. The clerk went down carrying the books against his chest.

The knife was under the bed. The money lay on the table. Two men waited outside, and Harl was in slippers with one wrist swelling beneath his sleeve. Lyra could pay the crews before noon and close the warehouse without losing a single consignment.

She picked up the other packet on the table, a little set of route slips she had brought from the office.

“Who bought these?”

Harl looked at the papers, then at the door.

“What are they?”

“The unloading times you copied. The carter’s name. Which nights Corven would be at the shore house.”

“I didn’t copy those.”

“The clerk says you did.”

“He says what he’s paid to say.”

“He hasn’t told me yet. I was giving you a chance to be first.”

Harl looked toward the window again, though the drop would break his legs.

“Who?” she asked.

“I don’t know.”

“Who collected them?”

“A boy.”

“Name.”

“I don’t know.”

Lyra put the slips down. She thought of the carter lying under a broken wheel, trying to tell her where the other men had gone. They had cut his harness before striking him. He had shown her the clean ends because he wanted her to know he had not failed to keep his horses under control.

Corven watched her. The chain lay across his palm.

“Hold him,” she said.

Harl put both hands up, forgetting his wrist, and cried out. Corven turned the chain. The air in the room seemed to settle downward. Harl’s coat flattened against his chest. He stumbled back into the corner beside the bed, then stopped as though the walls had closed on him.

“Who collected them?” Lyra asked.

“A boy. A boy with a green cap.”

“Where did you meet him?”

“Here.”

“How did you send word?”

“I didn’t.”

His breathing grew loud. He tried to pull the coat away from his throat with his good hand. The fingers could not get beneath the cloth.

Lyra knelt to bring her face level with his. “You were paid more than once. You gave different times. Someone came back. How did you send word?”

“Please.”

“How?”

“There’s a woman at the lamp shop.”

“Which lamp shop?”

“By the red stair.”

“Name.”

“Dessa.”

Lyra stood. The red stair gave access to the lane behind their second store. There was a shop there, narrow enough that its owner had to stand outside while customers looked at the lamps. Dessa could have watched the unloading without leaving her door.

Corven looked at her.

She saw the question and did not answer it.

“Who does Dessa answer to?”

“I don’t know.”

Harl’s breath failed halfway through the last word. His face had darkened. Corven moved his thumb along a link, easing something just enough for Harl to take air.

“Lyra.”

She looked at Corven’s hand. He was waiting. She could say it now and have the man on the floor, crying, alive, with a name they could find. But Dessa might already have warned whoever paid her. Harl had time to send a message before they arrived. He could be preserving one last person who would help him run.

“The next name,” she said.

Harl shook his head. A thin clicking sounded in his throat. His good hand struck the bed frame once, then slid down its post.

“Who has the routes now?”

He tried to speak. She bent closer. His lips shaped something she could not understand.

“Ease it,” she told Corven.

The chain shifted. Harl drew a broken breath and began coughing. Blood flecked his lip. Corven’s eyes remained on her.

“Merev,” Harl said. “The tallyman. At the sail steps. Merev.”

“How do we find him?”

“The little office. Above the net shop. Please.”

“Release him.”

Harl fell sideways. Corven caught his shoulder so that his head did not strike the bed, then lowered him to the floor. The sound of his breathing filled the room. He lay with his knees drawn up and his good hand spread against his ribs.

Lyra waited for him to begin shouting again. He did not.

“Fetch the surgeon,” she said.

Corven stayed where he was. “Send someone.”

She went to the stair and called. The woman from the cart stood at the bottom with her second chest. She looked up at Lyra’s torn sleeve, then past her toward the room.

“Is he coming?”

“No.”

“Can I have my money?”

Lyra asked how much and paid her. The woman counted it, lifted the chest, and left without saying goodbye to Harl.

Merev’s office was empty by the time Orrel reached it. He found a hot cup, a half-eaten roll, and a drawer from which everything had been swept hurriedly. Under the roll lay one of Lyra’s old unloading slips. That was enough to close three compromised routes and move the next day’s cargo before the Court’s men arrived.

Dessa shut her lamp shop. Nobody admitted seeing where she went.

Harl lived. The surgeon found broken ribs and damage beneath them she could not mend by setting bone. She drained blood while Lyra held the basin. Harl kept his eyes shut and tried to turn his face away whenever Lyra spoke.

“He may never take a full load of air again,” the surgeon said outside the room.

“May.”

“He may. I won’t tell you something certain because you want a sentence to put in an account.”

Lyra flinched. The woman wiped her hands on a cloth already stained beyond washing.

“Was he armed?”

“At first.”

“When this happened?”

“No.”

The surgeon looked at her for a long time, then went back inside.

Lyra arranged a paid attendant. She left enough money for treatment in the surgeon’s keeping and ordered Harl’s remaining belongings sent to his sister if he asked. The small painting went with the bag. She did not open the letters.

The crews were paid before the evening meal. Tesset made each man count the coins himself and entered the date beside the false one. When she reached Orrel, she turned the book so he could compare his mark with the copied version. He laughed once, without pleasure, and made his own carefully below it.

“Will Harl come back?” Tesset asked after the last man left.

“He won’t be managing that warehouse.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

“He’s alive.”

“How much of him?”

Lyra looked at her. Tesset’s face was pale, but her hands were steady as she closed the book.

“I’ll keep the true account,” she said. “I won’t write that the injury happened while he attacked you.”

“I haven’t asked you to.”

“Good.”

She put the book in its place and went home. Lyra remained at the desk until the shadows of the window bars vanished into the rest of the room.

Corven came up carrying two bowls. He put one beside her. Steam rose from it, bringing the smell of cabbage and smoked fish. She pushed it a little farther away.

“You could have told me to stop,” he said.

“I did.”

He sat opposite her.

“Eventually,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Would he have told us otherwise?”

“I don’t know.”

“The name was useful.”

“It was.”

She waited for him to give her something she could fight. He ate a spoonful of soup and looked at the spoon as though he had forgotten what it was for.

“Do you think I shouldn’t have?” she asked.

“I think you knew what you were waiting for.”

She looked down at her cold hands. The right sleeve had been pinned together where Harl’s knife cut it. There was no wound beneath.

“Will you still work for me?”

“Yes.”

He answered without hesitation. She wanted to thank him. The words would not come, and she pulled the bowl toward her instead.

“Eat,” he said. “It’ll be worse cold.”

She ate. He stayed until she finished.

A week later, a landing owner who had disputed her fee for months brought back the signed agreement. He accepted her first figure and apologized for taking so long. Lyra watched his thumb tremble against the paper while he waited for her signature.

“You can sit,” she said.

“I’m quite comfortable.”

He stood until she had signed. At the door he stepped aside for Corven, who was carrying a broken stool to the carpenter.

Lyra went to the window after the man left. Below, a loader looked up and straightened. His companion followed his glance. They returned to work before she could tell them she had come for air.

That evening Maeron stopped beside her on the stair.

“They’re leaving our people alone,” he said.

“For now.”

“For now matters.”

He touched her shoulder. She let the touch remain, feeling the familiar cold through the cloth, until someone called him from below.

Chapter 19 Callista at the table

The woman asked for a return credit on an empty case.

Tesset brought her upstairs because the case bore the split reed seal. The seal itself had been broken and pressed together with ordinary candle wax, badly enough that nobody could mistake it for an authorized closure. Blue cord hung from one corner. The woman’s hands were wrapped in cloth despite the warmth of the day.

“She says it was delivered short,” Tesset said.

“It was.”

The woman’s rough voice was loud enough to carry down the stair. It caught when she drew breath. She wore a dark veil folded back from her face. One eyebrow had been shaved where an old cut crossed it. White grains clung to the roots of her hair.

“Sit down,” Lyra said.

“I would rather stand.”

“Then put the case on the table.”

The woman did, carefully. Lyra recognized the shallow oblong shape. There were eleven of them in the return store downstairs, waiting to go back with the next delivery of mineral preparations. She had argued for standard fittings so that her workers could stack them without crushing the lids. This one had the new brass corners.

“Where did you receive it?”

“At the end of an arrangement I regret.”

“That isn’t a delivery point.”

“No.”

Tesset remained by the door. The woman looked at her, then at Lyra.

“I need to speak without a clerk.”

“My clerk has heard worse than an unpaid claim.”

“I know.”

Lyra was irritated, and not yet sure she had a right to be. The woman’s eyes flicked toward the curtain, the window, the second door leading to the little store. She was measuring exits in the manner of someone who expected to use one badly.

“Leave the door open,” Lyra told Tesset. “Stay below.”

Tesset looked at the visitor’s covered hands. “Corven is in the yard.”

“I know.”

When Tesset had gone, the woman began untying the case. Her fingers had difficulty with the knot. Lyra reached to help. The woman pulled it toward herself so quickly that the brass corner struck the table.

“Sorry,” she said.

Lyra waited.

The lid opened on velvet padding with narrow hollows for small glass vessels. One hollow still held a vial. The liquid inside was almost clear. When the woman moved it, a dull gleam remained on the glass a little longer than the liquid itself.

“Do you know what it is?”

“Something delivered under a seal.”

“You’re very exact.”

“I’ve had practice.”

“Vile Tears.”

Lyra looked at the case again. She had seen the name on the lips of boys outside a tavern, had heard it in a dispute between a woman and the brother who had sold her copper pot. Neither had carried one of these cases. She had imagined a trade in packets, little hidden quantities passed in the street. Her own loads required two men and a cart.

“Where did you get this?”

“I’ve paid for eleven. That one is the twelfth.”

“You brought it to me for a refund?”

“I brought it because I thought you’d believe something you’d carried.”

The woman unwound one hand. The cloth stuck at the palm. Beneath it the skin was grey and split along the creases, white salt packed into the small wounds. Two fingers had stiffened together. She spread them with her other hand, carefully, and gave a short breath that might have been a laugh if it had contained any pleasure.

“Callista,” she said. “You should know my name before somebody tells you I’ve forgotten it.”

Lyra stood. “I’ll fetch water.”

“Fresh.”

“Yes.”

“People see the salt and bring salt water. It doesn’t help.”

Lyra went to the door and asked Tesset for fresh water and clean cloth. She heard Corven’s voice below, answering a question about a damaged wheel. The ordinary work seemed suddenly too loud.

When she returned, Callista had put the last vial back in the case. She sat now, shoulders bowed, her uncovered hand resting on a folded corner of the veil.

“Did you know Aria Nocturne?”

“I know the name.”

“Did you hear her?” Callista’s covered hand began to beat time against her knee.

“Once. At a distance.”

“Then you don’t.”

Lyra left the correction unanswered. Callista touched the vial with one rigid finger.

“The first time I took it, I could hear her again. Not a memory. I knew it wasn’t a memory because I heard her do something she’d never done while I was there. I heard her trying to stop.”

“Stop what?”

“The scream.”

Tesset brought the water. Lyra took it at the doorway and shut the door halfway, leaving the gap she had promised. She put the bowl beside Callista. The woman lowered her damaged hand into it and closed her eyes. Salt clouded the water.

“Who gave you the first dose?” Lyra asked.

“A woman who said she’d found a way to hear the lost songs. She didn’t lie about that.”

“Where is she?”

“I don’t know. I don’t remember her face.”

Callista watched a line of blood appear in the bowl.

“There are other things I don’t remember. Some days I know they’re missing. Some days somebody tells me.”

“And you kept taking it.”

“Would you like me to say I was forced?”

“No.”

“Good. I wanted to hear her. Then I wanted the next thing because I couldn’t bear what happened between. Then I thought if I could bear enough, I would find a way to reach her. I have reasons for all eleven. I can give them to you in order. They haven’t made my hand better.”

Lyra pulled her own chair closer. “Where is Aria?”

“Under the Opera House. Inside a crystal. They take the Tears from her.”

Lyra looked at the little vial. She had signed storage allowances for these. She had asked whether the preparation spoiled in warmth and been told that it lost value. A perfectly intelligible commercial answer.

“How do you know?”

“I’ve seen her.”

“How?”

Callista’s mouth tightened. “You want to check. I know. I came because you’re supposed to check.”

“Yes.”

“There’s a stair behind the lower stores. A narrow one. A man takes clean cloth down twice a day. I paid him to leave the door unlatched. I thought I could follow him. There were men below. Men who weren’t men anymore.”

“The Worn.”

“Yes. I got as far as the last grille. You can see the chamber from the bend if the outer lamp is lit. The crystal hangs inside a frame. She’s standing in it, but she isn’t standing. Her feet don’t reach the bottom. Her mouth is open.”

Lyra put a hand flat on the table.

“There is a sound,” Callista said. “You can hear it even through the wall. I had heard it in the Tears. That’s how I knew. I called to her.”

“Did she answer?”

“I don’t know.”

“What happened?”

“They found me. One of the men who brought the cloth said I was his sister. He said I was ill. I had money left then.”

“Who?”

“I won’t give you his name.”

“I need a way to check.”

“You need many things. He let me leave.”

Lyra sat back. She respected the refusal even as she began looking for another way in.

“Why come now?”

“Because the Festival is coming. Because the Prince has had a frame made to carry her. Because they’ve been bringing in parts for something that will make the whole house hear her when she finally cries out.”

“The whole house hears singers every evening.”

Callista looked at her with sudden anger. “You think I’ve come to complain about a performance?”

“I think you need to tell me what you mean.”

“The wards will break. The men below know it. They have been promised the rooms above. I’ve heard them speak about whose rooms they’ll take.”

“Who told you about the frame?”

“I watched it arrive. Three pieces, wrapped in felt. One curved piece higher than a man. The carter dropped the smallest and swore because they made him wait while they checked it.”

Lyra remembered the delay. She had charged for it. The recipient disputed the charge because the handling failure belonged to her carrier. They had settled for half.

“What color felt?”

“Green. Very dark. Someone had cut it from old curtains. There was a border stitched inside.”

Lyra stood and took the disputed delivery book from the shelf. Callista watched her turn the pages. At the relevant entry a scrap of green felt had been pinned to the carrier’s statement to show how thin the wrapping was.

“That’s it,” Callista said.

Lyra closed the book.

“What are you asking me to do?”

“Get me in. Break the crystal. Burn the Court’s files while the house is open.”

“What happens to Aria if the crystal breaks?”

“She dies.”

“And the wards?”

Callista’s hand shook in the bowl. She lifted it, pressed the cloth around it, and held it tightly between her knees.

“They may break anyway. I don’t know. There must be a way to do it before they bring her into the prepared place. She can’t stay there.”

“Has she asked you to kill her?”

“She is screaming.”

“Has she asked?”

Callista looked at the floor. The silence stretched long enough for a cart to be unloaded beneath the window. Lyra heard the planks laid down, the wheels rolled onto them, the short calls of men easing weight into place.

“I don’t know if she can ask,” Callista said.

“Then I won’t promise to kill her.”

“You’ll leave her.”

“I haven’t said that.”

“They all mean something better when they say wait.”

Lyra flinched. Callista saw it. For a moment the woman looked pleased to have hurt her, then the expression collapsed into exhaustion.

“I’m sorry,” she said. “I don’t know who you were before this. I know you get goods where you say they’ll go. I know men who laughed at your name have stopped laughing. I know the Prince needs you.”

Lyra looked down at the case. The new corners were a good design. She had saved money by ordering enough to make fitting them worthwhile.

“I’ll trace these,” she said. “I’ll find out what I can about the room. I won’t agree to anything about Aria until I’ve tried to learn what she wants.”

“How?”

“I don’t know yet.”

Callista nodded slowly. “That may be the most honest thing I’ve been told in months.”

Callista would not stay at the shore house. Lyra offered the bed without saying Maeron’s name, but Callista knew whose house it was.

“I can endure one congregation at a time,” she said.

Lyra found her a room above a washer’s yard, paid in advance, with the money held by the woman renting it. Corven came as far as the lower stair. Callista stopped when she saw him.

“Will he be outside?”

“Only if you want him.”

“I don’t.”

Corven inclined his head and walked away. Callista watched until he turned the corner.

“He does what you say.”

“Mostly.”

“Does that make it easier?”

Lyra picked up the case. “There are three steps here. Hold the rail.”

The room was small, with a bed, a basin, and a window opening over the wash lines. Sheets moved below them like pale bodies turning in sleep. The washer had put a stool near the bed so that Callista could reach the water without standing. She asked whether the woman needed someone overnight.

“Not yet,” Callista said.

The washer looked at Lyra. Lyra made herself let the answer stand.

When they were alone, Callista took the vial from the case and held it to the light.

“Do you want me to keep it?” Lyra asked.

“No.”

“I can put it somewhere you can’t get to easily.”

“I can buy another. That’s what all your deliveries are for.”

Lyra had no answer to that. Callista lowered the vial.

“If I give it to you,” she said, “I’ll think only about getting it back. If it is here, I can think about not opening it.”

“Does that work?”

“Sometimes.”

She placed it in the bedside drawer. Lyra wanted to lock the drawer. The room’s key was in the door; the drawer had no lock. She put her hands behind her back.

“Do you have anyone?” she asked.

“I’m trying to have you.”

Lyra sat on the stool. Callista lay down fully dressed, her wrapped hand on her stomach. For a while they listened to the women below argue about a missing linen mark. A child ran through the wash lines and was called back to hold a peg basket.

“Aria sang about a girl once,” Callista said. “A girl who stole a loaf and then went back because she hadn’t brought one for her brother. People laughed. It was a foolish song. I was laughing too. Then she sang the same verse slower and I couldn’t laugh at it anymore.”

She tried the beginning. Her voice held for two words, then failed. She swallowed and tried again, more softly. Lyra waited.

“That’s who she is. Everyone tells you about the public accusation. The names she sang. That’s true too. But she could make you stay with a foolish girl until you wanted her to get away.”

“You loved her.”

“Yes.”

“Did she know?”

Callista smiled a little. “She knew I was there. Some nights that was enough.”

She closed her eyes. Lyra stayed until her breathing became regular, then rose very slowly. At the door she looked back. One hand had moved toward the drawer in sleep but had not reached it.

At the yard, Tesset had taken the empty cases out of the return store. They stood in a row on the office floor. She had opened none of them.

“The seals,” she said. “You asked me to compare.”

Lyra knelt beside the first. Split reed, blue cord, a maker’s scratch under one brass corner. Each had space inside for twelve vessels. She knew because she had ordered the padding after the earlier glass breakage.

“How many cases in the last delivery?”

“Twenty-eight.”

“And before that?”

“Twenty-four. Do you want all of them?”

“Yes.”

Tesset brought the book. Lyra counted the consignments again, then counted the returns. The discrepancy was small enough that she had accepted replacement charges without asking why certain vessels never came back.

“Is something wrong with the preparations?” Tesset asked.

“Yes.”

“Are they poison?”

Lyra looked at the numbered column.

“I need to find out how they are being used.”

Tesset sat on the floor beside her. “Do you want me to stop the next load?”

The next load would go directly from the lower Opera stores to a warehouse in the Salt Ward. If she stopped it now, the Prince’s clerk would ask why before she had traced a single destination. The case beside her smelled faintly sweet beneath the salt, like flowers left too long in stale water.

“Not yet,” she said.

Tesset waited for an explanation. Lyra gave her the route slips.

“Make me a copy of every name.”

Chapter 20 What the loads contained

The empty cases weighed too much.

Lyra had charged the Prince for damaged fittings often enough to know what each replacement weighed. The cases returned from three Salt Ward stores were consistently heavier than those returned from shops. Their lids had been washed. A fine white deposit remained in the joins beneath the velvet, where nobody looked unless a fitting had worked loose.

Tesset brought the scales to the upstairs office. They took the cases apart in the afternoon while ordinary work continued below. Lyra used a blunt knife to lift the padding. In the first case she found a little strip of cloth. It had been caught under the brass corner and pulled free of something larger. A laundry mark remained along one edge.

“That’s from the lower stores,” Tesset said.

“How do you know?”

“They return their cloth through us. Ada’s group washes it.”

“Jorin’s Ada?”

“Yes.”

Lyra set the strip on a clean sheet. She could remember Ada coming to the office with a basket on her hip and Jorin walking backward in front of her, trying to take it. They had argued pleasantly about whether he wanted to help or merely look helpful where his employer could see. Lyra had laughed. Jorin had looked delighted to have made her.

“When did he last come for his pay?”

“He doesn’t. The lower stores pay him now.”

“Does Ada collect anything here?”

“Only for the washing.”

“Ask her to come.”

Tesset put down the little screwdriver she had borrowed from the carpenter.

“Lyra.”

“What?”

“What are we finding?”

Lyra looked at the disassembled cases. She had been giving Tesset one task at a time, hoping the next fact would be easier to tell her.

“The preparations are Vile Tears. There is a woman held below the Opera House. They come from her.”

Tesset’s face emptied.

“All of these?”

“I think so.”

“And we keep them cold.”

“Yes.”

“The first ones broke because we put them under the iron shipment.”

“Yes.”

Tesset looked at her hands. A little white dust lay along the side of one finger. She wiped it on a cloth, folded the cloth over, and wiped again.

“Who told you?”

“The woman who came.”

“She’s ill.”

“She is.”

They sat without speaking while a man downstairs asked whether the west boat would take another half load. Somebody said there was room if the weight could go forward.

“Do you want to go home?” Lyra asked.

“I want you to stop asking people to do things before you tell them what they’re doing.”

Lyra bowed her head. “All right.”

“Have you stopped the loads?”

“No.”

“Why?”

“Because access to the stores is the only way I can find out where she is and what they intend. If I stop now, they replace me and shut the doors.”

“So we keep carrying it.”

“For the moment.”

Tesset stood and moved away from the table. She went to the window, opened it wider, and put both hands on the sill. Lyra waited. Below, the answer about the half load changed; a horse had gone lame, and the weight would have to wait until morning.

“I’ll copy the records,” Tesset said eventually. “I won’t write the next delivery order.”

“I’ll write it.”

Tesset remained at the window a little longer before returning to the table.

Ada wore one of Jorin’s shirts beneath her apron. Its cuffs had been folded back three times. She sat on the edge of the chair and said she had only a short while because the linen at the yard would sour if left soaking.

“Has he written?” Lyra asked.

“No. He can’t much. You know that.”

“Has anyone brought word?”

“He sends money.”

“How?”

“A man comes. Sometimes a different man. They say he’s working nights.”

“When did you last see him?”

Ada turned a little toward the door.

“Have they dismissed him?”

“No one has told me that.”

“Because he said he could do the hours. He told them he could. We need the money until my mother’s debt is finished.”

“I’m trying to find out how he is.”

“I asked at the stores. They said I’d disturb his rest.”

“What did you do?”

“I went away.” She looked up, angry now. “What was I going to do? Go through them?”

Lyra thought of the men at the lower doors and the exact force it would take to go through. She had that force, at least some of it. Ada had a basket and a question.

“I’ll ask,” Lyra said.

“Don’t tell him I complained.”

“I won’t.”

“If there’s something wrong, tell me. Don’t bring me his money and let me go on talking.”

“I will,” Lyra said.

Ada rose, then sat again.

“He’s a fool about a better wage,” she said. “He thinks it means somebody has seen him properly. I told him we could finish the debt more slowly. He said my mother shouldn’t have to wait on him. My mother doesn’t care. She likes him.”

She pressed her lips together until the trembling stopped. Lyra offered water. Ada shook her head, stood, and went back to her washing.

A damaged hinge got Lyra into the lower Opera stores. She had kept it in her desk because the Prince’s clerk disputed the cost of the replacement. Corven carried a case fitted with the new pattern, and a carter brought the old one so that nobody could say she had come without evidence.

The lower entrance smelled of damp rope and expensive cooking. Above, someone was rehearsing a passage on a stringed instrument, the same five notes repeated until the last became unbearable. Men moved salt sacks through the passage under the music. A stage worker crossed with a painted branch taller than himself and made everyone stand aside while he tilted it around the turn.

The Prince’s clerk inspected the hinge. He had narrow hands and clean nails, one of which clicked repeatedly against the brass while he explained that storage corrosion belonged to the carrier’s liability.

“Then your store should have been dry,” Lyra said.

“Our store was dry.”

“The case was here for six days before it came back.”

“Your return boat leaked.”

“Not upward into a shelf.”

He glanced toward Corven. Corven was looking at the ceiling, apparently interested in the music.

“Leave it,” the clerk said. “I’ll consult.”

“I’ll wait.”

That inconvenienced him. Lyra sat on a closed salt barrel and took out the return list. The carter leaned against the wall with his cap in his hands. After a little while the clerk went through the inner door, leaving a guard to watch them.

Jorin came out carrying two full sacks.

At first Lyra recognized the way he set them down. Two quick taps on the boards with his left hand before he took the strain off his back. He had done that in the yard when the planks were damp, checking footing without looking. Then she saw his thumbnail, split along the outer edge from the crate that fell the previous winter.

The rest of him took longer to reach her. His neck had thickened with a white crust that broke in plates where he turned his head. His jaw looked heavy. A grey film covered the exposed skin beneath his shirt, and the sweat on his face dried to pale grains while she watched. He stood with both hands open at his sides, waiting for another instruction.

“Jorin.”

He turned toward the sound.

Lyra rose. Corven moved from the wall and stopped beside her.

“Jorin. It’s Lyra.”

The man watched her mouth. His own lips parted. A little dry click came from his throat.

“Lyra,” he said at last.

She wanted the name to mean more than the effort it had taken him to say it.

“Ada asked after you.”

His eyes shifted toward the sacks. He made one of the two little tapping movements against his thigh.

“Home,” Lyra said. “Do you want to go home?”

The guard stepped closer. “He’s on duty.”

“I can see that.”

“He needs to finish.”

Corven turned his head toward the guard. The man stopped.

“Do you remember Ada?” Lyra asked.

Jorin’s mouth moved. His teeth had a film of salt along the gum. He lifted one hand slowly and touched the cuff of his shirt, smoothing it down as though somebody had folded it badly.

“Washing,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Good.”

“She wants to see you.”

He looked toward the inner doorway. Beyond it someone called a number. He turned away from Lyra and bent for the sacks.

“Wait.”

He paused with his hands on them. The clerk came through the door before she could speak again.

“That worker is assigned elsewhere,” he said. “You’ll need to make an appointment.”

“I employed him.”

“You released him from that employment.”

“Can his wife see him?”

“An appointment can be made.”

“Make it.”

He looked at the guard, then at Corven, and forced a thin smile.

“I’ll consult.”

Jorin took up the sacks. The weight would once have required two men. His feet left white prints on the dark floor as he carried them through the inner door.

Lyra remained standing after he had gone. The repeated music above changed at last. Somebody played the five notes correctly, and a voice said, “Again.”

Katerina found her at the return store after dark. Lyra had sent three messages. The third said she would go to the Prince herself if there was no answer.

“You should not write that,” Katerina whispered.

“You should answer the first two.”

Katerina closed the door and checked the window. Her scarf had slipped low enough to show the scar across her throat. She drew it up without looking in the glass.

“You saw Jorin.”

“Yes.”

“He is among the retained.”

“His wife calls it married.”

Katerina lowered her eyes. Lyra had intended to wound her and was disappointed by how little satisfaction the success brought.

“The Tears,” she said. “Did he know?”

“He knew they would help him endure the work.”

“Did he know about the twelfth?”

“I wasn’t there.”

“Would they have told him?”

Katerina said nothing.

“Aria Nocturne is under that building.”

“Yes.”

“The cases come from her.”

“Yes.”

“And you have brought me contracts for them.”

“Yes.”

Lyra pushed a stack of copies across the table. “There were eight cases a fortnight when we began. Then twelve. Then the cold store was finished, and the losses stopped, and you asked for more regular departures. You said the preparation spoiled when it waited.”

“It does.”

“How many people like Jorin?”

“I don’t know.”

“How many?”

“I don’t know, Lyra.” The whisper frayed. Katerina put a hand to her throat and waited before trying again. “Some take it for the work. Some buy it. Some are given it. The men who oversee the retained keep records I don’t see.”

“You see enough.”

“Yes.”

Lyra got up and went to the window. Across the yard two workers were cleaning mud from a cart wheel. She could tell by the shape of the shadows which one had the stiff shoulder. He was singing quietly to himself while his companion held the lantern.

“Callista says he means to use Aria at the Festival.”

Katerina’s hand remained at her throat.

“Has she been here?”

“She brought one of my cases.”

“You must move her.”

“From where?”

“Wherever she is.”

“Why?”

“They have been looking for someone who took a sealed portion from the lower stores. The case was counted.”

“Then tell me how they count her.”

“A woman with salt in her hair. One hand stiff. A devotion to Aria that makes her easy to draw out.”

Lyra turned. “Did you help put Aria there?”

Katerina closed her eyes.

“I brought her.”

“Willingly?”

“No.”

Lyra had let herself imagine that Katerina came afterward: another intermediary, someone who knew how the place worked. Now Katerina stood before her with one hand pressed against the scar.

“How?”

“I can tell you. I will. But if you send for her now, openly, you will close what access I still have.”

“You want me to trust your access.”

“I want you to use it. You can decide what to call me afterward.”

Lyra looked at the copies on the table.

“Can I see her?”

“Perhaps.”

“Find out.”

“I need time.”

“You’ll have until the next return load. After that I start giving names to people outside both your houses.”

Katerina shook her head. “Some of them will sell the names back.”

“Then give me a reason not to try.”

For the first time that evening, Katerina looked directly at her.

“I will try to get you in,” she said. “Move Callista tonight.”

They moved Callista to another room in the same washer’s family, across the canal and above a store used for clean linen. Lyra told her why. Callista insisted on carrying the case herself, though it was empty now except for the final vial. Corven walked behind them at a distance. At the bridge he stopped to let a pair of late revelers pass, and one of them recognized Lyra. The man’s laughter died. He touched his friend’s sleeve, and both went to the far side of the bridge.

Callista watched them.

“Will they leave us alone?”

“For tonight.”

“Good.”

Lyra helped Callista up the new stair, pleased that for tonight, at least, she could keep those men away.

The next morning she went to Ada before the washer could hear from anyone else. She described Jorin exactly, including the word he had spoken and the cuff he had touched. Ada listened without interrupting. When Lyra finished, the woman picked up a wet shirt from the basin and began wringing it.

“Will they let him come home?”

“I’ve asked.”

“He’ll ruin the sheets.”

“We can bring others.”

Ada twisted the shirt harder. Water ran onto the floor.

“He’ll frighten my mother.”

“Do you want me to explain before he comes?”

“I want you to bring him.”

“I’ll try.”

Ada let go. The shirt fell back into the water.

“Don’t send money instead.”

Lyra took the wet shirt and hung it where Ada indicated. She stayed for another half hour, helping lift a heavy sheet through the rollers. Ada gave instructions in a flat, practical voice.

Maeron was at the submerged room when she found him. The water stood below the working platform, black between the boards. Two followers sat with their bare feet in it while repairing a net. They greeted Lyra, then made room when they saw her face.

“Stay,” she told them. “I only need a word.”

Maeron led her to the little chamber beyond, where the lower stones remained wet even at low tide. He had been sorting vessels recovered from a deep wreck. Several contained water whose surface did not move when the vessels were tilted.

“How much did you know about the Tears?” she asked.

He set down the cup in his hand.

“I knew the Prince used them.”

“On workers.”

“Yes.”

“On ours.”

“They were offered work elsewhere.”

“Jorin can’t remember his wife’s name.”

Maeron’s face tightened. “I’m sorry.”

“Did you know where the preparations came from?”

“I knew there was a singer.”

“A captive.”

“I understood she was held.”

“You told me not to open the cases.”

“They were under seal.”

“You told me the interference would endanger the agreement.”

“It would.”

“Would you have told me if I hadn’t found out?”

Maeron looked past her toward the room where his people sat with their feet in the water.

“I thought there was more you could do while the arrangement continued.”

“For whom?”

“For them. For yourself. For the people who now come here because you can offer more than a blanket.”

“And Aria?”

“I have not known how to reach her.”

“You knew how to reach me.”

He looked at her then. The cold in the small chamber seemed to thicken between them.

“The Prince’s people are becoming something different from us,” he said. “You must understand that. What they do with names, what their hunger takes—”

“I understand that Jorin isn’t one of your followers. I understand he did not become what I saw because he sat at our table. I am asking why you helped keep the cases shut.”

Maeron lowered his head.

“Because I was afraid of losing what we had gained.”

The answer sat within her own chest too comfortably. She had written the next delivery order before coming here.

“We keep the access,” she said. “For now. We stop sending people to his stores. No recommendations. No man told he should be grateful for the better wage.”

“All right.”

“And you tell me anything else you know.”

He began with names. She made him repeat them while she wrote. When she had filled both sides of a sheet, she held it above the damp stones until the ink dried. Beyond the doorway a follower laughed at something his companion had said. The laughter was warm and familiar. Maeron looked toward it with love so plain that Lyra could not pretend she had imagined his care.

That evening she took out the delivery order. Under the quantity she added a second instruction in her own hand: No substitution of carriers without my knowledge. She sanded the ink, folded the sheet, and sent it below.

Chapter 21 The Lady Under the Waves

Marda refused to serve boiled fish at the feast.

“They eat boiled fish when there’s nothing else. They can have something they don’t eat with a worry.”

“The cooks need to know by noon,” Lyra said.

“Then they can know it now.”

There were onions on the table between them, three sacks of flour against the wall, and a list of debts on Lyra’s knee. The feast had been planned before Callista came. It marked the end of a profitable season and the opening of the repaired quay after the Court’s men failed to keep it closed. Lyra had considered cancelling it twice. Each time she imagined telling the people who had bought cloth for their children and invited relatives from across the water that their pleasure was inconvenient to her new knowledge.

Marda picked up an onion and inspected its root.

“You could have said no a month ago.”

“I haven’t said no now.”

“You’ve looked it.”

Lyra put the list down. “What do you want?”

“Meat in pastry. The little ones. People like being given a thing they can hold.”

“They cost more.”

“I know what they cost. I’ve been feeding people since you were being told not to spill your milk.”

Lyra laughed. Marda’s face softened, and she pushed the onion toward her.

“Peel that while you tell me why we can’t afford it.”

They bought the pastries. Lyra found the money by postponing the replacement of a desk she had begun to hate. Marda said there was nothing wrong with the desk, and Lyra said that was why it continued to exist. For a little while the work was only work: counting plates, checking whether the hired benches would fit through the chapel door, making sure the workers preparing the feast would be paid for the day they spent serving it.

Maeron wanted the old tidal chapel. Lyra preferred the quay, where people could come and go without passing through an observance. They compromised by putting the food outside and leaving the chapel for those who wished to join the evening rite. He agreed readily. She checked the opening hours against the tide herself.

“We will need the lower steps cleared,” she said.

“Corven is doing it.”

“And lights along the return path.”

“Yes.”

“Some people will be drinking.”

“I hope they enjoy themselves.”

She looked up from the plan. He stood in the doorway holding a bundle of small cups, each wrapped in cloth to keep it from knocking against the others. A child’s drawing had been tied around one. It showed a boat with enormous people in it and an eel nearly as large as the boat.

“You’ve been avoiding me,” he said.

“I’ve been working.”

“Both can happen.”

She gave him no denial. Since their conversation in the submerged room she had seen him only with others present. He had supplied the names she demanded, then brought two additional ones without being asked. She could not decide whether that was an effort to repair trust or proof that he had kept something back the first time.

“Will you stand with me tonight?”

“For what?”

“To thank them.”

“They know I’m grateful.”

“Let them hear it.”

Lyra looked at the cups. They were the ordinary brown ones used by workers who could not afford their own. Several had been mended. She had paid the potter before the repair work because his daughter needed treatment, and afterward he had given her a small blue bowl with a glaze flaw he insisted she should not pay for. She used it for breakfast.

“I’ll stand,” she said.

Maeron smiled. “Thank you.”

“What else will you ask?”

His smile remained, but something behind it became still.

“I’d like you to let them be glad of you.”

The quay looked smaller with tables on it. Lyra had spent so much time using the space for loads that she had forgotten how many people could fit there when no cargo stood between them. Children ran along the clear strip beside the water until Corven made them move inland. He showed them the line they were not to cross, then occupied it himself with a plate in his hand.

Tesset came wearing a yellow scarf Lyra had never seen. She brought a woman from the house where she lodged and introduced her by name. Lyra forgot the name immediately because the woman was looking at her with open curiosity, trying to see the person Tesset had described. She made herself ask again and listen this time.

“You’ve been told terrible things,” Lyra said.

“About your hours,” the woman answered. “Yes.”

Tesset laughed. It was a laugh from somewhere outside the office, freer and louder. Lyra felt a small pang, then pleasure that it existed.

Bast came on crutches. The surgeon had approved the journey on the condition that he keep the injured leg raised and leave before the damp became severe. His wife had brought a folded blanket despite his complaints. They put him at the end of a bench with room for the leg, and three bargemen gathered to tell him what work was waiting when he was ready.

“Don’t make him promise tonight,” Lyra said.

“We’re arguing about who has to put up with him,” one said. “Let us finish.”

Bast looked pleased. The pale patch had stopped spreading. He could feel the side of his foot again, though the surgeon warned that standing too soon could undo their progress. Lyra asked after the voyage book. He said the author had now sunk a ship by sailing east into a westward current and he meant to send a correction if the man was still alive.

“I’ll write it for you,” she said.

“You’ll improve the language.”

“Probably.”

“Then I’ll ask Tesset.”

Neris arrived late, carrying a dish covered with a cloth. She set it on the serving table and came to stand beside Lyra. Her hair was wet. Lately it never seemed entirely dry, even after she had spent a day inland.

“You came,” Lyra said.

“I eat.”

“I meant—”

“I know.” Neris touched her arm. “I wanted to.”

They watched Corven break a pastry in half for a child who found it too hot. The child accepted both halves and ran away. Corven looked at his empty hand, then went back for another.

“Are you going in?” Lyra asked.

“For the thanks. Maybe the cup.”

“No one needs you to dive.”

“I remember.”

There was no gratitude in the answer. Neris took another bite of pastry, and Lyra stood with her until she had finished.

Maeron moved among the tables, carrying plates to people who could not rise. He sat with a woman whose husband had not returned from a coastal voyage unrelated to their work. She talked for a long time. He listened, one hand flat on the table between them, and when she began to cry he did not look around to see whether others had noticed.

Lyra watched him, then looked away because she had no place ready for the tenderness she felt.

She had set aside a plate for Ada. The washer did not come. Jorin’s wages had arrived again the previous day, carried by a different man. Katerina had secured an appointment for Ada, then withdrawn it when the lower stores changed their watch. Lyra knew the reasons and had no use for any of them. She covered the plate and asked a boy to take it to Ada’s house, with no message beyond the fact that it was hers.

When the first lamps were lit, Maeron called people toward the chapel. Some rose. Others remained over their food. Tesset lifted a hand to Lyra, then turned back to her companion. Nobody tried to persuade her.

Lyra went inside with Neris.

The water lay below the lowest step, close enough for reflected light to move across the roof. The chapel’s old carvings had lost their faces. Generations of wet hands had polished the stone beside the entrance, leaving a smooth place at shoulder height where people steadied themselves. Lyra touched it as she passed.

She knew the order of the observance now. The knife would go from person to person. The small spiral, shallow enough to close cleanly. The cup. The hope spoken so quietly that the water seemed to hear it before anyone else. She had come nearly six months from Iven’s loft to stand here again, knowing whose dead had paid for the lamps outside and what moved through the stores she kept supplied.

Corven stood near the door, his coat open. His familiar’s vessel rested on a shelf where he could see it. The eel had risen along the glass with its pale underside showing, mouth opening and closing slowly.

Maeron waited until the movement in the room settled. He thanked the cooks first. Marda was absent, staying with a sick woman at the shore house, and he named her so that the acknowledgment could be carried home. He named the carpenters who had repaired the quay and the people who had kept the boats moving while it was closed. Lyra heard Orrel’s name, and thought of him holding the lamp over the dead sailors’ belongings.

Then Maeron asked her to come forward.

She had expected a short speech. She had prepared one about the winter reserve, the injury money, the debts they no longer needed to accept. He took her hand before she began and turned her toward the people.

“They used to ask us what we could bring up,” he said. “Then they told us what it was worth. They waited until our hands were empty and our houses hungry before they named the price.”

Several people murmured assent. Lyra saw the old buyer’s warehouse in her mind, the weights set badly, the wool that had looked impossible until she put a hand into it.

“She asked what they owed us. She stayed until they answered.”

A woman near the back laughed and said, “Long time.”

“Yes,” Maeron said. “A long time.”

Lyra smiled despite herself. The woman was a net mender whose rent she had paid during a broken wrist. She had repaid it in small amounts without missing a week, then brought Lyra a bag of walnuts because the last coin felt insufficient.

Maeron continued. He spoke of wages arriving when promised, boats repaired before someone had to choose between a dangerous hull and no income. He spoke of work refused. That made Neris look up. He said Lyra had made it possible to say no and still eat.

She hadn’t expected him to praise her for that. He knew what it meant to Neris. He knew what it meant to her.

A man at the side raised his cup. Others followed. The movement traveled through the room. Lyra wanted to speak before it became more than she could answer, but Maeron began the low song beneath their ordinary voices.

It entered through her feet.

She felt the stone’s cold rise along her legs, familiar at first, almost welcome after the crowded quay. Then the pressure passed the place where she expected it to stop. Her breath caught. Around her, cups trembled. A woman put a hand against the wall and laughed softly, amazed.

Lyra turned toward Maeron.

“What are you doing?”

“Stand with them.”

His spoken words lay above the low vibration, and she could not understand how he continued both. The water below the step flattened. Reflected light stopped moving on the roof.

Corven had left the doorway. He was watching Maeron, one hand on the edge of the shelf. His eel pressed against the glass so hard its body seemed to lose thickness. Neris sat suddenly on the step behind her. A man beside her knelt with his cup held out before him, his face full of joy.

Lyra knew this could reach Marda in the other house. It could reach the man sleeping after a night shift, the woman who had stayed with her child. She had made Maeron admit it long ago. There was no shelter from this in declining one evening’s song.

“Maeron.”

He tightened his hand around hers.

“They are with you.”

The pressure deepened. Something in Lyra’s chest settled lower than it had any right to sit. Her next breath seemed unnecessary for a moment, and the absence of urgency was so pleasant that she nearly closed her eyes. She saw other people discover it. Faces eased. Shoulders lowered. A few struggled against it, taking quick breaths that made the room sound full of small frightened animals.

She could step away from Maeron. She could tell them to leave, interrupt the ceremony, make the next hours about what he had done. She looked at Neris, who had put both feet firmly on the stone and was concentrating on lifting her head. Corven’s gaze found Lyra’s. He held out his hand a little, offering a place to go.

Then the net mender said her name.

Others answered. They were looking at her, pleased, proud, frightened, wanting the person who had made difficult things work to tell them what this one meant.

Lyra straightened.

Maeron raised their joined hands. “You have brought a house beneath the reach of those who would close every door to us. You have carried the deep ashore and kept its people. Lady Under the Waves, will you stand for them?”

The title entered the silence left between two breaths. Lyra had not heard it before. She looked from Maeron to the people, saw recognition forming as if he had named something they had already felt.

She thought of Aria under the Opera House. Of the Prince’s clerk telling her to make an appointment. Of the doors that would open to someone with this room behind her.

“Yes,” she said.

Her voice came from far away.

Maeron’s hand lay in hers. He was close enough that she could see the fine cracks around his mouth, but the word seemed to reach him across water. He smiled. In the front row a woman began to cry, and the man beside her put an arm around her shoulders without lowering his cup.

“Say it again,” Maeron whispered.

Lyra heard the request clearly.

“I will stand,” she said.

The hollow distance remained. The room answered her. She felt their voices through the floor and through Maeron’s cold hand. For a moment the affection was so large that she could not find an edge to it. She stood within it and let them say the title again.

The cups passed afterward. Lyra cut the spiral into her palm with a steadier hand than she expected. The blood gathered darkly, slow to run. She took the brine and bent over it.

“Let me keep them safe.”

Her own voice came back altered. She could not be sure which word had changed. Someone beside her was sobbing, and someone else had begun to laugh. She held the cup until the surface stopped trembling, then drank.

No voice answered from below. The water remained water in a mended cup. Around her, people embraced and compared their hands. A man said he could feel where the old chapel continued beneath the wall. Neris asked him whether he could also feel the stair he was blocking. He moved, abashed, and helped her rise.

Lyra went with her toward the door.

Outside, the quay seemed too open. The lamps stood at separate points in a darkness that had grown uncomfortable between them. Lyra moved close to the chapel wall without deciding to. Beside her, Neris did the same.

“Your voice,” Neris said.

“I know.”

“Does it hurt?”

“No.”

Neris looked back into the chapel. “Mine doesn’t either.”

The end of her sentence carried a faint hollow note. Her skin glistened as though she had come directly from the water. She wiped her forehead with a sleeve. It shone again immediately.

Tesset approached carrying the yellow scarf over her arm. She stopped when Lyra greeted her.

“What happened?”

“The song went further.”

“Did you know it would?”

“No.”

Tesset looked at the people coming out behind them. Some walked normally. One man kept his palm against the wall until the last possible moment, then crossed to a post and stood holding it. A girl laughed at the way her shadow lagged when she moved the lamp. Her mother pulled the lamp away.

“Do you need help?” Tesset asked.

“Find seats near the wall. Water. Ask who wants someone to walk home with them.”

Tesset nodded and went, calling for chairs in her own familiar voice. Lyra listened until she was out of hearing.

Marda came near midnight. She had left the sick woman with a neighbor after the new cold arrived. Her hair was damp down to the neck, though there had been no rain. She took Lyra’s hands and looked at the cut in her palm.

“I was holding the basin,” she said. “Thought I’d fainted standing up. Then I realized I hadn’t breathed for longer than I could count.”

“Are you all right?”

“I am here.”

“Where is he?”

Marda looked toward the chapel. Maeron stood inside with a man who had begun to weep and could not stop. He held both the man’s hands and spoke close to his face.

“Did he tell you?” Marda asked.

“No.”

Marda looked at Lyra’s face, then at her joined hands. “They say you accepted.”

“I did.”

The older woman let go gently. “Then they’ll be asking you what comes next.”

Ressa waited until the benches were nearly cleared. She had spent the feast washing cups and refused payment until Lyra put the coins into her apron pocket herself. Her son Tavin had joined the deep crews during the previous season. He was good with rope and shy about being praised.

“Could you speak to him?” Ressa asked.

“About what?”

“He’s down in the workroom. He says he needs to stay below a few days. Maeron says it will help him understand.”

“Does he want to?”

“He doesn’t know what he wants. He says he should ask you before coming out.”

Lyra looked toward the chapel. There were still people waiting to speak with her. A woman needed the cart for her mother. The cook had cut a thumb. Corven stood with a man who could no longer make himself cross the open stretch to the road.

“He can come out,” she said.

“Will you tell him?”

“Bring him to the office tomorrow. We’ll speak then.”

Ressa nodded. She looked relieved enough that Lyra thought the matter settled until the woman asked, “In the morning, my lady?”

The title sounded different in that mouth, spoken softly above the cups that still needed drying.

“In the morning,” Lyra said.

She turned to the woman waiting for the cart. Behind her, Ressa gathered the last wet cloth and folded it neatly over the rim of the basin.

After the last cart left, Lyra went back into the chapel to fetch her coat. Someone had folded it on a bench. She put it around her shoulders and sat down with the fastenings undone.

The room was almost empty. A stack of clean cups stood beside the door, and two lamps still burned. She could hear water moving under the steps again. When she rested her back against the stone, something eased along her ribs. She sat more heavily, letting the wall take her weight.

Maeron came from the lower passage with a cloth over one arm.

“Everyone home?” she asked.

The sound startled her. She had been giving instructions for hours, hearing it among other voices. Alone with him, she stopped before the next question. She touched her throat.

He sat at the other end of the bench.

“Everyone who wanted to go.”

She tried to hum the first few notes of a tune she knew. The little rise would not go where she expected. She stopped and swallowed.

“Will it stay like this?”

“I don’t know.”

He held out the cloth for her hand. She drew her hand beneath her coat.

“I was going to thank them.”

“They heard you.”

“I had something to say.”

He lowered the cloth. She had written the speech that afternoon, crossing things out until it sounded like herself. The paper was in her coat. She could feel its folded edge against her wrist.

“I know,” he said.
