“You don’t.”

For a while she wanted him to ask about it. She would have made him listen. He remained at the end of the bench, looking at the cloth in his hands, and eventually she grew tired of preparing the words.

The net mender’s voice came back to her instead, calling her name. She remembered faces turned toward her, people rising, the pleasure of standing before them without wanting to make herself smaller. She wanted another moment of that. Her eyes stung.

Maeron reached toward her and stopped. She hated having to decide about his hand too.

“Sit there,” she said.

He settled back. She closed her eyes. The wall was cool through her coat. She let her breath go and waited for the need to take another. Water slid against the step below them. One lamp gave a small crackle.

When she opened her eyes he was watching her.

“How long?” she asked.

“I wasn’t counting.”

She drew air in slowly. Nothing hurt. Beneath the unfamiliar depth in her chest she felt a quiet pleasure that made her want to try again.

“I need to know what I can do with it.”

Maeron nodded. He took a small sandglass from his coat and set it upright on the bench between them. A few grains had caught against the narrow throat. He tapped them loose with a fingernail.

“When you’re ready.”

Lyra slipped the coat from her shoulders and laid it over her knees. Then she turned the glass herself.

Chapter 22 The surviving charter

Tavin arrived before the office opened, carrying the rope he had been issued for the deep crews.

He had coiled it carefully. Ressa stood beside him with a bag containing his dry clothes. Neither came farther than the threshold until Lyra asked them in. The boy was a grown man, taller than his mother, with a narrow face made narrower by the way he held his jaw shut. He looked at the wall beside her desk as though there were something he wanted there.

“Sit,” she said.

He sat close to the wall.

“Your mother says you want to come out of the workroom.”

His eyes moved to Ressa. She put the bag on the floor and folded her hands.

“Yes,” he said.

“Then come out.”

“Maeron says a few days will make it easier.”

“Do you want the few days?”

“I don’t know.”

“Would you rather be home while you find out?”

He nodded. His mother looked away quickly.

Lyra took out his work agreement. There was no required term beyond the current recovery, which had ended before the feast. She showed him the line. He leaned over the table without touching it.

“Do I owe the rope?”

“You can return the rope.”

“And the food?”

“No.”

“The rooms?”

“No.”

He drew a breath, held it without apparent discomfort, and let it go much later than she expected. Watching him, Lyra realized she had done the same.

“Can I still come for the evening meal?”

“Yes.”

Ressa took her son’s hand. He looked embarrassed and relieved, and let her keep it.

Lyra wrote a note to the workroom keeper. Tavin is going home. His place at meals and any treatment continue. No further permission is needed. She signed it and gave it to him.

He read the last line twice. Then he tucked the paper inside his coat as carefully as though it were money and picked up the rope.

“Leave that,” she said. “I’ll have it checked.”

He put it on the floor beside the desk. Ressa thanked her in the new manner, using the title softly. Lyra told her she could still use her name. The woman smiled, agreed, and used the title again at the door.

After they left, Lyra looked at the empty chair. She had meant to tell him that he never needed to ask. The note in his pocket bore her signature.

Sera Vesper came with a message from Callista and no request for money.

That made Lyra suspicious before the woman sat down. Sera appeared accustomed to it. She took off her gloves, set a leather document case on the floor between her boots, and waited while Lyra read the note.

She knows where the charter is. Hear her. C.

The last stroke wandered. Callista’s hand had been worse when Lyra visited that morning. She had refused the final vial again, kept it in the drawer, and asked three times whether Katerina had sent word.

“What charter?” Lyra asked.

“The Silk Coin’s.”

“The Coin was broken.”

“Yes.”

“And you believe a document will mend it.”

“I believe a document survived. Whether anything can be mended is why I’m here.”

Sera was dressed plainly in clothes chosen to last. Ink had settled beside one thumbnail. A small scar divided the soft skin beneath her lower lip, pale enough that Lyra would not have noticed it if the woman had not pressed her mouth together while waiting.

“Where?”

“The Opera House. In a strongbox among the original property instruments. Below the Gallery, above the Prince’s stores.”

“How do you know?”

“I saw the receipt when the box was moved. I know the man who repaired its lock. And I have this.”

She opened the case and took out a folded rubbing on thin paper. The image showed part of a seal and a short passage of text. A corner was missing.

“A copy.”

“A rubbing from the outer plate. The seal on the instrument is inside.”

“Who made it?”

“A man who would like to survive helping us.”

Lyra looked up. Sera met her eyes without apology.

“Then give me something I can check,” Lyra said.

Sera smiled faintly. “Callista said you’d say that.”

She took out a second sheet. It held the name of a former municipal recorder, the date of an old registration, and a description of the Opera House’s foundations that matched the lower freight agreement in two particulars Lyra had not made public.

“The old clerk is still alive,” Sera said. “He will tell you what office held the duplicate. The duplicate was burned. He can also tell you who was paid to enter the loss. Ask him in his own house. He won’t come here.”

Lyra folded the sheet and set it beside the rubbing.

“What does the charter give you?”

“A claim to revive the Coin as a body, with its surviving rights. A claim to administer property acquired in its name. An account of who may act for it and who must be heard. The Opera House was one of its holdings before the Court made its own arrangements.”

“The Prince has leases below.”

“Yes.”

“Serafine has guards above.”

“Yes.”

“Staff expect their wages from those people.”

“Which is why I haven’t walked up with a piece of paper and asked them to leave.”

Lyra leaned back. Her voice had sounded strange at the beginning of the conversation. Now she noticed Sera working to follow it whenever she turned toward the window. The sound seemed to belong just beyond the person speaking.

“Does that trouble you?” she asked.

“Your voice?”

“Yes.”

“I can hear you.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

Sera considered. “I’m more interested in what you intend to say with it.”

Lyra almost smiled. She stopped herself because the answer was too useful to be accepted as reassurance.

“What do you want from me?”

“The things a charter cannot do. Boats that come when called. People paid before they are asked to stand in a doorway with a knife. Supplies that don’t disappear when a buyer is threatened. You have made the Prince difficult to starve out. You could make someone else difficult too.”

“Your Coin.”

“If we can agree what it will be.”

She spoke the words steadily, but her hands had closed over one another. Lyra had seen men ask for large sums with less emotion.

“Were you part of it?”

“After its fall. I worked with people trying to recover what was taken. Some wanted their own goods. Some wanted enough to begin again. A few wanted the name because they believed it had meant something worth keeping.”

“And you?”

“I wanted them to have a place where being robbed was enough reason to be heard. Then I found I wanted to be the person who could make that happen.”

“That can become an appetite.”

“Yes,” Sera said. “I thought you might understand.”

Lyra spent the afternoon checking. The old recorder lived above a knife grinder’s shop and had become almost deaf. His daughter interpreted the questions loudly until he shouted that he could hear perfectly well and answered the previous one. He recognized the description of the charter. He remembered being told to mark the duplicate lost. He would not sign anything at first.

“People who want old wrongs corrected generally arrive with new ones,” he said.

Lyra asked him whether he would identify the original if it were placed before him with independent witnesses. He considered the matter over a cup of thin beer, then said he would if the meeting happened somewhere his daughter could see the door.

The old property description matched a stone marker at the western service entrance. Lyra went there before dark, pretending to wait for a delayed cart while she looked. The lower edge had been covered by later paving. She scraped mud away with the end of a stick and found the old Coin mark under the Court’s newer number.

A porter watched her.

“Lost something?”

“A measurement.”

“Most people use a cord.”

“I should have brought one.”

He grinned and went back inside. She waited until he was gone before copying the mark. Above her, the Opera’s evening audience began arriving. Carriages drew up at the public entrance. Footmen carried cloaks. A woman with pearls sewn into her hair complained that the air smelled of fish.

A loaded cart passed Lyra and went down to the Prince’s stores. She knew its driver, its axle repair, the weight it could carry without fouling the turn. One shallow case showed beneath the covering cloth. Blue cord lay across its lid.

She stood until the door closed behind it.

Sera returned the next day with bread and cheese, having been told by Tesset that Lyra sometimes forgot to stop for meals. She put the food on a clear portion of the table and took the chair she had used before.

“Does everyone you bargain with get fed?” Lyra asked.

“Only if I expect the bargain to take a while.”

They ate first. Sera cut the cheese with a knife whose handle had been repaired with thread. She offered Lyra the larger piece without remark. Outside, rain began, thin at first, then hard enough to interrupt conversation when it struck the copper gutter.

“I can get people away,” Sera said. “That is the other offer.”

Lyra looked at her.

“Boats. Places outside the two wards. Some work elsewhere. Enough for those who choose it, if we begin now. You could withdraw from the Prince’s trade, sell what can be sold, and use the proceeds.”

“Some won’t leave.”

“I know.”

“Some can’t travel easily.”

“Then they need more help.”

“The stores don’t sell for what they’re worth when the buyer knows why you’re leaving.”

“No.”

Lyra put down the bread. She had wanted Sera to offer an impossible escape, something she could dismiss after finding the missing boat or the promise that depended on a stranger’s generosity. Instead Sera offered a costly, incomplete departure that Lyra could begin to arrange.

“Where would you go?” Sera asked.

“I have a room here.”

“You could have another.”

Lyra thought of that first room with its key. How she had shut the door and stood behind it, waiting for the feeling that someone would knock and require her to give it back. She had built so much since then that she scarcely went there except to sleep. The idea of leaving with a trunk made her angry before she could put words to why.

“And Aria?” she asked.

“We would still try to reach her. With less ability to help if they shut us out.”

“And Jorin? The others?”

“The same.”

Lyra moved to the window. The rain had driven the workers under cover. A boy carried soup across the yard with his coat over the bowl. He reached the shelter and lifted it triumphantly, dry except for his own hair. The men made room for him.

“I can keep those people paid,” she said.

“For how long under the Prince?”

“Not under him.”

Sera waited.

“The Opera House,” Lyra said. “We take it. Its working doors, its stores, its machinery. We stop the harvesting and keep the crystal out of whatever he has prepared. Your people get the charter. We make a place neither of them can close with a message to a landlord.”

Sera set her cup down very carefully.

“You’re speaking about holding it afterward.”

“Yes.”

“Against both.”

“They won’t both be in the same position afterward.”

“You don’t know that.”

“No. I know what I can move through the building. I know who repairs its fittings and which doors open only because someone has remembered to oil them. I know where the Prince has made his stores dependent on ours. I can make preparations look like work because they are work.”

“And when the fighting comes?”

“We choose where we can survive it.”

Sera looked at the rain. “Some people won’t.”

“I know.”

“Do you? Or do you know you’ll say their names afterward?”

Lyra turned from the window. For a moment she wanted Sera out of the room. The woman had brought a claim and now demanded to inspect the person expected to make it real.

“The Mercy Bell,” Lyra said. “Do you know the name?”

“No.”

She told her. She kept it short and plain: the prior preparations, the song, the dead, the sale, the compensation. She told her about the Grey Petrel too. Sera listened without taking notes.

“You stayed,” she said when Lyra finished.

“Yes.”

“And the man who did it is still your priest.”

“My teacher. The Cantor of the congregation.”

“Does that distinction help anyone who has to obey him?”

“They don’t have to.”

Sera’s eyes moved to the coiled rope beside the desk.

Lyra looked at it too. “I know they can come to believe otherwise.”

“Do they believe the same of you?”

“Some.”

The admission cost less than pretending would have. Lyra sat opposite her again.

“I won’t tell you that makes me fit to decide for everyone,” she said. “I’m telling you what I can do, and what I’ve done, because if you mean to work with me, you should know both.”

Sera leaned forward and rested her forearms on the table.

“Then we’ll need terms that survive your belief that you have a better reason.”

They called Tesset in to write. Lyra offered her the account of the Mercy Bell and Grey Petrel before beginning. Tesset said she already knew most of it from the books and wanted the rest written down later, in the same place as the compensation. Lyra agreed.

Sera’s first condition concerned the people recruited for the operation. They would know whether they were being hired to carry goods, guard a passage, or enter a room held by enemies. Wages already owed would not depend on joining. Families would receive the agreed provision even if the worker refused an additional task at the point of danger.

“Refused any task?” Lyra asked.

“An additional task.”

“If they refuse what they’ve agreed?”

“Then you may dismiss them from future work. You may not leave them below because they’ve become inconvenient.”

Corven had come upstairs while they spoke. He stood in the doorway listening. At that he said, “Put it in.”

Sera looked at him.

“I will,” Tesset said before Lyra could answer.

For Aria, they agreed: no one would destroy the crystal on an assumption about her wishes. They would seek communication, verify what could be verified, and make her preference part of every subsequent decision.

“Callista won’t like that,” Sera said.

“She already knows,” Lyra answered. “I need her help hearing. I won’t buy it by promising a death.”

Then they came to the files. Sera wanted evidence of the Court’s crimes made available to those who could use it against the Court. She would not agree to the collected secrets becoming the private means of compelling the same people again.

“Some of those files expose victims,” Lyra said.

“Then those people must have a say. Their injuries aren’t public property because we seize the room.”

“And evidence that would prevent an attack?”

“We can present it.”

“If presenting it exposes someone?”

“Then we ask them.”

“And if there isn’t time?”

Sera looked at her. “You’re already looking for the room in which the promise won’t apply.”

Lyra drew a breath. The new depth in her chest made it easy to hold the silence. She saw Sera notice her stillness and resist looking away.

“No one buys obedience with what we find,” Lyra said.

Tesset wrote it down exactly.

The charter would remain with Sera and witnesses of the revived Coin. It would support a public administration, with accounts that could be examined and representatives chosen beyond Lyra’s own following. Holding a doorway would not make its keeper the sole owner of the building.

“I’ll be supplying most of the money,” Lyra said.

“You’ll be paid what we agree,” Sera answered. “You won’t be buying every future answer.”

Corven sat on the chest by the window. He appeared to be enjoying the conversation. Lyra shot him a look. He did not bother to hide his smile.

They argued over reserves for most of the afternoon. That was easier. A reserve either existed or did not; a boat could be inspected; a promise to feed people could be reduced to bread arriving at a known door. Sera knew which sympathizers had money and which had only courage they had not yet tested. Lyra respected her more each time she refused to call those the same thing.

By evening they had the beginning of an agreement and a page of things that might prevent it from being carried out. Nobody called the page complete.

Sera read the conditions aloud. Lyra listened, following the words in Tesset’s hand.

She signed beneath them.

After Sera left, Corven took the paper to the window and read it slowly. Tesset had gone home. The yard below was nearly empty, its planks dark with rain.

“You’ll need more than me,” he said.

“I know.”

“More than men like me.”

“I know that too.”

He nodded toward the page. “People who can keep a stair clear while everybody wants to use it. People who can pick up a stranger and go on carrying him when he starts fighting.”

“Neris knows how to get frightened divers through a narrow place.”

“Ask her.”

“I will.”

“Don’t put her name down first.”

Lyra looked at him. He held her gaze, then laid the agreement on the desk.

“What about Maeron?”

“He knows more of the lower water than any of us.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

She turned the question over. She could keep him ignorant and lose what he could offer. She could tell him everything and give him time to decide her safety required another arrangement. Neither prospect let her keep him as the man who had brought pears to a lesson and waited while she remembered how to heal.

“I’ll tell him what he needs to do,” she said. “I’ll watch what he does.”

Corven looked tired.

“Will that be enough?”

“It will have to begin there.”

He picked up the eel’s vessel from the shelf. The little creature had become hard to see even when the lamp was behind it. Its outline seemed to sink farther into the water than the pot was deep. Corven turned the vessel gently, checking the animal, then wrapped it in a cloth.

At the door he stopped. “When we go below, find the way up first.”

“I’ve heard you say that.”

“You’ll hear it again.”

He left. Lyra sat alone with the signed agreement. Her hands lay pale against the wood, the spiral cut already closed beneath a thin new skin.

Katerina’s message came after the lamps had been trimmed. It contained no name and no greeting: a time, a service entrance, a warning to bring only the people who must hear. Lyra read it twice, then placed it beside the conditions concerning Aria.

She took out a fresh sheet for Callista. Before she began, she went downstairs and told the night carter that the first departure would need to wait. He asked how long. She gave him an hour and paid for it immediately.

Upstairs again, she wrote: There may be a way to see her. Come in the morning. Bring what you remember of her voice.

Part Four Preparations for the Festival

Chapter 23 The woman in the crystal

Katerina brought three pairs of gloves and made them try each pair with their eyes shut.

“Pick up the needle.”

Lyra felt along the edge of the table. The first pair had been lined with felt. She could distinguish a crease in the cloth beneath her hand, but the needle might have been anywhere. When she opened her eyes, it lay between her first and second fingers.

“Those are for the men who carry it,” Katerina said.

Callista was already pulling on the thinnest pair. Her little finger did not bend with the others. She caught its end between her teeth and dragged the glove into place.

“The middle ones,” Katerina told her.

“I need to feel.”

“You need to keep your hand.”

“Both would be convenient,” Lyra said. She tried the second pair. Stiff cloth had been sewn between two skins of leather; the seams stood out like the seams of cheap shoes. She found the needle, lifted it, and set it beside the others. “What does the cloth do?”

“Deadens sound. The strips are from the curtains around the crystal.”

“Does that make touching it safe?”

“No.”

Katerina put a little bowl on the table. It held wax softened in oil, smelling faintly of cloves. She showed them how to press a small amount inside the outer ear, leaving enough unclosed to hear an ordinary voice. Lyra leaned close to hear her whisper.

“The maintenance man will count the time. When his glass runs out, you leave. If you hear your own voice answering from somewhere else, you leave sooner. Don’t put your face against it. Don’t let her draw your blood.”

“Aria?” Callista asked.

“The crystal.”

“That is a distinction you might have made first.”

Katerina accepted the rebuke without looking up. She had cut an old leather belt into lengths, each long enough to wrap around a wrist. Now she pushed a length toward each of them.

Lyra left hers on the table.

“What is that for?”

“To pull you back without touching your skin.”

“Then the loose end stays in my hand.”

Katerina met her eyes. “Until you need somebody else to hold it.”

“Until I ask.”

After a moment Katerina nodded.

They were in the little room above the shop where Callista slept. Rain moved down the shutter in pale diagonal lines. Below them, someone was beating a carpet against the yard wall with a zeal which suggested a personal grievance. Every fourth blow caught the edge of the shutter and made the bowl of wax tremble.

Callista’s coat hung from a peg, its pockets sagging with things she would not leave unattended: a torn program, a comb with three teeth, the soft purse with no money in it. Lyra had watched her touch the purse twice since Katerina arrived.

“Have you taken any more?” she asked.

Callista’s hands stopped.

“No.”

“I need to know before we go.”

“And now you do.”

Katerina stood. “We have to be at the west court before the kitchen ash comes out. I can get three people through a service inspection. I cannot get three people through another one tomorrow.”

Lyra put the gloves inside her coat.

Callista did not move from the table. Her gaze had gone to the needle.

“What if she asks us?” she said.

Lyra knew what she meant. She waited anyway.

“What if she asks to die?”

“Then we will have heard her ask.”

“You’ll make another arrangement.”

“I don’t know what I’ll do.”

Callista looked up sharply, as though this were the first thing Lyra had said that afternoon in a language she understood.

“Neither do I,” she said.

The Opera House smelled of onions at its working entrance. A boy was tipping peelings into a cart while two kitchen women argued over whether fish sent back from a rehearsal supper could be made into something else. Above them, a plaster goddess reached from the pediment with one arm newly painted and one still gray with dirt.

Lyra had brought the salt-store returns. She knew the watchman who took them. He had once asked her to explain a deduction from his brother’s wages, and now, when she put her mark in the book, he moved the lamp closer without being asked.

“Late inspection,” he said.

“Late discrepancy.”

“Same thing, then.”

He admitted Katerina with a glance at her seal. His glance at Callista lasted longer.

“My singer,” Katerina whispered.

The watchman stepped aside.

Inside, Callista brushed past Lyra hard enough to make the papers knock against the wall. She said nothing until they reached the hoist room.

“Your singer.”

“It was something he would accept.”

“I’m sure it was.”

The freight hoist stood at the center of the room, a square timber platform with iron corner posts and a sliding rail. A padded wheeled cradle occupied most of it. On an ordinary day Lyra would have asked who had left a conveyance in the path of the flour. Tonight she looked at the cradle’s six wheels, the width of its handles, the clamps fixed to its sides.

Katerina saw her looking.

“Not this way,” she said.

They took the working stair. One flight down, the warmth of the kitchens disappeared. A cross-passage door separated the lower hoist landing from the salt stores. Katerina held it while Lyra and Callista passed, then made sure its wooden stop sat firmly in the jamb. The lock could be opened from the stores’ side; someone coming back from the hoist would need it left open. Beyond the stores, a narrower corridor turned beneath the stage. Lyra could hear feet overhead, then an isolated stroke of a drum. Somebody shouted for the feet to do it again.

At the corridor’s end a man sat by a curtain with a cup pressed between his palms. White scales crowded the base of his neck beneath the collar. He put the cup down when he saw Katerina.

“You said two.”

“I said the inspection and assistance.”

“You said two.”

Katerina took a folded paper from inside her sleeve. He read it, looked at Callista, and read it again.

“Half a glass,” he said.

“A full one.”

“She was drawn this morning.”

Lyra felt Callista move beside her.

Katerina’s whisper sharpened. “Then you should have kept to the interval.”

“I keep to what comes downstairs.”

He took a brass key from a cord, opened the door behind the curtain, and stood away from it. Lyra passed close enough to smell the sweet tea in his cup.

The Hex-Vault was smaller than she had expected. No room should have needed to be large enough for this. There were shelves, a washstand, two stools, a bucket beneath a tap. Cloth hung between the walls and the central crystal. The folds drank the lamp’s light. What remained lay on a woman’s cheek.

Aria was standing, or had been when she was caught. One foot lay ahead of the other. Her right arm was raised with the hand turned outward. The other hand had closed on empty air beside her waist. Her mouth was open.

Fine lines ran through the crystal, meeting and separating. Some ended at metal fittings along its base. A narrow silver channel carried moisture into a bottle no longer than Lyra’s thumb.

Callista took two steps and stopped.

“Aria.”

The sound inside the chamber was almost below hearing. Lyra felt it first in the roots of her teeth. It rose, thinned, and fell away. Her own voice had become strange to her since the feast, distant even in a small room. This was different. There was effort in it. Someone was doing something over and over without enough room to finish.

Callista began to sing.

Only three notes. She stopped at the fourth, put a hand over her mouth, and tried again. The sound that answered was too faint for Lyra to distinguish from the ringing in her ears.

“She used to make us begin there,” Callista said. “Before the words. She said you could hear where a breath was going to fail.”

“Can she hear you?”

“I don’t know.”

Lyra approached the crystal without touching it. Cold settled beneath her breastbone. She put on the gloves, flexed her fingers, and spoke toward Aria’s raised hand.

“My name is Lyra. I move goods for the man who keeps you here. I learned what those goods were. I’ve come to ask what you want us to do.”

Something struck the cloth at the back of the room. A soft, snapping sound. Lyra looked, but nothing moved.

Callista had begun crying without covering her face. She wiped each tear away as it came, impatient with it.

“Can you hold one note?” Lyra asked. “If you can hear us.”

The low sound gathered. It lasted long enough for Lyra to count three breaths and then broke.

Katerina went to the curtain. The man outside had turned the glass.

“One for yes,” Lyra said. “Two, separated, for no. If you cannot answer, we will not decide what silence means.”

Callista came beside her. “Let me try.”

Lyra moved back half a pace.

“Did I sing with you?” Callista asked.

One note.

“Was my name Elian?”

Two, with a gap that hurt to listen through.

“Was my name Callista?”

One.

Callista’s shoulders shook. She pressed her wrist against her mouth until the movement stopped.

“Have I brought my little brother?”

No answer.

“Callista.”

“I haven’t got a brother.”

“That may be more than she can follow.”

They waited. Behind the crystal Aria’s hair had spread in a dark curve. A strand crossed her eye. Lyra had an absurd desire to move it.

She changed places with Callista and held up her empty right hand.

“Can you see this hand?”

One note.

She put a copper coin on the gloved palm.

“Is there a coin in it now?”

One.

She closed her fist and opened it again, empty.

“Am I still holding the coin?”

Two.

They repeated it with Callista’s hand. On the second attempt the response came too slowly. Lyra lowered her arm and waited until the faint steady sound returned.

“We’re tiring her.”

“She’s tired already,” Callista said. “Ask.”

Lyra faced the crystal. Her breath made a little cloud and cleared.

“Do you want the drawing of your tears stopped?”

One note, stronger than before. The bottle beneath the silver channel trembled against its bracket.

“Do you want it continued?”

Two notes.

Callista’s gloved fingers closed on Lyra’s sleeve.

“Do you want us to break this?”

No answer.

Lyra felt the grip tighten.

“Ask properly,” Callista said.

“If breaking the crystal kills you,” Lyra said, “do you want us to break it?”

The sound fell so far that she thought it had ended. Then it returned once, stopped, and returned again.

Callista drew in a breath through her teeth.

“It’s me,” she said. “I wouldn’t leave you here. You know that. I wouldn’t let this go on.”

The crystal’s inner lines flashed. For a moment Lyra saw a second mouth laid over Aria’s, open at a different angle. She could not tell whether it belonged to the woman or to her reflection.

Two notes sounded again.

Katerina touched Callista’s elbow. Callista turned with such violence that her shoulder hit the padded wall. She pulled away, but Katerina had already released her.

“She heard us,” Lyra said.

“You heard something.”

“Yes.”

“You want her alive because you haven’t seen enough of this.”

“Then help me check.”

Callista looked from her to the crystal. Lyra let the silence last. The grains in the glass kept falling. Behind the door, a man waited to resume his work.

At last Callista drew herself upright.

“Aria,” she said. “Do you want to remain alive while we try to get you out?”

One note.

The note held. Callista bent toward it, one hand lifting. Lyra caught her wrist before it reached the crystal.

“Not bare.”

“I’m wearing—”

The glove had split along the side of the crooked finger. Lyra could see Callista’s pale skin. She turned the hand gently and showed her.

Katerina pulled a cloth from her belt. Together they wound it around the finger while the note dwindled. Callista stared at their hands.

“I thought it was the only thing left I could do for her.”

“There’s this,” Lyra said.

She went back to the crystal. Her knees had begun to shake. She was cold in a way she had not been since the naming, deep inside, where the air couldn’t reach.

“We will stop the harvesting. We will keep you alive while we find out how you can leave. I can’t promise I know how yet.”

One note sounded, very faintly.

Katerina’s hand came down on her shoulder.

“Now.”

Lyra looked at the little bottle. Two drops had gathered while they were speaking. A third lengthened at the lip of the channel and fell.

“Can we stop it now?”

Katerina shook her head.

The door opened. The maintenance man stood beyond the curtain with the empty glass.

“If you interfere with the fittings,” he said, “you are responsible for the consequence.”

“What is the consequence?”

He hesitated. Katerina’s hand tightened.

“We’re leaving,” she whispered.

Lyra went. At the door she turned once more. Aria’s raised hand was unchanged. The little bottle had begun filling before their arrival and would continue after they had gone.

In the kitchen court Callista sat on an overturned basket and was sick between her feet. Katerina held back her hair. Lyra brought a bucket of water from the pump, then another for the ground.

A kitchen woman came to see what had happened. Lyra told her one of them was ill. The woman looked at the water, the gloves, Katerina’s hand on Callista’s neck, and went indoors. She returned with a piece of coarse cloth.

“Don’t use your coat,” she said.

Callista took it. After a while she could sit upright. Her face had gone the color of the wall.

“You heard her say no,” Lyra said to Katerina.

“Yes.”

“You must say that when somebody asks.”

“I will.”

Callista stared at the cloth in her hands.

“They’ll say she’s afraid,” she said. “They’ll say she doesn’t understand what’s being done to her.”

“Then we tell them what we asked.”

Callista folded the cloth, unfolded it, and pressed it to her mouth again.

“The last time I sang that exercise, I was angry with her. She kept stopping me in the same place. I thought she wanted everybody to know I couldn’t do it.”

“Did you tell her?”

“No.”

Lyra sat beside her on the basket. The wood creaked under their combined weight. Katerina stood with the bucket, looking toward the door they had come through.

“Will I get to ask her?” Callista said.

“I don’t know.”

Callista leaned against her. Lyra put an arm around her and held it there while the kitchen women began carrying out the ash.

Chapter 24 The agreement above them

The fish had been ordered twice.

Lyra sat with the kitchen requisitions spread across her desk and a piece of cold bread in one hand. The first order was for the Festival supper, allowing for every occupied box. The second requested six portions of white fish, served without sauce, together with a particular bread and two bottles already held in the Court’s own cellar. There was no box number on that order. There was only a room: the little withdrawing chamber behind the seventh mirror.

She knew the bread. The Prince had broken a piece of it while discussing a delayed consignment, leaving the crumbs on a plate nobody else touched.

Katerina stood at the window. The lowered shutter cut across her shoulders. Lyra could see one gloved hand on the sill and the rain falling behind it.

“Tell me what happens in that room.”

“Sometimes they agree.”

“How long have you known?”

“About the meeting? Four days.”

“And the agreement?”

“There isn’t one yet.”

Lyra put down the bread.

Tesset had gone to the copying room to collect an invoice. Through the partition Lyra could hear her explaining to someone that the color of a string did not change the date a thing was due. Lyra was glad to hear her. She folded the second order small enough to fit beneath her palm.

“What is he offering?”

“Access to the coastal trade. A share of the new salt works. The end of certain interruptions.”

“Those are his to offer.”

Katerina turned from the window.

“The independent warehouses would be registered through a joint office. Yours among them. The Court would appoint the inspectors. The Prince would guarantee their safety.”

“And my workers?”

“Existing obligations honored.”

“By whom?”

“By the office.”

Lyra waited. Katerina looked at the edge of the desk.

“Some of the crews would be reassigned. The people who have changed most are considered useful for work beyond the breakwater.”

“Considered by whom?”

“You know by whom.”

Lyra stood so abruptly that her chair struck the wall. From the copying room Tesset asked whether she was all right. Lyra said yes, too loudly, and heard her own altered voice return from the corners.

Katerina did not move.

“Has Maeron been told?” Lyra asked.

“He has been asked what the crews could endure.”

“When?”

“Yesterday.”

She wanted to go to him immediately. She could see him clearing a space beside his cup, inclining his head to listen. He would let her finish. He would answer the question he judged most important, and she would have to haul each of the others back between them.

Outside, a cart wheel caught in the broken paving and the driver began to swear.

“Why are you telling me?”

Katerina looked up.

“Because I went into the vault with you.”

“Is that enough?”

“No.” Katerina put her hands on the back of the chair. “It is what happened.”

Lyra freed the chair leg from the mat and sat down. She hated Katerina watching her fumble with it.

“Will he still use Aria?”

“If the meeting fails. If it succeeds and he sees a better opportunity. I have been told to put the carriage ready.”

“The one in the hoist.”

“That is the outer cradle. The crystal has its own frame. They join them in the vault.”

Lyra drew a clean sheet toward her.

“Sit down,” she said.

Sera brought two people to the meeting and made Lyra admit them before she would put anything on the table.

The first was Berris, a broad woman in a work coat with a little comb tucked behind one ear. Lyra knew her by sight from the Opera House, where she maintained scenery and had once refused to let six men carry a painted palace through a door built for a real person. The second was a narrow man who copied petitions for people unable to pay the established scriveners. Sera introduced him as a witness and did not offer his name.

“He’s putting his name to anything?” Lyra asked.

“When we’ve agreed what it is.”

“Mine has been on things for months.”

“Yes,” Sera said. “That is part of the difficulty.”

They met over a sailmaker’s shop nearer the theater than the loft where Lyra had spoken with Iven. The sailmaker’s wife had agreed to lend it until supper. Below them, needles clicked in their thimbles. One of the women sang a verse and stopped halfway through a word; someone else supplied it from the far side of the room.

Lyra explained the proposed settlement. She had meant to be brief. When she reached the divers, she found she was naming them individually. Berris listened without interruption, turning her comb between her fingers.

“Are they being taken before the Festival?” Sera asked.

“No.”

“Can they leave now?”

“Yes.”

“Will you tell them?”

“I’ll tell them there is a threat.”

“You’ll tell them the threat may be met by leaving.”

Lyra looked at her. “If every person who can help goes, the ones who can’t will be here without them.”

“If they stay because you have concealed their chance of going, they haven’t helped you.”

Berris set the comb down.

“My sister works in wardrobe,” she said. “She can’t carry her husband down three flights. I can’t move him and be at the theater. What will you do for her?”

There was no accusation in the question. That made it harder to answer.

“A cart and two people tomorrow,” Lyra said. “Somewhere to sleep outside the Silk Ward. She can take him there herself.”

“Without losing her place?”

“If I have any say in who has a place afterward.”

Berris nodded once. “I’ll ask her.”

The petition writer sharpened his pen. Sera unfolded a drawing of the Opera House. It was an old plan, stained along one edge, with a stair that had since been walled in. Lyra marked the change. Berris marked three more.

“You get people into the Gallery from here,” Berris said, tapping a service landing above the dress circle. “The Court’s clerks come down the other stair. That door is usually locked from their side.”

“Can you open it?”

“I can remove its hinge pins. You’d be surprised how often I have to do it for a wardrobe.”

Sera put a finger on a little square beneath the east offices.

“The charter was put in this strongroom with seized titles. I have a man who helped catalogue them. He can identify the bundle. He isn’t fighting anyone.”

“Neither should he,” Lyra said.

“He will have two people with him. They remain with him until he is outside.”

“And afterward?”

Sera looked at her. “Afterward we take the charter to people who can recognize it. With witnesses to how we recovered it.”

“How quickly can you get it recognized?”

“The recorder will come the next morning, if his daughter believes he can get home safely. The others will need more than his word.”

Lyra studied the map. The table had a deep cut across it, made by a knife that had gone through whatever the sailmaker was trimming. Her finger followed the cut into the drawn outline of the stage.

“The administration must have money to open the doors that morning,” she said. “I can provide that.”

“For how long?”

She gave a sum. Berris’s comb stopped turning.

“That pays the theater people too?”

“It pays the people we need to keep it standing.”

Berris’s expression changed.

Lyra heard what she had said. She rubbed her palm against the table.

“Everybody owed wages through the night,” she corrected. “Then we agree who has work. Before anyone is asked to begin it.”

The witness wrote that down.

Sera reached across the plan.

“And Aria?”

“We stop the harvesting. We keep the crystal intact. She has refused to have it broken.”

“Who heard?”

“Katerina, Callista, and I. We can show you how to ask.”

“I’ll need to hear.”

“I know.”

For the first time that evening Sera relaxed her shoulders. She had been sitting as though her coat pinched under the arms.

“Then we can begin,” she said.

At Cooper’s Cut the tide left a strip of greasy steps below the landing. A woman in a patched blue coat was pushing a dead rat off the lowest one with the end of her boot. She watched it float away before turning to Lyra.

“You’ve come at the wrong height.”

“I can climb.”

“So can a wet cat. I don’t lend those an evening either.”

Her name was Odel, and she spoke for the Ragman at this landing. Lyra had waited three days for this meeting. Now the woman walked uphill without offering a hand. Lyra followed her past stacked fish baskets into a narrow court where laundry hung between houses. Two children were trying to retrieve a ball from a gutter with a spoon tied to a stick.

Odel took the stick, reached up, and knocked the ball down. She gave the stick back and told them to wash the spoon before their mother saw it.

Inside her door, a man lay with his leg propped on a folded blanket. He nodded at Lyra and went on mending the handle of a basket.

“The Cut,” Odel said. “You want it open.”

“For our boats. Closed to the Prince’s.”

“He’s got more boats.”

“I need one tide.”

Odel poured hot water over leaves already used once. Her kitchen smelled of damp wool and garlic. Lyra took the cup she offered. The warmth was almost painful against her hands.

“And when his people come through these houses to get behind us?”

“We don’t pursue anyone into the houses.”

“I asked about his people.”

“I can put a boat with loaded chain across the narrow mouth. It will stop the large craft long enough for yours to deal with the small ones. My people can bring the chain and leave.”

“Loaded with what?”

“Scrap iron. Wrapped, so it won’t announce itself every time the boat moves.”

Odel looked toward the man with the basket. He shrugged without looking up.

“The old salt pier has pilings enough,” he said. “Near one’s rotten.”

“We use the second,” Lyra said.

“You’ve looked.”

“I’ve paid men to unload there. They complain.”

The man smiled briefly.

“I can supply the chain,” Odel said. “One tide’s hire. After that it comes back, whether you’re finished with it or not.”

“Put it in the advance.”

Odel sat opposite her. “No armed men through my court. No strange congregation put in my neighbors’ rooms because you’ve called them refugees. No fighting from the windows.”

“If people come out injured—”

“They can come out injured. They leave their weapons at the bottom step.”

“Corven won’t leave his chain.”

“Then he doesn’t come up.”

Lyra drank the tea. It tasted mostly of the cup.

“Agreed.”

“The landing stays open until the water covers the third white mark. After that we draw off. I won’t have my people caught under the eastern arch when the tide turns.”

It was less time than she wanted. She asked for more. Odel refused, then got up and took her outside to show her what the rising water did at the arch. A net had caught there, wound so tightly around the stone that it looked like part of the masonry. Two floats shivered beneath the surface.

“My cousin,” Odel said. “Last winter. I told him the same thing.”

Lyra looked at the net, then at the white tide marks painted on the quay.

“The third mark,” she said.

Odel held out her hand. Lyra put the advance into it. The woman weighed the purse once, opened it, and counted in front of her.

When Lyra returned, Tesset was still at the office. She had put a blanket over her knees and was adding a column by the light of a single candle.

“I thought you were going home.”

“I was. Then I found what you intend to spend.”

Lyra took off her wet coat. Her sleeves clung to her wrists.

“There’s enough.”

“For this.”

“Yes.”

“And after this?”

Lyra hung the coat where it would drip onto the stone instead of the mat. The Opera House could earn. Its stores could be let. A restored Coin could arrange trade free of some existing charges. None of these things had happened.

“If we hold it, there will be work.”

Tesset looked at her over the candle.

“Then write that where you’ve written expected receipts. I can’t add a sentence to a sum.”

Lyra sat beside her and crossed out the figure.

They worked until the candle guttered. Tesset went home carrying two days’ pay advanced in case the office became inaccessible. Lyra stayed to seal the remaining money into separate bags: wages, boats, food, treatment. There was less in the last bag than she wanted. She added the little purse from her own drawer and counted it again.

Chapter 25 Who has agreed

Vessa’s boat lay open from end to end. She had taken off the little shelter where the helmsman stood in bad weather, leaving pale rectangles on the wood. The dismantled boards were stacked on the landing, guarded by her daughter and a dog too old to get up when Lyra approached.

“You said a covered boat,” Lyra said.

“I said I had a covered boat. You said you wanted room for a long cradle.”

“Can it go back?”

“Yes. After.”

Vessa was a short woman whose gray hair had been cut unevenly beneath her cap. She stood with one foot on the gunwale, watching Lyra look over the deck. The boat had carried Lyra’s rope and lamp oil through two interruptions at the larger quays. Lyra had once secured payment for a cargo Vessa’s customer claimed had arrived wet. They had emptied the sound barrels in front of him. Vessa still described the occasion when she wanted to make her daughter laugh.

Now she wanted six times her ordinary fee.

“That’s the price of keeping her here,” she said. “Whether you use her or not.”

“I understand a retainer.”

“Then understand this one.”

Lyra stepped aboard and the boat moved under her. She felt the water against its hull, a soft, steady pressure she had found harder to ignore since the feast. In the office she sometimes knew a rain barrel had been filled before she saw the water run off its rim.

“Three journeys,” she said. “People from the shore house before dusk. Equipment from Eel Stair. Then you wait at Cooper’s Cut.”

“Until the mark.”

“Until the mark.”

“And whoever gets in comes out at the other end. I don’t want six people put aboard and an argument halfway.”

Lyra put her hand on the gunwale. A splinter lifted beneath her thumb.

“What have you been told?”

“Enough to charge six times.”

Vessa’s daughter looked up from the dog.

“The same passage for every person,” Lyra said. “No additions on the water.”

“I meant your argument, Lady.”

The title sounded different here. No chorus held it up. Vessa said it as she might acknowledge a rank before getting on with the fare.

“If somebody wants to get off,” Lyra said, “you land them where it is safe.”

Vessa nodded. “Now we can talk about the money.”

Tesset had drawn two lines through a name on the crew sheet.

Lyra set the boat agreement beside it. “What happened to Pevan?”

“He said no.”

“He was told it was a night delivery.”

“He was told there might be fighting. He said no.”

On the list Pevan had been assigned to the forward-left corner of the crystal carriage. He was twenty, broad through the shoulders, and could match another man’s pace without looking at his feet. Lyra had put him there because the turn outside the vault needed precisely that steadiness.

“Who told him?”

“I did.”

“We don’t know that there will be fighting.”

“We don’t know that there won’t.”

Lyra drew the sheet closer. Tesset kept one finger on it.

“We need him for that corner.”

“Then ask him.”

“I thought you had.”

“I have. He said no.”

Someone was laughing in the yard, a loud, delighted laugh that made Lyra want to shut the window. She put down the pen she had picked up.

“Does he understand what happens if we fail?”

“Do you want me to tell him he’ll be responsible?”

“No.”

Tesset removed her finger.

Lyra went outside. Pevan stood beside the water butt, washing a tar stain from his forearm with sand. He looked up and immediately stopped scrubbing.

“I’m sorry,” he said.

“Don’t begin there.”

“All right.”

“Why can’t you go?”

He glanced toward the office. Lyra disliked herself for hearing an accusation in that glance.

“My mother needs somebody to lift her. My sister does the rest. If I don’t come back, she can’t.”

“We can arrange help.”

“They don’t know the men you’d send.”

“Women, then.”

He let the sand fall from his hand. “I don’t want to be there.”

At the window above them, Tesset closed the shutters. She did it quietly. Lyra wished she had slammed them.

Pevan’s forearm was rubbed red. A little crescent of tar remained near the elbow, where he could not reach it comfortably.

“You keep your work here,” Lyra said. “If there is no work here for a few days, you have your wages through them.”

He searched her face.

She took a piece of rag from the edge of the butt, dipped it in the little pot of oil kept for the purpose, and gave it to him.

“Sand only takes your skin off.”

“Yes, Lady.”

She went back inside before he could thank her for letting him decline.

The practice load was made of paving blocks, sacks of wet sand, and an old beam. Together they weighed a little more than Katerina’s estimate of the crystal with its inner frame. Lyra had paid to borrow the blocks. The mason who owned them stood in the corner eating an apple and asking who would pay for any chipped edges.

“Me,” Lyra said for the third time.

“I only ask because they’re dressed.”

Berris lifted the rear of the frame. “If you mention their education again, I’ll put them on your foot.”

The mason moved farther into the corner.

They had marked the vault passage on the floor with chalk, then built the awkward corner from stacked empty crates. The first attempt failed before the front wheels reached it. One wheel caught the raised edge of a floorboard and the left handle swung across the chalk line into the crates. A stack fell with a crash. The mason caught his apple against his coat.

In the actual passage that movement would have driven the crystal against stone.

“Nobody move,” Corven said.

The four carriers held. He crouched beside the wheel, looked at the pin, and gestured for them to lower the load onto the blocks prepared beside it.

It took longer than Lyra liked. They were strong workers, but the front pair could not see what the back pair were doing, and one side came down too quickly. Berris swore at herself.

“Again,” Lyra said.

“After we change it,” Corven told her.

He had taken off his coat. The chain lay coiled in a bowl of salt water, wrapped so the mason could not read its links. Without it, and with his sleeves rolled above his elbows, he looked like one of the men whose dangerous work Lyra had spent years making less wasteful. The resemblance did not comfort her.

“The front wheel must turn independently,” Berris said. “And those handles need to fold.”

“Can you do it?”

“Not with the fittings on this.”

“We have four days.”

“Then I need the fittings tomorrow.”

Katerina stood just inside the door. She had been watching the attempt with both hands in her coat.

“The real cradle has fixed wheels,” she whispered.

“Then we don’t use it,” Lyra said.

“They expect to see it.”

“We use its frame over ours.”

Berris looked from one woman to the other. “Can somebody bring me the measurements of the thing we’re actually moving? All of them?”

Katerina took out a folded strip of cloth. Marks had been made along it in three colors. She handed it over.

Lyra hadn’t known Katerina had brought it. When their eyes met, she let herself smile.

They worked until the light failed. By then the load could turn, but it took six people, one guiding the rear, one lifting the short removable rail across the threshold. The sixth person needed to go ahead. That left nobody to watch behind.

“I do that,” Corven said.

“You’re holding the salt passage.”

“Then someone else watches behind.”

“Who?”

He looked at Lyra. She felt the same impatience as she had with Pevan. One person to watch behind. Surely she could find one person.

“We can close the door behind you,” she said.

“And if the way ahead is lost?”

“We reopen it.”

“From which side?”

She looked at the drawing. The cross-passage door lay between the salt stores and the lower hoist landing, beyond the bend Corven would hold. Its lock opened from the stores’ side. Once the carriage passed it, a shut door would leave six people and Aria cut off from Corven, facing whatever happened at the hoist.

Berris leaned across her and drew a bar beside the door.

“A removable stop. We put it in the jamb. It stays there until we know nobody has to come back.”

“Then they follow,” Lyra said.

“Yes,” Corven told her.

He was patient. She wanted him less patient.

“How far can you keep them back?”

“To the bend. After that, they can come at me from both stairs.”

“Then you hold the bend until the carriage reaches the hoist. Berris’s sixth leaves the stop in place. If the hoist is lost, everyone comes back to you and through the salt stores to the north freight stair.”

“And the woman in the crystal?”

Lyra looked at the load on the floor.

“We build rails down the north freight stair. Into the old measuring room beside the canal landing.”

Berris closed her eyes. The mason stopped eating.

“It’s three short flights,” Lyra said. “With landings. The room’s floor stands above the high-water line. We can keep her there if the boats can’t take her. You’ve lowered painted horses larger than this.”

“The horses don’t kill half the audience if I drop them.”

“No.”

Berris opened her eyes. “I need more rope.”

Callista came to the second rehearsal carrying a bundle of folded paper. She waited until the load had made the turn, then laid the papers on Lyra’s table.

Programs. Old ones, creased and softened. Lyra recognized names from songs she had heard in houses where the singers themselves would never have been admitted.

“This is the order they used when Aria last performed there,” Callista said. “They’ve put three of the same songs into the Festival.”

“What does that tell us?”

“That whoever arranged it knows what they are doing. This one ends without the resolving note. The next begins where it ought to have resolved. You can hold an audience like that without their knowing why they haven’t moved.”

“Will it help her sing?”

“I don’t know. It will make it easy to hear her.”

Lyra looked toward the practice frame. Berris was lying on her back beneath it, tapping a wheel pin out with a hammer. There had been a time when Lyra could hear an unfamiliar threat and imagine asking someone better equipped to deal with it. Now every unfamiliar threat came accompanied by a question about how many people she could spare.

The musicians would notice a change to the order, Callista said. They would have to be told to stop by someone they trusted.

“Berris knows the stage manager.”

Callista shook her head. “They’ll trust the man who pays them if he’s standing behind the person you send.”

They worked out a different interruption. The scenery for the second song required two long painted screens. Berris could leave a locking pin in one track. An ordinary delay: the musicians would repeat the interlude while the stagehands fixed it. That would give Lyra’s people time if the vault work ran late. It would also trap Berris’s own crew onstage under the eyes of the Court.

Berris agreed when asked. She did not smile about it.

After the others left, Callista put a finger on one of the old programs.

“Which of these did you hear?”

Lyra pointed. “I don’t remember the words.”

“Neither did the first man I heard sing it. He kept a copy behind his wine.”

Lyra hummed what she could remember. Her altered voice took the last note somewhere she had not intended. She stopped, but Callista was already tapping the beat on the table.

“Try it slower.”

She did. Callista joined her quietly, keeping the phrase steady when Lyra began to hurry. At the end they held the same note for a moment. Lyra could feel it through the wood beneath her fingers.

“Better,” Callista said.

“Better than the man with the wine?”

“I would need to hear you after the same amount.”

Lyra laughed. Callista smiled and tapped the beginning again. They sang it twice more before Lyra tried to return the program.

“Keep it. You can get it right next time.”

Lyra folded it carefully and put it inside her coat. Then she began wrapping the colored cloth measurements around a short ruler so they would not crease.

“The files,” Callista said.

“We know the way to them.”

“You know what I asked.”

“Yes.”

“Say it.”

Lyra put down the ruler.

“The Court’s means of keeping people must be destroyed.”

Callista watched her. “The letters. The pictures. The names of people they can threaten. All of it.”

“We have to let people out before we set a room alight.”

“Then you carry it out and burn it somewhere else.”

“There may be evidence of what they did.”

“There’s evidence in everybody who comes out.”

Lyra thought of a magistrate faced with a clerk who could no longer remember when a particular visit had happened, a servant afraid to speak, a dead person’s family with no money to keep asking. She also thought of the names she might find. People who could make the next week possible, if she could get them to help.

Callista stepped closer.

“You’ve heard her now. You know I was wrong about one thing. Don’t use it to make me wrong about everything.”

“I won’t.”

“The files?”

“We will destroy what they use to hold people.”

Callista stayed there until Lyra met her eyes.

“Don’t make me discover what you meant afterward,” she said.

They settled the signal on the last afternoon of rehearsal. No bell could be trusted in a theater. No shouted phrase would travel unchanged through three floors. Lyra took a length of white cord and tied three blue knots into it, spaced to be felt in a closed hand.

“Until this arrives, each of you does the work you came to do,” she said. “If we’re stopped, if I cannot get away, if a meeting takes longer than we thought, you don’t begin on the strength of a noise.”

Berris took the cord, felt the knots, and passed it to Katerina.

“After the third interlude,” Lyra went on, “if there is no cord, you leave by the working entrances. Take the people assigned to you. Don’t wait for me.”

Corven looked down at the table.

“I mean that,” she said.

“I heard you.”

There were three pieces of cord. Berris’s runner would carry one up the working stair to Sera. Another would go to Katerina beside the salt stores. Corven would receive the third at the bend outside the vault. Each bearer had been named; each had agreed. Tesset had made Lyra read those names aloud.

When the meeting ended, Lyra kept the cords. She tested every knot with her eyes closed, as she had tested the gloves, then put them inside the small pocket sewn against her ribs.

Chapter 26 The refuge below

Store Six had once held cheeses.

The smell had survived three changes of use, two floods, and the Prince’s attempt to cover it with salt. Lyra remembered complaining when she first took possession. Now Marda stood in the doorway with a basket on her arm and said it reminded her of somewhere she had lived as a girl.

“Was that a recommendation?” Lyra asked.

“No. They had mice.”

There were no mice here. The room stood one level below the salt stores, reached by a short flight that turned twice around the back of the freight stair. At the bottom, a raised path ran along the wall before descending three steps to the main floor. Water had left a dark line halfway up the lowest step.

Maeron waited beneath a lamp with a child’s shoe in his hand. The child was explaining why it had gone into a flour bin.

“I was keeping it safe.”

“From what?”

“The other one.”

Maeron considered this. He looked up when Lyra came in and smiled at her over the child’s head.

“You’ve come in time to judge a difficult question.”

“The shoe goes on the foot.”

“That was my first suggestion.”

The child took it and ran to a woman spreading blankets beside the wall. There were already a dozen people in the chamber. Lyra had expected four men measuring for beds.

“How long have they been here?”

“Since this morning,” Maeron said. “There was room in the cart.”

“The cart was for timber.”

“It carried timber too.”

She looked at the piles of blankets, the kettle, the little bundle of clothes tied inside an adult’s coat. Marda had stopped beside her. Beyond the lamp a young man was hanging cloth over an opening in the wall.

“We haven’t inspected the exit.”

“I’ve inspected it.”

“We said together.”

Maeron gave the hanging cloth a gentle tug to straighten it. “Then we’ll do it together.”

He didn’t sound annoyed. She could have answered annoyance.

Corven came down the stair carrying a lantern and a length of measuring line. He had left his eel at the shore house with Neris; a fight had broken out between two children over whose turn it was to feed it, and he had entrusted it to neither. Lyra had heard the argument while leaving. For a moment she wanted to go back and settle that instead.

“The north return stair first,” she said.

The route rose dry from the back of Store Six. This was the narrow return stair, separate from the broad freight stair they meant to use for the crystal. An adult could carry a child up its first flight without turning sideways. Above, it joined a landing off the working stair between the salt level and the west receiving court. A person leaving the refuge did not have to pass the Hex-Vault or the stage.

At the top a door had been barred from the other side.

Maeron looked at the bar through a crack beside the hinge.

“For security,” he said.

“Remove it.”

“The Court uses that landing.”

“Then put a watch on it. The bar comes off.”

Corven went around by the main stair. They waited while his footsteps crossed overhead. A scrape of wood, a brief exchange with someone Lyra could not see, and the door opened.

A stocky young follower stood behind him. His name was Jorad. He had been helping Maeron bring people from the shore house. Salt filmed the seams of his coat; when he breathed, his collar moved although his chest scarcely seemed to.

“I was told to keep it secure.”

“From below?” Lyra asked.

He looked past her to Maeron.

“From strangers,” Maeron said.

Lyra held out her hand for the bar. Jorad gave it to her. She placed it against the wall on the refuge side, where anyone using the stair could reach it.

“This stays here. Nobody needs permission to leave by this door.”

“The Lady has decided,” Maeron told Jorad.

The young man bowed his head. She disliked the satisfaction she felt at seeing him obey.

They brought up a loaded handcart next. Its handles scraped on the lower turn. Corven and Jorad carried it between them, but they could not have done so with someone in it. They tried a stretcher. It cleared the turn with a little room to spare when the rear bearer lowered his hands.

“We need two here,” Lyra said. “One at the bottom. One at the turn. Not in a cupboard.”

Marda wrote the instruction on the back of a flour label. She had begun making her own list as soon as the bar appeared.

At the upper landing they met a wardrobe woman carrying a sack of stockings. She looked at the stretcher, then at the four people testing it, and shifted her sack onto the other shoulder.

“Anybody hurt?”

“Not yet,” Marda said.

The woman wished them luck and went on.

At the far end of Store Six, the floor rose to a maintenance walk overlooking the canal chamber. The air there was colder. Lyra could feel the enclosed water behind the stone before they reached it.

An iron inspection gate occupied an opening above a low sill. Its upper edge vanished into a slot in the masonry. A rope passed from the gate over a pulley to a small counterweight, reducing the effort needed to lift it. The weight was not enough to hold it open. A toothed catch did that, worked by a hand lever on the room side.

Beyond the gate, a wheel stood on a short projecting shaft. Turning it raised the sluice in the wall separating Store Six’s lower channel from the tidal basin. A narrow wooden plank allowed a maintainer to climb through the inspection opening and reach the outer fittings when the water was low.

Corven lifted the gate. The catch clicked four times. He let it take the weight, then reached through the opening to turn the wheel.

Water stirred beneath the plank.

“Close it,” Lyra said.

He did. The movement stopped. She could still feel the weight of the basin beyond the wall.

Maeron leaned beside her.

“With the opening controlled, the room can remain comfortable for everyone. The upper walk stays dry. Those who need the water have it.”

“How fast does it fill?”

“That depends on the tide.”

“Now.”

Corven looked toward her. She nodded. He marked the position of the wheel with chalk, opened it a quarter turn, and counted while a thin sheet of water entered the lower channel. The channel ran along the outer wall, separated from the main floor by a lip of stone. At this height the water would stay within it. With the sluice fully open on the rising tide, it would cross the lip and spread through the sleeping space.

Lyra asked him to close it again.

“Who has the wheel?”

“Jorad and I,” Maeron said.

“Marda as well.”

Marda looked up from her label. “I should hope so.”

Maeron smiled. “Of course.”

Corven showed her how to lift the gate and release its catch. The first time Marda let it fall too quickly. Its lower rail struck the sill with a noise that brought everyone in the room to silence.

A child began to cry. Marda called an apology.

“Hold the rope as you release,” Corven said. “Let it travel through your hand. Otherwise anything under it stays under it.”

He looked at Lyra when he said that. She had been about to reach through for the wheel while he demonstrated.

“Show me again.”

He did. She worked the lever herself, felt the gate’s weight pull against the rope, and lowered it carefully. The metal met stone with a dull click.

A rack beside the opening held a boat hook, a short salvage hook with a worn wooden handle, and an iron rake for clearing debris from the sluice mouth. The short hook had a notch near its ferrule where someone had struck it against something harder than rope.

“These stay here,” Corven said. “People borrow them and forget.”

Marda wrote another line.

The second exit was farther along the maintenance walk, beyond a low door that opened onto the canal steps. At low water a shallow boat could put its bow under the arch. At higher water the top of the arch left too little room for a seated passenger. Lyra went down the steps until the water lay just below her boots.

Across the basin she could see the white marks at Cooper’s Cut. The third mark lay above her head.

“This one closes before the Cut,” she said.

“By half an hour, sometimes less,” Corven replied.

“Paint a line here. Where it stops being usable. Anyone can see it. No guessing.”

He marked the stone with chalk. Tomorrow he would return with paint.

Maeron stood above them in the low doorway. The lamplight touched his face and left his body in shadow.

“It’s a good place,” he said. “Better than the rooms they have been trying to endure.”

For several of the followers that was true. Lyra had watched them relax as they came down. A woman who slept badly in the shore house had lain on a blanket here and fallen asleep before taking off her coat. Lyra’s own shoulders had loosened in the enclosed air. She could have stayed down here. That was no reason to stop asking about the stair.

“We keep the north return stair open,” she said.

“Yes.”

“And when this is over, they choose where they live.”

Maeron stepped aside to let her pass.

“When this is over,” he said, “we may have more room to offer them.”

They ate beside the water channel because the tables had not yet arrived. Marda passed bread and a pot of onions cooked until they were sweet. The child with the shoe fell asleep against her mother while still holding a crust.

Maeron gave Lyra the bowl with the unchipped rim. She noticed because he had been doing it since the first month she came to the shore house. He never mentioned it. There had always simply been an undamaged bowl at the place where he expected her to sit.

“You’re tired,” he said.

“So is everyone.”

“Everyone isn’t here.”

She broke her bread. The onions smelled good, but she found she wanted the colder food at the bottom of the pot.

“The Prince asked what our crews could endure.”

Maeron continued eating. “He asks that often.”

“He means to put them beyond the breakwater under another office.”

“He means to put every useful thing under an office of his choosing.”

“What did you tell him?”

“That they have exceeded what he thought possible.”

“You might have told him they weren’t his.”

“I believe he knows.”

“Does he know you know?”

Maeron put down his bowl. Around them, conversations continued. A man was explaining to Marda that his wife would come after she collected a sewing frame; Marda was telling him she would find somebody to help carry it.

“I have no intention of giving them to him,” Maeron said.

“Then help me keep them able to refuse.”

“I have been doing that since before we met.”

She looked at him. His face was familiar in this light, with the lines beside his mouth deeper than they appeared by day. She remembered the practice wound closing beneath her hands, his pleasure when she had gone back to tell him she could do it alone.

“I know,” she said.

He reached for his bowl again.

She put her hand over it.

“I need your promise about the door.”

“You have it.”

“No bars on the other side. No one held because they are frightened or changed. No rites required before they can leave.”

Maeron looked at her hand. She hadn’t meant to use force, but the bowl had begun to pale beneath her fingers. A film of cold spread across its glaze.

“You have my promise that I will keep them safe,” he said.

“The door, Maeron.”

A little silence opened between the neighboring conversations.

“The door stays available,” he said. “No one will be required to perform a rite to use it.”

She removed her hand. He picked up the bowl and warmed it between his palms, though his hands were no warmer than hers.

Marda followed her up the stair with the empty pot.

At the landing she stopped Lyra by touching her sleeve. Through the open doorway came a breeze from the court, warm with the smell of baking pastry. Marda held her face toward it, then withdrew a little, as though the air hurt.

“They brought bed frames,” she said.

“I saw.”

“And chests. The little shelves from the chapel. Jorad says there won’t be a need to carry everything back.”

“Jorad doesn’t decide that.”

“Jorad decides who gets a hand with the carrying.”

Lyra leaned against the wall. A headache had begun behind her left eye. She had promised to meet Berris before the woman went home, and the boats still needed the new loading order.

“I’ll have porters available afterward. Separate from his people.”

Marda studied her.

“You can tell them that,” Lyra added.

“I will.”

“Do you want to stay here?”

“For a few nights. It’s easier on my chest.”

“Then stay.”

Marda shook her head, smiling tiredly.

“I was telling you. I wasn’t asking.”

Lyra let her pass. At the top of the stair Corven waited with the lantern. He had put a fresh wick in it while they spoke.

“Good exit?” she asked.

“While it stays open.”

She took the lamp from him and went out into the kitchen court.

Chapter 27 The yard burns

The first man came for the buckets.

Lyra saw him through the office window carrying two in each hand. She thought he was taking them to the landing, where a barrel had split that morning and left the boards slick with fish oil. She might have called to ask whether someone had found the sand. Instead Tesset put a revised list in front of her and said that three of Berris’s people could not read.

“They know which package is theirs.”

“Until someone changes the wrapping.”

“Then mark them with shapes.”

Tesset took the list back. Lyra looked through the window again. The man had gone. So had the remaining buckets beneath the pump.

She stood.

Smoke came under the store door in a flat dark line. For a moment it looked like water. Then the line lifted and the latch began to rattle from the other side.

Lyra ran into the passage.

“Open it!” someone shouted.

The latch was too hot to hold. She wrapped her sleeve around it, lifted, and pulled. The door came only a hand’s breadth before something stopped it. A bolt had been driven through the lower hinge from outside the store.

“Back from it.”

She heard coughing. Somebody struck the door from within.

“Back!”

She put her shoulder to the gap and pushed sideways. Nothing yielded. The smoke thickened against her face. Through it she could feel only movement and weight. It told her no more than the sounds of a struggle behind a wall.

Corven caught her by the coat and pulled her away.

“Get out of the passage.”

He had a short crowbar. She did not know where he had found it. He put its point beneath the lower hinge, braced one foot against the wall, and pulled. The wood split. With his next pull the iron fitting came free and the door tilted toward him. He caught it badly; its lower edge struck his shin. He dragged it aside and a man fell through the opening onto his hands.

Two more followed. Then a woman carrying a bundle that moved.

The child had its face pressed into the woman’s coat. For an instant Lyra felt the cloth against her own mouth. She caught the woman’s elbow and helped her over the fallen door.

“Who is left?” Lyra asked.

The man on the floor coughed and pointed.

Inside, sacks burned along the back wall. Orange lines ran between them. Someone had poured oil into the spaces. Smoke hid the roof, and beneath it Ernet clung to the upper loading platform with one leg caught between boards. He had gone up to check the new rope against the receipt. She had told him to do it that morning.

Neris came in carrying a length of wet cloth. She tied it over her mouth and took another piece to Corven.

“The ladder?” he asked.

“Burning.”

“Get the long hook.”

Lyra went for it. By the pump she saw the bucket man again. He was trying to open the side gate. Another man waited beyond it with a sword held down along his leg.

The gate’s drop-bar lay on the ground. Lyra picked up one end before the man could get through. He turned, put a hand on his knife, and saw her face.

“You should go inside,” he said.

She shoved the bar against the gate. He had to jump backward to avoid being struck. The man outside drove his shoulder into the planks, forcing them inward.

Lyra took the iron vial from her pocket.

The bucket man ran at her. She threw herself sideways, caught the gatepost with her left hand, and felt his knife pass through the cloth of her right upper sleeve. The pain arrived after she had pulled the stopper with her teeth. She tipped the vial. A drop of water ran over the wood.

She pushed the stopper home, thrust the vial into her pocket, set her right hand against the gate, and spoke.

The planks groaned. The man outside struck again and stopped as though he had run into stone. On this side the bucket man recoiled from the shuddering wood.

He could still move. He came toward her with the knife.

She had no hand free to defend herself.

Something hit him behind the knee. Neris stood over him with the long hook. Before he could turn she struck again, across his wrist. The knife fell. She kicked it under a cart and pulled Lyra away from the gate.

The working ended. A board cracked inward. Neris thrust the long hook into the iron sockets where the missing bar had rested.

“Will that hold?” Lyra asked.

“Long enough to get another.”

Neris looked at her sleeve. Blood had begun dripping from the cuff.

“Can you lift?”

“Yes.”

“Then lift.”

They ran back through the smoke.

Ernet had stopped shouting. Corven stood below him with both arms raised while two people pushed a freight frame against the wall. He climbed its crosspieces, caught the trapped ankle, and said something Lyra could not hear.

“Water,” Neris called.

“There aren’t any buckets.”

“The kitchen pots.”

Lyra went through the little cookroom, found two pots, and carried them to the pump. Tesset was there with a basin held under the spout. Her face was gray with soot. She passed it to someone and took the pots without asking.

“Send for the fire watch,” Lyra said.

“Sent.”

“And the landing crews.”

“Sent.”

Lyra picked up the basin. A child had appeared beside the pump, holding a cup.

“Where’s your mother?”

The child pointed toward the gate where Neris had left the hook. Lyra gave him to Tesset with no better instruction than “Keep him,” and went back.

Corven had freed the foot. When he lifted Ernet, the burning platform shifted. One support came away from the wall. Lyra saw its iron fastening slide out of the softened wood, slow enough to watch and too fast to change.

“Down!”

Corven jumped. Ernet fell across him. Both struck the sacks beneath the platform before the upper boards came down.

Neris went in first. Lyra followed her, seized the loose coat she could reach, and pulled. It was Corven’s. He was trying to push Ernet ahead of him with his injured leg. Together they got them clear. A shower of sparks landed in Lyra’s hair. Neris slapped them out while still dragging.

Outside, they laid Ernet on the damp paving. He looked at Lyra with wide, annoyed eyes, as though she had delayed him over something unimportant. Then his face contracted.

His boot had burned through. Below it the skin was white in places and black in others. Lyra cut the laces without trying to remove the leather stuck to him. Someone brought water. She poured it gently over the foot and kept pouring while he gripped her wrist.

“I’m here,” she said.

He tried to speak. She bent close. All she heard was a wet rasp.

Corven got to his feet. His trouser leg was torn, the skin beneath purple where the door had struck him. He looked toward the burning store, then at the second building.

“The roof will carry it across.”

Lyra wanted to tell him to sit. Instead she pointed toward the tool rack. “Bring it down.”

He went with three others. They pulled the low connecting shed apart while the yard filled with people passing whatever would hold water. One woman used a child’s bath. A man brought a brewer’s tub with the handles already loose. The proper fire watch arrived after the shed had fallen and began ordering everyone away from the pump they had been using.
