Lyra stood.

“No.”

The watch officer looked at her. “Madam, clear this space.”

“You put your men beside ours. If you move them, you stop the water.”

“We have a procedure.”

She took him by the sleeve and turned him toward the burning roof.

“Then use it there.”

He pulled away, saw Corven coming toward them with the crowbar, and called his people forward.

Tesset caught Lyra’s elbow.

“He’s gone quiet,” she said.

Ernet lay with his head turned toward the pump. His eyes were open. Lyra knelt, pressed her fingers beneath his jaw, and found nothing she could trust. She tried the other side. His skin was warm with the wrong heat.

She opened his coat. His chest rose once, very little.

The old healing phrase came readily. She knew every part of it. There was no torn edge for her to join, no single wound that would let the air reach him. She set her hands on his chest anyway and tried to ease what had swollen inside. For a moment his breath moved more freely. His fingers closed on her sleeve.

“Ernet. Again.” She bent close to his face. “Please.”

Nothing answered her hands. She changed their position, then stopped.

Tesset had begun to cry. She cried quietly, with her lips pressed together. Lyra could hear the fire watch calling for more water behind her.

She stayed beside the body until somebody touched her shoulder and told her that the second store could be saved.

By afternoon the rain had begun. Steam rose from the ruined building. The man who had stolen the buckets sat tied to a cartwheel, his wrist wrapped in a strip of cloth. Neris had made the binding. She had also set someone to watch him when Corven went to examine the side gate.

They found the buckets in the alley, overturned and pierced through their bottoms. Beyond them lay a broken half-mask with a rose pressed into its inner leather. Lyra looked at it and told Corven to keep it. It was enough to ask questions with.

People brought her the losses in interrupted sentences. Rope. Felt. Two crates of the new wheel fittings. Most of the blankets intended for Store Six. The wages chest had been carried out by Tesset. The oil had not, and the quantity remaining in the separate shed could not run all the lamps at the Opera House through the night.

Neris sat on a barrel while a woman wrapped her burned forearm. She watched Lyra count damaged crates.

“I can go to Ropewalk Steps,” she said.

“You’ve been hurt.”

“So have you.”

Lyra looked at her own arm. Someone had tied a cloth above the cut. She did not remember who.

“You don’t have to.”

“I know. I’m telling you where I can go.”

She sounded so like Marda that Lyra glanced at the woman tending the burn.

Neris followed the glance and laughed, though it hurt her face.

“We do talk when you’re elsewhere.”

Lyra knelt beside the barrel. She checked that Neris’s fingers were moving, then touched the unburned part of her hand.

“I need rope brought by the back water. Vessa can take it from Eel Stair after dark. The larger coils first.”

“Send somebody who can carry.”

“Two people.”

“And money.”

“Yes.”

Neris squeezed her hand before letting it go.

The nearest covered wagon belonged to a woman who fired painted tiles in a kiln behind her house. It stood in her court loaded with dry wood, each piece cut to the length her furnace took. Lyra knew the place because they had used its side wall as a landmark when moving supplies around a blocked street.

The woman came out with her sleeves rolled up. She had clay in the creases of her palms.

“I heard,” she said. “I’ve got a bucket. My son took it.”

“Thank you. We need the wagon.”

“For the hurt people?”

“And blankets. Food. Things that must move before night.”

The woman looked toward the shafts. “The horse is hired.”

“We’ll pay.”

“I have to fire tonight. That wood’s been drying since last month.”

“We’ll unload it.”

“Where?”

Rain struck the wagon cover. Beneath the shelter the carefully split wood remained pale and dry. The woman’s little court had no second roof. The kiln mouth stood open behind her, packed with unfired tiles whose white faces caught the light.

Lyra considered the time needed to move every piece into the house. Two rooms, a narrow door, the sick husband or sleeping children she could already imagine being produced as further obstacles. She stopped that thought before the woman had said anything about her family.

“I can leave six people to put it under cover.”

“There isn’t cover.”

“We’ll bring cloth.”

“When?”

Lyra looked at the wet street. Behind her two porters waited with a stretcher. They had come because she had told them she could obtain a wagon.

The woman saw them and folded her arms.

“I can take your injured in the morning. After the firing.”

“We need it now.”

“Then find another.”

There was another across the canal, owned by a man who had refused every request since Lyra rejected the Court’s terms. Getting it would mean passing a guarded bridge, going to his house, spending time persuading him. The rain was worsening. At Low Bell Yard three burned people lay under a sheet stretched between poles.

Lyra took out her purse.

“I’ll pay for the wood and the firing.”

“You can’t pay for it. The roofers expect those tiles tomorrow. If they buy elsewhere, they won’t come back for the next lot.”

“I’ll bring them work.”

“You don’t know what they make.”

Lyra turned to the porters.

“Unload it against the far wall. Keep the cover over as much as you can.”

The woman stepped between them and the wagon.

“I said no.”

“I heard you.”

For several seconds neither moved. Water dripped from the eaves behind Lyra. The woman looked at her face, then at her hands, and moved aside.

Lyra put the purse on the sill. The woman did not pick it up. She watched the first piece of dry wood laid on the wet paving.

By evening the injured were under a roof. The blankets came from three households, a sail loft, and the stock of a shop whose owner demanded a receipt before letting Tesset take anything. Lyra signed it. Rope arrived by the route Neris had chosen. Berris came herself to select what could be used for the alternate stair.

Ernet lay in a clean shirt. They had found someone who knew where his wife lived. When she arrived, Lyra sat beside her. The woman wanted to speak to whoever had been with him; Lyra had to explain that she was that person too.

“Was he frightened?”

Lyra looked at the woman’s hands. They were clasped so tightly that the knuckles had gone pale.

“Yes.”

“Did he know?”

“I don’t know.”

“Was he alone?”

“No.”

The woman nodded. After a while she took the clean shirt’s sleeve between two fingers and rubbed it. She said it would have annoyed him to be given a new shirt after he had put every spare coin into the window fund. The frame in their room had rotted at the bottom. They had been going to fix it before winter.

Lyra laughed once, unexpectedly. Then the woman did too, and then they were both crying.

Outside, work continued. She could hear Tesset giving out money. Every person received the same two days’ advance, including Pevan, who had returned with his sister’s largest pot and was waiting to take it home.

Later, while Lyra stood beneath the surviving shed, a crew woman came to ask whether she might send her son away that night.

“His uncle’s boat is leaving before dawn,” she said. “If you can spare him.”

“What work does he do?”

“He’s seven.”

Lyra stared at her.

The woman waited.

“Send him,” Lyra said. “You don’t need to ask.”

“Yes, Lady.”

She went away reassured. Lyra stayed under the shed until Corven brought her the coat someone had found beneath the office table. One sleeve was burned. The other was wet with blood. He helped her put it on anyway because the rain had made her begin to shake.

Chapter 28 Working entrances

The borrowed wagon had a painted sun on its tailboard. Lyra had not noticed it in the tile maker’s court. Now it moved ahead of her through the Festival crowd, bright yellow beneath a fringe of dirty tassels. Whenever she looked at the wagon, she saw the sun first.

The horse disliked masks. It shied from a man wearing a silver bird’s face, turned its head against the rein, and set one hoof on the hem of a woman’s skirt. Vessa’s daughter, hired to walk beside the shaft, freed the cloth before the woman began shouting. She apologized. The woman told her to watch the animal. The horse put its nose into a seller’s basket and stole a mouthful of greenery.

Farther down the street someone laughed so hard that the laughter became a cough.

Lyra walked beside the rear wheel. Her cut arm had stiffened overnight. She could raise it to shoulder height, but the movement pulled at the stitches and sent pain along the inside of her elbow. Under her clean coat the dressing was already damp. She had put the stitches in herself. The person doing the bandaging had fourteen people waiting and only one sound lamp.

Tesset had objected to the coat.

“You’ll bleed through it.”

“Then I’ll take it off before anybody sees.”

“That’s worked well for everyone in every difficult room.”

Lyra had laughed. Tesset had waited for her to finish, then sewn a second piece of folded linen inside the sleeve.

Now the extra cloth chafed. Lyra kept her arm against her body and counted doorways to the turn.

The west court lay behind a temporary screen painted to resemble a garden. The screen hid the carts from people entering by the steps. Through a gap Lyra could see lamps above the public doors, women lifting skirts away from the damp, a young man adjusting his companion’s mask with fingers gentler than the gesture required.

At the working gate the watchman had been replaced.

The new man held out his hand without looking at her face. Lyra gave him the returns, folded so the authorization seal showed first. He held them beneath the lamp.

“Where’s the second oil load?”

“Lost in the fire.”

“These say two.”

She opened the fold and showed him the amendment.

“These say one.”

He read it twice. The horse stamped. Behind the wagon another carter called to ask if anybody intended moving before winter.

The watchman looked at Lyra’s face then. His eyes went to her hands and stayed there.

“Lady.”

“Can we take the flour inside?”

He moved the chain across the entrance. She walked beneath it while he was still lifting.

They entered in three loads, each with its own reason to be there. Food first, then lamp oil, then the long boxes of replacement scenery fittings Berris had requested after an accident in rehearsal. Within the last lay the folding handles, rail sections, padded braces, and the removable wheel assemblies they would use beneath Aria’s frame.

Nothing in the boxes was itself forbidden. Lyra had said that to herself while they packed them. The arrangement was what she would have to explain.

Berris met them in the hoist room with a pencil tucked into the comb behind her ear. She had painted her cheeks for the Festival, two red circles that made her look permanently furious. A stagehand behind her wore antlers and carried a broom.

“You’re late.”

“The gate.”

“The gate doesn’t have to lift the moon before the singers get here.”

She took the first box and sent it toward the working stair. The antlered stagehand followed, sweeping flour from the route as he went. Lyra had seen him lift the practice frame; he was one of the six. His broom handle had been measured to the width of the vault’s narrowest doorway. Laid across a landing, it would tell him whether the rails would fit.

Corven came in with the second load. He wore the coat he used for dirty work, its elbows patched, his chain hidden beneath a coil of ordinary rope. The vessel containing his eel rested inside a padded crate with air holes cut beneath the lid. He carried it himself.

“Where?” he asked Berris.

“At the lower landing, beside the empty meal bins. Nobody touches those until the kitchen gives up.”

He checked the lid and carried the crate to the stairs, the coil over his shoulder. His shin had swollen. Lyra could see him favor it when he turned.

“Can you manage?” she asked.

He looked at her sleeve.

“Yes,” he said.

She let him go, then followed the fittings down to the salt-level landing. Berris came down with a crate of mirrored scenery which had been blocking the upper hoist. They left it hanging over the platform while a stagehand cleared a place for it below.

Neris arrived with three people bound for Store Six. One carried a basket of sewing. Another had a small girl asleep against his shoulder. The third was an old woman who had dressed for a performance and refused to remove her mask, though it made her breathing worse. Neris held her elbow and kept up a conversation about whether the feather on it was real.

“It was real when I paid for it,” the woman said.

“Then it probably still is.”

“Things change.”

Jorin stood at the salt-store door.

Lyra had not seen him arrive. Salt had gathered along his jaw, thickest beneath the ear, and the old split in his left thumbnail showed white against the darker nail. He tapped the doorpost twice with his fingers. It was the movement he used before taking a load. Lyra remembered him doing it to a wagon whose owner had asked him to stop marking the paint.

He looked at Neris.

For a long moment he said nothing.

“Neris,” he said.

She stopped. The old woman stopped with her.

Jorin’s eyes moved to the three people. Then to Lyra. The order in his hand was folded around a narrow piece of wood so he could hold it without closing his fingers fully.

“Below,” he said.

“They’re going below,” Lyra told him.

“Work.”

“Refuge. Store Six.”

He looked at Neris again. His fingers tapped the post.

She shook her head. “I don’t dive anymore, Jorin.”

The old woman lifted her mask a little and stared. Neris eased it back into place.

The Worn opened the paper. Lyra saw columns of names, the beginnings of a new allocation. She did not know whether Neris appeared there. She could not risk him finding out in front of the Prince’s new gate watchman.

“Katerina authorized this,” she said.

Jorin turned toward the inner corridor. He lifted his right hand. At its movement the watchman by the stair came forward.

Lyra saw Berris beside the hoist, one hand on a long crate of mirrored scenery. Its lifting sling passed over a hook set too high for the carpenter to reach from the ground. The sling was their spare, borrowed after the fire, sound from end to end. They would need it if the one already on the crystal carriage failed.

Lyra looked at Berris, then at the sling.

Berris stared back.

Lyra made the smallest movement she could with her hand.

The carpenter’s mouth tightened. She pulled her knife from her belt and cut halfway through the taut rope. Then she stepped clear and shouted.

The crate fell across the edge of the hoist. Glass broke within it, a crashing cascade that silenced the whole room. The hook swung against the iron post. One end of the crate slid toward the open shaft.

“Catch it!” Berris shouted. “That’s the west mirror!”

The watchman ran to her. Jorin turned at the sound. He moved faster than Lyra had yet seen him move, catching the low end of the crate against the platform while two stagehands got a beam underneath.

Neris did not wait to be told. She took the old woman’s arm and led the group down the working stair.

Lyra went to the fallen crate. A strip of silvered glass lay on the floor. In it her face was divided at the mouth.

“What happened?” she asked.

“Your rope,” Berris said loudly.

She held up the cut end.

The watchman looked at it. Lyra took it before he could.

“Then we’ll replace it.”

“Before the moon comes down, I hope.”

Berris turned away. Jorin continued holding the crate. His split thumbnail pressed into the wood. He could have crushed it, Lyra thought. Instead he held it as carefully as any good carrier, his fingers clear of the place where it would settle.

“Thank you,” she said.

He looked at her after a long pause.

“Load,” he said.

“Yes.”

They lifted it off him. The watchman followed Berris to inspect the hoist. Lyra took the severed sling into the stairwell and leaned there until her breath stopped shaking.

Katerina found her beside the salt stores.

“Jorin is asking about a list.”

“I know.”

“It’s been taken from him.”

“By whom?”

“Me.”

Lyra straightened. The narrow passage behind Katerina led toward the Hex-Vault. A new lamp burned at its bend. Beside it stood the maintenance man from their visit, wearing a clean collar that irritated the salt at his neck.

“He hasn’t started joining the frames?” Lyra asked.

“Not yet. He has orders to begin after the first interlude.”

“That soon?”

“Those are the orders.”

Katerina touched the edge of Lyra’s sleeve. Blood had come through the inner dressing despite Tesset’s care.

“You’ll need that arm.”

“I have it.”

Katerina took a folded linen square from her own pocket and tucked it inside the upper sleeve against the dressing. Her hands were as steady as when she had held Callista’s hair.

“Did you hold Aria?” she asked.

Katerina stopped.

“When you took her. Did you hold her yourself?”

“Yes.”

“Did she know you?”

“No.”

“What did you tell her?”

“That someone was hurt. Someone who had sung with her. She came out by the little door beside the rehearsal rooms.”

Lyra looked toward the man at the bend. He was turning his glass over in his hand without setting it down.

“Callista?”

“No. Another name. I had been told enough to choose one.”

“Did the Prince tell you to use it?”

“He told me to bring her. I chose how.”

The whisper was almost lost beneath the noise from above. Katerina tucked the last edge of the linen into place.

“I’m telling you because she may never be able to,” she said.

Lyra lowered her arm.

“I’ll remember.”

Katerina nodded, then turned and went toward the vault.

Callista arrived through the wardrobe passage in a cloak that smelled of cedar. Sera had found it for her, along with a plain mask, its ties lengthened to spare the sore places behind her ears. She carried a tray of cups. One rattled continuously against the others.

Lyra took the tray before anyone could notice.

“Where is Sera?”

“Above. With the man who knows the bundle.”

“Has she reached the east offices?”

“The landing. Not the door.”

They stood in a little space behind the stair, where cleaning brushes hung over a sink. Callista took off her mask. Sweat shone on her face although the passage was cold.

“Have you seen her?” she asked.

“Not tonight.”

“I should go.”

“When the cord comes.”

Callista bent over the sink and put water on her wrists. Her hands had become clumsy. Lyra wanted to help. She remembered the gloves, Callista’s sudden fury at being touched.

“Can I hold your sleeve?”

Callista nodded. Lyra held it clear of the water while she washed.

“It’s still in the drawer,” Callista said.

Lyra knew which drawer.

“Good.”

“I thought about bringing it.”

“I know.”

“No, you don’t.”

Lyra let the words stand. Water ran over Callista’s crooked finger and dripped into the stained basin.

“At the room,” Callista said, “I thought I could leave it and walk away. Halfway here I wanted to turn back. I could tell myself I needed it. That I’d be stronger. That she might hear me better.”

“What did you do?”

“Kept walking.”

Lyra released the sleeve when Callista straightened. The wet cloth stuck briefly to her thumb.

Callista put her mask back on.

“I’d like something else to think about now.”

“Then listen to the first interlude. Tell Berris if they alter it.”

Callista looked past her toward the stage passage. Music sounded above, a short run repeated by several instruments trying to agree on where it should end.

“I can do that,” she said.

The rails for the north freight stair were in place by the time the public doors opened. Berris showed Lyra from the salt-level landing. Two narrow timber runners lay beside the wall, disguised beneath rolls of old canvas. They could be fixed across each flight with hooks set into existing sockets. A checked rope around the central post would control a loaded carriage’s descent one flight at a time.

“Have you tested the post?”

Berris looked at her.

“I watched you test the other one.”

“This one’s sound. I hit it until the wardrobe mistress threatened to hit me.”

Lyra pressed her hand against the wood. Old, dense, dry at its core. She could not learn its strength by touching it. Berris had done what could be done.

“The spare sling?”

“Gone.”

“We can splice it.”

“For sacks, yes. Not for her.”

Lyra looked down the stair. The first flight vanished beneath the landing, then emerged at a broad turn. At the bottom stood the open door of the measuring room, its stone threshold raised above the canal landing. Beyond the wall she could hear bowls being carried into Store Six. The narrow return route from that chamber had its own entrance farther along the salt-store passage.

“We move it once,” Berris said. “If we have to move it twice, we use a fresh line and take the time. Nobody gets to become inspired with a knife and a prayer.”

“Agreed.”

“Tell your man with the chain.”

“I will.”

Marda appeared from the refuge passage carrying an empty tray. She had flour on her cheek and looked younger than she had in the yard.

“Enough food?” Lyra asked.

“For tonight. For breakfast, if nobody discovers a second stomach.”

“The door?”

“Open. Jorad’s at the landing.”

“Can you get past him?”

Marda lifted the tray. “Apparently.”

Lyra smiled. Marda did not entirely return it.

“Come down when you can,” she said. “People would like to see you.”

“I will.”

The first bell sounded through the building. Berris swore and ran for the stage.

It was early. Lyra knew it was early because the last food cart had not yet come through the court, because Sera had not reached the office door, because Corven’s cracked shell was still wrapped on the shelf beside the meal bins. For several seconds she could only listen as the audience began to settle above them, hundreds of small movements gathering into one expectant quiet.

A page in Court livery appeared at the landing. He held a folded card and looked from Marda to Lyra.

“Madam Serafine asks for you.”

Lyra took the card.

“The seventh mirror?” she asked.

The page bowed.

In her inner pocket the three knotted cords lay flat against her ribs. She put a hand over them, then followed him up.

Chapter 29 The private box

The page took her by the public stair.

Lyra would have preferred the service passage, where she could have looked into the landings as they passed. Here the walls were mirrored between gilt columns. In one reflection she saw a woman with an expensive coat and no mask. In the next the coat’s sleeve had darkened. She turned her arm toward her body before the page looked back.

“May I have a cloth?”

He stopped at once.

“For your hands, madam?”

“For my sleeve.”

He looked, flushed, and ran to a serving table. Lyra waited beside a vase of flowers large enough to occupy a bath. She could hear the audience beyond the doors, the little waves of applause when a face was recognized, somebody calling down to a person seated below. A man passing with his wife began to speak to Lyra, then saw her eyes and continued without finishing.

The page returned with a folded napkin. It was fine linen, embroidered with a rose. She wrapped it around the sleeve, pinned it with the small fastening from her cuff, and thanked him.

“Will you be all right?” he asked.

“For the stair.”

He hesitated. She smiled first.

At the top, the seventh mirror stood beside a pair of double doors. Its frame was darker than the others, polished almost black. Lyra could see a thin space between the frame and the wall. It was an ordinary access panel concealed by expensive workmanship. Someone had to get behind mirrors too.

A guard stood beside it. He examined the card, opened the double doors, and let her through.

Serafine sat at a table laid for three. The Salt Prince occupied the chair nearest the inner door. His coat hung from his shoulders with its familiar, appalling weight. Salt glimmered along its folds. A servant stood by a tray, holding a bottle neither guest appeared to want.

Serafine appeared to smile at her. Then she took the pin from between her teeth and secured the loose edge of her veil.

“Lyra,” Serafine said. “Sit down.”

The third chair had been drawn back for her.

“I’ve come from the deliveries.”

“Yes. We wondered when you would finish.”

Lyra sat. The chair was unexpectedly comfortable. Her legs had begun to tremble on the public stair, and she was grateful for its support before she remembered whom she would be grateful to.

The servant poured water. Serafine noticed the napkin around her sleeve.

“You were hurt.”

“At my yard.”

“I heard.”

“Ernet was killed.”

The servant’s hand stopped over the water glass. He resumed pouring before it overflowed.

Serafine inclined her head. “I’m sorry.”

Lyra looked at the fish on the table. Six portions, no sauce. The kitchen order had been accurate.

“His wife will need more than that.”

“She will have it.”

“From whom?”

“From the settlement, if we reach one.”

The Prince put down the piece of bread he had been holding. His fingertips left pale marks on its crust.

“We have reached several,” he said. “The question now is whether people can be made to understand them.”

Lyra placed both hands in her lap. Her right hand hurt. Her left was so cold that the fabric beneath it began to stiffen.

“What do you need me to understand?”

Serafine took a folded paper from beside her plate.

“The fighting has become expensive.”

“For everyone,” Lyra said.

“Yes. We propose to stop paying for it.”

She opened the paper. No flourish, no delay. Lyra recognized the form of a preliminary schedule, intended to record what still required a signature.

The outer warehouses would be registered through a new office. Coastal duties would be shared. Routes interrupted by either faction would reopen. Existing labor obligations would pass to the administrator of the properties in which they had been incurred.

The words were competent. Each provision meant exactly what its writer needed it to mean.

“My warehouses,” Lyra said.

“Among others.”

“The crews aren’t attached to buildings.”

“Their employment is,” the Prince said.

“Their agreements are with me.”

“Your agreements were made possible by credit I supplied.”

“Which has been repaid according to its terms.”

“Some of it.”

“All of what fell due.”

He looked at her. The air around the table seemed to dry. Lyra felt salt collecting in the old cut at the corner of her mouth, though she had not touched any. The water at the rim of his glass whitened into a rough ring. He drew a slow breath through his nose and carefully lifted his hand from the glass.

Serafine put one finger on the paper.

“There is no need to quarrel over the old arrangement while considering a better one.”

“Better for whom?”

“For Ernet’s wife, if we can prevent another yard burning tomorrow.”

Lyra looked up sharply.

Serafine did not withdraw the sentence. The servant came forward to remove a plate. His sleeve brushed the Prince’s chair. A white crust joined the cuff to the carved wood; when he pulled away, a little blood showed through the linen. The plate shook in his other hand.

“Careful,” the Prince said. “That belongs to our hostess.”

The man steadied it against his chest.

“Leave us,” Serafine said.

The man went through the inner door. Beyond it Lyra glimpsed a smaller passage and a stair rising toward the offices. She heard the door close at the bottom, then a bolt.

Serafine turned the schedule around so Lyra could read it.

“We can put the shore businesses under one administration without taking your place from you. I would prefer that. You know how they work. The Prince’s people have particular needs which you have learned to accommodate. There is no reason to replace competence because it has been inconvenient.”

“You’d like to keep it convenient.”

“I’d like to keep it paid.”

The Prince reached for his water. “The Cantor can advise on the crews. There are works beyond the breakwater that require what his people have become. He has already described their capacities.”

“They can decline.”

His glass touched the table with a small, hard sound.

“Under the new office,” Serafine said, “there would be agreed conditions.”

“Can they decline?”

Neither answered immediately.

From the theater came the beginning of the overture. A single instrument found the melody and held it while the others gathered. The phrase was from one of the songs in Callista’s programs. Its ending would matter. At the table it served only to make the silence less conspicuous.

“The marked people will remain with their own,” the Prince said. “They are less comfortable elsewhere.”

“Some of them are less comfortable where they are.”

“The men in my lower stores no longer ask to be elsewhere.”

Lyra pictured Jorin holding the crate at exactly the proper angle. His split thumbnail. The pause before he found her name.

She set the paper down.

“No transfers without the person’s agreement. Each agreement witnessed by someone neither of you pays.”

The Prince’s mouth moved. It might have been amusement.

“You would have me begin again with each of them, every morning.”

“If that’s what they require.”

“You have expensive ambitions for a contractor.”

“Then you’ll recognize them.”

Serafine laughed. It was brief and unguarded enough to surprise Lyra.

“There she is,” she said.

For a moment Lyra saw the appeal of the room. They were listening to her. She had come under her own name, and two people who had disposed of much greater names wanted her at their table. She could argue a clause and make it change. Outside, people pushed through wet streets toward work they might lose by morning. Here a servant would come if she rang.

Serafine slid a second paper across the table.

“This is for you.”

The offer included the Low Bell Yard replacement lease, enough money to rebuild its stores, immediate payment of the injured workers’ treatment, and three months’ wages for households displaced by the fire. Lyra would retain a share of the trading profits. She could leave the city for a season and return to an office already operating. Her private acts in the recent disputes would not be pursued through the Court’s channels.

She read that last provision twice.

“You have been busy,” she said.

“You have been visible.”

The Prince rose. Both women looked toward him.

“I have instructions to give.”

“We haven’t concluded,” Serafine said.

“My people can’t suspend their work whenever we speak.”

He went through the inner door. This time Lyra heard voices beyond it. The maintenance man from the vault spoke first, apologetic and quick. The Prince answered too low for her to hear the words. Metal touched metal. A key ring, perhaps. Then footsteps withdrew down the private stair.

Serafine watched the closed door. Her hand lay flat beside her plate.

“He keeps the crystal ready,” Lyra said.

“I know what he keeps below my stage.”

“Do you know why he means to bring it up?”

“He has promised an appearance that will remind several people of their obligations.”

“He means to break it if he doesn’t get what he wants.”

Serafine looked at her.

“Who told you that?”

“Someone who has stood beside it.”

“You?”

“Yes.”

The music swelled. Beneath it Lyra heard a wheel turning. She did not know whether the noise came from the stage machinery or the salt level. She looked at the floor, then forced her gaze back to Serafine.

“The woman inside is alive. She can hear. She has asked for the harvesting to stop.”

“Has she.”

“We checked.”

“How?”

Lyra explained the held note and the two separated notes. She told Serafine about the coin in the glove, the reversed questions. The woman’s attention sharpened as she listened. It was the same attention she had given the list of warehouses.

“And he doesn’t know?”

“I don’t know what he knows.”

“The wards beneath the stage were laid for that crystal. He cannot simply shatter it and expect the rest of us to stand politely in the way.”

“Then why is he joining it to a carriage?”

“Because he would like us to be frightened.”

“Are you?”

Serafine touched the edge of her water glass.

“Enough to consider what you want me to believe.”

Lyra might have been as suspicious. She was warning Serafine about the Prince just as they were offering to pay her for giving up the power to refuse.

“Send someone you trust to the vault,” she said. “Stop the carriage until you’ve looked.”

“If I close his stores while we negotiate, he will assume the negotiation is a trap.”

“Perhaps it is.”

“For him?” Serafine asked.

Lyra did not answer.

Serafine sat back. For a while she looked older, the lamplight showing fine tired lines beneath the preparation on her face. She picked up the offer and smoothed its lower edge against the table.

“You have done something difficult,” she said. “I don’t imagine it became possible merely because somebody was kind enough to help. You’ve kept people working when they should have been scattered. You’ve made people who dislike each other depend on you. You should understand what that is worth.”

“I do.”

“Then let it survive you being angry.”

Lyra looked at the sum offered for the yard. It was enough. A mason could be paid tomorrow. Pevan would have work through the repairs. Ernet’s wife could have the window fixed and leave the room if she could no longer bear it. Neris could rest. All of it was possible, if she accepted the paper beneath Serafine’s hand.

“What happens to Maeron?”

“He retains his observances under conditions. No wrecking without coastal authorization. No recruitment among people already contracted elsewhere. No song in occupied public buildings without prior agreement.”

Lyra nearly laughed. Serafine saw it.

“You think those conditions foolish?”

“I think you intend to ask him to obey them.”

“And you don’t?”

Lyra saw the lower chamber again, Maeron leaning over the bowl. She had let his promise shrink to words she could accept because she needed to leave.

Serafine continued more softly.

“You can name the people who need to leave the city. We will provide conduct for them. The workers who remain will have work. You can keep your teacher from a worse reckoning than he may deserve. There are places in this where I can give you what you’re asking.”

“And Aria?”

“The harvesting can be addressed.”

“Stopped.”

“Then stopped, while we determine what may safely be done.”

“By whom?”

“People capable of doing it.”

“Chosen by whom?”

Serafine’s hand stopped smoothing the paper.

“You cannot inspect every hand that reaches toward her.”

“No.”

“Nor guarantee every result.”

“No.”

“Then choose an arrangement with enough strength to endure an unhappy result.”

Lyra looked through the narrow opening into the private box. Beyond it the audience sat in rings of light. She could see only fragments: a white feather nodding, a hand lifting opera glasses, the curve of a bald head. None of those people knew the sound beneath the music was a woman whose mouth could not close.

She folded Serafine’s offer along its existing crease.

“I need to speak to my people.”

“You may send for them.”

“All of them?”

Serafine looked at the door the Prince had used.

“No.”

Lyra set the paper beside her untouched plate.

“Then I cannot accept.”

The Prince returned before she could stand.

He smelled of wet salt and a faint sweetness like spoiled flowers. There was fresh moisture along one sleeve. He sat, picked up the bread, and looked at the folded offer.

“She would like to consult,” Serafine said.

“How many?”

“Enough to make it impossible tonight.”

Lyra rose. Pain went through her arm as the chair brushed it.

“I have deliveries to finish.”

The Prince watched her. “The carriage first.”

“My people know their work.”

“They will be paid after it.”

“The contract requires the advance.”

His eyes narrowed slightly.

Serafine reached toward the bell. “That can be provided.”

The Prince put his hand over hers before she touched it. No pressure that Lyra could see. Serafine looked at his fingers, then at his face.

“The advance is available in the lower office,” he said. “Katerina has authority to draw it. Your workers may collect when they have completed the movement.”

“Then it isn’t an advance.”

He smiled.

“Tonight people will need to trust one another.”

Lyra felt the three cords against her ribs. She could give them out on the stair. Berris would be waiting for her return; Sera would be looking at the office door, counting passages of music. Corven would have set his shell where he could reach it. They had agreed to begin on her word. None of them could know what was being offered in this room.

She could still sit down. She could ask Serafine to call a clerk, have the figures copied, secure the provisions one by one. There would be losses in doing that, but there were losses waiting outside too. She knew their names. She had written some of them herself.

She bowed her head just enough to end the audience and went to the door.

The guard opened it. Behind her the Prince said something about the first interlude. Serafine replied, too quietly for Lyra to hear.

The door closed.

For several steps Lyra kept walking at a guest’s pace. She could not make herself hurry. She passed the flowers, the serving table, the boy who had brought her the napkin. He glanced at the wrapped sleeve and offered to guide her down.

“Thank you,” she said. “I know the way.”

She turned through a service door before she reached the public stair.

Berris was waiting on the landing with a hammer in one hand and a small wooden horse in the other. Somebody’s prop had broken at the neck. She put both down when Lyra appeared.

“Well?”

“They mean to take the yards. The crews. They’ll pay us to help.”

Berris stared at her for a moment. “Do we know where Sera is?”

“Where she was supposed to be.”

“That isn’t knowing.”

“No.”

“The last cart isn’t in.”

“What does it hold?”

“Food. Two of our bearers. They’re stuck behind a troupe with a dragon.”

“How long?”

“I’ve sent someone.”

Below them a door opened. Warm air rose from the kitchens. Lyra could smell pastry, lamp oil, damp coats. She could no longer tell which floor the sounds came from. There were so many people still going about their work.

“The Prince has ordered the crystal moved after the first interlude,” she said.

Berris looked toward the stage.

“That one’s nearly upon us.”

“I know.”

“You can wait for the cart. You can wait to hear from the landing. You can’t do both before then.”

Lyra reached inside her coat. The cords caught on the edge of the dressing. She freed them with her left hand and laid them across Berris’s palm.

For a moment neither woman released the small weight.

“Three knots,” Berris said.

“Yes.”

“Sera. Katerina. Corven.”

“Yes.”

“And if the landing isn’t held?”

“The north freight stair. As we practiced.”

Berris closed her hand.

Her runner waited behind the open service door, a woman with a tray cloth over one arm. She took the first cord and put it beneath the cloth. The second went to the antlered stagehand, who had removed his antlers so he could pass through the low salt passage. For the third, Berris called down the stair. A young porter came up, felt the knots with his eyes shut, and ran back down.

Lyra watched the last white end disappear around the turn.

Onstage, a singer opened her mouth. A note rose through the floor, clear and effortless. Lyra turned toward the stair that led to the Gallery.

Part Five The Opera House and its keeper

Chapter 30 The files she keeps

The upper door came away from its hinges without opening.

Berris’s man had driven the pins upward and caught them in his cap. He stood aside while the woman with the tray cloth took the door’s weight. It was heavier than either had expected. Lyra put her left hand beneath its lower edge and helped them ease it into the passage. On the far side, the key still sat in the lock.

A clerk looked up from a desk.

For a moment he only stared. Behind him the Gallery extended along the back of the theater, its outer wall broken by cabinets and narrow windows. The windows looked into an air shaft. No view had been wasted on the people who worked there.

“You can’t,” he said.

Lyra stepped across the empty threshold.

“Put your hands on the desk.”

He looked at the woman beside her, at the hammer in the man’s belt, then back at Lyra. Recognition moved across his face. He had been at Harl’s warehouse, trying to close a drawer on too many papers. She remembered the sleeve caught in it.

“They hired you,” she said.

He put his hands down.

A second clerk rose from a table farther along the room. She wore spectacles on a ribbon and had an expression of such intense irritation that Lyra almost apologized for arriving unannounced.

“What is this?”

“I’m taking this room.”

“That isn’t an answer.”

“No. Sit down.”

The woman remained standing. The man at the first desk said her name, very quietly. Lyra did not catch it. After a moment she sat.

There were three interior doors. One led toward the private boxes; another to a little room containing a bed and a washstand; the third to the stair down to the strongroom. Lyra sent the hammer man to the first door and the runner to the third.

“Lessen, don’t lock it until you know who’s outside. Eda, find Sera.”

The clerk from Harl’s watched her.

“Who holds the cabinet keys?” she asked.

He pointed toward the woman with spectacles.

“No,” the woman said.

Lyra approached her table. A tray stood beside the ink, carrying half a roll and a little pot of jam. The jam had set at the rim where the spoon rested. She thought the woman must have been eating at this desk for years.

“Who else is in the rooms?”

“People whose business you have no authority to inspect.”

“Who needs to get out?”

The woman glanced toward the room with the bed.

Lyra went to it. A young man sat on the bed wearing a stage costume open at the throat. He had been crying. Someone had given him water and left the glass beyond the length of the chain fixed around his ankle.

He looked at Lyra’s coat first, then her face.

“I did what they asked,” he said.

She called for the keys.

The clerk did not move. Lyra went back, lifted the woman’s writing hand from the table, and put it flat beside the paper.

“The keys.”

Her own hand was cold enough to make the clerk gasp. She had not begun a working. She did not need to. The woman looked toward the door, found nobody there who would stop Lyra, and took a ring from inside her bodice.

The first key failed. The second turned stiffly. Lyra bent over the chain, her wounded arm throbbing against her side, and eased the iron cuff away from the swollen ankle.

The young man did not rise.

“Can you stand?”

He nodded, then shook his head.

“Lessen,” she called. “Help him. Down the working stair, to Marda. Tell her he needs a place.”

“His name?” Lessen asked.

The young man answered before she could. His name was Bel. He spoke it as though making a difficult correction.

Lessen offered an arm. Bel used it to get to his feet. His costume trailed behind him, all silver leaves and pale ribbon. At the door he stopped.

“My mother doesn’t know,” he said.

“We won’t send for her unless you want.”

He looked at the clerk. “She knows.”

The woman had begun removing her spectacles. She folded them carefully and placed them in their case.

The cabinets were arranged by use rather than by name. Lyra understood that after opening the third. Family papers occupied one wall, property transactions another. A small case beside the private stair held things currently being used: letters laid open at particular passages, debt notices marked for delivery, portraits with descriptions pinned to their backs.

She found Bel there. A miniature of a woman in an old-fashioned gown had been wrapped in a report of her son’s meetings. The report listed what his mother would receive if he refused them. Lyra read the first two lines, then folded it around the miniature again.

“Give him this.” She handed the packet to Lessen as he returned. “Tell him there may be copies.”

The clerk made a sound.

“How many?” Lyra asked.

“I couldn’t say.”

“You could try.”

The woman looked at her hand, where Lyra’s fingers had left pale marks.

“One. In the family cabinet.”

Lyra found it. She set both versions together. Lessen took them below.

Sera arrived carrying a small ironbound box. The old catalogue man followed with two companions, one before and one behind as promised. His face was wet with sweat. He held a packet of seals as carefully as though the wax might still be soft.

“We have it,” Sera said.

“Have you opened it?”

“We opened it in front of witnesses. The instrument, the founding seal, the first additions. They match.”

Lyra put a hand on the box. Its iron corners were worn bright.

“This is what they kept of you.”

“Part of it.” Sera looked past her at the open cabinets. “What have you found?”

“Enough to explain why nobody comes to their meetings empty-handed.”

Sera set the box on the table but kept a hand on its lid.

The music changed beneath them. The first song had ended. Applause came through the floor in a soft continuous rush. Lyra looked toward the stair.

“Get it outside,” she said. “Your witnesses must be clear before the doors close.”

“I’ll come back.”

“Don’t come back by the public stair.”

Sera nodded. She looked at the growing piles on the desk: family packets, property papers, the current instruments of coercion from the small case.

“You’ll remember what we signed.”

“Yes.”

“I’ll hold you to it.”

“I know.”

Sera took the box. Her companions went with her. The old catalogue man paused beside the woman clerk and said something Lyra could not hear. The woman answered with one sharp word. He shook his head, then followed the others down.

Lyra opened the next drawer.

The files named the men at her gate.

She found the order beneath a record of warehouse concessions. It listed a payment, the purchase of oil, a proposed interruption to one evening’s work. No names of workers. No allowance for the man whose wife had rubbed the new shirt between her fingers. In the margin, someone had written that the proprietress should be allowed to survive if practicable.

Lyra read that twice.

Beside it lay a letter from a magistrate who had delayed the examination of a dead porter because a property holder preferred the death entered as an accident. The property holder’s name appeared on a bank of loans supporting three Court houses. Behind the letter was an older note concerning the magistrate’s daughter. It was intimate, irrelevant to the porter, and plainly the reason the magistrate had obeyed.

She could separate those papers. The first could accuse him. The second could make him listen before he reached for an excuse.

Her fingers rested on the daughter’s name.

Callista came in without her mask. She had left the tray somewhere below. Her eyes moved over the open cabinets, the piles, the frightened clerks.

“Where’s the fire?”

“Not yet.”

“We can carry them to the grate.”

Lyra looked toward the small fireplace at the far end. A copper guard stood before it. No fire had been lit; the room was already warm from the crowded building.

“Some are going downstairs.”

“To burn?”

“To be secured.”

Callista came closer.

Lyra put the magistrate’s papers beneath the arson order. “We have evidence of what they did.”

“Then show it.”

“Not tonight.”

“You told me—”

“I know what I told you.”

Callista’s face changed. The anger left it for a moment. Lyra was frightened by what remained.

“You know exactly.”

“They can burn the yard again. They can close every shop that sells to us. These name the people who make that possible.”

“And the people they’re hurting.”

“Yes.”

Callista put a hand on the packet beneath Lyra’s palm.

“Whose daughter?”

Lyra did not move her hand.

“It’s written there,” Callista said. “You don’t have to tell me. I can see the word.”

The clerk with spectacles looked down. The other clerk stood motionless by the first desk.

“Release it,” Callista said.

Lyra kept her hand where it was.

A shout sounded beyond the private stair door. Lessen had returned and was bracing it with his shoulder. Someone on the other side struck the panel with a hard object.

“We have to move,” he said.

Callista did not look away from Lyra.

“Burn what we can’t use as evidence,” Lyra told the clerk. “These come with us.”

The woman stared at her.

“You know where the kindling is. Begin.”

“Use it against whom?” Callista asked.

Lyra gathered the papers into a portfolio. Beneath them lay further bundles: shipping licenses, paid false testimony, favors traded against family secrets. She gave those to Eda, who had returned from Sera’s departure with two empty canvas sacks.

Callista caught one sack before Eda could take it away.

“No.”

Lyra turned toward her. The room had become quiet except for the blows against the door.

“Let her pass.”

“Tell her what she’s carrying.”

Eda looked at the open sack.

“The Court’s records,” Lyra said.

“You promised to destroy them.”

“I have to answer for what happens after they’re destroyed.”

“No. You’re keeping them.”

The next blow split the panel beside Lessen’s head. He ducked. A narrow blade came through the opening, searching for the bar.

Lyra moved the sack out of Callista’s hands. Callista’s grip gave way. Lyra passed it to Eda and told her to take it to the lower office.

Callista watched the sack go.

She did not raise her voice. “I heard her say no in the crystal. I’ll remember that I heard this too.”

Lessen lost the door before the clerks finished burning the first cabinet.

The wood split around the bar. He fell back with a cut across his forehead, and a guard in a dark half-mask came through with his blade held low. Lyra had a cabinet between them. She pushed it toward him, but it was fixed to the wall. The drawers spilled instead, scattering letters across the floor.

The guard stepped onto them and slipped.

Lessen struck him with the hammer. The blow landed on the shoulder, breaking something beneath the coat. The man shouted, swung blindly, and caught Lessen across the thigh. Both went down.

A second guard appeared in the doorway. Lyra seized the copper fireguard and thrust it toward his face. He knocked it aside. The impact drove pain through her right arm so sharply that she nearly dropped it. Callista put both hands on the frame beside hers.

Together they held it between them and the blade.

“Back,” Lyra said.

They moved toward the stair. The guard followed, unwilling to commit himself to the narrow point between the overturned table and the burning papers. Behind him the woman clerk had gone to her knees beside Lessen. She was pressing her folded apron against his leg.

The other clerk ran out through the original doorway.

Lyra could not hold the fireguard much longer. Her wrist shook. Callista’s crooked finger had turned white around the copper.

Then the antlerless stagehand came up the stair carrying a rail. He hit the guard across the shin. When the man fell, he struck him again across the side of the head.

The second blow made a different sound.

The stagehand stood over him with the rail lifted. Lyra told him to stop. He lowered it slowly, looking at the body as though it had appeared there by mistake.

“Help Lessen.”

He looked at her.

“Now.”

The clerk guided his hands beneath the injured man’s shoulders. Callista took his feet. Lyra wanted to say she should not lift, but Callista was already moving, her face set against the effort.

The Gallery smelled of scorching paper and hair. Embers caught the loose sheets on the floor. Lyra took the last portfolio from the desk, wrapped its strap around her good wrist, and followed the others out.

Behind her, the woman clerk saved her spectacles, then pulled a drawer free of the cabinet before the fire could reach it. She took a box of records from the drawer and held it out.

Lyra stopped.

“The payments,” the woman said. “You missed them.”

Lyra took the box.

At the stair Callista looked back and saw it added to the load. She turned away and bent to lift Lessen’s feet again.

Chapter 31 The unfinished scream

They lowered Lessen onto a stretcher at the working-stair landing. Blood ran from his trouser leg onto the edge of the canvas. He kept asking whether the other man was dead. Nobody who had seen the blow would answer him.

“Down to Marda,” Lyra said. “Keep the cloth on his leg.”

The woman clerk had caught up with them at the landing and pressed her apron back against the wound. Now she looked up, holding the sodden cloth in place.

“Who takes this when I arrive?”

“Tell her I sent you. She’ll find someone.”

“Who holds the cloth while I fetch her?”

Lyra looked at the blood soaking the apron. She knelt beside it and found the narrow place from which it came, a cut above the knee that had opened a vessel without taking the leg apart. She put two fingers beside the clerk’s hand.

“Move only when I tell you.”

Her fingers shook as she began the old working. Through the music from the stage she heard the faint wrong music below. Still, the edges of the cut drew together. Blood rose between them, slowed, and stopped.

The clerk watched her face.

“Now,” Lyra said.

They changed the cloth. Lyra bound it firmly and told Lessen he must be carried even if the leg felt better. He asked about the guard again.

“I don’t know,” she said.

He closed his eyes. The clerk lifted the foot of the stretcher with the stagehand. Callista stood aside to let them pass. Her hands were red to the wrists.

They took the short turning stair to Store Six, easing the stretcher round the bend. The clerk went backwards, finding each step with her heel. Aria’s carriage would have to go by the broad freight stair beyond the salt passage, on the rails laid for it.

From below came the sound of metal being dragged across stone.

Lyra picked up the portfolio. She gave it to Eda at the lower-office door, with the keys she had taken upstairs.

“Keep these here. Nobody removes them.”

Eda looked at Callista before accepting the keys.

Lyra saw the look. She went past them toward the vault.

The maintenance man had already joined the crystal’s inner frame to the Prince’s cradle. Its rear clamps stood open. Two handlers were tightening the front ones with a bar passed through their screw heads.

Katerina stood beside the washstand holding the key ring. At the sight of Lyra she closed her hand around it.

“You’re late,” the maintenance man said.

“The first interlude hasn’t ended.”

“He wants her ready.”

The crystal filled more of the room now that its curtain had been drawn aside. Lyra could see the raised hand, the strand of hair across Aria’s eye, the open mouth. Beside the base a fresh bottle hung beneath the silver channel. It was nearly full.

Callista went directly to it.

The maintenance man caught her wrist.

“Leave that.”

Katerina put her knife beneath his chin.

“Take your hand off her.”

He froze. One handler turned. The other still held the tightening bar, uncertain whether he could release the load without loosening the clamp.

“Katerina.”

“Let go.”

He did.

Callista stepped back, pressing her wrist. Katerina kept the knife where it was.

“Tell them to undo the front clamps.”

“We can’t. The carriage—”

“Tell them.”

The man swallowed against the blade. “Loosen. Half turn. Together.”

The handlers obeyed. Lyra called for Berris.

The carpenter came through the doorway with the replacement wheels on a board between her and Orrel. Two carriers followed with the padded braces, one still catching his breath from the delayed food cart. They had changed their coats since the receiving court; the chalk marks on their cuffs were the same ones they had used in rehearsal. The antlerless stagehand returned from taking Lessen to Marda, wiping blood from his hands. Behind him came Vessa’s daughter, flushed from running, with the last bearer beside her.

“The dragon,” the girl said breathlessly. “They wouldn’t move its tail.”

“All six?” Berris asked.

“All six.”

“Then hands where we practiced.”

They put on the thick padded gloves and pressed the prepared wax into their ears, leaving enough open to hear Berris. Lyra pulled on her middle pair and checked both seams. The leather resisted her stiff right hand. Callista helped settle the cuff without touching the dressing above it.

The maintenance man’s gaze moved from their faces to the new wheels and the padded braces.

“You’ve never moved this,” he said.

“Neither have they,” Lyra answered, looking at the Prince’s handlers.

He opened his mouth, shut it, and glanced toward the silver channel.

“What must stay attached?” she asked.

“The inner bands. The lower sounding plate. Don’t lift it by the ribs.”

“Show Berris.”

Katerina moved the knife enough to let him turn his head. He pointed. Berris studied the fittings and asked three questions Lyra did not understand. His answers grew steadier.

“Can we stop the drawing?” Lyra asked.

“Not while the channel’s open.”

“Then close it.”

“The collecting valve is under the rear band. It goes in sequence.”

“What sequence?”

He looked toward Katerina’s knife.

She lowered it. “Show her.”

They used a tool from the maintenance shelf, a narrow fork of bone worn smooth at its handle. The man turned one fitting, waited for the little bottle to cease trembling, then turned another. A third adjustment brought a thin metal leaf against the channel’s mouth. The last drop gathered, held, and was drawn back into the crystal.

Callista covered her face.

Lyra stood beside Aria’s raised hand.

“We’ve stopped it,” she said. “We’re moving you out of his control. We will keep the crystal whole.”

One note sounded from within.

Berris heard it. Her hands stopped on the front brace.

“That was her?”

“Yes.”

“Tell her we know she’s in there.”

Lyra did.

The first resistance came at the bend outside the vault, between them and the hoist. Corven’s shell took the shouting man’s voice before the alarm reached the working stair. Lyra heard the shout end, then a hard impact and the noise of a body striking the wall.

She could not see around the doorway. She could see Corven’s eel in its vessel on the shelf beside the curtain. Its thin body lay against the glass, stretched in an angle that made the small pot seem suddenly too shallow. The cloudy water in the cracked shell shivered beside it.

Berris had removed the outer cradle’s front axle. Orrel lifted on her count while the other bearers held the weight. Lyra stood by the doorstop, watching a bolt travel past the edge of its socket. It cleared by the width of a fingernail.

“Down,” Berris said.

The new wheel assembly took the load. Nothing broke. Somebody let out a breath and laughed.

Outside, Corven called Katerina’s name.

She gave the maintenance man to Lyra with a look. Lyra pointed him toward the washstand.

“Sit there. Hands where I can see them.”

He sat. Callista stood beside him holding the bone fork. It would not make a useful weapon. He seemed aware of this, but remained where he was.

They changed the rear assembly. It went less well. The old axle pin had swollen with salt and would not come out. Berris struck it gently, then less gently. The crystal’s low sound changed with each blow. Lyra felt the blows in her teeth. She was watching Aria’s mouth when Berris raised the hammer again.

“Stop,” Lyra said.

“I need it out.”

“Not like that.”

Berris looked at the maintenance man. He pointed to a jar on the shelf. She smelled it, applied a little to the pin, and waited while it dissolved the white crust.

A blade scraped stone beyond the doorway. Katerina whispered an order. “You don’t give orders here,” a man shouted. His answer ended in a grunt.

The pin came free.

They took the weight again. This time the rear brace shifted inside its padding. A metal corner touched the lower band.

The sound that came from the crystal was so sharp that Lyra dropped to one knee.

A fine white line appeared beside Aria’s foot. It traveled upward, stopped, and divided.

Every lamp in the room bent its flame toward it.

“Don’t move,” Lyra said.

The bearers held. Orrel’s face had gone red. The weight was still partly in their hands; they could not keep it there indefinitely.

The maintenance man was standing.

“The rear band,” he said. “Tighten it.”

Callista caught Lyra’s sleeve.

Two notes sounded from the crystal. Faint, separated, unmistakable.

“No,” Lyra said.

“The band takes the strain,” the man insisted.

“Ask,” Callista said.

Lyra looked at Aria. The woman’s face had changed in the fractured light. It might have been movement, or might have been the new line passing across it.

“Will tightening the rear band hurt you?”

One note.

“Should we lower the rear onto its pad?”

Nothing.

Orrel made a sound deep in his chest. Berris put her shoulder beneath the handle beside his.

“Ask the other end,” she said.

“Should we lower the front first?”

One note, barely audible.

“Front first,” Lyra told them.

The front pair lowered a fraction. The crack stopped whitening. Berris adjusted the pad beneath the rear band without taking its pressure. She moved it until the edge lay directly under the fitting the maintenance man had shown her.

“Now?” Lyra asked.

One held note thinned into a ragged breath, without a second beginning. The crystal clouded around Aria’s mouth. A thin red line appeared beneath her nose.

“That’s enough,” Callista whispered. “Don’t make her keep doing it.”

They lowered the rear. The band settled. The flames straightened in the lamps.

For several moments no one moved.

Lyra went close enough to the crystal to feel cold reaching through her gloves.

“We’re going to keep this position,” she said. “You don’t have to answer.”

The faint sound inside had become irregular. Callista stood beside the raised hand and began the three-note exercise, quietly, without attempting the fourth. The sound within did not follow. She repeated it anyway while Berris fastened the new pads in place.

Corven fell against the doorway as the carriage began to move.

Blood soaked the right side of his coat. He had his chain wrapped once around a forearm armored in white salt. The rest of the Worn stood beyond the curtain, one shoulder pinned awkwardly against the bend. Corven pulled, bracing his sound leg, and the chain tightened. A cracking sound came from the Worn’s neck. It dropped without becoming any less heavy.

Katerina caught Corven before he fell.

“Knife,” he said.

“I see it.”

The wound lay below his ribs, on the side opposite the older scar from the bridge fighting. He pushed Katerina’s hand away long enough to catch the shell from the shelf and thrust it into his pocket. She retrieved the eel’s vessel for him.

“Back to the bend?” Lyra asked.

He tried to straighten. His face gave her the answer before he spoke.

“For a little.”

“Take two people.”

“Not the bearers.”

She looked toward the men who had come with the Prince’s maintenance crew. One had fled when the fighting began. The other stood by the door, pale and silent.

“Will you help hold the corner?”

He shook his head.

“Then go up the working stair to the west court. Keep your hands empty.”

He went so quickly he nearly fell.

Corven watched him go. He did not look at Lyra for permission before taking up his chain again.

The carriage reached the door. They removed one folding handle, crossed the threshold, put it back. The rear bearer checked the stop at the cross-passage door beyond the bend. He left it wedged while they approached the hoist; they might still need to come back this way. Lyra remained last with the maintenance man.

“Come with us.”

“He’ll kill me.”

“Which way would you like to go?”

The man looked at the empty place where the crystal had stood. The collecting bottle remained on the wall, full and useless to anyone who wanted what had made it.

“With you,” he said.

Lyra took him by the elbow and led him out.

At the bend Jorin stood facing Corven. His hands were empty. He looked past the chain at the carriage.

“Load,” he said.

Corven lifted his free hand. Lyra caught his wrist.

“Wait.”

Katerina stepped forward. “Jorin. Clear the passage. Cargo through.”

He looked at her. The old two taps sounded against the wall. He moved one foot, then the other, putting his back against the stone to make room.

Lyra passed so close that the salt at his jaw touched her sleeve. She wanted to say Ada’s name. She did not know what it would make him do, and the carriage was already moving.

She went on.

The hoist had begun to rise without them.

Two Court men stood at its winding gear. One turned the handle while the other watched the stair. Above, a stage opening widened, letting warm light fall down the shaft. The Prince stood beyond the gear, one hand on the rail, waiting for a load they were meant to put beneath his feet.

Katerina stopped at the bottom step.

“Hold,” she whispered.

The bearers held. The carriage’s front wheels rested on the flat stone below the hoist room. Behind them the cross-passage stood open, with the salt stores and freight stair beyond it. Corven came up last, moving badly.

The Prince looked down at them.

“Why isn’t it on the platform?”

“The rear band shifted,” Lyra said. “It can’t go up.”

“Then correct it.”

“No.”

He looked at Katerina. She stood with the eel’s vessel under one arm and her knife in the other hand. Blood had dried on the knife’s guard.

For a moment he stared at her. Then he took his hand off the rail.

“Bring her here.”

Neither Court man moved. The one at the gear glanced toward his companion.

Lyra saw the uncertainty and used it.

“Madam Serafine has been told what he intends. If that crystal breaks beneath the stage, ask him where your people will stand.”

The Prince reached toward the nearer man. Katerina threw her knife.

It struck the Prince’s forearm and remained there. He looked at it as though it were a misplaced tool. The man at the gear let go of the handle.

The hoist dropped a hand’s breadth before its catch engaged. The impact shook the room. A crack appeared in the painted plaster above the stair, tracing the edge of a ward worked into the wall. Fine dust fell on Lyra’s hair.

“North freight stair,” she told Berris.

They began turning the carriage. It was the practiced turn, the one the first wheel had failed. This time the wheel moved cleanly. The bearers guided the rear past the wall with an inch to spare.

The Prince pulled the knife from his arm. No blood came with it. Salt fell to the floor. The cut opened wider as he turned his wrist. White ridges shifted inside it, withdrawing past one another into a depth his forearm could not contain. Lyra heard the dry rubbing again. He drew his sleeve over the wound, and the sound stopped.

“You don’t have a place to put it,” he said.

“A dry room within my lease. The floor is sound.”

“You don’t have people who can hold it.”

“They are holding it.”

He looked past her toward the stairs. From the court came a shout, answered by several others. The man who had been guarding the working gate ran in, wet to the waist.

“The Cut’s closed,” he said. “Chain across. They won’t let the boats through.”

The Prince turned on him. The watchman stopped short of the rail.

“Who?”

“Odel’s people. They say the landing is under—”

“I know whose people they are.”

The Prince looked at the crystal before going to the window. The new fracture showed plainly from above, a white branching line beside the foot. His gaze moved from it to the dust falling from the ward, then to the empty place where his maintenance men should have stood. One lay at the bend. Another was behind Lyra, holding the little book of fittings against his chest.

The Prince raised a hand as though to order the Court men forward. The nearer man looked at the fracture and took an involuntary step back. Through the hoist opening the audience applauded something it had been shown. The sound made the damaged crystal tremble in its pads.

The Prince lowered his hand to the rail. A white crust spread beneath his fingers. He watched it reach the first joint in the wood, then lifted his fingers one at a time.

He went to the narrow window overlooking the basin. Lyra could see nothing through it but the reflected light on his face. He remained there while the carriage cleared the turn.

A messenger entered from the public side with Serafine’s seal. He spoke into the Prince’s ear. The Prince listened, looking at Katerina all the while.

When he came back to the rail, he laid her knife on it.

“Keep that,” he said. “I wouldn’t want you to feel helpless.”

She did not move.

He went out through the public passage with the messenger. The two Court men followed after a moment. The watchman remained, staring at the knife until Corven told him to get out of the way.

They brought Aria down the broad north freight stair one flight at a time. Beside the upper landing, the narrow return branch led away toward Store Six; the carriage could never have taken that turn. Berris fed the rope around the tested post while Orrel watched the pads and the other bearers steadied the frame. Lyra could not help with the weight. She stood below each landing and kept people from crossing their path.

On the second turn the maintenance man told them to stop. One pad had crept outward. They adjusted it while the rope held, then continued. The extra time cost them the low-tide exit. By the time they reached the measuring room, water had covered the painted cutoff beneath the canal arch.

Odel’s runner arrived to say the third mark was nearly reached. Lyra thanked him and gave him the last of the agreed payment. He looked at Aria, then deliberately looked away.

Berris closed the measuring-room door. Its hinges shook against the low sound within. The maintenance man knelt beside the damaged band and began fitting supports cut from the timber they had brought for the journey.

Callista sat on the floor with her back against the wall. Katerina set a cup of water beside her, then lowered herself next to it. Her hands had begun to tremble.

Lyra knelt by Corven. He opened his coat. The wound beneath his ribs was still bleeding.

“Let me.”

“Ordinary way,” he said.

“Yes.”

She pulled off the gloves, cleaned the wound, brought the edges together where she could, and bound the rest. The deeper injury would need someone with more time and steadier hands. He watched her without speaking. His eel moved slowly in the vessel beside his boot.

From the refuge stair came a sound like furniture being dragged. Lyra lifted her head.

Then Marda called her name.

Chapter 32 The refuge closes

Marda had lost her tray.

It was the first thing she told Lyra, standing in the doorway with one hand against the frame. She sounded annoyed, as though the tray mattered more than the water running from her skirts.

“I put it down by the beds. Somebody moved it. I couldn’t find it when he started.”

“Started what?”

“The song.”

Corven had begun fastening his coat. He stopped with the buttons halfway done.

“Where are the people?” Lyra asked.

“Where you left them.”

“The stair?”

Marda looked at her. “Jorad says no one is to use it.”

Lyra stood too quickly. The room tilted. She caught the doorframe beside Marda’s hand and waited for the floor to settle.

“Why?”

“He says there are men in the court.”

“There were. The Prince has gone.”

“He says they will come back.”

Lyra looked toward the little bed they had made for Callista. She lay with her eyes closed, one hand curled beside her mouth. Katerina sat on the floor next to her. Berris remained at Aria’s frame with the maintenance man, adjusting the new supports. None of them could leave what they were doing without handing it to someone else.

“I’ll go,” Lyra said.

Corven rose. Blood darkened the fresh binding beneath his shirt.

“Stay here.”

He looked at her.

“If they come back for the crystal, Katerina can’t hold them alone.”

“Maeron has our people.”

“I know. I’m going to speak to him.”

“You’ve spoken.”

She thought of his bowl beneath her hand. His precise promise about the door. She did not want Corven beside her while she asked for it to be honored. She recognized the wish and disliked him for making it visible.

“I need you where we agreed.”

Corven looked past her at Marda. The older woman stood with her palm still against the frame. She had begun to breathe through her open mouth.

“Go,” he said to Lyra. “I’ll be along.”

It was not agreement. She had no time to turn it into one.

Jorad stood at the top of the narrow return stair. The bar lay across its door again, on the side away from the refuge. Beside him, a young follower held a lamp so tightly that oil spilled over his fingers.

“Take it off,” Lyra said.

Jorad bowed his head. “My lady, there’s fighting above.”

“Then we put people somewhere else. Take it off.”

“The Cantor says—”

“I heard what he said when we were here together.”

Jorad looked at Marda. The older woman put her hands on the bar and lifted it out of its sockets. Neither man stopped her. She set it against the wall, turned the handle, and pulled.

The door did not move.

Lyra put her good hand beside Marda’s. The wood was wet. She recognized the pressure on its other face, deep and patient, gathering in every gap in the frame.

She pulled harder. The latch moved. The door stayed shut.

“What has he done?”

Jorad’s eyes had begun to shine.

“He’s keeping us.”

Marda struck him across the face.

The lamp bearer gasped. Jorad put a hand to his cheek. He did not strike back.

“My son’s coat is down there,” Marda said. “I brought it because it was dry. I was going to give it to somebody who needed one. Open that door.”

“I can’t.”

The answer came with sudden force. Jorad looked frightened at last, but Lyra could not tell whether he feared Marda or the admission itself.

“There is another way,” he said. “The water door.”

“The tide is over it.”

“It will come back down.”

Lyra looked at his face. He meant it. He would leave them there until the tide turned.

From beyond the door a child shouted. The words were indistinct. Lyra heard an adult answer, then something strike the lower panel.

“Who has the other key?”

“There isn’t a key.”

“The working stair entrance.”

“The Cantor is there.”

She turned and ran for it.

The main entrance to Store Six remained open.

For an instant Lyra felt foolish. Water shone on the lower steps, and a crowd filled the raised path, but the doorway itself stood clear. A person could have climbed it. Then she tried to descend and found a man sitting across the third step with both hands locked around the rail.

“Move,” she said.

He shook his head.

“People need the stair.”

“I can’t.”

She crouched beside him. He was one of the deep crews, broad through the chest, capable of carrying a coil she would have struggled to drag. Now he held the rail as though the empty air above him might tear him away.

“Look at me.”

He did. His pupils were wide. Water beaded along his lower eyelids without falling.

“Can you move one hand?”

“I’ll fall.”

“You’re sitting on a step.”

He made a small desperate sound and clung to the rail.

Behind him a woman tried to climb past with a child. Her shoulder caught the wall. She stepped backward into another person, and the child began to scream.

Lyra reached for the seated man’s wrist. His skin was slick. At her touch his grip tightened enough to whiten the fingers.

Neris appeared below him, water to her knees. She had no shoes. Her wet hair clung to her face.

“Don’t pull,” she said. “He’ll hold harder.”

“How long have you been here?”

“Long enough.”

She climbed beside the man, put her foot against the lower rail, and placed his free hand on her shoulder.

“Here,” she told him. “I’m another piece of the wall.”

He began to shake.

“I won’t move quickly. You know I won’t.”

She waited until his weight came toward her. Then she shifted one foot, no more than the width of her sole. He moved with her. Lyra helped the woman and child past while Neris worked him up the stair a little at a time.

At the bottom Maeron stood beside the water channel. His hands rested at his sides. His chest scarcely moved with the low song. Lyra felt it pass through the stone beneath her feet and climb into her knees.

“You must stop,” she said.

He looked up. His face changed when he saw the bandage around her arm.

“Who hurt you?”

“The yard. Yesterday.”

“I should have come.”

“You’re here. Stop this.”

The pressure continued. Around him people sat on the raised walk with their feet in the water. Some had closed their eyes. A woman leaned against her husband’s shoulder, smiling with an expression of exhausted relief. Farther away, two boys were trying to lift a chest onto a bed whose legs had already disappeared beneath the rising water.

Maeron followed Lyra’s gaze.

“They’ll lose the blankets,” he said. “The chest won’t help.”

“Then let them take the blankets upstairs.”

“They can’t bear it yet.”

“They can decide that when they get there.”

A tremor passed through the room. The water’s surface flattened. Lyra felt her chest cease wanting air.

She had known longer breaths. She had known the stillness that came with them. This reached farther. For several heartbeats there was no need to draw another breath, and the absence of need spread through her with a pleasure so complete that she forgot the pain in her arm.

Then she tried to breathe anyway.

Her throat would not open.

She put a hand to it. Maeron stepped toward her.

“Don’t fight the interval.”

She backed away. Water struck the backs of her knees. She had not realized she had come down from the raised path.

He held out his hand. The same hand had waited beside her first successful working, offering nothing until she chose to take it.

“Let it pass through you.”

Behind him the woman who had been smiling began to choke. Her husband tried to lift her head. She pushed his hands away and slid from the walk into the deeper channel, where she lay with her face just above the water. Her expression eased immediately.

The two boys stopped lifting the chest. One looked delighted. The other began to cry.

Lyra’s throat opened. She drew a great breath that hurt down to the roots of her ribs. Her own shadow lay heavy in the water, thicker than the shadows of the bedposts around it.

She looked at Maeron.

“You’re doing it again.”

“They need enough to endure what is coming.”

“They didn’t ask.”

“They asked us to keep them safe.”

“Some asked to go home.”

His face tightened with grief, real enough that she hated seeing it.

“Look at them,” he said. “Tell me which of their homes will receive them.”

Marda reached them by the raised path. She had left her shoes on the stair. Her skirts dragged heavily, and she paused twice to press a hand against the wall. Behind her, the lamp bearer came down carrying the bar from the north door.

He looked at Maeron with open worship.

“I felt it above,” he said. “Even there.”

Maeron smiled at him. The young man began to weep.

Lyra could still feel the change moving through her. Water no longer seemed cold against her legs. The air beneath the roof had become thin and irritating, while the damp darkness beneath the maintenance walk called to her body as plainly as a bed after a long illness. She wanted to kneel. She wanted to stop holding herself against it.

Marda caught her hand.

“Stay up,” she said.

Lyra looked at the older woman’s face. Marda’s jaw shook with the effort of speaking. She felt it too.
