A child nearby had begun asking his father why Lyra’s dress was floating when the water did not move. The father told him to be quiet. The child asked again, louder, and his mother laughed and then cried in the same breath.

“They need the dry stair,” Marda told Maeron.

“Some do.”

“Then open it.”

“The upper door will open when they can bear the ascent.”

“Who decides?”

Maeron looked at her. “You know what happens when we bring a diver up too quickly.”

“This isn’t a dive.”

“It is the same body.”

Marda took a step toward him. Lyra held her back. The pressure beneath Maeron’s voice had changed. People were turning toward him.

A man from the deeper crews stood beside him. He had removed his coat. Wet marks spread across his chest like the outlines of ribs seen through skin.

“Let him finish,” the man said. “For once let him finish helping us.”

Marda looked at him. “Your daughter is on the stair.”

“She’ll understand.”

“She is six.”

The man looked away. He remained beside Maeron.

Some closed their eyes more firmly. Some watched the stair. A woman pushed her small bundle toward Marda and whispered that it held medicine for her mother. She did not ask to go herself.

Lyra took the bundle.

“Where is she?”

“By the north door. She can’t climb.”

“We’ll carry her.”

The woman began to thank her. Maeron watched the exchange with a sadness that made Lyra want to strike him.

“You can help us,” she said. “Stop the song. Open the dry way. Let the people who want to stay stay afterward.”

“If I stop now, they’re between what they were and what they need to be.”

“They were between before you began.”

“And you were keeping accounts of the cost.”

She flinched. He saw it and lowered his voice, though the pressure beneath it did not lessen.

“I don’t say that to hurt you.”

“Then listen to what you’re saying.”

For a moment she thought she had reached him. He looked toward the people in the water, the children being passed from hand to hand along the raised path, the woman whose breath only came easily with her cheek nearly submerged. His mouth softened.

“We can give them enough,” he said.

He turned toward the maintenance walk.

Lyra understood where he was going before he reached the inspection gate. She moved after him, but two followers stepped between them. Neither raised a weapon. One was the man whose daughter waited on the stair. The other had helped carry furniture down that morning.

“Please,” the second man said. “Don’t take this from us.”

Beyond their shoulders Maeron lifted the gate. The catch clicked four times. He reached through and began turning the sluice wheel.

Water struck the lower channel with a new, heavier sound.

The lamp bearer knelt. Around him others followed. Lyra felt the movement in her own knees and braced herself against the edge of a bed.

On the stair above, Corven called her name.

Chapter 33 The way back up

Corven came down without his coat.

He had left it somewhere above, and the bandage showed dark against his shirt. His chain hung from one hand. In the other he carried a carpenter’s hammer. He looked at the water, at the people on the steps, and then at Lyra.

“The return door?”

“Held shut.”

“Where’s the bar?”

“Off.”

He looked toward Maeron. The Cantor had both hands on the wheel. Water poured across the lip of the channel, lifting a blanket from the floor. A little wooden bowl turned in the current and caught against a bed leg.

Lyra tried to reach Corven. The follower before her moved aside, then put out a hand as though he regretted allowing it. She pushed past him.

“Who’s with the crystal?”

“Katerina. Berris. The bearers.”

“They aren’t guards.”

“Neither are the people down here.”

“You were meant to hold—”

“I am bringing them home.”

He scarcely had breath for it. Lyra remembered how readily she had promised him that, how pleased she had been with herself.

“Take the upper frame out,” she said. “Berris’s people have the hinge punches.”

“Already sent for them.”

He went past her toward the return stair. People moved to make room. Some reached for him. He let them touch his shirt, his arm, the coil of chain, but he did not stop walking.

Marda had found two benches and laid them between the beds and the raised path. The wood bent under the first person who crossed. She made the next wait, then asked the young lamp bearer to stand in the water and steady the middle.

He looked toward Maeron.

“It’s a bench,” she told him. “You know how.”

He put down his lamp and got beneath it. When the woman crossing thanked him, he began crying again.

Lyra lifted the medicine bundle above the water. Its owner pointed toward the north wall, where an old woman sat on a bed with her feet drawn up. The mattress had begun to float.

“I’ll take one end,” Lyra said.

“You can’t.”

“I have another arm.”

Together they drew the woman onto a folded blanket. She was lighter than Lyra expected, but moving her required both hands. Pain tore along the cut. Lyra lost her grip, caught the edge again, and felt the stitches part.

The old woman looked at her apologetically.

“I’m slipping.”

“We have you.”

Neris came from the main stair. She took Lyra’s corner without comment and helped carry the woman across the benches. Her bare feet gripped the slick wood. Beneath them the water darkened around Lyra’s blood.

“Keep that arm down,” Neris said when they reached the raised path.

“I need it.”

“Then keep it attached.”

For a moment they looked at each other. Neris smiled, tired and furious, and Lyra laughed despite the pain. The sound came out with a hollow edge that made the old woman turn her head.

They put the woman on the stretcher by the wall. Marda brought the medicine and tucked it beneath her hand. The woman held it as though it were a ticket that must not be lost.

At the top of the return stair Corven struck the doorframe.

The hammer blows came through the stone, distinct from Maeron’s deeper pressure. Jorad stood near him, one arm across the passage. He was no longer attempting to bar it. He seemed unable to decide what else to do.

Berris’s stagehand drove a punch beneath the upper hinge. Corven steadied the tool, then stepped back as another blow lifted the pin. The door did not move. Pressure held it against the frame even without the hinge.

“Bottom,” Corven said.

The stagehand went to his knees. Lyra watched from below, keeping the stretcher clear of the steps. She could not reach them without abandoning the old woman, and nobody else was ready to take her place.

Maeron called her name.

She turned. He had left the wheel and stood at the edge of the maintenance walk. The current continued around his ankles.

“The upper air hurts them,” he said. “You can hear it.”

On the main stair a man was coughing so hard he had begun to vomit. Neris held his shoulders while his wife supported him from above. Between coughs, he kept reaching for the next step.

“They can come back down if they choose.”

“After all of that?”

“Yes.”

“Why require it of them?”

“I’m not requiring it.”

He looked at the hammer striking the doorframe.

Lyra put the old woman’s hand into Marda’s. “Stay with her.”

She went back through the water. It was above her knees now. Every step drew cloth against her wound. She had to go around the bedframes; a floating chest blocked the straight route.

The man who had told her to let Maeron finish sat beside the channel with his daughter in his lap. The child was awake, face turned against his shirt. He was speaking to her very quietly. Lyra stopped.

“Does she want to stay?”

“She wants to be with me.”

“Will you go up with her?”

He looked at the maintenance walk. Maeron watched them from it.

The child lifted her head. “It’s making my ears.”

“Making them what?” Lyra asked.

She shook her head violently, unable to find the words.

The man put his palms over her ears. It did not help. She began hitting his wrists with the small helpless anger of a child whose parent has failed to understand something obvious.

“Go up with her,” Lyra said.

He stood. The water seemed to pull at his legs as it pulled at hers. He took two steps, stopped, then went on toward Marda. Maeron watched him leave.

“I would have helped her,” he said.

“Then do it. Open the door.”

Above, the frame split around the lower hinge. The crack ran into softened timber where old water had entered. Corven drove the hammer’s claw into it and pulled. Berris’s man braced beside him. Together they tore out a length of the jamb.

The door shifted. Pressure moved across the room like a sudden change of weather. Lyra’s ears closed, opened, and filled with a ringing so loud that she could no longer hear the people on the steps.

Through the gap above came a draft of warmer air.

Maeron looked up sharply.

For the first time that night the low song faltered.

Corven put his shoulder into the damaged door. It came away from the frame and struck the passage wall. He staggered with it. The stagehand caught him by the belt and pulled him clear.

The way stood open.

Marda began calling names. She told each person where to place a foot, whose hand to take, how many steps remained to the first turn. The old woman went first on the stretcher. Jorad put his shoulder beneath one end. Nobody thanked him. Nobody told him he could not.

Maeron drew breath.

Lyra moved toward him.

“No.”

He looked at her with such sorrow that she knew he would continue.

The next pressure drove her against the maintenance walk.

She caught its edge with her left hand. Water struck her mouth. For a terrible instant she breathed it as though it were air. It entered cold and heavy, and her body did not reject it until she tried to stand.

She coughed, bent double. Maeron came to her and put a hand between her shoulders.

“There,” he said. “Let it out slowly.”

She pushed at him. Her right arm failed. He held her more firmly, helping her straighten.

“I know you’re frightened.”

She tried to answer. Water came out instead. He waited, rubbing her back with the same patient motion he had used when an early working left her shaking over the basin.

Above them people cried out. The new change had reached the stair. A woman went to her knees and could not lift them. Someone began laughing with wild relief. A boy whispered that he could see the steps with his eyes closed. Marda’s voice cut through them, calling for hands, for a cloth, for somebody to keep the turn clear.

Lyra drew one usable breath.

“Stop touching me.”

Maeron released her at once.

She hated him for remembering that instruction now.

He stepped back. His eyes moved to her arm, the blood spreading through the wet sleeve.

“You’ve pulled it open.”

“Yes.”

“Come here. I’ll close it.”

“No.”

“Lyra.”

“You can stop all of this.”

“They won’t survive being left as they are.”

“You don’t know.”

“I’ve brought people through this.”

“You’ve brought them where you wanted them.”

He looked away. The low pressure continued beneath his ordinary speech. Lyra could feel it vibrating in the bones of her face.

“I wanted them fed,” he said. “I wanted them warm enough to sleep. When the sailors’ temples closed their doors, I wanted somewhere to put the children. You saw what we had.”

“I saw.”

“You helped me make more.”

“Yes.”

“We can keep them.”

She looked past him at the wheel. Its chalk mark had turned nearly all the way around. The rising tide pressed through the open sluice. If he did nothing further, water would keep coming. If she could close it, there would still be people changed on the stair, frightened in the upper rooms, drawn toward a depth she could not name without feeling it in herself.

“The song first,” she said. “Then the wheel. Then we help them.”

He shook his head.

She did not argue again. She stepped toward the gate.

Maeron caught her left wrist.

It was not a violent grip. It did not need to be. She pulled once and could not get free. He was stronger than she had allowed herself to remember, a man who worked among divers and lifting crews, whose stillness had never meant weakness.

“You’ll hurt yourself.”

“Let go.”

“Let me finish.”

She struck him with her right hand. Pain blinded her. The blow landed badly, more against his collar than his throat, and he took that wrist too.

The cold of his grip brought back the naming, her hand raised in his while the room answered her.

“Listen to me,” he said.

She drove her knee upward. It struck his thigh. He grunted, turned with the blow, and pushed her against the stone sill beneath the raised gate. The edge caught her lower back.

“Stop,” he said, frightened now.

The pressure thickened. Her lungs ceased asking for air. She felt an enormous calm arrive, unwelcome and intimate. She could remain here. She could stop making every movement difficult. Maeron’s hands were steady; the water was no longer cold. Somewhere above, people she loved were shouting in an air she no longer needed to enter.

She looked at the lever beside the gate.

Corven had shown her how it worked. Hold the rope while releasing. Let the gate descend. Anything beneath it stayed beneath it.

Maeron loosened one hand when he felt her stop struggling.

She reached past him for the wheel.

“No,” he said, and turned to keep her from it.

He thrust his right arm through the inspection opening. His forearm lay above the sill, hand closing on the wheel’s rim. He meant to prevent her from turning it. His other hand still held her left wrist.

Lyra struck the release lever with her wounded arm.

The catch jumped.

The gate fell.

Its lower rail hit his forearm with a sound she would hear afterward whenever anything heavy struck stone.

Maeron screamed. The low song broke around the scream but did not end. Pressure slammed against the room’s walls. A bed overturned in the water.

He let go of her wrist and seized the gate’s rope with his left hand. The small counterweight began to descend. The rail lifted a fraction from his trapped arm.

Lyra took out the vial.

She dropped the stopper. There was no time to find it. She tipped a little water into the joint where the gate’s side met the stone, wasting more than she needed, then thrust the vial back into her coat. One hand on the fixed jamb. One on the moving face. The load was upward. The frame was sound.

She spoke the enclosure into the seam.

The gate stopped lifting.

Maeron stared at her.

For one breath she saw him understand what she had learned.

Her right arm would not hold.

She kept her palm against the iron by leaning the weight of her body through it. Blood ran down her sleeve and over her fingers. The gate trembled. Maeron pulled the rope with his free hand, dragging on the counterweight, and the seam answered across Lyra’s teeth.

“Lyra,” he said.

She could not spare the breath to answer.

He was still singing beneath the word. She felt it in her knees. He had not chosen to stop, even now.

Corven came through the water.

He had left his chain at the stair. One hand pressed against his wound. With the other he took the iron rake from the tool rack and thrust its shaft through the gate’s bars, beneath the fixed crosspiece of the nearest side guide. The metal scraped. He shifted it, trying to wedge the shaft where an upward movement would drive it against the crosspiece.

“I can’t hold both,” Lyra said.

“I know.”

Maeron looked at him.

“Corven. Help her.”

Corven pushed the rake farther in. Its iron head caught against the stone. The shaft bowed.

“I’m trying.”

“She’s hurting herself.”

Corven looked at Maeron’s trapped arm. His face had gone gray beneath the blood and water.

“Then stop pulling.”

The Cantor pulled again. The gate shuddered against Lyra’s hands. She made a sound she could not have repeated deliberately.

Corven got both hands on the rake. His bandage opened at the side. He pushed until the iron shaft wedged beneath the guide’s crosspiece.

“Let it settle,” he said.

Lyra eased the working. The gate shifted against the rake, a small movement that brought a cry from Maeron. The iron held the gate against his pulling. Lyra peeled her left hand from the jamb, then the right from the gate.

Her fingers would not close.

She turned toward the rack. The salvage hook hung where she had inspected it, its short wooden handle dark from other people’s work. She took it in her left hand. It slipped. She caught it against her body, wrapped her fingers again, and turned back.

Maeron saw the hook.

“Don’t,” he said.

For a moment she heard no pressure beneath it, only his voice. She remembered the pears he had brought to a lesson, the unchipped bowl, the first time he had smiled when her hands remembered how to heal. She waited for him to stop. Her body waited before she had decided to give him that moment.

The water moved around her knees. The low song continued.

She drove the hook into his throat.

It struck the side rather than the center. He jerked, pulling the point deeper across the soft place beneath his jaw. Blood ran over the handle. He caught her wrist with his free hand and held it there.

She tried to pull away. He came with her until the trapped arm stopped him. His eyes stayed on her face.

Corven caught his free wrist. Lyra pulled the hook out.

The sound ended.

Maeron’s mouth worked once. Blood came from it without a word. His knees folded. The caught arm held him half upright against the gate, head falling toward the water. Corven kept hold of his other wrist until Lyra lowered the hook.

For several breaths nobody moved.

Then Marda shouted that the last stretcher was at the turn.

Corven took the gate’s weight while Lyra worked the rake free of the bars. They raised the gate enough to free Maeron’s arm and let its catch engage. He fell against them with the full weight of a dead man. Lyra nearly went down beneath him.

They laid him on the maintenance walk. His hand fell palm upward. The old spiral scars were visible there, crossing and recrossing the creases.

Lyra reached for the wheel. Corven stopped her.

“Other hand.”

Her right sleeve hung red and open. She used her left. Together they turned the wheel until the chalk marks met. The inflow slowed, then stopped. Water already in the room remained around the beds.

At the stair people were still coughing. The woman who could only breathe easily near the channel lay on a blanket with wet cloths over her face. Her husband sat beside her. Neither had changed back.

Jorad came down and saw Maeron.

He stopped so suddenly that the man behind him nearly fell.

“No,” he said.

Lyra put the hook on the rack. It would not sit straight because her hand was shaking. She laid it on the stone instead.

Jorad knelt beside the body. He touched Maeron’s shoulder, then his face. He looked up at Lyra. His face twisted with hatred, but he stayed on his knees.

“He was bringing us through.”

Lyra said nothing.

“You killed him while he was helping.”

Behind Jorad, a woman began to weep. Farther up the stair someone called Lyra’s title and thanked her. The two voices reached her together.

Corven put his hand beneath her good elbow.

“Come up.”

She looked at the body. She had thought she might need to close the eyes. They were already almost shut.

“Lyra.”

She went with him. On the first turn she stopped to help lift the old woman’s stretcher around the corner, using her left hand beneath the frame. Marda told her to let go. Someone else had it.

At the upper landing the air hurt her throat. She took another breath anyway and found that it went in.

Chapter 34 Possession of the house

At dawn a kitchen woman asked Lyra who would pay for the eggs.

She had brought six baskets to the office outside the measuring room. One contained broken shells packed in straw. The others were intact, though several had been set on the floor too close to the wall and were now wet. Lyra could smell them through the open door, ordinary eggs in a building where nobody had slept.

“They were ordered for supper,” the woman said. “Now everybody wants breakfast.”

“Use them.”

“Who pays?”

Lyra reached for the wage purse. It was empty. She looked at it for a moment, unable to remember where the money had gone.

Tesset took it from her.

“There is another in the box.”

“Then pay her.”

“She’s the cook. The supplier needs paying.”

The kitchen woman waited. Lyra had been capable of this distinction yesterday. Today she could feel the empty purse in her palm after Tesset removed it.

“The receipt,” she said. “Let me see.”

She read it twice. The price was fair. She signed beneath the old authorization and put her own name where the Court’s purchasing officer had expected to put his.

The cook took it, then looked at Lyra’s arm.

“You should get that seen.”

“Yes.”

“People keep saying yes.”

She went away with the baskets. Tesset closed the door behind her and sat Lyra in the chair by the window.

The window looked into the same basin as the measuring room’s little vents. The tide had begun to fall. A boat moved beyond the landing, its oars stirring pale foam. Lyra watched it until the surgeon arrived.

The stitches had pulled out through the skin on one side of the cut.

The surgeon cut away what remained, cleaned the wound, and asked Lyra to describe every movement she had made since the knife entered. Lyra said she could not remember them all. The surgeon told her to remember the ones that hurt.

“There isn’t time,” Lyra said.

“There is this time.”

Tesset stayed. When Lyra tried to get up at the sound of someone calling from the stair, Tesset put a hand on her uninjured shoulder.

“Somebody else can answer.”

She let herself be held still. The surgeon’s needle entered clean flesh beside the torn edge. Lyra looked away. She had tolerated worse things in the last hours; this small deliberate pain made tears rise before she could stop them.

The surgeon waited while she breathed.

“I’m sorry,” Lyra said.

“For what?”

She did not know. The woman continued stitching.

Corven had been taken to a wardrobe room with space enough for a bed. His deeper wound required cleaning that made him curse loudly enough to be heard on the landing. The people carrying food upstairs smiled at the sound. Lyra wanted to go to him. The surgeon kept her until the dressing was complete and the sling arranged.

“No lifting.”

“I understand.”

“No holding something because you call it steadier than lifting. No pushing a door with that shoulder. No working that requires both hands fixed.”

Lyra looked at her.

“Tesset told me,” the surgeon said.

Tesset sat by the window with the treatment accounts on her knee. She did not apologize.

When Lyra was permitted to stand, her legs felt weak. She went to Corven’s room slowly, one hand on the rail.

He lay on his back while Neris cleaned his eel’s vessel in a basin. The creature had curled beneath a piece of broken tile and would not come out. Neris tipped the vessel enough to see it, then restored it carefully.

“He hasn’t eaten,” Corven said.

“Neither have you.”

“I don’t eat raw liver.”

“Not yet.”

He smiled. It faded when Lyra came in.

She sat beside the bed. For a while neither spoke. The empty sleeve of his coat hung from a chair, dark with dried blood. His chain lay immersed in a deep bowl. One link had caught over the rim. Lyra put it back beneath the water.

“Did we get everyone out?” she asked.

“Everyone we could find alive.”

She closed her eyes.

“Two by the channel,” he said. “One on the lower step. Marda has their names.”

“Did they drown?”

“I don’t know what to call it.”

Neris put down the vessel. “We should use their names first.”

Lyra nodded. She would write them when she could hold a pen without shaking.

Corven moved his hand on the blanket. She took it.

They sat that way until the kitchen woman arrived with eggs for both of them.

Ressa brought Tavin on the second morning.

They had not been at the Opera House. Tavin had gone home after leaving the deep crews and spent the Festival evening repairing a net for a neighbor. Ressa had been washing cups at the shore house. She explained this twice, as though Lyra might otherwise find a way to place them in the chamber.

“He dropped the needle,” she said. “That’s how he knew when.”

Tavin sat near the wall. A wet sheen lay across his throat. He swallowed repeatedly, then put his hand against his chest and waited for the urge to breathe to return.

“He couldn’t stand the room,” Ressa went on. “He went into the cellar. We found him sitting in the washing water.”

“Were you alone?” Lyra asked him.

He nodded.

“Could you hear anything?”

“No.”

“Did you know what was happening here?”

“No.”

Ressa looked at Lyra. “Neither did I. I was holding a cup and suddenly I didn’t know how long I’d been standing there. There wasn’t a song. Not where we were.”

“I know.”

“You told him he could leave.”

“Yes.”

“He had the paper.”

Tavin took it from inside his coat. The folds had softened with damp. Lyra recognized her own words: no further permission was needed.

“I meant it,” she said.

Ressa’s mouth tightened.

“It didn’t stop it.”

“No.”

For a while Tavin looked at the paper. Then he folded it again, more carefully than before.

“Is he dead?”

“Yes.”

“Who will teach us what to do now?”

Lyra had prepared herself for blame. The question reached somewhere she had not defended.

“We’ll find what helps. The surgeon is writing down the changes. You can have a room by the basin if it’s easier, or stay with your mother. The food continues.”

Tavin looked at the wall. “He knew things.”

“Yes,” Lyra said. “He did.”

Ressa began to cry. She was angry while doing it, wiping her face with the side of her hand as though tears were another task someone had imposed. Lyra wanted to embrace her. She did not know whether she would be permitted. Instead she moved the water closer and stayed until Ressa could ask what the room by the basin would cost.

“Nothing,” Lyra said.

Tesset wrote it down with no condition about work or worship.

Jorad came for Maeron’s body with four followers.

They had brought clean cloth and a narrow bier. One of the women held a cup wrapped in a handkerchief. She looked at Lyra with a hatred quieter than Jorad’s, and made no effort to hide it.

Marda had washed the body. She told Lyra this in the corridor, before they entered the room where it lay.

“I couldn’t leave him like that.”

“No.”

“I washed the people he sent home too.”

“I know.”

Marda looked exhausted. Her hair was braided tightly, but water had gathered at its roots. She had slept in the lower room because she could no longer bear the warmth of the kitchen loft.

“They want the chapel,” she said.

“They can have it.”

“For the night. They asked.”

“I heard.”

Marda touched the sling. Her fingers rested there lightly, without trying to inspect what lay beneath.

“I don’t know what I think yet,” she said.

“You don’t have to tell me when you do.”

“I expect I will.”

They went in together. Maeron’s throat had been covered. Without the wound showing, he looked like a man who had finally slept after tending everyone else. Lyra stood by the door while the followers arranged the cloth.

Jorad tested the bier’s binding. Then he turned to her.

“You will not speak over him.”

“No.”

“You will not take his cup.”

“I won’t.”

“Nor tell us what we are allowed to remember.”

Lyra looked at the woman holding the wrapped cup. She had begun to tremble. She must have stood in this man’s congregation for years. She must have eaten food he found and listened while he named the missing.

“Take him where you need,” Lyra said.

They lifted the bier. At the door the younger of the bearers stumbled, and Lyra put out her good hand before remembering the surgeon’s order. Marda caught the rail instead. The bier steadied. Jorad thanked Marda and did not look at Lyra.

After they left, the room held a basin, folded cloths, and the dark shape where the bier had rested on the floor. Lyra stood looking at it until Marda took her by the hand and led her to breakfast.

The building could not wait for anyone to finish grieving.

Its doors needed guards who understood which people were allowed to leave. Its kitchens needed food. The stage machinery needed inspection before scenery above the public stair came down on somebody’s head. A hundred unpaid bills surfaced within three days, carried by people who had suddenly discovered the Court could no longer make them wait in silence.

Lyra had the bills brought into the old box office, where there were chairs for the people presenting them. Tesset checked each against what had actually arrived. The ones they could verify were paid first. Lyra had the disputed bills left on a separate table, where their owners could still find them.

Odel came on the fourth morning to collect her chain.

“It wasn’t a gift,” she said.

“We paid for the use.”

“You paid for one tide.”

Lyra arranged the return. The chain had been taken up after the third mark as promised. Its links lay piled beneath the west shed, wrapped around with weeds. Corven’s people had wanted to keep it across the Cut another night. Odel had removed it herself.

“You left us open,” Lyra said.

“I left you what I agreed.”

They looked at each other across the desk.

Odel put down a small basket. Inside were two loaves and a jar of pickled onions.

“For the people in the kitchen. They fed my boy.”

“Which boy?”

“The runner. The one you sent back before the water rose.”

Lyra touched the basket’s handle. Odel turned to leave.

“The Prince’s boats are using the east pier again,” she said. “You should know that.”

“I do.”

“And the Court’s people are buying properties along the outer road.”

“I know that too.”

“Good. People sometimes think one night is all the nights.”

She left with a receipt for the chain and no further promise.

That afternoon the tile maker came for her wagon. Lyra had it washed, its cover patched, and the horse returned to the man who owned it. She had also prepared payment for the lost firing and the spoiled wood.

The woman counted the money.

“The roofers bought elsewhere,” she said.

“I can speak to them.”

“They’ve already laid the tiles.”

“For the next order.”

“They won’t need to mend that roof twice.”

Lyra stood beside the painted sun on the tailboard. Its yellow had been rubbed away in one place by a stretcher’s foot.

“What else can I do?”

The woman put the money in her purse.

“Remember I said no before you needed me to be grateful.”

She climbed onto the wagon and drove it out. Lyra watched until it passed beneath the gate. Then she went back inside to meet the men who would decide what name could be written above it.

Sera brought the old recorder, his daughter, the petition writer, and two merchants whose money had paid for portions of the operation Lyra had never seen. The charter remained in Sera’s case. She opened it on the table but kept the case beside her knee.

The recorder examined the seal with a glass. His daughter sat where she could see the door, as he had requested. He compared the text with his recollection and with the surviving register entries Sera had gathered from other houses.

When he finished, he laid down the glass and rubbed his eyes.

“The instrument is genuine.”

Lyra felt her shoulders loosen.

“The claims will be contested,” he added.

“I know.”

“By people with existing tenancies, by the Court’s purchasers, by creditors whose security was taken against this property after the Coin’s dissolution. I can attest what this says. I cannot make them agree that it extinguishes everything they have done.”

“It doesn’t need to,” Sera said. “We need recognition of an interim administration while the claims are heard.”

The merchants exchanged a look. One asked who would guarantee the building’s ordinary debts. Lyra answered. The other asked who would inspect the accounts. Tesset put a second chair beside her desk and said anyone appointed by the joint signatories could sit there during working hours.

“And armed authority?” the petition writer asked.

Lyra looked at Sera.

“Entrance guards remain under Lyra for now,” Sera said. “Their appointments and pay are recorded. The Coin council may challenge or replace them under the provisional agreement.”

“Replace with whom?” Lyra asked.

“A new watch appointed under three council signatures. The person being relieved need not sign.”

“While a gate is under attack?”

“During an attack, the person at it acts. Afterward, they answer.”

Lyra thought of the gate beneath the Opera House. The iron against Maeron’s arm. She looked down at her own hand and found it lying flat on the table.

The recorder waited. She gave her assent.

They worked until evening. The resulting document was shorter than the charter and longer than anyone wanted. It named an administration, a way to receive claims, a wage guarantee, and limits none of them could yet be certain of enforcing. Each person signed what they were prepared to defend.

Sera dried the ink. She put the charter back in its case.

“I’ll need a copy,” Lyra said.

“You’ll have one.”

“The original should be kept in the building it concerns.”

“No.”

The answer came without anger. Sera fastened the case and stood.

“Two witnesses have copies of where it’s lodged. You won’t be able to close one door and become the only person who can read it.”

Lyra looked at her. Around the table the others had stopped gathering their things.

“Understood,” she said.

Sera left with the case. Lyra remained to count the money needed for the next morning’s bread.

On the sixth morning Lyra went to the washer’s yard.

Ada was sewing a laundry mark into a sheet. She had unpicked the old one and laid its scraps along the table, keeping them together so they could be counted against the returned linen. When she saw Lyra, she put the needle through the cloth and left it there.

“You’ve found him.”

“No.”

Ada’s hand closed over the edge of the table.

“I saw him the night of the Festival. In the passage beside the vault. He was alive when we went through.”

“Did you speak to him?”

“Katerina told him to make room. He did.”

“Did you?”

Lyra looked at the needle. A little loop of thread had caught beneath its point.

“No.”

Ada drew the sheet toward her. “You were bringing the woman out.”

“Yes.”

“He told my mother he’d mend the stair before he took the new job. It’s still loose. Every time I step on it I think he’ll be ashamed when he comes home.”

Lyra put the papers on the table. Tesset had copied a surviving labor tally from the salt stores. It listed crews by number; its identifying sheets were missing. The named list Katerina had taken from Jorin concerned new assignments; his own name was not among them. Two receiving houses had answered her inquiries. A third had returned the letter unopened. None would say where the retained workers had gone after the fighting.

Ada read the first sheet, then the second. She moved her finger down the numbers.

“Which is his?”

“I don’t know.”

“Who does?”

“The clerk who assigned them. He left with the Prince’s people. Katerina is trying to find him.”

Ada folded the sheets and set them beside the scraps of old marking.

“May I keep these?”

“They’re your copies.”

“I’ll ask at the landings.”

“Let me send someone with you.”

Ada shook her head. “Someone who knows you, they’ll tell to come back tomorrow. I can wait where the boats unload.”

Lyra wanted to forbid it. She pressed her good hand against her knee until the wish passed far enough for her to speak.

“Which landing first?”

Ada named it. Lyra knew the woman who sold food beside its gate. She wrote down the woman’s name and the hours when the clerk changed, then explained where Ada could wait under cover. Ada listened and corrected the time of the washing boat. She knew that one better than Lyra.

“Come here on the morning after,” Ada said. “Before the linen goes out. Even if there’s nothing.”

“I will.”

Ada picked up the needle. Lyra stayed while she freed the caught thread, then went back to the office and entered the visit beside the meetings she could not miss.

On the twelfth day, the eggs came without a demand for immediate payment.

The supplier’s boy brought them through the working gate and asked Tesset where to put the receipt. He had been told the new office paid on the day it promised. Tesset showed him the tray. When he left, she let herself smile at Lyra over the top of the baskets.

They ate in the kitchen that evening. Marda made the little pastries again, complaining that nobody could work from such a badly arranged cupboard. Berris drew her a better cupboard on the back of a bill. They argued over its shelves until Corven, propped near the stove with his leg raised, offered to build one so inconvenient that the argument would have a proper subject.

Neris laughed and nearly dropped her plate. Callista managed half a pastry. Katerina sat beside her, speaking so quietly that Lyra heard only the answer: a short, surprised laugh from the sick woman.

For a little while Lyra let herself enjoy the room. People were eating food she could pay for. The doors stood guarded. Nobody drawing tears sat behind the wall, and those who had wanted Maeron’s funeral had been allowed to go and return. When the kitchen grew too hot, Marda opened the lower door instead of asking anyone to endure it.

After the meal, Callista asked for help carrying a chair to the little room where she slept.

“Another chair?” Lyra said.

“One that doesn’t look at the wall.”

The bed had been placed where the surgeon could reach both sides. That left the room’s only chair facing an empty space beneath a shelf. Katerina moved it while Lyra carried the cushion. Callista directed them from the doorway, increasingly particular as the angle improved.

“There. A little farther.”

“If we go farther, you’ll be in the passage,” Katerina whispered.

“Then people can admire my position.”

They laughed. Callista lowered herself into the chair and remained quiet while the small effort passed. Lyra waited by the bed. From here, with the door open, Callista could see the window at the end of the corridor and a strip of sky beyond it. Somebody had been putting flowers on that sill. Today’s were yellow, their stems supported in a cracked jug.

“Who brings those?” Callista asked.

“I don’t know.”

“Find out before someone throws them away.”

Lyra said she would. Katerina went to fetch the medicine, leaving them together.

For a while Callista watched the yellow flowers. Her hand lay palm upward on the cushion. The crooked finger had swollen again. The surgeon had wrapped the joint lightly and left the tip visible so she could watch its color.

“Does it hurt?” Lyra asked.

“Sometimes. Sometimes it feels as if it belongs to a person sitting beside me.”

“Can you move it?”

Callista tried. The smallest joint bent. She looked pleased, then exhausted by the pleasure.

“I used to teach little children to hold a phrase by moving a finger along the table,” she said. “Like a caterpillar. If they lifted it before the note ended, the caterpillar fell off something terrible.”

“What terrible thing?”

“Depended on the child. Soup, generally. They had strong views about soup.”

Lyra pulled a stool beside the bed. She sat without asking to inspect the hand.

“Aria made us do it too,” Callista said. “The grown ones. You couldn’t be grand with a caterpillar on the table. She liked that.”

“Was she grand?”

“Oh, horribly. When she wanted to be. She had a cloak with a lining that cost more than the rest of us spent on clothes in a season. She said the audience could hear cheap cloth.”

Callista smiled at the memory. The smile remained long enough for Lyra to see how young she might have looked without the illness.

“Could they?”

“No. We hid it before a rehearsal. She sang perfectly well in my coat.”

“What did she do when she found out?”

“Made me wear hers. I couldn’t move my arms properly.”

Lyra laughed. Callista began laughing too, then coughed. Lyra reached for the water. Callista accepted it, drank, and rested the cup on her knee until her hand stopped trembling.

“I don’t want her to be only what’s happened to her,” she said.

“She isn’t.”

“I know that. I’m telling you.”

Lyra looked toward the flowers. The last light had moved onto the wall beyond them. Somewhere downstairs a group of workers had begun singing badly over the washing. One voice missed a note and another voice missed it with such determination that it became intentional.

Katerina returned with the medicine. Callista made a face at its smell, then took it without argument. When she had swallowed, she asked Katerina to remain for a while.

Lyra stood. Neither woman asked her to stay. It hurt more than she expected, though there had been no unkindness in it.

At the door Callista said her name.

“The papers. I haven’t forgotten.”

“I know.”

Callista nodded. Then she leaned back against the cushion and looked toward the strip of sky.

Later Lyra carried a plate to the measuring room. The maintenance man had a name, Dorel. She had learned it on the second day and made herself use it ever since. He had agreed to remain while another person learned the fittings. Payment went to his sister as well as to him, so that leaving would not mean beginning with nothing.

Aria’s fracture had stopped spreading. Thin padded braces held the rear band clear of it. The collecting channel remained closed. The crystal still hummed.

Lyra put on the middle gloves.

“We’re finding people who know more about this,” she told Aria. “Sera has sent inquiries outside the wards.”

One note sounded.

Lyra stood beside the raised hand until the plate had gone cold. When she returned upstairs, a messenger waited with a proposal for moving Aria out of the city.

Chapter 35 The vessel is ready

The first proposal had a bad landing.

Lyra found it on the drawing and sent Sera back to ask whether the quay steps could be avoided. A frame carrying the crystal could not be lowered over six wet stone treads and then turned beneath a low lintel. She knew because they had spent an afternoon making a lesser turn work with paving blocks.

Sera returned two days later with a different destination.

“Disused cutting house beside a quarry basin,” she said. “The loading door opens directly to the old lifting floor. No steps. The crane has been examined. They won’t use it.”

“Who examined it?”

Berris raised a hand from the doorway.

Lyra looked up. The carpenter came in carrying a rolled plan and a packet of nails she had forgotten to put down.

“I said the floor would take it. I didn’t say the crane would.”

“So how does it get ashore?”

“Gangway on trestles. Four people on the check lines. Six at the frame. We move at the level tide.”

Lyra unrolled the plan. Measurements ran along its edges in Berris’s blunt handwriting. The doorway had room to spare. The ground beyond it had been cleared of the old saw beds. A shaded area marked where padding and sound-deadening curtains would be hung without touching the crystal.

“Who owns it?”

“A quarry cooperative,” Sera said. “They’ve leased it for six months, paid in advance.”

“By whom?”

“The two merchants who witnessed our agreement. A former member of Aria’s company. Three others whose names are in the account.”

She put the account beside the plan.

Lyra read the amounts. They were enough for the lease, the attendants, food, the vessel, and a reserve. She checked the addition. It was correct.

“Who controls the money afterward?”

“Three trustees. None employed by you, the Prince, or the Court.”

Lyra turned the page.

Katerina stood near the window. Callista sat in the armchair, wrapped in a blanket despite the warm afternoon. She had gained a little strength, enough to climb one flight with help. Her hands remained swollen and awkward. A bowl of sliced pear rested on her knee. She had eaten one piece.

“They’ll have to come here first,” Lyra said. “The people taking charge.”

“They’re downstairs,” Sera answered.

The older woman introduced herself as Avenna. She had once taught breath and phrasing to traveling companies, including one in which Aria had sung before Silkstrand. She did not claim that familiarity would let her cure what had happened.

“I know how she worked,” she said. “I know people who knew her before I did. We can begin there.”

The man beside her repaired glass instruments used in navigation and surveying. His hands were broad, the nails cut short. He had brought padded supports, measuring cords, and a list of things he did not know. Dorel read the list and added two more.

Lyra liked him for doing that. She wished she did not.

They went down to the measuring room together. Before anyone approached the crystal, Lyra explained the gloves, the wax, the straps. Avenna listened and repeated the instructions in her own words. The glassworker asked to inspect the cracked band without touching it. Dorel showed him where the fracture had stopped and how the pads carried the weight.

The supports now looked ordinary. That was the difficulty. Timber, cloth, carefully set wedges. The woman within them had become easier to live beside because her continued captivity no longer required a fresh act every hour.

Avenna stood where Aria could see her.

“It’s Avenna. From the winter company at Orse. You needn’t answer until you’re ready.”

Lyra watched the woman’s face. She waited long enough that the silence became uncomfortable, then asked a question about a rehearsal room whose window had rattled in a particular wind. One note sounded.

Avenna asked whether they had used a blue curtain over it. Two notes answered.

“Red,” she said softly. “I hated it.”

Her hand rose toward the crystal, stopped, and lowered. She had remembered the rule without being reminded.

They did not ask about the transfer that day. Avenna read a little from a book she had brought, an account of a singer arriving in the wrong town with all the company’s money. Callista laughed at a passage before Avenna reached its end. She knew the story.

Lyra stood behind them, listening. Through the crystal the faint tone continued. For the first time since the Festival she was not the person giving Aria news about what would happen next.

The boat had been built to carry scenery.

Its deck was broad and low, with removable rails and covered housings for the check lines. It bore no resemblance to Vessa’s smaller craft except in being a boat. Two men were replacing a length of padding along the central cradle when Lyra arrived. Berris stood over them with a hammer and an opinion about every nail.

The master showed Lyra the route. They would go inland by the wide channel while the tide rose, cross the inner basin at slack water, and make the quarry landing before the current turned. The narrow arches on the coastal route would be avoided. An escort boat would go ahead and another behind. The hired guards had worked for the quarry cooperative before; the vessel’s master knew them by name.

“What if the inner lock is closed?” Lyra asked.

“We have its booking.”

“Bookings can change.”

“Then we tie at the broad pier before it. Plenty of depth, no arch overhead. The carriage stays secured. We wait.”

“Who supplies food?”

The master pointed to three sealed bins. She opened one. Dry bread, dried fruit, salted meat wrapped separately. Enough for longer than the journey required.

“Fresh water?”

He showed her. She checked that too.

Katerina watched from the rail. Her scar looked pale in daylight. She had given up the Prince’s coat and wore a plain one with room to move her shoulders.

“You’re escorting?” Lyra asked.

“As far as the landing.”

“Then staying?”

“For the first week. If Aria agrees.”

“Does she know you would go?”

“Not yet. We’ll ask separately.”

Lyra inspected the carriage points. The repaired band would remain in its present orientation. The transport did not require turning the crystal upright or undoing the inner bands. Berris had built a support beneath the old frame so the weakened section would not carry the weight of a lift. Two fresh ropes ran independently through separate fittings. The spare they had lost on Festival night had been replaced, and then replaced again so that neither failure would leave them without another.

The glassworker had marked the checks in a book. Dorel had written beside each the corresponding check at the receiving room. Both signed that they had walked the route.

“Will this release her?” Lyra asked.

“No,” the glassworker said.

“Can you promise you won’t have to break it?”

“No one has authority to break it on her behalf. That’s in the arrangement.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

“I can’t promise the crystal will never fail. I can tell you what the supports are built to hold and what we will do if the fracture changes.”

He showed her. Some of the choices matched her own. Others were better; she went back and read them again. He had spent the last fortnight on this frame while she was trying to keep the whole building fed.

She read the plan through to the end.

“It’s workable,” she said.

Katerina lowered her eyes. Sera, who had joined them at the landing, let out a breath.

“Then we ask her,” Sera said.

They asked Aria the following morning.

Avenna explained the journey in short portions with long pauses between them. The destination would be outside the city. The crystal would remain intact. There would be new attendants, independent funds, people who could investigate release without answering to the Prince or the Court. They could not promise success. Aria could refuse any new working before it began, and her responses would be heard by more than one person.

Lyra stood beside the raised hand with the questions written on a board. She had insisted that they prepare them, so nobody would confuse a complicated choice with a simple wish to go outside.

“Do you want more time before we ask?” she said.

Two notes.

“Do you understand that this journey moves the crystal, and does not itself free you from it?”

One note.

“Do you want the journey made?”

One note.

Callista closed her eyes. Katerina remained still beside the door.

Lyra looked down at the next question.

“Would you prefer to remain here while the people come to work with you?”

Two notes.

There was no uncertainty in the separation. Lyra waited, then checked the response with the reversed question as they had agreed.

“Do you want to leave this building in the care of the people we have described?”

One note, held as long as Aria could manage without its becoming a strain.

Lyra lowered the board.

Sera wrote the answers down. Avenna signed beneath them. Dorel added his name as a witness. Callista could not hold the pen long enough to write clearly; she made a mark and asked Katerina to witness that it was hers.

Katerina asked her own question afterward. She explained that she would go on the escort if permitted, remain at the destination for a week, then return. Aria could refuse her presence without changing the journey.

For a long time no note came.

Katerina waited. She did not look at Lyra for help.

At last one note sounded.

Katerina bowed her head. When she straightened, her face was wet.

They left Aria to rest. In the passage Sera began speaking about the tide two mornings later. Berris needed the frame accessible before dawn. The attendants would sleep in the building the night before. Callista could travel by a separate boat so her illness would not delay the cargo if she needed to stop.

Lyra listened until they reached the office.

“We need to discuss the release of custody,” she said.

Sera stopped.

“We have just discussed it.”

“The transport. We discussed the transport.”

Katerina turned from the stair.

Lyra opened the office door and went inside.

The accounts lay on the table where Tesset had left them. A kitchen bill stood beside a request for new locks on the east offices. The guards had been paid through the next week. After that, the building would need the receipts from two new storage agreements and a payment held by a merchant waiting to see whether the Court challenged his use of their former quay.

Lyra looked at the papers before sitting down.

Sera remained standing.

“What are you asking?”

“The receiving trustees must agree to consult us before any attempt to open the crystal.”

“They’ll inform everyone Aria asks them to inform.”

“Consult this administration.”

“Why?”

“The result could affect the city.”

“Then the city can be warned. It doesn’t get to prevent her release.”

Lyra rubbed the edge of the sling where it crossed her neck. The cloth had begun to irritate her skin.

“The Prince hasn’t stopped wanting what she can do.”

“No,” Sera said. “Which is one reason to move her beyond his ordinary reach.”

“He’ll know where she’s gone.”

“He may. We have escorts, guards, independent access. You inspected them.”

“What happens when he comes here afterward?”

There was a small silence.

Katerina sat down opposite her.

“He’ll ask whether you’re willing to defend what you took,” she whispered. “As he would with her below your feet.”

“With her here he has something to fear losing.”

“He has already lost her.”

“He believes he can still regain her.”

“And you want him to continue believing it.”

Lyra looked at her. Katerina did not look away.

Outside, someone was practicing scales on an instrument with a dull reed. Three notes, a pause, the same three notes again. A patient sound. The theater had begun to employ people who made those sounds for hours before anyone heard the result.

“I have people in this building who can’t survive another siege,” Lyra said.

“Then let us strengthen the building’s defenses,” Sera answered. “Ask the other signatories for help. Let the escorts remain here after the transfer.”

“There isn’t enough money for everything.”

“The transfer has its own funding.”

“I know. I read it.”

“Then don’t make her pay for what we’re short.”

Lyra stood. The movement brought her face close to Sera’s. The other woman remained where she was.

“You can carry the charter elsewhere,” Lyra said. “You can lodge it among witnesses and ask them to recognize what it says. I have to keep the doors open while they consider.”

“And she has to stay in a crystal until they agree?”

“I didn’t put her there.”

Katerina moved her hand on the table.

“I did,” she said. “I helped. I held her while they fastened the first band. I told myself what the Prince would do if I refused.”

Lyra looked at her.

“I won’t offer you that story as an excuse,” Katerina said. “Don’t offer it to me.”

Lyra went to the window. In the court two children were playing with a wooden hoop. One was the boy whose mother had asked permission to send him away. He had returned with his uncle that morning. She had seen him run into his mother’s arms, both laughing, and had felt the pleasure of having made a place in which that could happen.

If Aria left, the Prince would no longer need to preserve the measuring room. He could burn the west side, buy the guards, starve the kitchens. So could the Court. Some of those things he could do now. She knew that. She could also imagine sitting at the negotiating table without the weight below it, laying out the wages and repair bills while other people decided whether she was worth answering.

She turned back.

“The transfer will not take place.”

Sera’s face changed very little.

“On what authority?”

“The authority that controls the room.”

Katerina stood so abruptly that her chair struck the table. Sera lifted a hand, not to restrain her but to ask for a moment.

“You signed that her preference would govern what we did,” Sera said.

“I signed that it would be part of the decision.”

“You heard the question. You helped write it.”

“Yes.”

“And you know she wants to go.”

“Yes.”

Sera looked at her for a long time. Then she gathered the transfer papers, leaving the independent funding account on the table.

“That remains available,” she said. “So the next time you tell someone there isn’t a way, they can ask to see it.”

Katerina went to the door. At it she turned.

“Callista leaves with me tomorrow.”

“She’s ill.”

“The surgeon has approved a short journey to a quiet house. She has agreed. The room is paid for. I am telling you.”

Lyra nodded.

Katerina waited a moment longer, perhaps for another objection. When none came, she left. Sera followed with the papers held against her chest.

Lyra stood at the window until the children took the hoop out through the gate.

By late afternoon Sera had returned with an order replacing the west gate watch.

The petition writer came with her, and one of the merchants who had paid for the transfer. Four hired guards waited on the street side of the gate. Lyra recognized the woman with the grey braid; she had inspected the escort boat with its master. The woman held the order open while Seldo read it.

He had exchanged his receiving apron for a guard’s coat that pulled across the shoulders. The gate keys hung from his belt. When Lyra came down, he put his hand over them.

“Three signatures,” Sera said. “The appointment is valid.”

Lyra took the paper. Sera, both merchants. The proposed watch had already been paid from a separate account. Its instructions allowed the attendants their usual access and required the receiving court kept clear for the carriage.

The petition writer indicated the clause beneath the names.

“Your signature is not required.”

“I remember.”

The woman with the braid held out her hand for the keys. Seldo looked at Lyra.

“Keep your post,” Lyra said.

He closed his coat over the ring.

Sera took the paper back. “You have been relieved. Your wages already earned are secure. Tesset has the accounts.”

“I’ve worked for her since Ropewalk,” Seldo said.

“You work for the administration now.”

He looked down at the order, then toward the loading floor. Two men there had stopped shifting a crate. One came to stand behind him. Lyra knew both of them; she had brought their pay from her own room when the kettle buyer withheld his money. She had counted it on the desk with Tesset waiting for Seldo’s missing day.

“I’ll finish the watch she gave me,” Seldo said.

The second gate guard moved beside him. The woman with the braid lowered her open hand.

“Shall we enter?” she asked Sera.

There was room between the men for a person to pass. Nobody stood aside. Lyra felt her good hand closing and made herself lay it flat against her skirt.

Sera looked at her. “Tell them to let the new watch through.”

Behind Sera, the merchant caught the petition writer’s sleeve. He spoke quietly, but the waiting court had become quiet too.

“My carters are still in there.”

“Then ask them to come out,” the writer said.

The merchant’s eyes went to the loaded wagons, then to Lyra. “Are they free to go?”

“Whenever they finish.”

“Now, Lyra,” Sera said.

Lyra called for the nearest wagon to be brought forward. Seldo opened the gate for it. The others followed, their drivers watching the hired guards as they passed. When the last was clear, Seldo shut the gate again.

Sera’s guards remained outside.

The merchant took his hand from the writer’s sleeve. “My signature stands. I will pay for the journey. I won’t hire more men to fight these.”

Sera looked down at the order. The woman with the braid waited without touching her weapon.

“Stand back,” Sera told her. “Stay with the vessel.”

The four guards withdrew. The merchant remained long enough to tell Lyra that there would be no further advances against the quay until the council had heard what happened here. His voice shook on the last words. She nodded; she would have to find the money elsewhere.

The petition writer asked Seldo for the time his watch had begun. Seldo answered. The man entered it on his copy, with the names of the guards still at the gate and Lyra’s instruction beneath them.

Sera pinned the council’s order to the board beside the loading hours. Nobody stopped her.

“This stays,” she said.

Lyra inclined her head.

After they left, the man who had come from the loading floor returned to his crate. His companion took the other end. Seldo remained at the gate, one shoulder against the post, waiting for Lyra to tell him when his watch would finish.

Corven came after supper.

He carried his chain in a cloth bag and had put on a clean shirt. The movement of his right arm remained guarded, but he could walk without help. His familiar’s vessel stood in the crook of his elbow, wrapped against the evening chill he could no longer feel properly himself.

Lyra knew what he had come to say before he put the vessel on the desk.

“Katerina told you.”

“Yes.”

“I wanted to explain.”

“Then explain.”

She did. She told him about the Prince’s continued reach, the Court’s money, the people occupying rooms whose alterations she had paid for because ordinary houses made them ill. She told him that trustees with six months’ funding might lose their resolve, that the city might send an officer to seize what those trustees could not defend. She knew the arguments well. Some remained true even as she heard herself use them.

Corven sat through all of it.

When she finished, he looked at the eel. It had emerged from beneath its tile and was moving slowly along the glass.

“Have you found something wrong with the journey?”

“No.”

“With the place?”

“No.”

“With the people?”

“I don’t know them well enough.”

“Does she want to try?”

Lyra looked at the desk.

“You know she does.”

He nodded. “Then I can’t guard her.”

“You’ve guarded her for three weeks.”

“Against men coming to use her.”

“They may still come.”

“Then the people taking her will need guards.”

“You’re going with them?”

“If they ask. If she agrees. After that, I don’t know.”

“You’ll leave the people here to pay for your clean choice.”

Corven looked at her. He did not answer immediately. She wanted him to be wounded enough to remain, and the wish made her angrier.

“There isn’t a clean choice in my bag,” he said at last. “I held Harl while you waited. I knew he couldn’t fight us anymore.”

“You could have stopped.”

“Yes.”

He touched the chain bag. “I can stop this one.”

She looked at his hands. The skin was still wet at the creases. His shadow lay heavily beneath the chair, though the lamp stood on the other side of him.

“You promised to release people when I told you.”

“I did.”

“I haven’t told you to release her.”

“No.” He swallowed. “That’s why I’m leaving.”

The room was very quiet. Lyra could hear the little movement of water in the eel’s vessel.

“You’re owed through the end of the month,” she said.

“I’ll take what I’ve earned.”

“You’ve earned it.”

She wrote the instruction to Tesset with her left hand. The letters were clumsy. She had to stop and begin his name again. He watched without offering to take the pen.

When she handed him the paper, he folded it and put it inside his coat. Then he lifted the vessel.

For a moment she thought he would embrace her. He looked as though he thought it too. Neither moved toward the other.

“Keep the arm still,” he said.

She nodded.

He left. She listened to his steps on the stair until she could no longer separate them from the other movements of the house.

Later she sent for two of the guards who had been receiving-room porters before the Festival. She explained the measuring-room watch, the two-person entry rule, the prohibition on opening the outer door without her authorization. Neither asked where Corven had gone.

They took the keys for the night watch. Lyra retained the master key on the cord beneath her collar.

Chapter 36 Permission to leave

Neris brought the boots back clean.

She set them beneath Lyra’s desk, toes together, and put a little packet of money beside the ink. The boots had been bought when her old pair split after the fire. Lyra remembered authorizing them. She did not remember asking for repayment.

“They were yours,” she said.

“They rub now. Somebody else can use them.”

“And the money?”

“What was left of the advance.”

“You worked the days.”

“I didn’t work all of them.”

“You were hurt.”

Neris looked at her. Water shone faintly along the line of her jaw. Her voice still sounded as though the last part of each sentence had traveled a little farther than the beginning.

“Then take it off what you owe me.”

Lyra pushed the packet back.

“We’ll count properly. You’ll need boots that fit.”

Neris sat in the chair by the window. She had brought a travel bag, one with a new strap and an old patched bottom. It held less than Lyra expected. A folded coat lay over it, light enough for the coast and too light for the inland places from which Lyra had once imagined people could simply send for help.

“Tomorrow?”

“Before noon.”

“The tide will be low.”

“I know.”

Lyra looked at the departure paper. The vessel’s master had signed beneath the day and the fare. Neris’s name appeared without title or profession. Just a woman traveling with one bag.

“Have you seen the room?”

“My aunt has.”

“And described what you need?”

“Yes.”

“Some people say they understand the damp and then discover they don’t mean—”

“Lyra.”

She stopped.

Neris looked around the office. The desk had belonged to a Court factor. Its drawers moved smoothly. The window opened onto a small patch of water bright enough to make the room seem larger. Lyra had put her old blue bowl on one corner. Everything else had been provided by the building or bought from money moving through it.

“You can ask,” Neris said. “I don’t mind the asking. But listen after.”

Lyra set down the pen.

“All right.”

“The room has a stone floor. There’s a door onto the little canal, and I can keep water where I sleep. My aunt lives above. She knows I won’t be the niece she saw three years ago. She says she wasn’t the aunt I remember either, and that we can be disappointed over breakfast.”

Lyra laughed. Neris smiled.

“The work?”

“Rope inspection. Receiving. Teaching the boys to check a line before their employer tells them it’s sound.”

“No deep work.”

“No deep work.”

“If they change the offer?”

“I leave.”

“Will you have enough?”

“That is partly why I’m here.”

Lyra picked up the pen again. Her right arm was out of the sling, but the wound pulled if she wrote too long. She placed the accounts where her elbow could rest and began with the last day already paid.

Tesset joined them with the employment copies and a plate of bread.

She put the bread where both women could reach it, then opened the book. A reply from one of the Salt Ward receiving houses lay among the papers. It said only that Jorin did not work there. Tesset set it beside the notice of Lyra’s next visit to Ada and turned to the wages.

Neris had wages due for the rescue work, additional pay for the nights she spent helping the changed followers, and a share from a recovery completed before the Festival. There was also a deduction entered for damaged equipment.

“That’s wrong,” Neris said.

Lyra looked at it. “Which equipment?”

“The hook at the gate. It bent when the men outside pushed.”

“That came from my yard.”

“The storekeeper said I was using it.”

“You were keeping them from coming through.”

Tesset crossed out the deduction. She left it readable, wrote the reason beside it, and had Lyra initial the correction.

They counted the wages twice. Neris asked for part in small coin and part in a draft that could be exchanged at her destination. Lyra checked the name of the receiving house, then sent a messenger to confirm it still honored the drafter’s mark. Neris rolled her eyes, but she ate the bread while they waited.

The return passage provision occupied longer.

Lyra had arranged for a sum to remain available in Silkstrand if Neris needed to come back. Reading it now, she struck through the place of collection and wrote the name of a house near the destination quay.

“Here,” she said. “You must be able to collect it there.”

Tesset looked at the alteration and nodded.

Neris leaned over the table.

“I might not come back.”

“It can be used for another passage.”

“Not only here?”

“Not only here.”

“You’re paying for me to go somewhere else twice.”

“I’m making sure the first place doesn’t have you merely because you can’t afford the second.”

Neris sat back. For a while she looked at the boots under the desk.

“Thank you,” she said.

Lyra let herself hear it. For a moment she left the papers alone. The thanks warmed something the room could not reach.

The messenger returned. The receiving house would honor the draft. Tesset made the alteration to the passage provision in both copies and left space for the house’s confirming mark. It would arrive before the vessel departed; otherwise Lyra would send coin in Neris’s own keeping.

Neris signed. Her hand was steady. When she finished, she folded the paper into the little pocket sewn inside her bag.

“Do they want a letter from you?” she asked.

“The employer?”

“Yes.”

“I can write one.”

“About the work. Not the rest.”

Lyra looked up.

“I’ll tell them the rest when I know them,” Neris said.

“Of course.”

She took a clean sheet and wrote about rope, judgment, and the ability to move frightened people without making their fear worse. She omitted the title, the congregation, and the things Neris had endured because of Lyra’s choices. Those belonged to the woman sitting opposite her.

Neris read the letter before accepting it.

“You make me sound expensive.”

“You are.”

“Good.”

They went down to the kitchen together.

Marda had saved a jar of the pickled onions Odel brought. She put it into Neris’s bag, took it out when she saw how the glass pressed against the folded clothes, wrapped it in another cloth, and put it back.

“Don’t open it on the boat unless you want everybody asking.”

“I could tell them no.”

“You could. I would, but you might not.”

Neris embraced her. The embrace lasted. Marda closed her eyes and put both hands against the younger woman’s back, holding her without instructions. When they separated, each wiped her face with the heel of a hand and pretended the damp belonged to the changes they could no longer conceal.

Lyra stayed by the door. She had come to say something about the luggage cart and found herself unwilling to interrupt.

The kitchen had changed since the feast. Lower shelves held the things Marda reached for most often. A small table stood near the open door for people who needed the cooler air. Another had been placed by the stove for those who did not. Nobody was required to explain where they chose to sit.

At the lower table a man was carving the little brown cups used by the Chorus. He had asked for work when his hands could no longer manage a net in dry air. At first the cups split as they dried. Now he seasoned them beside the basin and complained that the wet ones took up more room than a whole boat’s worth of rope.

He looked up when Lyra entered.

“Lady. The new ones are ready.”

“Marda will tell you how many she needs.”

“They’re for the observance.”

Lyra stopped.

The man waited, a half-finished cup in his hands.

“Whose?”

“Ours. At the chapel. Jorad said we could use the cups if we paid the wood.”

“You can.”

“Will you come?”

Marda had turned toward her. Neris watched beside the packed bag.

“Not tonight,” Lyra said.

The man nodded and returned to the rim. He did not apologize for asking. Lyra was glad of that and uncomfortable with her gladness.

At the door Neris touched her sleeve.

“You could come down to the boat tomorrow.”

“I will.”

“If you’re free.”

“I’ll be free.”

Neris looked at her for a moment, then took the bag from Marda and went up to the room where she would sleep for one more night.

Sera came in the afternoon with a copy of the objections lodged against Lyra’s custody of the Gallery files.

The paper named the agreed conditions, the records Lyra had kept, and the people who could challenge their use. She read it looking for an exaggeration and found none.

“Who has seen this?” Lyra asked.

“The signatories. The recorder. People whose records we can identify without exposing them to strangers.”

“The Court will hear of it.”

“Yes.”

“They’ll use the disagreement.”

“They already know we disagree. Now other people know what about.”

Lyra read the proposed arrangement. Records of the Court’s crimes could be examined by appointed witnesses. Papers concerning private people would be identified under safeguards and returned or destroyed with their participation. Copies could not remain undisclosed in another room merely because Lyra called them working notes.

“You’ve been thorough,” she said.

“We said we would be.”

She laid the paper down. “I won’t sign it today.”

“It doesn’t need your signature to be an objection.”

Outside the office, somebody dropped a stack of plates. The crash was followed by a silence in which everybody waited for anger. Then Marda said that if the person was unhurt, the broken pieces could be put in the bucket already provided for the purpose.

Sera smiled at the voice. The expression disappeared when she looked back at Lyra.

“How is Callista?” Lyra asked.

“Sleeping better. Still ill.”

“Does she need anything?”

“She knows you asked.”

That was not an answer. Lyra accepted it as one.

Sera took a second paper from her case. The transfer account. It had been renewed for another interval. The boat could be hired again. The receiving room remained ready. The independent attendants were still prepared to come.

Lyra did not touch it.

“Nothing has changed,” she said.

“I know.”

Sera placed it beside the objections.

“The offer remains.”

When she left, Lyra put both papers in the drawer she used for matters that would require another meeting. She did not lock the drawer. There were other locks between it and the street.

At the quay the following morning, Neris was arguing with the vessel’s master about the location of her bag.

He wanted it below with the other passengers’ things. She wanted it where she could reach the medicines and the letter. When he complained that every passenger had a reason, she said she was pleased they had become accustomed to reasons and would like him to consider this one.

Lyra stood a little way off, holding the confirmed passage draft. She did not intervene.

The master found a place beneath the forward bench. Neris tested that she could reach it, nodded, and came ashore for the paper.

“The mark arrived,” Lyra said.

“Good.”

She showed Neris where the sum could be collected. The house would pay it without requiring a letter from Silkstrand. The instruction said so plainly. Neris folded it with the other papers.

For a while they watched the last cargo come aboard. A basket of hens complained from beneath a cloth. Someone had brought a small tree wrapped at the roots. Its leaves brushed a man’s face each time the boat moved, and he endured it twice before shifting his seat.

“Do you remember the first wound?” Neris asked.

“Yes.”

“You looked more frightened than I was.”

“I probably was.”

“I thought you were going to tell me to find somebody else.”

“So did I.”

Neris turned toward her. “I’m glad you didn’t.”

Lyra felt tears rise. She had not expected them here, among the hens and the small tree, with the master waiting for his last passenger.

“I am too.”

Neris hugged her carefully, keeping clear of the injured arm. Lyra held her with the other. She could feel the cool wetness of the younger woman’s hair against her cheek.

“Write if you want,” she said.

“I will if I have something to say.”

“You generally do.”

Neris laughed against her shoulder. Then she stepped away and climbed aboard.

The master drew in the gangplank. Lyra remained until the boat had passed the first turn. Neris stood beneath the little tree, one hand on the rail. She lifted that hand once before the wall hid her.

On the walk back, Lyra bought pears from a woman beside the fish market. She chose them one by one, turning each to check the soft places, and had them wrapped so they would not bruise on the way home.

The measuring room had two attendants now.

Dorel was teaching the second one to record the condition of the bands without touching them. Avenna had continued visiting despite the refused transfer. She came three afternoons a week, read or sang quietly when Aria agreed, and wrote down every answer she heard. Copies went to Sera. Lyra had permitted that arrangement. She sometimes found herself resenting it, then made herself read the copies too.

The crystal had not healed. The fine fracture remained beside Aria’s foot, supported and watched. Its presence kept everyone careful. The collecting bottle had been removed entirely, the closed valve covered by a small plate bearing the date and the witnesses to its sealing.

Lyra gave the pears to Dorel.

“For you both.”

He thanked her. The new attendant went to fetch the gloves, but Lyra had brought her own. She checked their seams before putting them on. One had begun to wear at the thumb; she made a note to replace it.

Aria stood within the light, one foot ahead of the other, hand raised toward a world that had continued around her. Lyra had come often enough that the angle of that hand no longer startled her. Today she noticed a little crease beneath the thumb, preserved as clearly as a line on a living palm.

“Neris has gone,” she said. “To shore work. She helped bring people out the night we moved you. She’s been paid, and the passage money is ready where she’ll need it.”

The low sound continued.

“I think she’ll like her aunt. They seem likely to quarrel usefully.”

Lyra smiled. The expression faded before she turned toward Dorel.

“Has anything changed?”

“The readings are the same. No widening. She answered Avenna yesterday.”

“What did she ask?”

Dorel looked at the other attendant, then at the board on which the questions were written.

“The journey. Whether she still wished it.”

Lyra took the board. She already knew its contents. Her own corrections remained along the lower edge, where a complicated sentence had been divided into two questions so no answer would have to carry more than it could.

She faced Aria.

“Do you still wish to go to the place described?”

One note.

“Do you wish to remain here while we seek another arrangement?”

Two notes.

The attendants waited. Dorel held the pencil but had not begun writing. Lyra nodded, and he recorded what they had heard.

“The receiving room remains available,” she told Aria. “The vessel can be hired again. Sera has kept the money separate.”

One note sounded. It had an eagerness that Lyra could not prove belonged to it. She did not write that down.

“I haven’t authorized the movement.”

The crystal’s faint song thinned. Lyra stood very still.

“There are people here who depend on this place remaining beyond the Prince’s reach. The Court is challenging our claim. Until we have a stronger position, I can’t release what keeps them careful.”

Two notes.

She had not asked a question. She knew what the answer opposed.

“I understand.”

The low tone held. Through it she heard the two notes again, separated with an effort that made a little whiteness gather beside the fracture.

“Don’t strain,” Lyra said. “You don’t need to keep answering. We’ve heard.”

She turned to Dorel.

“Put the request in today’s account.”

He wrote. The new attendant watched his hand.

Lyra went to the supports. She could see that one pad had worked slightly outward beneath the frame’s uneven weight. Dorel checked it when she pointed. Together they eased it back without moving the crystal. The ordinary care relieved her for a moment. Here was something wrong that she could make right, and the improvement did not require anyone to agree.

When it was done, she stood beside Aria’s raised hand once more.

“I’ll come tomorrow.”

No answer followed. The faint song remained, neither assent nor refusal.

Lyra took off the gloves and laid them flat so the seams would dry. She told the attendants to take their breaks separately and made sure the second man knew where his supper would be kept. Dorel asked whether Avenna’s next visit still stood.

“Yes,” Lyra said. “Let her come.”

At the outer door the guard waited while she passed. He looked beyond her toward the crystal, then at the key in her hand.

“Any change, my lady?”

“Not today.”

She closed the door, turned the key, and drew it back onto the cord beneath her collar.
